    • A patient presents with a painless, progressively enlarging cervical lymph node for 3 months with night sweats and weight loss. Dx: Suspected lymphoma — needs lymph node biopsy.
  20. Minimal Access & Laparoscopic Surgery
    • Describe the principles and physiology of pneumoperitoneum.
    • Explain the indications and contraindications for laparoscopic surgery.
    • Describe common laparoscopic instruments and their setup.
    • Explain the recognition and management of laparoscopic complications.
    • Describe the advantages and limitations of minimal access surgery.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient with symptomatic gallstones undergoes laparoscopic cholecystectomy through 3 small port-site incisions. Point: illustrates advantages of minimal access surgery.
    • During laparoscopic surgery, a patient develops sudden hypotension and subcutaneous emphysema. Dx: CO2 gas embolism — complication of pneumoperitoneum.
  21. Basics of Transplantation Surgery
    • Describe the principles of organ donation and recipient selection.
    • Explain the basic immunology of graft rejection.
    • Describe the principles of immunosuppression after transplantation.
    • Explain the common complications following organ transplantation.
    • Describe the ethical and legal aspects of organ transplantation.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A recipient develops fever and rising creatinine one week after a kidney transplant, with biopsy showing lymphocytic infiltration. Dx: Acute graft rejection.
    • A family is counseled regarding brain-death organ donation for a road traffic accident victim. Point: illustrates the ethical/legal process of deceased-donor transplantation.
Obstetrics &amp; Gynaecology
  1. Anatomy & Physiology of Reproduction
    • Describe the anatomy of the female reproductive organs.
    • Explain the physiology of the menstrual cycle.
    • Describe the physiological changes of pregnancy.
    • Explain the hormonal regulation of reproduction.
    • Describe the anatomy relevant to obstetric and gynecological procedures.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A resident identifies the ureter passing beneath the uterine artery during a hysterectomy. Point: illustrates the "water under the bridge" relationship critical to avoiding ureteric injury.
    • A woman undergoes a pelvic exam to assess uterine size for dating a pregnancy. Point: illustrates clinical application of pelvic anatomy.
  2. Antenatal Care & Normal Pregnancy
    • Perform a complete antenatal examination and risk assessment.
    • Describe the schedule and components of routine antenatal care.
    • Explain the diagnosis and confirmation of pregnancy.
    • Describe the nutritional and health advice given during pregnancy.
    • Explain the routine investigations performed during antenatal care.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A primigravida presents for her first antenatal visit at 12 weeks. Point: illustrates the standard antenatal booking investigation package.
    • A 28-year-old at 24 weeks gestation has an abnormal 75g OGTT result. Dx: Gestational diabetes mellitus.
  3. Abnormal Labour & Obstetric Emergencies
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of prolonged and obstructed labour.
    • Explain the management of malpresentation and malposition.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of umbilical cord prolapse.
    • Explain the approach to shoulder dystocia.
    • Describe the management of uterine rupture.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A woman with a previous cesarean section in labour develops sudden severe abdominal pain, cessation of contractions, and fetal distress. Dx: Uterine rupture — obstetric emergency.
    • During delivery, the fetal head retracts against the perineum ("turtle sign") after delivery of the head. Dx: Shoulder dystocia.
  4. Normal & Instrumental Delivery
    • Describe the mechanism and stages of normal labour.
    • Explain the conduct of a normal vaginal delivery.
    • Describe the indications and technique of instrumental delivery.
    • Explain the management of episiotomy and perineal tears.
    • Describe the active management of the third stage of labour.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A woman has a prolonged second stage of labour; forceps delivery is planned. Point: illustrates indications for instrumental delivery.
    • Immediately after delivery, oxytocin is given and controlled cord traction performed. Point: illustrates active management of the third stage to prevent postpartum hemorrhage.
  5. High-Risk Pregnancy & Medical Disorders in Pregnancy
    • Identify risk factors for a high-risk pregnancy.
    • Describe the management of diabetes and thyroid disease in pregnancy.
    • Explain the management of anemia in pregnancy.
    • Describe the management of cardiac disease complicating pregnancy.
    • Explain the approach to infections in pregnancy (TORCH).
    Clinical vignettes
    • A pregnant woman with pre-existing type 1 diabetes requires tight glycemic control throughout pregnancy. Point: illustrates management of pre-gestational diabetes.
    • A pregnant woman exposed to a febrile rash illness in the first trimester is worked up for congenital infection risk. Dx: Suspected TORCH infection.
  6. Antepartum & Postpartum Hemorrhage
    • Describe the causes and management of antepartum hemorrhage.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of placenta previa and abruption.
    • Describe the causes and management of postpartum hemorrhage.
    • Explain the use of uterotonic agents in hemorrhage management.
    • Describe the surgical options for uncontrolled postpartum hemorrhage.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A woman at 34 weeks presents with painless vaginal bleeding. Dx: Placenta previa.
    • A woman soon after delivery has a boggy, poorly contracted uterus with continued heavy bleeding. Dx: Postpartum hemorrhage due to uterine atony.
  7. Hypertensive Disorders of Pregnancy
    • Classify hypertensive disorders of pregnancy.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of preeclampsia.
    • Explain the management of eclampsia and its complications.
    • Describe the use of magnesium sulfate in eclampsia management.
    • Explain the timing and mode of delivery in hypertensive disorders.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A primigravida at 32 weeks presents with BP 160/110, proteinuria, and headache. Dx: Severe preeclampsia.
    • A woman with preeclampsia develops a generalized tonic-clonic seizure. Dx: Eclampsia — requires magnesium sulfate.
  8. Puerperium & Postpartum Care
    • Describe the physiological changes during the puerperium.
    • Explain the routine postpartum care and advice.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of puerperal sepsis.
    • Explain the management of postpartum psychiatric disorders.
    • Describe postpartum contraceptive counseling.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A woman on postpartum day 3 develops fever, foul-smelling lochia, and uterine tenderness. Dx: Puerperal sepsis.
    • A breastfeeding mother is counseled about the lactational amenorrhea method before starting other contraception. Point: illustrates postpartum contraceptive planning.
  9. Fetal Growth, Surveillance & Disorders
    • Describe the methods of fetal growth assessment.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of intrauterine growth restriction.
    • Describe the methods of antenatal fetal surveillance.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of multiple pregnancy.
    • Describe the approach to fetal congenital anomalies.
    Clinical vignettes
    • Ultrasound at 36 weeks shows estimated fetal weight below the 10th percentile with reduced amniotic fluid. Dx: Fetal growth restriction.
    • A routine 20-week anomaly scan reveals absence of the cranial vault. Dx: Anencephaly.
  10. Infertility & Assisted Reproduction
    • Describe the causes of male and female infertility.
    • Explain the basic investigations for an infertile couple.
    • Describe the principles of ovulation induction.
    • Explain the basic concepts of assisted reproductive techniques.
    • Describe the counseling required for infertile couples.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A couple presents after 2 years of unprotected intercourse without conception; the woman has irregular cycles. Point: illustrates systematic infertility workup of both partners.
    • A woman with tubal blockage on hysterosalpingography is counseled about in-vitro fertilization. Point: illustrates assisted reproduction options.
  11. Menstrual Disorders & Endocrinology
    • Describe the causes and management of abnormal uterine bleeding.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of amenorrhea.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of polycystic ovary syndrome.
    • Explain the approach to a patient with dysmenorrhea.
    • Describe the hormonal basis of common menstrual disorders.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A young woman presents with irregular periods, hirsutism, and acne; ultrasound shows multiple small ovarian follicles. Dx: Polycystic ovary syndrome.
    • A 45-year-old presents with heavy, prolonged menstrual bleeding without any structural pathology found. Dx: Dysfunctional uterine bleeding.
  12. Genital Tract Infections
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of vaginal discharge.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of pelvic inflammatory disease.
    • Describe common sexually transmitted infections affecting the genital tract.
    • Explain the management of genital tract tuberculosis.
    • Describe prevention strategies for genital tract infections.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A woman presents with foul-smelling vaginal discharge, itching, and dyspareunia. Dx: Vaginitis — requires workup for candidiasis/trichomoniasis.
    • A woman presents with fever, lower abdominal pain, and cervical motion tenderness. Dx: Pelvic inflammatory disease.
  13. Benign & Malignant Gynecological Tumors
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of uterine fibroids.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of ovarian cysts and tumors.
    • Describe the screening and management of cervical cancer.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of endometrial carcinoma.
    • Describe the staging and treatment principles of gynecological cancers.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A postmenopausal woman presents with postmenopausal bleeding; endometrial biopsy shows malignancy. Dx: Endometrial carcinoma.
    • A woman in her 50s presents with abdominal distension, early satiety, and a complex adnexal mass with raised CA-125. Dx: Suspected ovarian carcinoma.
  14. Uterovaginal Prolapse & Urogynecology
    • Describe the classification and diagnosis of uterovaginal prolapse.
    • Explain the conservative and surgical management of prolapse.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of urinary incontinence.
    • Explain the approach to a patient with pelvic floor dysfunction.
    • Describe the complications of prolapse surgery.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A multiparous woman complains of a "something coming down" sensation in the vagina, worse on straining. Dx: Uterine prolapse.
    • A woman leaks urine on coughing or sneezing but has no associated urgency. Dx: Stress urinary incontinence.
  15. Contraception & Family Planning
    • Describe the mechanism and use of various contraceptive methods.
    • Explain counseling for contraceptive choice.
    • Describe the complications and contraindications of hormonal contraceptives.
    • Explain the use of intrauterine devices.
    • Describe permanent methods of contraception.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A woman requests a reversible, long-acting contraceptive method after her second child. Point: illustrates counseling on IUCD or implants.
    • A couple who have completed their family opt for a permanent method. Point: illustrates counseling on tubal ligation/vasectomy.
  16. Medical Termination of Pregnancy
    • Describe the legal provisions for medical termination of pregnancy.
    • Explain the methods of first and second trimester termination.
    • Describe the complications of termination of pregnancy.
    • Explain the counseling required before and after termination.
    • Describe post-abortion care and contraceptive counseling.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A woman at 8 weeks gestation requests termination of an unwanted pregnancy within the legal gestational limit. Point: illustrates MTP Act eligibility and counseling.
    • A woman presents with incomplete abortion and heavy bleeding after taking abortion pills without medical supervision. Point: illustrates complications of unsafe abortion.
  17. Operative Obstetrics & Gynecological Surgery
    • Describe the indications and technique of cesarean section.
    • Explain the complications of cesarean delivery.
    • Describe the indications and technique of hysterectomy.
    • Explain the principles of laparoscopic gynecological surgery.
    • Describe the perioperative care in gynecological surgery.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A woman with fetal distress in labour requires an emergency cesarean section. Point: illustrates indications for operative delivery.
    • A woman with symptomatic large fibroids not desiring future fertility undergoes hysterectomy. Point: illustrates surgical management of fibroids.
  18. Multiple Pregnancy
    • Describe the diagnosis and classification of multiple pregnancy.
    • Explain the antenatal management of twin pregnancy.
    • Describe the complications specific to multiple pregnancy.
    • Explain the management of labour and delivery in multiple pregnancy.
    • Describe the fetal complications associated with multiple pregnancy.
    Clinical vignettes
    • Ultrasound at 12 weeks shows a twin pregnancy with a single placenta and a thin dividing membrane. Dx: Monochorionic diamniotic twins — at risk of twin-to-twin transfusion syndrome.
    • A twin pregnancy presents in preterm labour at 32 weeks. Point: illustrates the increased risk of preterm birth in multiple pregnancy.
  19. Preterm Labour
    • Describe the risk factors and diagnosis of preterm labour.
    • Explain the principles of tocolytic therapy.
    • Describe the use of antenatal corticosteroids for fetal lung maturity.
    • Explain the management of preterm premature rupture of membranes.
    • Describe strategies for the prevention of preterm birth.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A woman at 30 weeks presents with regular uterine contractions and cervical dilatation. Dx: Preterm labour — requires tocolysis and steroids for fetal lung maturity.
    • A woman with a history of previous preterm birth is started on prophylactic progesterone in the current pregnancy. Point: illustrates a preterm birth prevention strategy.
  20. Malposition & Malpresentation
    • Describe the diagnosis of malposition and malpresentation in labour.
    • Explain the management of occipitoposterior position.
    • Describe the management of breech presentation.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of transverse lie and shoulder presentation.
    • Describe the indications for external cephalic version.
    Clinical vignettes
    • On abdominal examination at 36 weeks, the fetal head is palpated in the fundus and the breech in the pelvis. Dx: Breech presentation.
    • A woman in labour has a prolonged first stage with the fetal occiput persistently posterior. Dx: Occipitoposterior position.
  21. Menopause & Perimenopausal Disorders
    • Describe the physiology and clinical features of menopause.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of perimenopausal bleeding.
    • Describe the indications and risks of hormone replacement therapy.
    • Explain the long-term health consequences of menopause.
    • Describe the non-hormonal management of menopausal symptoms.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A 50-year-old woman presents with hot flashes, night sweats, and irregular cycles over the past year. Dx: Perimenopausal syndrome.
    • A postmenopausal woman on long-term estrogen therapy is evaluated for the risks and benefits of continuing hormone replacement therapy. Point: illustrates HRT risk-benefit counseling.
Paediatrics
  1. Growth & Development
    • Assess growth using standard growth charts.
    • Describe the normal developmental milestones in children.
    • Explain the causes and evaluation of failure to thrive.
    • Describe the assessment of developmental delay.
    • Explain factors affecting normal growth and development.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A 2-year-old's weight and height are plotted on a growth chart and found below the 3rd percentile. Dx: Failure to thrive — needs evaluation.
    • A 9-month-old cannot sit without support and shows no response to name. Dx: Developmental delay — needs assessment.
  2. Newborn Care & Neonatology
    • Perform initial assessment and resuscitation of a newborn.
    • Describe the routine care of a normal newborn.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of neonatal jaundice.
    • Describe the management of a preterm and low birth weight infant.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of neonatal sepsis.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A newborn develops poor feeding, lethargy, and temperature instability on day 3 of life. Dx: Neonatal sepsis.
    • A preterm baby develops respiratory distress with grunting and retractions shortly after birth. Dx: Respiratory distress syndrome (surfactant deficiency).
  3. Nutrition & Nutritional Disorders in Children
    • Describe the principles of infant and young child feeding.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of protein-energy malnutrition.
    • Describe the management of micronutrient deficiency disorders in children.
    • Explain the WHO classification of malnutrition.
    • Describe complementary feeding practices.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A 2-year-old presents with bilateral pitting pedal edema, thin sparse hair, and a distended abdomen. Dx: Kwashiorkor.
    • A severely wasted infant with visible ribs and loose skin folds is brought in. Dx: Marasmus.
  4. Immunization
    • Describe the national immunization schedule.
    • Explain the storage and administration of vaccines.
    • Describe the contraindications and adverse effects of vaccines.
    • Explain the concept of cold chain maintenance.
    • Describe catch-up immunization schedules.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A mother brings her 6-week-old infant for scheduled vaccines. Point: illustrates the national immunization schedule.
    • A 3-year-old who missed vaccines due to migration needs a catch-up schedule. Point: illustrates catch-up immunization planning.
  5. Fluid, Electrolyte & Nutritional Rehabilitation
    • Describe fluid and electrolyte management in a sick child.
    • Explain the assessment and management of dehydration.
    • Describe the principles of nutritional rehabilitation in severe malnutrition.
    • Explain the use of oral rehydration therapy.
    • Describe the management of electrolyte imbalances in children.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A child with severe watery diarrhea presents with sunken eyes, lethargy, and a very slow skin pinch. Dx: Severe dehydration.
    • A malnourished child undergoing nutritional rehabilitation develops sudden heart failure after aggressive refeeding. Dx: Refeeding syndrome.
  6. Infectious Diseases in Children
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of common childhood infections.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of pediatric tuberculosis.
    • Describe the approach to a febrile child.
    • Explain the management of common exanthematous fevers.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of pediatric HIV.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A 4-year-old presents with high fever, a rash starting on the face, and conjunctivitis. Dx: Measles.
    • A 6-year-old presents with fever, sore throat, and a diffuse fine sandpaper-like rash. Dx: Scarlet fever.
  7. Respiratory Disorders
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of pneumonia in children.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of bronchiolitis.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of childhood asthma.
    • Explain the approach to a child with respiratory distress.
    • Describe the management of croup and epiglottitis.
    Clinical vignettes
    • An 18-month-old presents with barking cough, stridor, and hoarse voice, worse at night. Dx: Croup (laryngotracheobronchitis).
    • A 3-year-old presents with high fever, drooling, and preferring to sit leaning forward. Dx: Acute epiglottitis — emergency.
  8. Cardiovascular Disorders & Congenital Heart Disease
    • Describe the clinical presentation of common congenital heart diseases.
    • Explain the approach to a child with cyanosis.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of rheumatic fever.
    • Explain the management of congestive heart failure in children.
    • Describe the indications for referral in congenital heart disease.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A newborn is noted to be cyanotic with a harsh systolic murmur; echo confirms a cardiac defect. Dx: Tetralogy of Fallot (cyanotic CHD).
    • An infant has recurrent chest infections, poor feeding, and a continuous murmur at the left upper sternal border. Dx: Patent ductus arteriosus.
  9. Gastrointestinal Disorders
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of acute diarrhea in children.
    • Explain the approach to a child with chronic diarrhea.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of common surgical GI conditions in children.
    • Explain the approach to a child with abdominal pain.
    • Describe the management of GI parasitic infections.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A 3-week-old boy has projectile non-bilious vomiting after every feed with visible peristalsis. Dx: Hypertrophic pyloric stenosis.
    • A 6-month-old has recurrent colicky abdominal pain, drawing up of legs, and red currant jelly stools. Dx: Intussusception.
  10. Renal & Urinary Disorders
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of nephrotic syndrome in children.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of acute glomerulonephritis.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of urinary tract infection in children.
    • Explain the approach to a child with hematuria.
    • Describe the management of acute kidney injury in children.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A 5-year-old develops facial puffiness, cola-colored urine, and hypertension a week after a sore throat. Dx: Post-streptococcal acute glomerulonephritis.
    • A 3-year-old presents with generalized edema and heavy proteinuria with normal blood pressure. Dx: Minimal change nephrotic syndrome.
  11. Hematological Disorders
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of anemia in children.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of childhood leukemia.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of hemophilia.
    • Explain the approach to a child with bleeding manifestations.
    • Describe the management of thalassemia.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A 1-year-old presents with pallor, growth failure, and hepatosplenomegaly; hemoglobin electrophoresis shows raised HbF. Dx: Beta-thalassemia major.
    • A 3-year-old boy presents with excessive bruising and hemarthrosis after a minor fall. Dx: Hemophilia.
  12. Neurological Disorders
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of febrile seizures.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of childhood epilepsy.
    • Describe the approach to a child with developmental regression.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of cerebral palsy.
    • Describe the approach to a child with acute encephalopathy.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A 6-month-old presents with recurrent brief flexor spasms in clusters, with regression of milestones. Dx: Infantile spasms (West syndrome).
    • A 2-year-old presents with fever, neck stiffness, bulging fontanelle, and irritability. Dx: Acute bacterial meningitis.
  13. Endocrine & Metabolic Disorders
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of type 1 diabetes mellitus in children.
    • Explain the approach to a child with short stature.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of congenital hypothyroidism.
    • Explain the approach to ambiguous genitalia.
    • Describe the screening for inborn errors of metabolism.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A newborn on routine screening has a raised TSH with normal T4. Dx: Congenital hypothyroidism — needs urgent treatment to prevent developmental delay.
    • A neonate develops poor feeding, vomiting, and lethargy after starting milk feeds, with an unusual odor to the urine. Dx: Suspected inborn error of metabolism (e.g., maple syrup urine disease).
  14. Genetic Disorders & Congenital Malformations
    • Describe the clinical features of common chromosomal disorders.
    • Explain the approach to a child with dysmorphic features.
    • Describe the diagnosis and counseling for genetic disorders.
    • Explain the principles of antenatal diagnosis of congenital anomalies.
    • Describe the management approach to common birth defects.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A newborn has upward slanting eyes, a single palmar crease, and hypotonia. Dx: Down syndrome (Trisomy 21).
    • A newborn has a midline defect over the lower back with an underlying meningeal sac. Dx: Spina bifida (meningomyelocele).
  15. Behavioral & Developmental Pediatrics
    • Describe the assessment of common behavioral problems in children.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of attention deficit hyperactivity disorder.
    • Describe the identification and management of autism spectrum disorder.
    • Explain the approach to learning disabilities.
    • Describe strategies for positive parenting guidance.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A 3-year-old boy has poor eye contact, delayed speech, and repetitive hand-flapping movements. Dx: Autism spectrum disorder.
    • A 7-year-old has difficulty sitting still, is easily distracted, and impulsive both at home and school. Dx: Attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder.
  16. Pediatric Emergencies & Critical Care
    • Perform pediatric basic life support.
    • Describe the recognition and management of shock in children.
    • Explain the management of status epilepticus in children.
    • Describe the approach to a critically ill child.
    • Explain the principles of pediatric triage.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A choking 2-year-old suddenly cannot cry or cough and turns cyanotic. Point: illustrates need for immediate pediatric foreign-body airway management.
    • A febrile child presents with a single generalized seizure lasting 3 minutes that stops spontaneously. Dx: Simple febrile seizure.
  17. Social & Community Pediatrics
    • Describe the impact of social determinants on child health.
    • Explain the recognition and management of child abuse and neglect.
    • Describe national programmes for child health.
    • Explain the role of school health services.
    • Describe strategies for community-based management of childhood illness.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A community health worker uses IMNCI guidelines to assess a sick young infant. Point: illustrates integrated management of neonatal and childhood illness.
    • A child from a low-income family presents with recurrent respiratory infections linked to indoor air pollution and overcrowding. Point: illustrates social determinants of child health.
  18. Adolescent Medicine
    • Describe the physical and psychosocial changes of adolescence.
    • Explain the approach to common adolescent health problems.
    • Describe the assessment and management of adolescent reproductive health issues.
    • Explain the recognition of risk-taking behavior in adolescents.
    • Describe strategies for adolescent-friendly health services.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A 15-year-old girl presents confidentially with concerns about delayed menarche. Point: illustrates adolescent-friendly, confidential counseling.
    • A 16-year-old boy is brought by his parents for risk-taking behavior and substance experimentation. Point: illustrates adolescent psychosocial assessment (HEADSS).
  19. Pediatric HIV & AIDS
    • Describe the modes of transmission of HIV in children.
    • Explain the diagnosis of HIV infection in infants and children.
    • Describe the principles of antiretroviral therapy in children.
    • Explain the prevention of mother-to-child transmission of HIV.
    • Describe the management of opportunistic infections in HIV-infected children.
    Clinical vignettes
    • An infant born to an HIV-positive mother is started on prophylactic antiretroviral therapy at birth. Point: illustrates prevention of mother-to-child transmission.
    • An HIV-infected child presents with chronic cough, fever, and oral thrush unresponsive to standard treatment. Dx: Opportunistic infection in pediatric HIV/AIDS.
Orthopaedics
  1. Basic Sciences — Bone, Joint & Fracture Healing
    • Describe the structure and physiology of bone.
    • Explain the stages of fracture healing.
    • Describe the classification of joints and their biomechanics.
    • Explain factors affecting fracture healing.
    • Describe the principles of bone remodeling.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A postmenopausal woman sustains a distal radius fracture after a minor fall. Point: illustrates a fragility fracture due to reduced bone density.
    • An X-ray of a healing fracture at 6 weeks shows callus formation bridging the fracture line. Point: illustrates normal fracture healing stages.
  2. Principles of Fracture Management
    • Describe the classification of fractures.
    • Explain the principles of fracture reduction and immobilization.
    • Describe the indications for conservative versus operative management.
    • Explain the complications of fractures and their management.
    • Describe the principles of internal and external fixation.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A young man with an open tibial fracture after a road accident is managed with debridement and external fixation. Point: illustrates open fracture management principles.
    • An elderly woman with an intertrochanteric femur fracture undergoes internal fixation with a dynamic hip screw. Point: illustrates fixation choice by fracture pattern.
  3. Upper Limb Trauma
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of common upper limb fractures.
    • Explain the management of shoulder dislocation.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of elbow injuries.
    • Explain the management of wrist and hand fractures.
    • Describe complications specific to upper limb trauma.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A child falls on an outstretched hand and presents with a swollen, deformed elbow and an absent radial pulse. Dx: Supracondylar fracture of the humerus with vascular compromise.
    • After a mid-shaft humerus fracture, a patient cannot extend the wrist and fingers. Dx: Radial nerve injury (wrist drop).
  4. Lower Limb Trauma
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of common lower limb fractures.
    • Explain the management of hip fractures and dislocations.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of knee ligament injuries.
    • Explain the management of ankle and foot fractures.
    • Describe complications specific to lower limb trauma.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A young man on a motorcycle sustains a closed femoral shaft fracture with a tense, painful thigh. Point: raises concern for compartment syndrome/fat embolism.
    • An elderly woman after a fall presents with a shortened, externally rotated, and adducted lower limb. Dx: Fracture neck of femur.
  5. Spine Trauma & Disorders
    • Describe the initial assessment of a patient with suspected spinal injury.
    • Explain the classification and management of spinal fractures.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of common spinal disorders (e.g., disc prolapse).
    • Explain the assessment of spinal cord injury.
    • Describe the principles of spinal immobilization.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A man after a diving accident presents with loss of motor and sensory function below the neck. Dx: Cervical spinal cord injury.
    • An elderly osteoporotic patient presents with back pain and sudden loss of height after a minor fall. Dx: Osteoporotic vertebral compression fracture.
  6. Pelvic & Acetabular Injuries
    • Describe the classification of pelvic fractures.
    • Explain the initial management of a patient with pelvic trauma.
    • Describe the complications of pelvic fractures.
    • Explain the approach to acetabular fractures.
    • Describe the indications for surgical fixation of pelvic injuries.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient after a high-speed collision presents with pelvic instability and hypotension. Dx: Unstable pelvic fracture with hemorrhage — life-threatening.
    • A patient sustains a fall directly onto the hip with the femur driven into the pelvis. Dx: Acetabular fracture.
  7. Joint Dislocations
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of shoulder dislocation.
    • Explain the management of hip dislocation.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of knee dislocation.
    • Explain the complications of joint dislocations.
    • Describe the principles of closed reduction techniques.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A young man falls on an outstretched abducted arm and presents with a flattened shoulder contour and inability to move the arm. Dx: Anterior shoulder dislocation.
    • A patient after a dashboard injury in a road accident presents with a flexed, adducted, internally rotated hip. Dx: Posterior hip dislocation.
  8. Bone & Joint Infections
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of acute osteomyelitis.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of septic arthritis.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of tuberculosis of bone and joint.
    • Explain the approach to chronic osteomyelitis.
    • Describe the principles of antibiotic therapy in bone infections.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A child presents with high fever, severe pain, and refusal to move one limb, with local warmth and swelling near a long bone. Dx: Acute hematogenous osteomyelitis.
    • A patient has a chronically discharging sinus over a previously fractured bone. Dx: Chronic osteomyelitis.
  9. Bone Tumors
    • Describe the classification of primary bone tumors.
    • Explain the clinical and radiological features of common bone tumors.
    • Describe the approach to a patient with a suspected bone tumor.
    • Explain the principles of biopsy in bone tumors.
    • Describe the management of metastatic bone disease.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A teenager presents with progressive knee pain and swelling, with X-ray showing a "sunburst" periosteal reaction. Dx: Osteosarcoma.
    • An elderly man with known prostate cancer develops new severe back pain; X-ray shows sclerotic bone lesions. Dx: Metastatic bone disease.
  10. Metabolic Bone Disease
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of osteoporosis.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of rickets and osteomalacia.
    • Describe the orthopedic manifestations of hyperparathyroidism.
    • Explain the approach to a patient with pathological fracture.
    • Describe the role of bone density scanning.
    Clinical vignettes
    • An elderly woman sustains a fracture after a trivial fall; DEXA scan shows a T-score of -3.0. Dx: Osteoporosis.
    • A child presents with bowing of the legs, delayed walking, and widened wrist joints. Dx: Rickets.
  11. Pediatric Orthopedics & Congenital Deformities
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of developmental dysplasia of hip.
    • Explain the management of clubfoot.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of common pediatric limb deformities.
    • Explain the approach to limb length discrepancy.
    • Describe the management of pediatric fractures.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A newborn is noted to have asymmetric thigh folds and a positive Ortolani/Barlow test. Dx: Developmental dysplasia of the hip.
    • A 2-year-old presents with a rigid, medially deviated foot noted since birth. Dx: Congenital talipes equinovarus (clubfoot).
  12. Degenerative Joint Disease & Arthroplasty
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of osteoarthritis.
    • Explain the indications for joint replacement surgery.
    • Describe the complications of arthroplasty.
    • Explain the principles of rehabilitation after joint replacement.
    • Describe the conservative management options for degenerative joint disease.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A 65-year-old obese woman presents with knee pain worse with activity and morning stiffness lasting less than 30 minutes. Dx: Osteoarthritis of the knee.
    • An elderly patient with severe disabling hip osteoarthritis unresponsive to conservative treatment is planned for total hip replacement. Point: illustrates indication for arthroplasty.
  13. Sports Injuries & Arthroscopy
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of common sports injuries.
    • Explain the indications for arthroscopic surgery.
    • Describe the management of meniscal and ligamentous knee injuries.
    • Explain the principles of sports injury rehabilitation.
    • Describe the prevention strategies for sports-related injuries.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A young footballer twists his knee, hears a pop, and develops swelling and instability. Dx: Anterior cruciate ligament tear.
    • A basketball player has recurrent shoulder dislocations during overhead movements. Dx: Recurrent shoulder instability.
  14. Peripheral Nerve Injuries
    • Describe the classification of peripheral nerve injuries.
    • Explain the clinical assessment of common nerve injuries.
    • Describe the management options for nerve injury.
    • Explain the expected recovery patterns following nerve injury.
    • Describe the role of nerve conduction studies in diagnosis.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient after prolonged pressure over the outer knee (e.g., a plaster cast) develops foot drop. Dx: Common peroneal nerve palsy.
    • A patient presents with numbness of the lateral 3½ fingers and thenar wasting, worse at night. Dx: Carpal tunnel syndrome (median nerve compression).
  15. Amputations & Rehabilitation
    • Describe the indications for amputation.
    • Explain the levels and techniques of amputation.
    • Describe the principles of prosthetic rehabilitation.
    • Explain the management of phantom limb pain.
    • Describe the psychosocial aspects of amputation.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A diabetic patient with a gangrenous foot undergoes below-knee amputation. Point: illustrates indications and level selection for amputation.
    • A patient after limb amputation reports persistent pain sensation in the missing limb. Dx: Phantom limb pain.
  16. Orthopedic Emergencies
    • Describe the recognition and management of compartment syndrome.
    • Explain the management of open fractures.
    • Describe the approach to a patient with fat embolism syndrome.
    • Explain the management of neurovascular injury in trauma.
    • Describe the initial management of a polytrauma patient with orthopedic injuries.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient with a tibial fracture develops severe pain out of proportion, pain on passive stretch, and tense swelling of the leg. Dx: Compartment syndrome — surgical emergency.
    • A patient with an open fracture and a heavily contaminated wound is assessed for urgent debridement. Point: illustrates open fracture emergency management.
  17. Disorders of the Hand & Foot
    • Describe the common congenital and acquired deformities of the hand.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of common foot deformities.
    • Describe the management of diabetic foot.
    • Explain the approach to a patient with hand trauma.
    • Describe the common tendon and nerve injuries of the hand.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient with a palm laceration is unable to flex the fingertip at the distal joint. Dx: Flexor digitorum profundus tendon injury.
    • A middle-aged patient presents with progressive flexion contracture of the ring and little fingers. Dx: Dupuytren's contracture.
  18. Prosthetics, Orthotics & Assistive Devices
    • Describe the principles of prosthetic fitting after amputation.
    • Explain the indications for common orthotic devices.
    • Describe the role of assistive devices in mobility rehabilitation.
    • Explain the team approach to prosthetic and orthotic rehabilitation.
    • Describe the complications associated with prosthetic use.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A below-knee amputee develops skin breakdown at the prosthetic socket contact area. Point: illustrates prosthetic fitting complications.
    • A child with cerebral palsy is fitted with an ankle-foot orthosis to improve gait. Point: illustrates orthotic use in pediatric rehabilitation.
Ophthalmology
  1. Anatomy & Physiology of the Eye
    • Describe the anatomy of the eyeball and its layers.
    • Explain the physiology of aqueous humor formation and drainage.
    • Describe the anatomy of the visual pathway.
    • Explain the accommodation and pupillary reflex mechanisms.
    • Describe the blood supply and innervation of the eye.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient with sudden painless loss of vision is found to have a pale retina with a cherry-red spot on fundoscopy. Dx: Central retinal artery occlusion.
    • A patient with diplopia and ptosis is found to have a dilated, non-reactive pupil. Point: illustrates localization of third cranial nerve palsy.
  2. Optics & Refraction
    • Describe the principles of ocular optics.
    • Explain the types and correction of refractive errors.
    • Describe the procedure for visual acuity testing.
    • Explain the principles of prescribing spectacles and contact lenses.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of presbyopia.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A 45-year-old with previously normal vision now has difficulty reading fine print up close. Dx: Presbyopia.
    • A child struggles to see the blackboard clearly from the back of the classroom. Dx: Myopia — needs refraction correction.
  3. Diseases of the Eyelids & Lacrimal Apparatus
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of common eyelid infections.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of ptosis.
    • Describe the causes and management of epiphora.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of dacryocystitis.
    • Describe common eyelid malpositions and their management.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient presents with a painful red swelling at the eyelid margin near the eyelash line. Dx: Stye (external hordeolum).
    • An elderly patient has the eyelid margin turned inward with lashes rubbing against the cornea. Dx: Entropion.
  4. Conjunctiva & Cornea Disorders
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of conjunctivitis.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of corneal ulcers.
    • Describe the approach to a red eye.
    • Explain the management of corneal foreign bodies and abrasions.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of keratitis.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient presents with redness, watering, and mucopurulent discharge in one eye that spreads to the other. Dx: Bacterial conjunctivitis.
    • A contact lens wearer presents with severe eye pain, photophobia, and a white corneal opacity. Dx: Microbial keratitis (corneal ulcer).
  5. Sclera & Uveal Tract Disorders
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of scleritis and episcleritis.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of anterior uveitis.
    • Describe the systemic associations of uveitis.
    • Explain the complications of uveitis.
    • Describe the approach to a patient with painful red eye and photophobia.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A young adult presents with painful red eye, photophobia, and a small irregular pupil with circumcorneal congestion. Dx: Acute anterior uveitis.
    • A patient presents with localized painful redness of the eye with a bluish-red hue over the sclera. Dx: Scleritis.
  6. Cataract & Lens Disorders
    • Describe the classification and clinical features of cataract.
    • Explain the indications and techniques for cataract surgery.
    • Describe the complications of cataract surgery.
    • Explain the management of congenital cataract.
    • Describe the postoperative care following cataract surgery.
    Clinical vignettes
    • An elderly patient presents with gradual, painless blurring of vision and a whitish pupillary reflex. Dx: Age-related cataract.
    • A patient a few days after cataract surgery presents with sudden pain, redness, and reduced vision. Dx: Postoperative endophthalmitis — emergency.
  7. Glaucoma
    • Describe the classification and pathophysiology of glaucoma.
    • Explain the diagnosis and screening for glaucoma.
    • Describe the medical and surgical management of glaucoma.
    • Explain the diagnosis and emergency management of acute angle closure glaucoma.
    • Describe the follow-up and monitoring of glaucoma patients.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient presents with sudden severe eye pain, headache, vomiting, and a mid-dilated fixed pupil with a hard eyeball. Dx: Acute angle-closure glaucoma.
    • An elderly patient is found on routine screening to have gradually progressive peripheral vision loss with raised intraocular pressure. Dx: Primary open-angle glaucoma.
  8. Vitreous & Retinal Disorders
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of diabetic retinopathy.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of retinal detachment.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of age-related macular degeneration.
    • Explain the approach to a patient with sudden visual loss.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of hypertensive retinopathy.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient with long-standing diabetes presents with gradually worsening vision; fundoscopy shows microaneurysms and hemorrhages. Dx: Diabetic retinopathy.
    • A patient describes sudden flashes of light, floaters, and a curtain-like shadow over part of the visual field. Dx: Retinal detachment.
  9. Neuro-ophthalmology & Optic Nerve Disorders
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of optic neuritis.
    • Explain the approach to a patient with papilledema.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of optic atrophy.
    • Explain the assessment of visual field defects and their localization.
    • Describe the neuro-ophthalmic examination.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A young woman presents with sudden painful loss of vision in one eye, worse with eye movement. Dx: Optic neuritis.
    • A patient with a brain tumor is found to have bilateral optic disc swelling on fundoscopy. Dx: Papilledema due to raised intracranial pressure.
  10. Strabismus & Ocular Motility
    • Describe the classification and diagnosis of strabismus.
    • Explain the management of amblyopia.
    • Describe the assessment of ocular motility.
    • Explain the indications for surgical correction of strabismus.
    • Describe the approach to a child with squint.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A parent notices their 3-year-old's eyes are not aligned, with one eye turning inward. Dx: Convergent squint (esotropia) — needs early correction to prevent amblyopia.
    • A child fails to develop clear vision in one eye despite a normal eye exam, due to a long-standing untreated squint. Dx: Amblyopia.
  11. Orbital Diseases
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of orbital cellulitis.
    • Explain the approach to a patient with proptosis.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of thyroid eye disease.
    • Explain the approach to orbital trauma.
    • Describe common orbital tumors and their presentation.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A child presents with rapidly progressive proptosis, redness, pain, and fever. Dx: Orbital cellulitis.
    • A patient with thyroid disease presents with bilateral bulging eyes and lid retraction. Dx: Thyroid eye disease (Graves' ophthalmopathy).
  12. Ocular Trauma & Emergencies
    • Describe the initial management of ocular chemical injury.
    • Explain the management of a penetrating eye injury.
    • Describe the approach to a patient with sudden loss of vision.
    • Explain the management of ocular foreign bodies.
    • Describe the indications for emergency ophthalmic referral.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient presents after a chemical splash to the eye with severe pain and blurred vision. Point: illustrates the need for immediate copious irrigation before any other treatment.
    • A patient sustains a penetrating eye injury from a metal fragment while hammering. Dx: Open globe injury — surgical emergency.
  13. Community Ophthalmology & Blindness Control
    • Describe the causes and prevention of avoidable blindness.
    • Explain the national programme for control of blindness.
    • Describe the principles of community eye screening.
    • Explain the role of primary eye care in the community.
    • Describe strategies for prevention of childhood blindness.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A community vision screening camp identifies several children with uncorrected refractive errors. Point: illustrates the role of school eye screening programs.
    • A malnourished child presents with night blindness and dry, foamy patches on the conjunctiva. Dx: Vitamin A deficiency (xerophthalmia).
Otorhinolaryngology (ENT)
  1. Anatomy & Physiology of Ear, Nose & Throat
    • Describe the anatomy of the external, middle, and inner ear.
    • Explain the anatomy and physiology of the nose and paranasal sinuses.
    • Describe the anatomy of the pharynx and larynx.
    • Explain the physiology of hearing and balance.
    • Describe the anatomical basis of common ENT clinical examinations.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient with reduced hearing undergoes Rinne and Weber tuning fork tests to differentiate conductive from sensorineural loss. Point: illustrates clinical application of ENT anatomy.
    • An otoscopic exam is performed to visualize the tympanic membrane in a patient with ear pain. Point: illustrates standard ENT examination technique.
  2. Diseases of the External & Middle Ear
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of otitis externa.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of acute and chronic otitis media.
    • Describe the complications of middle ear infections.
    • Explain the management of tympanic membrane perforation.
    • Describe the surgical options for chronic ear disease.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A child presents with ear pain, fever, and a bulging red tympanic membrane after an upper respiratory infection. Dx: Acute otitis media.
    • A patient with long-standing ear discharge has a central tympanic membrane perforation. Dx: Chronic suppurative otitis media.
  3. Diseases of the Inner Ear & Hearing Loss
    • Describe the classification and evaluation of hearing loss.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of sensorineural hearing loss.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of Meniere's disease.
    • Explain the indications for hearing aids and cochlear implants.
    • Describe the approach to sudden sensorineural hearing loss.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient presents with episodic vertigo, tinnitus, and fluctuating hearing loss. Dx: Ménière's disease.
    • An elderly patient reports progressive bilateral hearing loss, worse for high-pitched sounds. Dx: Presbycusis.
  4. Diseases of the Nose & Paranasal Sinuses
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of allergic rhinitis.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of sinusitis.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of nasal polyps.
    • Explain the management of epistaxis.
    • Describe the approach to nasal obstruction.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient presents with facial pain, purulent nasal discharge, and fever after a cold that hasn't resolved in 2 weeks. Dx: Acute bacterial sinusitis.
    • A young adult has persistent one-sided nasal obstruction with a fleshy, pale, insensitive mass on examination. Dx: Nasal polyp.
  5. Diseases of the Oral Cavity & Pharynx
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of tonsillitis and its complications.
    • Explain the approach to a patient with a sore throat.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of adenoid hypertrophy.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of oral cavity and pharyngeal lesions.
    • Describe the indications for tonsillectomy and adenoidectomy.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A child presents with recurrent sore throat, fever, and enlarged tonsils with exudate. Dx: Recurrent acute tonsillitis — evaluate for tonsillectomy.
    • A young adult presents with severe unilateral throat pain, trismus, and a muffled "hot potato" voice. Dx: Peritonsillar abscess (quinsy).
  6. Diseases of the Larynx
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of laryngitis.
    • Explain the approach to a patient with hoarseness of voice.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of vocal cord nodules and polyps.
    • Explain the approach to laryngeal carcinoma.
    • Describe the indications for tracheostomy.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient presents with progressive hoarseness of voice for 3 months, a smoking history, and a neck mass. Dx: Suspected laryngeal carcinoma.
    • A patient with stridor and worsening breathing difficulty is planned for an emergency tracheostomy. Point: illustrates airway emergency management.
  7. Head & Neck Tumors
    • Describe the diagnosis and evaluation of a neck mass.
    • Explain the staging of head and neck cancers.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of nasopharyngeal carcinoma.
    • Explain the multidisciplinary management of head and neck malignancies.
    • Describe the follow-up care of head and neck cancer patients.
    Clinical vignettes
    • An elderly tobacco chewer presents with a non-healing ulcer on the lateral border of the tongue. Dx: Suspected oral cavity carcinoma.
    • A patient presents with a painless enlarging neck node found to be metastatic squamous cell carcinoma with no obvious primary. Dx: Occult primary head and neck malignancy — needs further workup.
  8. Salivary Gland Disorders
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of sialadenitis.
    • Explain the diagnosis and management of salivary gland calculi.
    • Describe the approach to a salivary gland swelling.
    • Explain the classification of salivary gland tumors.
    • Describe the surgical management of salivary gland disorders.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient presents with painful swelling below the jaw that worsens while eating. Dx: Salivary gland (submandibular) calculus.
    • A patient presents with a slow-growing, painless swelling in front of the ear. Dx: Pleomorphic adenoma of the parotid gland.
  9. ENT Emergencies & Foreign Bodies
    • Describe the management of foreign bodies in the ear, nose, and throat.
    • Explain the emergency management of epistaxis.
    • Describe the management of stridor in a child.
    • Explain the emergency management of a patient with airway obstruction.
    • Describe the initial management of facial and neck trauma.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A toddler suddenly develops choking, coughing, and stridor while playing with small toys. Dx: Suspected foreign body aspiration.
    • A patient presents with profuse nosebleed after trauma, unresponsive to simple pressure. Dx: Severe epistaxis — needs nasal packing/further management.
  10. Audiology & Vestibular Disorders
    • Describe the common audiometric tests and their interpretation.
    • Explain the assessment of a patient with vertigo.
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of benign paroxysmal positional vertigo.
    • Explain the principles of vestibular rehabilitation.
    • Describe the role of audiological evaluation in hearing loss diagnosis.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A newborn fails the routine otoacoustic emission hearing screening test. Point: illustrates the importance of universal newborn hearing screening.
    • A patient presents with sudden severe spinning vertigo, nausea, and vomiting after a viral illness, without hearing loss. Dx: Vestibular neuritis.
  11. ENT Manifestations of Systemic Disease
    • Describe the ENT manifestations of granulomatous diseases.
    • Explain the ENT complications of diabetes mellitus.
    • Describe the ENT manifestations of HIV infection.
    • Explain the ENT features of autoimmune disorders.

    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient with poorly controlled diabetes presents with severe ear pain, discharge, and granulation tissue in the ear canal. Dx: Malignant otitis externa.
    • A patient with granulomatosis with polyangiitis presents with nasal crusting, epistaxis, and saddle nose deformity. Point: illustrates an ENT manifestation of systemic vasculitis.
Dermatology, Venereology &amp; Leprosy

  3. Infective Dermatoses — Bacterial & Fungal
    • Describe the diagnosis and management of common bacterial skin infections.

  4. Infective Dermatoses — Viral & Parasitic

  5. Eczema & Papulosquamous Disorders

  7. Disorders of Pigmentation

  12. Leprosy

  13. Sexually Transmitted Infections

Psychiatry
  1. Psychiatric History Taking & Mental Status Examination

    • A patient is reluctant to share personal history; the interviewer uses open-ended, non-judgmental questions to build trust. Point: illustrates rapport-building technique.
  2. Classification of Psychiatric Disorders
    • Describe the major classification systems used in psychiatry (ICD, DSM).

    • A community mental health survey estimates the prevalence of depression in a population. Point: illustrates psychiatric epidemiology.

    Clinical vignettes
    • A young man presents with auditory hallucinations commenting on his actions, delusions of persecution, and social withdrawal for 8 months. Dx: Schizophrenia.
    • A patient develops sudden psychotic symptoms after a major stressor, resolving within a month. Dx: Brief psychotic disorder.
  4. Mood Disorders
    • Describe the clinical features and diagnosis of major depressive disorder.

    Clinical vignettes

    • A patient presents with elevated mood, decreased need for sleep, grandiosity, and impulsive spending for a week. Dx: Manic episode (bipolar disorder).
  5. Anxiety, Stress & Somatoform Disorders

    • A patient presents with recurrent sudden episodes of intense fear, palpitations, and a feeling of impending doom lasting minutes. Dx: Panic disorder.
    • A patient presents with multiple unexplained physical complaints over years despite negative investigations, causing significant distress. Dx: Somatic symptom disorder.
  6. Substance Use Disorders

  7. Organic Mental Disorders & Dementia

  10. Critical Care & Ventilator Management

  11. Anesthetic Complications & Emergencies
    • Describe the recognition and management of anaphylaxis under anesthesia.
    • Explain the management of malignant hyperthermia.
    • Describe the recognition and management of aspiration during anesthesia.

    • Describe the management of awareness under anesthesia.
    Clinical vignettes

    • A patient develops sudden bronchospasm and desaturation shortly after intubation. Dx: Laryngospasm/bronchospasm — anesthetic emergency.
  12. Chronic Pain Medicine
    • Describe the classification and mechanisms of chronic pain.
    • Explain the pharmacological management of chronic pain.
    • Describe the role of interventional techniques in chronic pain management.
    • Explain the multidisciplinary approach to chronic pain management.
    • Describe the management of cancer-related chronic pain.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient with diabetic neuropathy reports persistent burning pain in both feet despite standard analgesics. Point: illustrates a neuropathic pain management approach.
    • A terminal cancer patient's pain is managed by stepping up the WHO analgesic ladder to strong opioids. Point: illustrates cancer pain management principles.

Residency chapters

Chapter-level breakdown for each residency subject — use these as the unit of mapping when building competencies above.

Internal Medicine Residency
  1. Acute Coronary Syndromes
    • Direct primary PCI and fibrinolytic decision-making in STEMI.
    • Manage NSTEMI and unstable angina using risk-stratified antithrombotic therapy.
    • Manage cardiogenic shock complicating acute myocardial infarction.
    • Diagnose and manage mechanical complications of MI (papillary muscle rupture, VSD, free wall rupture).
    • Direct secondary prevention pharmacotherapy after acute coronary syndrome.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A 58-year-old man presents with crushing central chest pain for 45 minutes; ECG shows ST elevation in V1–V4. Dx: Anterior STEMI — activate primary PCI pathway, target door-to-balloon <90 min.
    • Three days after an inferior MI a patient develops sudden hypotension and a new pansystolic murmur with pulmonary oedema. Dx: Papillary muscle rupture causing acute mitral regurgitation — needs emergency surgery.
  2. Heart Failure & Cardiomyopathy
    • Manage acute decompensated heart failure with reduced and preserved ejection fraction.
    • Direct guideline-directed medical therapy titration in chronic heart failure.
    • Manage dilated, hypertrophic, and restrictive cardiomyopathy.
    • Direct inotropic and mechanical circulatory support in cardiogenic shock.
    • Manage cardiorenal syndrome in advanced heart failure.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient with known HFrEF on optimal GDMT is admitted with worsening dyspnoea; after diuretic uptitration the creatinine rises sharply. Dx: Type 1 cardiorenal syndrome — balance decongestion against renal perfusion.
    • A previously well woman develops severe breathlessness and reduced ejection fraction two weeks after delivery. Dx: Peripartum cardiomyopathy.
  3. Arrhythmias & Syncope
    • Manage atrial fibrillation including rate/rhythm control and anticoagulation decisions.
    • Diagnose and manage ventricular tachycardia and sudden cardiac death risk.
    • Manage bradyarrhythmias requiring temporary or permanent pacing.
    • Direct the work-up of unexplained syncope.
    • Interpret indications for implantable cardioverter-defibrillator therapy.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A 70-year-old with palpitations has an irregularly irregular pulse and a CHA₂DS₂-VASc score of 4. Point: rate/rhythm control plus anticoagulation decision-making in atrial fibrillation.
    • A young athlete has recurrent exertional syncope, a family history of sudden death, and QTc 480 ms. Dx: Long QT syndrome — risk of torsades de pointes; restrict competitive sport pending evaluation.
  4. Valvular Disease, Endocarditis & Pericardial Disease
    • Manage severe aortic stenosis and mitral regurgitation including intervention timing.
    • Diagnose and manage infective endocarditis including embolic and valvular complications.
    • Manage acute and constrictive pericarditis and cardiac tamponade.
    • Direct anticoagulation management in prosthetic heart valves.
    • Manage rheumatic heart disease complications in adults.
    Clinical vignettes
    • An intravenous drug user presents with fever, a new murmur, and septic pulmonary emboli on chest X-ray. Dx: Tricuspid valve infective endocarditis.
    • A patient develops pleuritic chest pain relieved by leaning forward with diffuse concave ST elevation on ECG, days after a viral illness. Dx: Acute pericarditis — watch for pulsus paradoxus signalling tamponade.
  5. Airways Disease — Asthma & COPD
    • Manage status asthmaticus requiring escalation to non-invasive or invasive ventilation.
    • Direct step-up biologic therapy in severe eosinophilic asthma.
    • Manage acute COPD exacerbation with respiratory acidosis.
    • Direct long-term oxygen and pulmonary rehabilitation therapy in COPD.
    • Manage bronchiectasis and its infective exacerbations.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A young asthmatic arrives unable to complete sentences, with a silent chest and PEFR <33% predicted. Dx: Life-threatening acute severe asthma — needs continuous nebulisation, IV steroids, senior/ICU input.
    • A known COPD patient has worsening dyspnoea, purulent sputum, and arterial pH 7.28 with hypercapnia. Point: non-invasive ventilation is indicated for acute hypercapnic respiratory failure not responding to initial therapy.
  6. Interstitial Lung Disease & Pulmonary Vascular Disease
    • Direct the diagnostic work-up of idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis and other ILDs.
    • Manage acute and chronic pulmonary embolism including thrombolysis decisions.
    • Diagnose and manage pulmonary arterial hypertension.
    • Manage hypersensitivity pneumonitis and connective-tissue-associated ILD.
    • Direct long-term antifibrotic therapy in progressive fibrosing ILD.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A 65-year-old man has progressive exertional dyspnoea, bibasal Velcro-like crackles, and HRCT showing honeycombing. Dx: Idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis.
    • A post-operative patient develops sudden pleuritic chest pain, tachycardia, and hypoxia with a raised D-dimer. Dx: Acute pulmonary embolism — risk-stratify for thrombolysis versus anticoagulation alone.
  7. Critical Respiratory Failure & Pleural Disease
    • Direct lung-protective ventilation in ARDS.
    • Manage massive pleural effusion and empyema.
    • Manage tension and spontaneous pneumothorax.
    • Direct weaning strategies from prolonged mechanical ventilation.
    • Manage acute exacerbation of chronic respiratory failure requiring NIV.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A trauma patient develops sudden hypotension, distended neck veins, tracheal deviation, and absent breath sounds on one side. Dx: Tension pneumothorax — immediate needle decompression, do not wait for imaging.
    • A septic patient has bilateral infiltrates and a PaO₂/FiO₂ ratio <100 despite high inspired oxygen. Dx: Severe ARDS — institute lung-protective low tidal volume ventilation.
  8. Acute & Chronic Kidney Disease
    • Direct the diagnostic work-up and staging of acute kidney injury by etiology.
    • Manage rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis.
    • Direct long-term CKD management including anemia and mineral bone disease.
    • Manage contrast-induced and drug-induced nephrotoxicity.
    • Direct the timing and modality selection for renal replacement therapy.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient's creatinine rises 48 hours after an iodinated contrast CT, with oliguria. Dx: Contrast-induced nephropathy.
    • A patient presents with haemoptysis, haematuria, and rapidly rising creatinine; anti-GBM antibodies are positive. Dx: Anti-GBM disease (Goodpasture syndrome) causing rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis.
  9. Glomerular Disease & Renal Transplant Medicine
    • Manage nephrotic syndrome from membranous nephropathy and FSGS.
    • Manage IgA nephropathy and lupus nephritis.
    • Direct immunosuppression protocols in renal transplant recipients.
    • Manage acute and chronic transplant rejection.
    • Manage electrolyte disorders specific to transplant immunosuppression.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient has nephrotic-range proteinuria with normal complement levels; biopsy shows segmental sclerosis. Dx: Focal segmental glomerulosclerosis (FSGS).
    • A renal transplant recipient develops fever and a rising creatinine; biopsy shows interstitial tubulitis. Dx: Acute T-cell mediated (cellular) rejection.
  10. Upper & Lower GI Bleeding, Liver Failure
    • Direct endoscopic and pharmacologic management of variceal hemorrhage.
    • Manage non-variceal upper GI bleeding including peptic ulcer disease.
    • Manage acute liver failure and hepatic encephalopathy.
    • Direct the management of decompensated cirrhosis and hepatorenal syndrome.
    • Manage spontaneous bacterial peritonitis.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A known cirrhotic presents with haematemesis and hypotension. Point: resuscitate, start octreotide and prophylactic antibiotics, arrange urgent endoscopy for suspected variceal bleed.
    • A cirrhotic patient becomes acutely confused with asterixis and an elevated ammonia after a GI bleed. Dx: Hepatic encephalopathy precipitated by GI bleeding.
  11. Inflammatory Bowel Disease & Pancreatico-biliary Disease
    • Direct step-up biologic therapy in ulcerative colitis and Crohn's disease.
    • Manage acute severe ulcerative colitis requiring rescue therapy.
    • Manage severe acute pancreatitis and its local complications.
    • Diagnose and manage ascending cholangitis.
    • Manage chronic pancreatitis and pancreatic exocrine insufficiency.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient with ulcerative colitis has >6 bloody stools a day, fever, tachycardia, and colonic dilation on abdominal X-ray. Dx: Acute severe ulcerative colitis with risk of toxic megacolon — needs IV steroids and surgical review.
    • A patient with epigastric pain radiating to the back has markedly elevated lipase and high Ranson/APACHE criteria. Dx: Severe acute pancreatitis — aggressive fluid resuscitation and monitoring for necrosis.
  12. Diabetes Mellitus & Acute Metabolic Emergencies
    • Manage diabetic ketoacidosis and hyperosmolar hyperglycemic state.
    • Direct insulin regimen individualization in complicated type 1 and type 2 diabetes.
    • Manage diabetic nephropathy, retinopathy, and neuropathy in the inpatient setting.
    • Manage severe hypoglycemia and its precipitants.
    • Direct perioperative glycemic management.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A type 1 diabetic presents with vomiting, Kussmaul breathing, glucose 450 mg/dL, and ketonuria. Dx: Diabetic ketoacidosis — fluids, fixed-rate insulin infusion, potassium replacement.
    • An elderly type 2 diabetic has glucose 900 mg/dL without significant ketosis, altered sensorium, and markedly raised osmolality. Dx: Hyperosmolar hyperglycaemic state.
  13. Thyroid, Adrenal & Pituitary Disorders
    • Manage thyroid storm and myxedema coma.
    • Diagnose and manage adrenal insufficiency and adrenal crisis.
    • Manage Cushing syndrome and its subtypes.
    • Diagnose and manage pituitary apoplexy and hypopituitarism.
    • Manage disorders of calcium and parathyroid function.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient develops fever, tachycardia, and agitation after surgery in the setting of untreated hyperthyroidism. Dx: Thyroid storm — beta-blockade, antithyroid drugs, iodine, steroids.
    • A patient with known adrenal insufficiency misses their steroid doses during a febrile illness and presents with hypotension, hyponatraemia, and hyperkalaemia. Dx: Adrenal (Addisonian) crisis — immediate IV hydrocortisone.
  14. Sepsis, Multidrug-Resistant & Opportunistic Infections
    • Direct source control and antimicrobial escalation in septic shock.
    • Manage carbapenem-resistant and other multidrug-resistant Gram-negative infections.
    • Manage invasive fungal infections in the immunocompromised host.
    • Diagnose and manage tuberculosis including drug-resistant forms.
    • Manage HIV-associated opportunistic infections including PJP and cryptococcal meningitis.
    Clinical vignettes
    • An ICU patient with a prolonged stay grows an isolate resistant to carbapenems. Point: carbapenem-resistant Enterobacteriaceae require combination therapy and strict infection control.
    • A patient with HIV and CD4 count <100 presents with dyspnoea and bilateral ground-glass opacities on CT. Dx: Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia.
  15. Rheumatologic Emergencies & Vasculitis
    • Manage ANCA-associated vasculitis with organ-threatening disease.
    • Manage catastrophic antiphospholipid syndrome and lupus flares.
    • Diagnose and manage giant cell arteritis and Takayasu arteritis.
    • Manage acute gout and septic arthritis.
    • Direct biologic and DMARD therapy in refractory rheumatoid arthritis.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient with chronic sinusitis develops haemoptysis and renal failure with a positive c-ANCA. Dx: Granulomatosis with polyangiitis (GPA).
    • An elderly patient has new headache, jaw claudication, and a tender temporal artery with ESR 100. Dx: Giant cell arteritis — start high-dose steroids immediately to prevent irreversible vision loss.
  16. Hematological Malignancy & Bleeding Disorders
    • Direct the diagnostic work-up and induction management of acute leukemia.
    • Manage tumor lysis syndrome and febrile neutropenia.
    • Manage disseminated intravascular coagulation and thrombotic microangiopathy.
    • Manage complications of hematopoietic stem cell transplantation.
    • Direct anticoagulation management in complex thrombotic disorders.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient presents with fatigue and bruising; peripheral smear shows blasts with Auer rods. Dx: Acute myeloid leukaemia.
    • A septic patient bleeds from IV sites with low platelets, prolonged PT/aPTT, and low fibrinogen. Dx: Disseminated intravascular coagulation.
  17. Stroke, Status Epilepticus & Neurocritical Care
    • Direct thrombolysis and thrombectomy decision-making in acute ischemic stroke.
    • Manage intracerebral and subarachnoid hemorrhage.
    • Manage refractory and super-refractory status epilepticus.
    • Manage raised intracranial pressure and herniation syndromes.
    • Diagnose and manage Guillain-Barré syndrome and myasthenic crisis.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient develops sudden right-sided weakness and aphasia two hours ago; CT shows no haemorrhage. Point: thrombolysis eligibility must be assessed rapidly within the therapeutic window.
    • A patient continues seizing for over 30 minutes despite two anti-epileptic drugs. Dx: Refractory status epilepticus — requires anaesthetic infusion and intubation.
  18. Encephalitis, Meningitis & CNS Infections
    • Direct empiric and targeted therapy in bacterial meningitis.
    • Manage viral and autoimmune encephalitis.
    • Manage tuberculous and cryptococcal meningitis.
    • Diagnose and manage brain abscess.
    • Manage neurological complications of HIV infection.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient presents with fever, neck stiffness, photophobia, and a non-blanching petechial rash. Dx: Meningococcal meningitis — give antibiotics immediately, do not delay for imaging or LP.
    • A patient develops fever with behavioural change; MRI shows temporal lobe changes and CSF PCR is positive. Dx: Herpes simplex encephalitis — start IV aciclovir empirically while awaiting confirmation.
  19. Septic Shock & Multi-organ Dysfunction
    • Direct hemodynamic resuscitation and vasopressor selection in septic shock.
    • Manage acute respiratory distress syndrome complicating sepsis.
    • Manage disseminated intravascular coagulation in critical illness.
    • Direct sedation and delirium management in the ICU.
    • Lead goals-of-care discussions in irreversible multi-organ failure.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient remains hypotensive despite adequate fluid resuscitation with a lactate of 4 mmol/L. Point: norepinephrine is first-line vasopressor; identify and control the septic source early.
    • A patient with irreversible multi-organ failure is discussed at a family meeting. Point: shared decision-making and goals-of-care conversations are essential in irreversible critical illness.
  20. Poisoning, Envenomation & Toxidromes
    • Manage severe organophosphate and paracetamol poisoning.
    • Manage tricyclic antidepressant and other cardiotoxic overdoses.
    • Direct antivenom therapy in severe snake envenomation.
    • Manage alcohol withdrawal and Wernicke's encephalopathy.
    • Direct extracorporeal removal techniques in severe poisoning.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A farmer presents with miosis, salivation, bradycardia, and fasciculations after pesticide exposure. Dx: Organophosphate poisoning — atropine and pralidoxime.
    • A snake-bite victim develops bleeding gums and a non-clotting 20-minute whole blood clotting test. Dx: Viper envenomation with coagulopathy — requires antivenom.
  21. Medical Education, Research & Quality Improvement
    • Design and deliver bedside teaching for undergraduate and junior trainees.
    • Critically appraise clinical literature relevant to internal medicine practice.
    • Lead a quality improvement or morbidity-mortality review project.
    • Apply principles of evidence-based medicine to complex clinical decisions.
    • Demonstrate competence in scholarly writing and case-based reporting.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A resident notices recurring medication errors on the ward. Point: a structured root-cause analysis should guide the design of a quality improvement project.
    • A junior doctor is asked to teach bedside clinical examination to medical students. Point: structured feedback frameworks (e.g., Pendleton's rules) improve the effectiveness of clinical teaching.
Family Medicine Residency
  1. Principles of Family Medicine & Continuity of Care
    • Apply the biopsychosocial model to patient-centered care.
    • Demonstrate longitudinal continuity of care across the patient lifecycle.
    • Coordinate care across multiple specialists and care settings for a single patient.
    • Apply family systems theory to patient management.
    • Manage care transitions between hospital, specialist, and primary care.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient followed for ten years for multiple chronic problems presents with vague new fatigue. Point: continuity of care allows subtle deviation from a known baseline to be recognised early.
    • A father's job loss is followed by stress-related symptoms across several family members. Point: a family-centred approach addresses the social context, not only the presenting symptom.
  2. Hypertension & Diabetes in Primary Care
    • Direct stepwise antihypertensive therapy including resistant hypertension.
    • Manage type 2 diabetes with insulin initiation and intensification in primary care.
    • Screen for and manage diabetic and hypertensive end-organ damage.
    • Manage metabolic syndrome and dyslipidemia in primary care.
    • Apply structured chronic disease review protocols for cardiometabolic risk.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient's blood pressure remains 160/100 despite three antihypertensive agents at adequate doses. Point: work up resistant hypertension — check adherence, technique, and secondary causes.
    • A patient with type 2 diabetes has HbA1c 9% and repeatedly misses follow-up visits. Point: stepwise therapy intensification alongside addressing barriers to adherence.
  3. Chronic Respiratory & Cardiac Disease in Primary Care
    • Manage stable asthma and COPD in the outpatient setting including inhaler technique.
    • Manage stable heart failure and atrial fibrillation in primary care.
    • Direct long-term anticoagulation monitoring in primary care.
    • Coordinate specialist referral for uncontrolled chronic cardiopulmonary disease.
    • Manage smoking cessation as part of chronic disease management.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A known COPD patient reports increasing breathlessness and sputum purulence without red-flag features. Point: outpatient exacerbation management with a short steroid course and antibiotics if indicated.
    • A stable heart failure patient reports 2 kg weight gain over one week. Point: early recognition and diuretic adjustment in clinic can prevent hospital admission.
  4. Musculoskeletal & Chronic Pain in Primary Care
    • Manage osteoarthritis and mechanical low back pain in primary care.
    • Direct chronic non-cancer pain management including opioid stewardship.
    • Manage common soft-tissue injuries and overuse syndromes.
    • Diagnose and initially manage inflammatory arthritis in primary care.
    • Direct osteoporosis screening and fracture prevention.
    Clinical vignettes
