    • A 50-year-old with chronic low back pain and no red-flag features requests an MRI. Point: reassurance and encouraging activity are preferred over early imaging in non-specific low back pain.
    • An elderly patient on long-term NSAIDs for osteoarthritis develops epigastric pain. Point: reassess the analgesic ladder and add gastroprotection.
  5. Undifferentiated Illness & Diagnostic Reasoning
    • Apply a structured approach to the undifferentiated presenting complaint.
    • Use pre-test probability to guide investigation in primary care.
    • Recognize red-flag symptoms warranting urgent referral in common presentations (chest pain, headache, abdominal pain).
    • Manage diagnostic uncertainty in the outpatient setting.
    • Apply cost-effective investigation strategies in resource-limited settings.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A young adult presents with fatigue and weight loss that do not fit a clear pattern. Point: structured follow-up and safety-netting are key in managing undifferentiated presentations.
    • A patient has recurrent non-specific abdominal pain despite normal prior work-up. Point: a biopsychosocial approach avoids unnecessary repeat investigation.
  6. Preventive Medicine & Screening
    • Apply age- and risk-appropriate cancer screening guidelines.
    • Direct immunization schedules across the lifespan including adult catch-up vaccination.
    • Counsel patients on lifestyle modification for cardiovascular risk reduction.
    • Apply cardiovascular risk stratification tools in periodic health examination.
    • Design a practice-level preventive care recall system.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A 50-year-old is due for colorectal cancer screening but is hesitant. Point: shared decision-making across available screening modalities improves uptake.
    • A long-term smoker asks for help quitting. Point: brief intervention combined with pharmacotherapy improves cessation rates.
  7. Antenatal Care & Obstetric Complications in Primary Care
    • Provide antenatal care and identify indications for specialist referral.
    • Manage gestational diabetes and hypertensive disorders of pregnancy in shared care.
    • Recognize and initially manage obstetric emergencies (postpartum hemorrhage, eclampsia).
    • Provide postpartum care including screening for postpartum depression.
    • Provide family planning and contraceptive counseling across methods.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A pregnant woman at 32 weeks has blood pressure 150/95 with new proteinuria. Dx: Pre-eclampsia — refer urgently for obstetric assessment.
    • A routine antenatal glucose challenge test returns abnormal. Dx: Gestational diabetes — dietary counselling and glucose monitoring.
  8. Child Health in Family Practice
    • Manage well-child visits including growth and developmental surveillance.
    • Manage common childhood infections and febrile illness in the outpatient setting.
    • Recognize and initially manage pediatric emergencies in primary care.
    • Direct childhood immunization and catch-up scheduling.
    • Screen for developmental delay and refer appropriately.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A 2-year-old presents with fever and repeated ear pulling. Dx: Acute otitis media.
    • A 9-month-old is not meeting expected developmental milestones. Point: early referral for formal developmental assessment.
  9. Geriatric Care in Family Practice
    • Perform a comprehensive functional assessment of the older adult in primary care.
    • Manage polypharmacy and deprescribing in elderly patients.
    • Manage falls risk, frailty, and cognitive decline in the outpatient setting.
    • Coordinate home-based and community care for frail elderly patients.
    • Support family caregivers of elderly or chronically ill patients.
    Clinical vignettes
    • An 80-year-old on multiple medications has a recent unexplained fall. Point: medication review for polypharmacy as a modifiable falls risk.
    • An elderly patient living alone shows declining self-care. Point: home assessment and referral to community support services.
  10. Mental Health & Substance Use in Primary Care
    • Screen for and manage depression and generalized anxiety disorder in primary care.
    • Apply brief intervention techniques for hazardous alcohol and substance use.
    • Recognize and initially manage acute psychiatric crises including suicidality.
    • Manage psychosomatic and somatoform presentations.
    • Support patients and families through grief and loss.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient reports low mood and anhedonia for several weeks with a high PHQ-9 score. Dx: Major depressive episode — initiate treatment or refer as appropriate.
    • A patient asks for help reducing alcohol intake. Point: brief motivational interviewing and validated screening tools (e.g., AUDIT) guide primary-care management.
  11. Dermatology & Minor Surgical Procedures in Primary Care
    • Diagnose and manage common dermatoses including eczema, psoriasis, and fungal infection.
    • Recognize suspicious skin lesions warranting biopsy or referral.
    • Perform common minor surgical procedures under local anesthesia.
    • Manage wound care and suturing in the outpatient setting.
    • Recognize and manage procedural complications in the outpatient setting.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient has a pigmented skin lesion with an irregular, changing border. Point: apply ABCDE criteria and refer promptly for excision biopsy if suspicious.
    • A patient has a painful, infected ingrown toenail. Point: partial nail avulsion is an appropriate in-clinic minor procedure.
  12. Community & Public Health Practice
    • Apply epidemiological principles to a defined practice population.
    • Design and implement a community health outreach initiative.
    • Respond to public health emergencies and outbreaks within a primary care setting.
    • Engage with local health systems and referral networks.
    • Apply principles of health promotion at the community level.
    Clinical vignettes
    • Several villagers develop diarrhoeal illness within days of each other. Point: outbreak investigation and prompt reporting to health authorities.
    • Immunisation coverage in a catchment area is noted to be low. Point: community health worker mobilisation can improve vaccine uptake.
  13. Palliative & Home-Based Care
    • Direct symptom management for patients receiving palliative care at home.
    • Manage terminal care including pain, dyspnea, and delirium at end of life.
    • Coordinate multidisciplinary home-based care for chronically ill patients.
    • Communicate prognosis and goals of care with patients and families.
    • Apply ethical principles to end-of-life decision-making in primary care.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A terminal cancer patient at home has uncontrolled pain. Point: systematic opioid titration alongside home-based palliative support.
    • A family reports being overwhelmed caring for a bedridden relative. Point: respite care and psychosocial support reduce caregiver burden.
  14. Practice Management & Health Systems
    • Apply principles of clinic workflow and quality improvement.
    • Apply principles of referral and health system navigation.
    • Manage the business and administrative aspects of a family practice.
    • Apply telemedicine principles to primary care delivery.
    • Describe the role of health information systems in primary care.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A clinic is receiving complaints about long patient wait times. Point: process redesign (e.g., appointment triage) can improve patient flow.
    • A new team member is unclear about referral pathways. Point: standard operating procedures support continuity and safe handover of care.
Geriatric Medicine Residency
  1. Comprehensive Geriatric Assessment & Frailty
    • Perform a structured comprehensive geriatric assessment across physical, cognitive, and social domains.
    • Diagnose and stage frailty and sarcopenia using validated tools.
    • Direct multidisciplinary team-based care planning for frail older adults.
    • Apply comprehensive geriatric assessment to hospital discharge planning.
    • Manage perioperative risk associated with frailty.
    Clinical vignettes
    • An 82-year-old presents with recent functional decline and multiple comorbidities. Point: comprehensive geriatric assessment identifies reversible contributors to decline.
    • A frail elderly patient has unintentional weight loss and slow gait speed. Dx: Frailty syndrome — requires a multidomain intervention plan.
  2. Dementia & Cognitive Disorders
    • Direct the diagnostic work-up differentiating Alzheimer's, vascular, Lewy body, and frontotemporal dementia.
    • Manage behavioral and psychological symptoms of dementia including agitation and psychosis.
    • Direct pharmacological and non-pharmacological dementia management.
    • Apply capacity assessment principles in cognitively impaired patients.
    • Support family caregivers of patients with dementia.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient has gradual memory loss over several years with impaired MMSE and an otherwise normal neurological exam. Dx: Alzheimer's disease.
    • A patient with vascular risk factors has stepwise cognitive decline. Dx: Vascular dementia.
  3. Delirium
    • Diagnose delirium using validated screening tools (e.g. CAM).
    • Direct the work-up of underlying causes of delirium in hospitalized elders.
    • Manage delirium using non-pharmacological strategies as first line.
    • Apply judicious pharmacological management of severe agitation in delirium.
    • Implement delirium prevention protocols in hospital settings.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A post-operative elderly patient develops acute confusion with fluctuating attention, worse at night. Dx: Delirium — search actively for a precipitant such as infection, medication, or urinary retention.
    • An elderly patient with a urinary tract infection develops sudden agitation and disorientation. Point: treat the underlying cause first; avoid sedatives as first-line management.
  4. Falls, Gait Disorders & Osteoporosis
    • Direct multifactorial falls risk assessment in older adults.
    • Manage gait and balance disorders including Parkinsonism-related falls.
    • Diagnose and manage osteoporosis and fragility fractures.
    • Direct home safety and environmental modification interventions.
    • Coordinate rehabilitation following a fall-related injury.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient with recurrent falls has a significant orthostatic drop in blood pressure. Point: a multifactorial falls assessment is required.
    • An elderly woman has hip pain and cannot bear weight after a minor fall; DEXA shows osteoporosis. Dx: Fragility hip fracture.
  5. Polypharmacy & Geriatric Pharmacology
    • Apply deprescribing principles to reduce inappropriate polypharmacy.
    • Recognize age-related pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic changes.
    • Identify and manage adverse drug reactions in older adults.
    • Apply explicit criteria (e.g. Beers, STOPP/START) for potentially inappropriate medications.
    • Direct medication reconciliation across transitions of care.
    Clinical vignettes
    • An elderly patient on twelve medications develops new confusion after an anticholinergic drug is added. Point: deprescribing guided by tools such as the Beers criteria.
    • A patient on long-term benzodiazepines develops falls and daytime drowsiness. Point: gradual taper and review of the original indication.
  6. Incontinence & Geriatric Urology
    • Direct the diagnostic evaluation differentiating stress, urge, and overflow incontinence.
    • Manage benign prostatic hyperplasia and urinary retention in elderly men.
    • Direct the management of fecal incontinence in geriatric patients.
    • Apply behavioral and pharmacological approaches to incontinence management.
    • Manage catheter-associated complications in elderly patients.
    Clinical vignettes
    • An elderly woman reports urgency and leakage before reaching the toilet. Dx: Urge incontinence.
    • An elderly man has dribbling, a weak stream, and incomplete bladder emptying. Dx: Overflow incontinence secondary to benign prostatic hyperplasia.
  7. Nutrition, Dysphagia & Pressure Injury
    • Direct nutritional assessment and management of malnutrition in older adults.
    • Manage dysphagia and aspiration risk including swallow assessment.
    • Direct the prevention and staged management of pressure injuries.
    • Apply enteral and oral nutritional support strategies appropriately.
    • Recognize the impact of frailty on nutritional needs.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A stroke patient coughs while drinking water. Point: a bedside swallow assessment is required before resuming oral intake.
    • A bedbound patient develops non-blanching sacral redness. Dx: Stage 1 pressure injury — requires repositioning and offloading.
  8. Cardiovascular & Metabolic Disease in Older Adults
    • Manage heart failure with preserved ejection fraction in the frail elderly.
    • Manage atrial fibrillation and anticoagulation decisions balancing falls risk.
    • Manage orthostatic hypotension and syncope in older adults.
    • Manage diabetes mellitus with individualized glycemic targets in frailty.
    • Manage hypertension with attention to age-related treatment thresholds.
    Clinical vignettes
    • An elderly patient has isolated systolic hypertension. Point: cautious, stepwise antihypertensive titration reduces the risk of orthostatic falls.
    • A frail diabetic patient has recurrent hypoglycaemia on a tight glycaemic target. Point: glycaemic targets should be relaxed in frail older adults.
  9. Palliative & End-of-Life Care in Geriatrics
    • Direct symptom management for older adults with life-limiting illness.
    • Facilitate advance care planning and advance directive discussions.
    • Apply ethical frameworks to end-of-life decision-making in the elderly.
    • Coordinate hospice and palliative care referral for geriatric patients.
    • Support bereaved families following the death of an older patient.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A patient with advanced dementia has recurrent aspiration pneumonia. Point: a goals-of-care discussion regarding feeding tube placement versus comfort-focused care is needed.
    • A dying patient develops terminal agitation. Point: symptom-focused comfort medications are prioritised over curative intervention.
  10. Perioperative & Hospital Geriatrics
    • Direct preoperative optimization of frail older surgical patients.
    • Manage postoperative delirium and functional decline in older patients.
    • Coordinate orthogeriatric co-management of hip fracture patients.
    • Direct discharge planning to prevent hospital readmission in older adults.
    • Apply principles of age-friendly hospital care.
    Clinical vignettes
    • An elderly patient on warfarin is being prepared for hip surgery. Point: a perioperative anticoagulation bridging plan is required.
    • A post-operative elderly patient remains immobile and at risk of deconditioning. Point: early mobilisation protocols reduce hospital-acquired functional decline.
  11. Elder Abuse, Ethics & Long-Term Care
    • Recognize and respond to suspected elder abuse or neglect.
    • Apply ethical and legal principles to decision-making capacity in older adults.
    • Direct the medical oversight of long-term care facility residents.
    • Coordinate community and institutional resources for dependent elderly patients.
    • Apply guardianship and advance directive principles in geriatric practice.
    Clinical vignettes
    • An elderly patient has unexplained bruises and appears fearful of their caregiver. Point: screen actively for elder abuse and initiate a safeguarding referral.
    • A family disagrees over decision-making for a relative with dementia. Point: a formal capacity assessment and identification of a surrogate decision-maker are required.
Paediatrics Residency
  1. Neonatal Resuscitation & Respiratory Distress
    • Direct the resuscitation and stabilization of critically ill neonates.
    • Manage respiratory distress syndrome and surfactant therapy in preterm infants.
    • Manage meconium aspiration syndrome and persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn.
    • Direct mechanical ventilation strategies in the neonate.
    • Manage transient tachypnea of the newborn and apnea of prematurity.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A term newborn is floppy at birth and not crying. Point: follow the NRP algorithm — stimulate and provide positive-pressure ventilation if not breathing.
    • A preterm infant develops grunting, retractions, and a ground-glass pattern on chest X-ray. Dx: Respiratory distress syndrome — surfactant therapy.
  2. Neonatal Sepsis, Jaundice & NEC
    • Manage early- and late-onset neonatal sepsis.
    • Direct the management of severe hyperbilirubinemia and exchange transfusion.
    • Manage necrotizing enterocolitis including surgical referral criteria.
    • Manage neonatal seizures and hypoxic-ischemic encephalopathy including therapeutic hypothermia.
    • Direct the long-term neurodevelopmental follow-up of high-risk neonates.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A preterm neonate develops temperature instability, lethargy, and poor feeding. Dx: Late-onset neonatal sepsis.
    • A neonate has abdominal distension, bloody stools, and pneumatosis intestinalis on abdominal X-ray. Dx: Necrotising enterocolitis.
  3. Paediatric Septic Shock & Multi-organ Dysfunction
    • Direct the recognition and fluid-refractory management of pediatric septic shock.
    • Manage disseminated intravascular coagulation in critically ill children.
    • Manage acute respiratory distress syndrome in children.
    • Lead pediatric advanced life support in cardiac arrest.
    • Manage multi-organ dysfunction syndrome in the pediatric ICU.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A child presents with fever, cold extremities, prolonged capillary refill, and hypotension. Dx: Cold septic shock — fluid bolus followed by inotropic support.
    • A child with sepsis develops oliguria and a rising creatinine. Point: monitor closely for evolving multi-organ dysfunction and adjust drug dosing accordingly.
  4. Paediatric Neurocritical Care
    • Manage status epilepticus in children including refractory cases.
    • Manage raised intracranial pressure and traumatic brain injury in children.
    • Manage acute stroke in childhood.
    • Diagnose and manage Guillain-Barré syndrome in children.
    • Manage coma and altered consciousness of unclear etiology in children.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A child after head trauma has a falling GCS and unequal pupils. Dx: Raised intracranial pressure with impending herniation — urgent neuroimaging and management.
    • A 2-year-old has a febrile seizure lasting more than 15 minutes. Point: distinguish complex febrile seizure from CNS infection and evaluate accordingly.
  5. Congenital Heart Disease
    • Direct the diagnostic work-up of cyanotic and acyanotic congenital heart disease.
    • Manage heart failure secondary to left-to-right shunt lesions.
    • Manage hypoplastic left heart syndrome and single-ventricle physiology.
    • Direct the perioperative management of children undergoing cardiac surgery.
    • Manage coarctation of the aorta and duct-dependent lesions.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A neonate has central cyanosis unresponsive to supplemental oxygen and a single loud S2. Dx: Transposition of the great arteries.
    • An infant has failure to thrive and a loud pansystolic murmur at the left sternal border. Dx: Ventricular septal defect.
  6. Acquired Paediatric Cardiac Disease
    • Diagnose and manage Kawasaki disease including coronary artery complications.
    • Manage acute rheumatic fever and rheumatic carditis.
    • Manage pediatric myocarditis and dilated cardiomyopathy.
    • Manage pediatric arrhythmias including SVT.
    • Manage infective endocarditis in children.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A child has fever, conjunctivitis, rash, and a strawberry tongue with concern for coronary artery involvement. Dx: Kawasaki disease.
    • A child develops migratory joint pain and carditis after streptococcal pharyngitis. Dx: Acute rheumatic fever.
  7. Severe Asthma & Chronic Lung Disease
    • Manage status asthmaticus in children.
    • Direct step-up biologic therapy in severe pediatric asthma.
    • Manage chronic lung disease of prematurity (bronchopulmonary dysplasia).
    • Manage cystic fibrosis pulmonary exacerbations.
    • Direct the diagnostic work-up of recurrent pneumonia in children.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A child has severe wheeze, cannot complete sentences, and SpO₂ is 88%. Dx: Life-threatening acute asthma.
    • A preterm infant remains oxygen-dependent beyond 36 weeks corrected gestational age. Dx: Bronchopulmonary dysplasia.
  8. Acute Kidney Injury & Nephrotic Syndrome
    • Manage acute kidney injury in children including hemolytic uremic syndrome.
    • Manage steroid-sensitive and steroid-resistant nephrotic syndrome.
    • Manage acute post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis.
    • Direct pediatric hypertension work-up and management.
    • Direct the indications for renal replacement therapy in children.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A child has periorbital oedema, frothy urine, and heavy proteinuria. Dx: Nephrotic syndrome (minimal change disease).
    • A child develops pallor, low platelets, and a rising creatinine after a diarrhoeal illness. Dx: Haemolytic uraemic syndrome.
  9. GI Bleeding, Liver Failure & Malabsorption
    • Manage GI bleeding in the pediatric patient.
    • Manage pediatric acute liver failure.
    • Direct the diagnostic work-up of failure to thrive and malabsorption including celiac disease.
    • Manage inflammatory bowel disease in children.
    • Manage biliary atresia and neonatal cholestasis.
    Clinical vignettes
    • An infant has painless rectal bleeding with a positive technetium scan. Dx: Meckel diverticulum.
    • A child develops jaundice, coagulopathy, and encephalopathy after a viral hepatitis illness. Dx: Acute liver failure.
  10. Diabetic Ketoacidosis & Endocrine Emergencies
    • Manage diabetic ketoacidosis in children with attention to cerebral edema risk.
    • Manage congenital adrenal hyperplasia and adrenal crisis in infants.
    • Direct the diagnostic work-up of disorders of growth and puberty.
    • Manage congenital hypothyroidism and its neurodevelopmental implications.
    • Manage disorders of sex development.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A child presents with polyuria, vomiting, Kussmaul breathing, and marked hyperglycaemia. Dx: Paediatric DKA — cautious fluid resuscitation to reduce the risk of cerebral oedema.
    • A newborn presents with ambiguous genitalia and a salt-wasting crisis. Dx: Congenital adrenal hyperplasia.
  11. Paediatric Oncology & Hematological Emergencies
    • Direct the diagnostic work-up of acute lymphoblastic and myeloid leukemia.
    • Manage tumor lysis syndrome and febrile neutropenia in children.
    • Manage sickle cell crisis and its complications.
    • Diagnose and manage severe hemophilia and bleeding disorders in children.
    • Manage complications of pediatric bone marrow transplantation.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A child has pallor, bruising, bone pain, and blasts on peripheral smear. Dx: Acute lymphoblastic leukaemia.
    • A child presents with an abdominal mass, hypertension, and haematuria. Dx: Wilms tumour.
  12. Severe Infections, Meningitis & Immunization
    • Direct empiric and targeted therapy in pediatric bacterial meningitis.
    • Manage congenital and perinatally acquired infections (TORCH).
    • Manage severe dengue, malaria, and enteric fever in children.
    • Direct the management of pediatric HIV and tuberculosis.
    • Manage vaccine-preventable disease outbreaks in a pediatric population.
    Clinical vignettes
    • An infant has fever, a bulging fontanelle, and irritability. Dx: Bacterial meningitis.
    • An unimmunised child develops a barking cough and stridor. Point: consider severe croup or diphtheria and check immunisation status.
  13. Inborn Errors of Metabolism & Genetic Syndromes
    • Direct the diagnostic approach to suspected inborn errors of metabolism presenting with acute decompensation.
    • Manage acute metabolic decompensation in a known metabolic disorder.
    • Direct the evaluation and management of common genetic syndromes (Down, Turner, DiGeorge).
    • Coordinate multidisciplinary care for children with developmental disabilities.
    • Direct early intervention planning for children with developmental delay.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A neonate develops poor feeding, lethargy, and metabolic acidosis shortly after starting feeds. Dx: Inborn error of metabolism (e.g. an organic acidaemia).
    • An infant has hypotonia, macroglossia, and an umbilical hernia. Dx: Consider congenital hypothyroidism or Beckwith–Wiedemann syndrome.
  14. Paediatric Trauma, Poisoning & Abuse
    • Direct the initial resuscitation of the critically injured child.
    • Manage pediatric poisoning and toxicological emergencies.
    • Manage acute abdominal and surgical emergencies in children (intussusception, appendicitis).
    • Recognize and respond to suspected non-accidental injury.
    • Direct triage and management of mass casualty events involving children.
    Clinical vignettes
    • An infant presents with multiple fractures at different stages of healing and an inconsistent history. Dx: Non-accidental injury (child abuse) — requires safeguarding assessment.
    • A toddler ingests unknown tablets and becomes drowsy. Point: urgent poisoning assessment and contact with a poison control centre.
  15. Academic Paediatrics, Ethics & Advocacy
    • Design and deliver structured teaching for medical students and junior residents.
    • Critically appraise pediatric clinical research and apply it to practice.
    • Lead a quality improvement project within a pediatric unit.
    • Apply ethical principles to consent and decision-making in pediatric care.
    • Advocate for child health policy at the institutional or community level.
    Clinical vignettes
    • A resident is asked to obtain consent for a procedure on a minor from a parent. Point: understand the distinction between parental consent and a minor's assent.
    • A paediatrician advocates for vaccine access in an underserved community. Point: the physician's role as a child health advocate extends beyond the clinic.
Management
  1. Cardiovascular
    • Acute inferior wall myocardial infarction: Aspirin, P2Y12 inhibitor, anticoagulation, and emergency reperfusion by primary PCI (or fibrinolysis if unavailable); avoid nitrates if RV involvement/hypotension.
    • Anterior STEMI: Immediate primary PCI (door-to-balloon <90 min) with dual antiplatelet therapy, anticoagulation, and secondary prevention.
    • Congestive cardiac failure: Diuretics for congestion plus ACE-inhibitor/ARNI, beta-blocker, and mineralocorticoid antagonist as guideline-directed therapy; treat the underlying cause.
    • Type 1 cardiorenal syndrome: Careful diuresis balancing decongestion against renal perfusion; consider inotropes or ultrafiltration if diuretic-resistant.
    • Peripartum cardiomyopathy: Standard heart failure therapy adapted for pregnancy/lactation safety; consider bromocriptine; anticoagulate if severely reduced ejection fraction.
    • Aortic dissection: Immediate blood pressure and heart rate control (beta-blocker first), then emergency surgery for Type A or medical management ± endovascular repair for Type B.
    • Cardiac tamponade (Beck's triad): Urgent pericardiocentesis; IV fluids as a temporising measure while preparing drainage.
    • Acute pericarditis: NSAIDs plus colchicine as first-line therapy, tapering slowly rather than stopping abruptly to reduce relapse risk; escalate to low-dose corticosteroids only if NSAIDs/colchicine are contraindicated or ineffective, since steroids increase recurrence risk; monitor for recurrence, effusion progression to tamponade, and treat any identified underlying cause (TB, autoimmune, uraemic); avoid NSAIDs post-MI (pericarditis) and in significant renal impairment.
    • Tricuspid valve infective endocarditis: Prolonged targeted IV antibiotics per blood culture sensitivity; surgery if uncontrolled infection, large vegetation, or recurrent septic emboli.
    • Papillary muscle rupture causing acute mitral regurgitation: Stabilise with afterload reduction/intra-aortic balloon pump and arrange emergency surgical repair or valve replacement.
    • Deep vein thrombosis: Anticoagulation (DOAC first-line, or LMWH/warfarin) for at least 3 months, extended indefinitely for unprovoked or recurrent VTE after weighing bleeding risk; compression stockings for post-thrombotic symptom relief; monitor renal function periodically on DOACs and platelet count in the first 2 weeks if using heparin (HIT risk); avoid DOACs in severe renal impairment and pregnancy (use LMWH), and consider IVC filter only if anticoagulation is contraindicated.
    • Hemorrhagic (hypovolemic) shock: Control the bleeding source, give crystalloid then blood products in a balanced ratio (massive transfusion protocol if severe).
    • Hypovolemic shock: Rapid IV fluid/blood resuscitation with identification and control of the underlying fluid or blood loss.
    • Long QT syndrome: Avoidance of QT-prolonging drugs and correction of electrolyte abnormalities (K+, Mg2+) as baseline management; beta-blocker therapy for symptomatic/high-risk congenital LQTS, escalating to ICD for those with breakthrough events on beta-blockade or aborted cardiac arrest; monitor QTc on any new medication and during illness (fever can unmask Brugada-like patterns in overlap syndromes); screen first-degree relatives.
    • Cold septic shock: Fluid bolus followed by inotropic support (e.g. adrenaline) targeting warm, well-perfused extremities.
    • Intermittent claudication due to peripheral arterial disease: Supervised exercise programme, risk factor modification, antiplatelet and statin therapy; revascularisation if lifestyle-limiting.
    • Acute hemolytic transfusion reaction: Stop the transfusion immediately, maintain IV access and diuresis, and send samples to blood bank for cross-match recheck.
    • Transfusion reaction: Stop the transfusion, support haemodynamics/airway as needed, and investigate the type of reaction before further transfusion.
    • Filarial lymphedema: Limb hygiene and compression, diethylcarbamazine for the underlying infection, and management of acute inflammatory episodes.
    • CO2 gas embolism: Stop insufflation, place patient in left lateral head-down (Durant) position, give 100% oxygen, and provide haemodynamic support.
    • Patent ductus arteriosus: NSAID (indomethacin/ibuprofen) to promote closure in the preterm neonate; surgical/catheter closure if persistent or symptomatic.
    • Tetralogy of Fallot (cyanotic CHD): Manage hypercyanotic spells (knee-chest, oxygen, morphine, beta-blocker), with definitive surgical repair.
    • Transposition of the great arteries: Prostaglandin E1 to maintain ductal patency, balloon atrial septostomy if needed, and surgical arterial switch repair.
    • Ventricular septal defect: Manage heart failure medically; surgical or catheter closure for large/symptomatic defects.
  2. Respiratory
    • Community-acquired pneumonia: Empirical antibiotics guided by severity score (e.g. CURB-65), supplemental oxygen, and supportive care.
    • Bronchial asthma: Inhaled short-acting bronchodilator for relief plus stepwise inhaled corticosteroid-based controller therapy.
    • Life-threatening acute asthma: Continuous nebulised bronchodilators, systemic corticosteroids, IV magnesium sulfate, and ICU input for possible ventilatory support.
    • Life-threatening acute severe asthma: Continuous nebulisation, IV corticosteroids, IV magnesium, and urgent senior/ICU involvement for possible intubation.
    • Idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis: Antifibrotic therapy (pirfenidone/nintedanib), pulmonary rehabilitation, oxygen if hypoxic, and transplant evaluation.
    • Acute pulmonary embolism: Anticoagulation as first-line; thrombolysis or embolectomy if massive/haemodynamically unstable.
    • Tension pneumothorax: Immediate needle decompression (second intercostal space, midclavicular line, or safe triangle) is definitive first-line, followed promptly by chest tube insertion; this is a clinical diagnosis and treatment must not wait for imaging; monitor haemodynamics and repeat imaging post-decompression to confirm resolution and tube position; recurrence risk warrants review of underlying cause and consideration of pleurodesis.
    • Severe ARDS: Lung-protective low tidal volume ventilation, prone positioning, conservative fluids, and treatment of the underlying cause.
    • Pulmonary tuberculosis: Standard four-drug anti-TB therapy (isoniazid, rifampicin, pyrazinamide, ethambutol) for two months then continuation phase.
    • Suspected pulmonary tuberculosis: Sputum microscopy/GeneXpert, airborne isolation, and empirical anti-TB therapy if strongly suspected pending confirmation.
    • Bronchopulmonary dysplasia: Supplemental oxygen, diuretics/bronchodilators as needed, nutritional support, and minimising further ventilator injury.
    • Respiratory distress syndrome: Surfactant replacement and respiratory support (CPAP/ventilation) in the preterm neonate.
    • Respiratory distress syndrome (surfactant deficiency): Antenatal corticosteroids if anticipated preterm birth; postnatal surfactant and CPAP/ventilatory support.
    • Ventilator-associated pneumonia: Empirical broad-spectrum antibiotics de-escalated by culture, plus prevention bundle (head-of-bed elevation, sedation holidays, oral care).
    • Atelectasis: Incentive spirometry, chest physiotherapy, early mobilisation, and adequate postoperative analgesia.
    • Acute mountain sickness: Halt ascent or descend, supplemental oxygen, acetazolamide; immediate descent and dexamethasone/nifedipine if HACE/HAPE develops.
    • Suspected foreign body aspiration: Urgent bronchoscopy for removal; back blows/abdominal thrusts if acutely obstructing airway.
    • Croup (laryngotracheobronchitis): Oral/IM dexamethasone; nebulised adrenaline and close observation if stridor at rest.
    • Acute epiglottitis: Do not examine the throat or agitate the child; secure the airway urgently in theatre, then IV antibiotics.
    • Laryngospasm/bronchospasm: Positive pressure oxygen, deepen anaesthesia, and give IV suxamethonium if laryngospasm persists.
  3. Gastrointestinal & Hepatobiliary
    • Acute appendicitis: Appendicectomy (laparoscopic or open); IV antibiotics and fluids pending surgery.
    • Acute cholecystitis due to gallstones: IV antibiotics, analgesia, and early laparoscopic cholecystectomy.
    • Gallstones (cholelithiasis): Elective laparoscopic cholecystectomy for symptomatic stones; conservative management if asymptomatic.
    • Duodenal peptic ulcer disease: Proton pump inhibitor therapy and H. pylori eradication if positive; stop NSAIDs.
    • Perforated peptic ulcer: Resuscitation, IV antibiotics, and emergency surgical repair (omental patch).
    • Intestinal obstruction: Nasogastric decompression, IV fluid resuscitation, and surgery if strangulation or failure to resolve conservatively.
    • Intussusception: Air or contrast enema reduction; surgery if reduction fails or peritonitis is present.
    • Anal fissure: Stool softeners, topical GTN/diltiazem, and sitz baths; surgery (sphincterotomy) if chronic/refractory.
    • Hypertrophic pyloric stenosis: Correct dehydration and electrolytes first, then pyloromyotomy.
    • Meckel diverticulum: Surgical resection of the diverticulum, especially if bleeding or complicated.
    • Acute liver failure: Supportive/ICU care, treat the precipitant (e.g. N-acetylcysteine for paracetamol), and urgent transplant evaluation.
    • Hepatic encephalopathy precipitated by GI bleeding: Lactulose ± rifaximin, identify and treat the precipitant, and correct the GI bleed.
    • Variceal bleed due to liver cirrhosis with portal hypertension: Resuscitation, IV octreotide/terlipressin, prophylactic antibiotics, and urgent endoscopic band ligation.
    • Acute severe ulcerative colitis with risk of toxic megacolon: IV corticosteroids, monitor for toxic megacolon, and escalate to rescue biologic/ciclosporin or colectomy if not responding.
    • Severe acute pancreatitis: Aggressive IV fluid resuscitation, analgesia, nutritional support, and management of local/systemic complications.
    • Acute viral hepatitis A: Supportive care with rest, hydration, and avoidance of hepatotoxic drugs; usually self-limiting.
    • Necrotising enterocolitis: Bowel rest, nasogastric decompression, IV antibiotics, and surgery if perforation/necrosis.
    • Suspected esophageal carcinoma: Endoscopy with biopsy for diagnosis; staging followed by surgery, chemoradiotherapy, or palliative care depending on stage.
    • Carcinoma esophagus: Multidisciplinary staging; surgical resection ± neoadjuvant chemoradiotherapy, or palliative stenting/therapy if advanced.
    • Suspected carcinoma head of pancreas: Cross-sectional imaging and tissue diagnosis; Whipple's procedure if resectable, otherwise palliative biliary drainage and chemotherapy.
    • Carcinoma head of pancreas (Courvoisier's law): Staging imaging and biopsy; surgical resection (Whipple's) if resectable, palliative stenting/chemotherapy if not.
    • Inguinal hernia: Elective surgical repair (herniorrhaphy/hernioplasty); emergency surgery if strangulated.
    • Wound dehiscence: Cover with saline-soaked dressing, resuscitate, and return to theatre for re-closure.
    • Vitamin B12 deficiency: Parenteral or high-dose oral B12 replacement, with investigation of the underlying cause (e.g. pernicious anaemia).
    • Iron deficiency anemia: Oral (or IV if intolerant/malabsorption) iron replacement and investigation for the source of blood loss.
  4. Renal & Urology
    • Acute kidney injury: Identify and treat the cause (pre-renal, renal, post-renal), optimise fluids, avoid nephrotoxins, and consider dialysis if refractory.
    • Contrast-induced nephropathy: IV hydration before/after contrast exposure and avoidance of additional nephrotoxins; usually reversible.
    • Chronic kidney disease: Manage blood pressure, glycaemic control, and proteinuria (ACE-I/ARB); plan for renal replacement therapy as disease progresses.
    • Focal segmental glomerulosclerosis (FSGS): Corticosteroids as first-line, with immunosuppressants (calcineurin inhibitors) for steroid-resistant disease.
    • Nephrotic syndrome: Corticosteroids (in minimal change disease), salt restriction, diuretics, and thromboprophylaxis if severe.
    • Nephrotic syndrome (minimal change disease): High-dose corticosteroid therapy, usually with excellent response; relapse managed with repeat steroid courses.
    • Minimal change nephrotic syndrome: Corticosteroids as first-line treatment with close monitoring for relapse.
    • Post-streptococcal acute glomerulonephritis: Supportive management of fluid/blood pressure, antibiotics for residual infection; usually self-limiting.
    • Haemolytic uraemic syndrome: Supportive care with fluid/electrolyte management and dialysis if needed; avoid antibiotics/antimotility agents in the diarrhoeal form.
    • Ureteric calculus: Analgesia, alpha-blockers to aid passage for small stones, and lithotripsy/ureteroscopy for larger or obstructing stones.
    • Ureteric calculus (renal colic): NSAID/opioid analgesia, hydration, and imaging to guide conservative versus interventional stone management.
    • Benign prostatic hyperplasia: Alpha-blockers and/or 5-alpha-reductase inhibitors; TURP for refractory or complicated cases.
    • Overflow incontinence secondary to benign prostatic hyperplasia: Catheterise to relieve retention, then definitive BPH treatment (medical or surgical).
    • Urge incontinence: Bladder training, anticholinergic or beta-3 agonist medication, and pelvic floor therapy.
    • Stress urinary incontinence: Pelvic floor muscle training as first-line; surgical options (e.g. sling procedures) if refractory.
    • Acute T-cell mediated (cellular) rejection: Pulse corticosteroids, with escalation to lymphocyte-depleting therapy if steroid-resistant.
    • Acute graft rejection: High-dose corticosteroids and adjustment of maintenance immunosuppression; biopsy to confirm severity/type.
    • Anti-GBM disease (Goodpasture syndrome) causing rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis: Plasma exchange plus corticosteroids and cyclophosphamide to remove and suppress anti-GBM antibodies.
  5. Endocrine & Metabolic
    • Diabetic ketoacidosis: IV fluid resuscitation, fixed-rate IV insulin infusion, and potassium replacement first-line, following a structured protocol; do not stop insulin once glucose normalises, but add dextrose-containing fluids instead to allow continued ketone clearance; monitor capillary ketones/blood glucose hourly and venous bicarbonate/pH regularly until resolution; identify and treat the precipitant (infection, insulin omission, new diagnosis) concurrently.
    • Paediatric DKA: Cautious, gradual fluid resuscitation with insulin infusion to minimise the risk of cerebral oedema.
    • Hyperosmolar hyperglycaemic state: IV fluid resuscitation first-line with cautious, slower correction than DKA given very high osmolality and older, often frailer patient population; insulin started at lower rates or delayed until fluids alone have begun lowering glucose, to avoid precipitous osmotic shifts; monitor osmolality, glucose, and neurological status closely, and consider prophylactic anticoagulation given high VTE risk from hyperviscosity; identify and treat the precipitant, often infection or a new cardiovascular event.
    • Thyroid storm: High-dose antithyroid drugs, iodine solution (given at least an hour after antithyroid drugs to avoid fuelling synthesis), beta-blockade, and corticosteroids together first-line, alongside aggressive supportive care (cooling, fluids); treat the precipitant (infection, surgery, non-compliance); monitor cardiac rhythm, temperature, and mental status continuously in an ICU setting; this is a medical emergency with significant mortality if treatment is delayed.
    • Adrenal (Addisonian) crisis: Immediate IV hydrocortisone and aggressive fluid resuscitation with dextrose if hypoglycaemic.
    • Graves' disease (hyperthyroidism): Antithyroid drugs (carbimazole), beta-blockers for symptoms, with radioiodine or surgery for definitive treatment.
    • Primary hypothyroidism: Levothyroxine replacement first-line, started at full dose in younger healthy patients but low-dose with gradual titration in the elderly or those with cardiac disease given risk of precipitating angina/arrhythmia; monitor TSH 6-8 weeks after dose changes, then annually once stable; increase dose in pregnancy (typically ~30% increment) as early as pregnancy is confirmed, and separate dosing from calcium/iron supplements which impair absorption.
    • Congenital hypothyroidism: Immediate levothyroxine replacement to prevent irreversible developmental delay.
    • Congenital adrenal hyperplasia: Glucocorticoid and mineralocorticoid replacement, with stress dosing during illness.
    • Consider congenital hypothyroidism or Beckwith–Wiedemann syndrome: Confirm with thyroid function/genetic testing; start levothyroxine promptly if hypothyroid, and screen for associated features if Beckwith–Wiedemann.
    • Gestational diabetes: Dietary and lifestyle modification first-line; insulin or metformin if glycaemic targets not met.
    • Gestational diabetes mellitus: Diet/exercise modification, glucose monitoring, and pharmacotherapy if targets not achieved.
    • Refeeding syndrome: Slow, gradual reintroduction of nutrition with proactive phosphate, potassium, and magnesium replacement and thiamine cover.
    • Rickets: Vitamin D and calcium supplementation, with correction of the underlying nutritional deficiency.
    • Scurvy (Vitamin C deficiency): Vitamin C supplementation and dietary correction.
    • Kwashiorkor: Cautious re-feeding per WHO protocol, treat infections, and correct micronutrient deficiencies.
    • Marasmus: Gradual, structured nutritional rehabilitation with monitoring for refeeding syndrome.
    • Vitamin A deficiency (xerophthalmia): Vitamin A supplementation per WHO schedule to prevent progression to blindness.
    • Dilutional hyponatremia: Fluid restriction and treatment of the underlying cause; cautious correction to avoid osmotic demyelination.
  6. Neurology
    • Acute ischemic stroke: Thrombolysis (if within window and no contraindication) ± mechanical thrombectomy for large vessel occlusion, plus supportive stroke unit care.
    • Subarachnoid hemorrhage: Urgent neurosurgical/interventional securing of the aneurysm (coiling/clipping), nimodipine, and blood pressure control.
    • Chronic subdural hematoma: Surgical burr-hole drainage if symptomatic; conservative monitoring if small/asymptomatic.
    • Extradural hematoma: Emergency surgical evacuation (craniotomy) — a neurosurgical emergency.
    • Migraine: Simple analgesia/triptans for acute attacks; prophylactic therapy (e.g. propranolol, topiramate) if frequent.
    • Generalized tonic-clonic seizure/epilepsy: Acute seizure terminated with benzodiazepines if prolonged; long-term antiepileptic drug therapy guided by seizure type.
    • Refractory status epilepticus: Escalate through benzodiazepine, then IV antiepileptic loading, then anaesthetic infusion with intubation.
    • Simple febrile seizure: Reassurance, antipyretics, and evaluation for the source of fever; usually self-limiting and benign.
    • Infantile spasms (West syndrome): Vigabatrin or ACTH/corticosteroids as first-line therapy; urgent work-up for an underlying cause.
    • Raised intracranial pressure with impending herniation: Head elevation, hyperosmolar therapy (mannitol/hypertonic saline), controlled ventilation, and urgent neurosurgical assessment.
    • Papilledema due to raised intracranial pressure: Urgent neuroimaging to identify the cause and treat the underlying raised intracranial pressure.
    • Herpes simplex encephalitis: Empirical IV aciclovir started immediately, without waiting for confirmatory PCR.
    • Meningococcal meningitis: Immediate IV antibiotics (e.g. ceftriaxone) without delaying for LP/imaging, plus supportive care and contact prophylaxis.
    • Bacterial meningitis: Empirical IV antibiotics (covering the likely pathogens by age/risk factors) started immediately, without waiting for lumbar puncture if this would delay treatment, plus dexamethasone (particularly for pneumococcal meningitis) to reduce neurological sequelae; monitor neurological status, and repeat imaging/LP if not improving as expected; contact prophylaxis for close contacts in meningococcal disease; this is a medical emergency where delay materially worsens outcome.
    • Acute bacterial meningitis: Urgent empirical IV antibiotics plus supportive care; adjust once organism/sensitivities known.
    • Wernicke's encephalopathy (thiamine deficiency): Immediate IV thiamine before any glucose administration.
    • Alcohol withdrawal: Symptom-triggered benzodiazepines and thiamine supplementation to prevent Wernicke's encephalopathy.
    • Vascular dementia: Control of vascular risk factors (BP, lipids, glucose) to slow progression; supportive/cognitive care.
    • Alzheimer's disease: Cholinesterase inhibitors first-line for mild-moderate disease, with memantine added or used alone in moderate-severe disease; monitor cognitive trajectory (standardised testing) and function; manage behavioural and psychological symptoms non-pharmacologically first-line given antipsychotic risks in dementia; newer anti-amyloid monoclonal antibodies are an option in select early, biomarker-confirmed disease under specialist supervision given infusion reaction and ARIA (amyloid-related imaging abnormality) monitoring requirements.
    • Delirium: Identify and treat the precipitant (infection, drugs, retention); reorientation and non-pharmacological measures first.
    • Delirium secondary to infection: Treat the underlying infection promptly alongside supportive delirium care measures.
    • Dementia: Optimise cognitive and functional support, treat reversible contributors, and manage behavioural symptoms non-pharmacologically first.
    • Frailty syndrome: Comprehensive geriatric assessment with a multidomain intervention (nutrition, exercise, medication review, social support).
    • Cervical spinal cord injury: Immobilisation, high-dose steroids controversial, and urgent surgical decompression/stabilisation as indicated.
    • Phantom limb pain: Multimodal analgesia, mirror therapy, and neuropathic pain agents (e.g. gabapentinoids).
    • Prolapsed intervertebral disc: Analgesia and physiotherapy first-line; surgical discectomy if progressive neurological deficit or refractory pain.
    • Carpal tunnel syndrome (median nerve compression): Wrist splinting and corticosteroid injection first-line; surgical decompression if refractory.
    • Radial nerve injury (wrist drop): Splinting to prevent contracture, physiotherapy, and observation for spontaneous recovery; surgical exploration if no improvement.
    • Common peroneal nerve palsy: Foot drop splint (AFO), physiotherapy, and removal of any compressive cause; nerve exploration if indicated.
    • Recurrent laryngeal nerve injury: Voice therapy for unilateral palsy; airway management/tracheostomy if bilateral with airway compromise.
  7. Hematology & Oncology
    • Acute leukemia: Induction chemotherapy per subtype/risk stratification, with supportive transfusion and infection prophylaxis.
    • Acute myeloid leukaemia: Intensive induction chemotherapy first-line in fit patients, with targeted agents (FLT3, IDH inhibitors) added where mutation-positive; allogeneic stem cell transplant for intermediate/high-risk disease in remission; less intensive regimens (hypomethylating agent plus venetoclax) for unfit/elderly patients; monitor for tumour lysis syndrome, count recovery, and minimal residual disease to guide post-remission strategy.
    • Acute lymphoblastic leukaemia: Multi-agent intensive induction chemotherapy first-line, with CNS-directed therapy (intrathecal chemotherapy) given as prophylaxis in all patients given sanctuary-site relapse risk; escalate to allogeneic transplant or CAR-T cell therapy for high-risk/relapsed disease; monitor minimal residual disease, which strongly predicts outcome and guides treatment intensity; screen for Philadelphia chromosome to add a TKI to the regimen if positive.
    • Disseminated intravascular coagulation: Treat the underlying trigger (sepsis, obstetric emergency, malignancy) first-line, as DIC will not resolve without addressing the cause; supportive transfusion (FFP, platelets, cryoprecipitate) guided by bleeding and laboratory parameters rather than numbers alone; monitor platelet count, PT/aPTT, fibrinogen, and D-dimer trend; anticoagulation is reserved for predominantly thrombotic presentations under specialist guidance.
    • Hemophilia: Factor replacement therapy (on-demand or prophylactic) and avoidance of intramuscular injections/NSAIDs.
    • Beta-thalassemia major: Regular blood transfusion with iron chelation therapy; curative option is stem cell transplant.
    • Wilms tumour: Nephrectomy with chemotherapy ± radiotherapy per staging.
    • Osteosarcoma: Neoadjuvant chemotherapy, wide surgical excision (limb salvage where possible), then adjuvant chemotherapy.
    • Metastatic bone disease: Analgesia, bisphosphonates/denosumab, radiotherapy for pain, and prophylactic fixation of impending fractures.
    • Suspected lymphoma: Excisional lymph node biopsy for histological diagnosis, followed by staging and chemo(radio)therapy.
    • Occult primary head and neck malignancy: Panendoscopy with biopsies and PET-CT to localise the primary before definitive treatment.
  8. Infectious Disease
    • Dengue fever: Supportive care with fluid management and platelet monitoring; avoid NSAIDs due to bleeding risk.
    • Malaria (P. vivax): Chloroquine (or ACT depending on resistance) plus primaquine for radical cure of hypnozoites (after G6PD testing).
    • Measles: Supportive care (hydration, antipyretics) is first-line, as there is no specific antiviral treatment; vitamin A supplementation reduces morbidity/mortality, particularly in malnourished children in endemic settings; monitor for complications (pneumonia, encephalitis, otitis media) which drive most measles-related morbidity; post-exposure vaccination or immunoglobulin for susceptible contacts within the appropriate window, and isolate to prevent nosocomial spread given very high infectivity.
    • Scarlet fever: Penicillin (or an alternative if allergic) for the underlying streptococcal infection.
    • Late-onset neonatal sepsis: Empirical broad-spectrum IV antibiotics after cultures, adjusted once sensitivities known.
    • Neonatal sepsis: Prompt empirical IV antibiotics after septic work-up, with supportive care.
    • Chancroid: Single-dose azithromycin (or an alternative macrolide/ceftriaxone regimen).
    • Primary syphilis (chancre): Single-dose intramuscular benzathine penicillin G.
    • Necrotizing fasciitis: Emergency surgical debridement plus broad-spectrum IV antibiotics — a surgical emergency.
    • Surgical sepsis: Source control (drainage/debridement) plus early broad-spectrum antibiotics and haemodynamic support.
    • Surgical site infection due to breach of asepsis: Wound drainage/debridement as needed, antibiotics guided by culture, and reinforcement of aseptic technique.
    • Puerperal sepsis: Broad-spectrum IV antibiotics, source control (retained products), and haemodynamic support.
    • Opportunistic infection in pediatric HIV/AIDS: Treat the specific infection alongside prompt initiation/optimisation of antiretroviral therapy.
    • Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia: High-dose co-trimoxazole first-line, with adjunctive corticosteroids for moderate-severe hypoxaemia (PaO2 <70mmHg) to reduce mortality; use alternative regimens (clindamycin-primaquine, IV pentamidine) for sulfa allergy; monitor oxygenation and treatment response; start/continue antiretroviral therapy within 2 weeks in HIV-associated PJP, and initiate secondary prophylaxis until CD4 recovery.
    • Suspected TORCH infection: Targeted serology/PCR testing of mother and infant, with pathogen-specific treatment where available.
    • Chronic suppurative otitis media: Aural toilet, topical/systemic antibiotics for active infection, and surgery (tympanoplasty) for definitive repair.
    • Malignant otitis externa: Prolonged anti-pseudomonal antibiotics and control of underlying diabetes; imaging to assess skull base extension.
    • Peritonsillar abscess (quinsy): Needle aspiration or incision and drainage plus IV antibiotics.
    • Acute otitis media: Analgesia; antibiotics if severe, bilateral in a young child, or symptoms persist beyond 48–72 hours.
    • Acute bacterial sinusitis: Analgesia and nasal decongestion; antibiotics if severe or persistent beyond 10 days.
    • Orbital cellulitis: IV antibiotics and urgent ophthalmology/ENT review; surgical drainage if abscess or vision threatened.
    • Impetigo: Topical antibiotics for localised disease; oral antibiotics if extensive.
    • Herpes zoster: Oral antiviral (valaciclovir/aciclovir) started within 72 hours of rash onset first-line, particularly in older patients or ophthalmic/disseminated involvement; add gabapentin/pregabalin or amitriptyline for post-herpetic neuralgia; monitor for ophthalmic zoster (urgent ophthalmology referral) and disseminated disease in the immunocompromised, which requires IV therapy; recombinant zoster vaccine is recommended for prevention in older adults.
    • Scabies: Topical permethrin applied to the whole body, with treatment of close contacts simultaneously.
    • Tinea corporis (dermatophytosis): Topical antifungal for localised disease; oral antifungal for extensive/refractory infection.
    • Onychomycosis (fungal nail infection): Oral antifungal therapy (e.g. terbinafine) for adequate duration; topical lacquer for mild disease.
    • Chronic osteomyelitis: Prolonged targeted antibiotics with surgical debridement of infected/dead bone.
    • Paucibacillary (tuberculoid) leprosy: 6-month course of dapsone plus rifampicin multidrug therapy per WHO regimen.
    • Leprosy reaction (Type 1 or Type 2): Corticosteroids for Type 1 reactions; thalidomide/corticosteroids for Type 2 (ENL) reactions, continuing antileprosy therapy.
    • Acute hematogenous osteomyelitis: Prompt empirical IV antibiotics, with surgical drainage if abscess or poor response.
  9. Rheumatology & Immunology
    • Rheumatoid arthritis: Early DMARD therapy (e.g. methotrexate) ± biologics for refractory disease, with symptomatic NSAID/steroid use.
    • Systemic lupus erythematosus: Hydroxychloroquine as baseline therapy, with corticosteroids/immunosuppressants escalated for organ-threatening flares.
    • Cutaneous lupus erythematosus (with SLE association): Sun protection, topical corticosteroids, and hydroxychloroquine; screen for systemic involvement.
    • Systemic sclerosis (scleroderma): Organ-specific therapy (e.g. ACE-inhibitors for renal crisis, vasodilators for Raynaud's), with close multisystem monitoring.
    • Giant cell arteritis: Immediate high-dose corticosteroids to prevent irreversible vision loss, confirmed by temporal artery biopsy.
    • Granulomatosis with polyangiitis (GPA): Induction with corticosteroids plus cyclophosphamide or rituximab for organ-threatening disease.
    • Acute gouty arthritis: NSAIDs, colchicine, or corticosteroids for the acute attack; urate-lowering therapy started once the flare settles.
    • Acute anterior uveitis: Topical corticosteroids and cycloplegics; investigate for an underlying systemic association.
  10. Dermatology
    • Psoriasis: Topical corticosteroids/vitamin D analogues for mild disease; phototherapy or systemic/biologic agents for severe disease.
    • Atopic dermatitis (infantile eczema): Regular emollients and topical corticosteroids for flares, avoiding known triggers.
    • Pemphigus vulgaris: Systemic corticosteroids as first-line, with steroid-sparing immunosuppressants for maintenance.
    • Stevens-Johnson syndrome/toxic epidermal necrolysis: Immediate withdrawal of the culprit drug and supportive burns-unit level care.
    • Vitiligo: Topical corticosteroids/calcineurin inhibitors and phototherapy; camouflage and psychological support.
    • Alopecia areata: Intralesional or topical corticosteroids; observation as spontaneous regrowth is common.
    • Acne vulgaris: Topical retinoids/benzoyl peroxide for mild disease; oral antibiotics or isotretinoin for moderate-severe disease.
    • Rosacea: Avoidance of triggers, topical metronidazole/azelaic acid, and oral antibiotics for moderate disease.
    • Melasma: Sun protection and topical depigmenting agents (e.g. hydroquinone); chemical peels for refractory cases.
    • Drug-induced (steroid) skin changes: Taper/discontinue the offending agent where possible and manage skin changes symptomatically.
    • Necrobiosis lipoidica diabeticorum: Optimise glycaemic control and use topical/intralesional corticosteroids for active lesions.
    • Venous stasis ulcer: Compression therapy, leg elevation, and wound care; treat underlying venous insufficiency.
    • Drug allergy/hypersensitivity reaction: Stop the offending drug immediately and treat symptomatically (antihistamines/steroids); adrenaline if anaphylaxis.
    • Basal cell carcinoma: Surgical excision (or Mohs surgery for high-risk sites) is curative in the majority of cases.
    • Suspected malignant melanoma: Urgent excision biopsy with adequate margins, followed by staging and sentinel node assessment.
    • Stage 1 pressure injury: Regular repositioning, pressure offloading, and skin protection to prevent progression.
  11. Ophthalmology
    • Acute angle-closure glaucoma: Immediate IOP-lowering therapy (topical/systemic) followed by laser peripheral iridotomy.
    • Primary open-angle glaucoma: Topical IOP-lowering drops as first-line, with laser or surgery for progressive disease.
    • Age-related cataract: Elective cataract surgery (phacoemulsification with lens implant) once visually significant.
    • Central retinal artery occlusion: Ocular massage and urgent IOP-lowering measures; treat as a stroke equivalent with urgent referral.
    • Retinal detachment: Urgent surgical repair (vitrectomy, scleral buckle, or pneumatic retinopexy).
    • Diabetic retinopathy: Optimise glycaemic/BP control; laser photocoagulation or anti-VEGF injections for sight-threatening disease.
    • Bacterial conjunctivitis: Topical antibiotic drops and hygiene measures to prevent spread.
    • Microbial keratitis (corneal ulcer): Urgent intensive topical antibiotic (or antifungal) therapy guided by corneal scraping culture.
    • Open globe injury: Protective eye shield, avoid pressure on the globe, and urgent surgical repair.
    • Postoperative endophthalmitis: Emergency intravitreal antibiotics ± vitrectomy.
    • Optic neuritis: High-dose IV corticosteroids to hasten recovery; assess for underlying demyelinating disease.
    • Scleritis: Systemic NSAIDs or corticosteroids; investigate for an associated systemic autoimmune condition.
    • Stye (external hordeolum): Warm compresses; topical/oral antibiotics if spreading infection.
    • Entropion: Surgical correction of the eyelid margin.
    • Amblyopia: Correction of any refractive error plus occlusion (patching) therapy of the stronger eye, started early.
    • Convergent squint (esotropia): Early correction with glasses/patching, with surgery if needed, to prevent amblyopia.
    • Myopia: Refractive correction with glasses or contact lenses.
    • Presbyopia: Reading glasses/multifocal correction as needed with age.
    • Thyroid eye disease (Graves' ophthalmopathy): Control thyroid status, lubricants for exposure, and corticosteroids/orbital decompression for severe disease.
  12. ENT
    • Nasal polyp: Intranasal corticosteroids first-line; surgery (FESS) for refractory or obstructive disease.
    • Severe epistaxis: Direct pressure and nasal packing; cautery or surgical/interventional radiology control if refractory.
    • Recurrent acute tonsillitis: Analgesia and antibiotics for episodes; tonsillectomy considered for frequent/severe recurrence.
    • Suspected laryngeal carcinoma: Laryngoscopy with biopsy for diagnosis, followed by staging and surgery/radiotherapy.
    • Suspected oral cavity carcinoma: Biopsy for tissue diagnosis, staging, and surgical resection ± adjuvant radiotherapy.
    • Thyroglossal cyst: Surgical excision (Sistrunk procedure) to remove the cyst and central hyoid segment.
    • Pleomorphic adenoma of the parotid gland: Surgical excision (superficial parotidectomy) preserving the facial nerve.
    • Salivary gland (submandibular) calculus: Sialagogues and hydration for small stones; surgical removal for larger/obstructing calculi.
    • Ménière's disease: Salt restriction and diuretics, vestibular sedatives for acute attacks, and surgery for refractory cases.
    • Vestibular neuritis: Vestibular sedatives short-term and early vestibular rehabilitation exercises.
    • Presbycusis: Hearing aids and communication strategies; regular audiological review.
  13. Orthopaedics & Trauma
    • Fracture neck of femur: Early surgical fixation or arthroplasty, with prompt analgesia and prevention of complications.
    • Fragility hip fracture: Surgical fixation within 24–48 hours where possible, plus osteoporosis assessment and treatment.
    • Colles' fracture: Closed reduction and immobilisation in a cast; surgical fixation if unstable/comminuted.
    • Acetabular fracture: Analgesia and traction initially, with surgical fixation for displaced fractures.
    • Supracondylar fracture of the humerus with vascular compromise: Emergency reduction to restore perfusion; urgent surgical fixation.
    • Posterior hip dislocation: Emergency closed reduction under sedation/anaesthesia, followed by assessment for associated fractures.
    • Anterior shoulder dislocation: Closed reduction with analgesia/sedation, followed by immobilisation and physiotherapy.
    • Recurrent shoulder instability: Physiotherapy for rehabilitation; surgical stabilisation for recurrent/refractory instability.
    • Compartment syndrome: Emergency fasciotomy — a surgical emergency; do not delay for imaging.
    • Osteoarthritis of the knee: Weight loss, physiotherapy, and analgesia first-line; joint replacement for end-stage disease.
    • Osteoporosis: Calcium/vitamin D and bisphosphonate (or alternative) therapy, with falls prevention.
    • Osteoporotic vertebral compression fracture: Analgesia and early mobilisation; vertebroplasty/kyphoplasty for refractory pain; osteoporosis treatment.
    • Developmental dysplasia of the hip: Pavlik harness in infancy; surgical reduction if diagnosed late or harness fails.
    • Congenital talipes equinovarus (clubfoot): Ponseti method of serial casting, followed by bracing.
    • ACL tear: Physiotherapy for partial/low-demand tears; surgical reconstruction for complete tears in active patients.
    • Anterior cruciate ligament tear: Physiotherapy and activity modification, or surgical reconstruction depending on patient demand and instability.
    • Dupuytren's contracture: Observation for mild disease; needle fasciotomy or surgical fasciectomy for progressive contracture.
    • Flexor digitorum profundus tendon injury: Urgent surgical repair to restore tendon continuity, followed by structured rehabilitation.
    • Unstable pelvic fracture with hemorrhage: Pelvic binder, resuscitation, and urgent interventional radiology/surgical control of bleeding.
  14. Obstetrics & Gynaecology
    • Pre-eclampsia: Blood pressure control, magnesium sulfate for seizure prophylaxis if severe, and timed delivery.
    • Severe preeclampsia: Antihypertensives, IV magnesium sulfate, and expedited delivery once maternal/fetal condition allows.
    • Eclampsia: IV magnesium sulfate for seizure control/prophylaxis, blood pressure control, and expedited delivery.
    • Placenta previa: Pelvic rest and monitoring if stable; planned caesarean section for delivery.
    • Suspected ectopic pregnancy: Urgent ultrasound/beta-hCG work-up; medical (methotrexate) or surgical management depending on stability and findings.
    • Uterine rupture: Emergency laparotomy for delivery and repair (or hysterectomy) with resuscitation.
    • Postpartum hemorrhage due to uterine atony: Uterine massage, uterotonics (oxytocin first-line), and escalation to surgical measures if bleeding continues.
    • Shoulder dystocia: Systematic manoeuvres (McRoberts, suprapubic pressure) to release the impacted shoulder.
    • Breech presentation: External cephalic version if eligible; planned caesarean section or vaginal breech delivery per protocol.
    • Occipitoposterior position: Trial of vaginal delivery with possible rotation; caesarean section if labour fails to progress.
    • Preterm labour: Tocolysis to delay delivery, antenatal corticosteroids for fetal lung maturity, and magnesium sulfate for neuroprotection if very preterm.
    • Fetal growth restriction: Serial growth/Doppler surveillance, with timed delivery if fetal compromise develops.
    • Monochorionic diamniotic twins: Close surveillance for twin-to-twin transfusion syndrome with serial ultrasound.
    • Dysfunctional uterine bleeding: Hormonal therapy (e.g. combined pill, levonorgestrel IUS) first-line; surgical options for refractory cases.
    • Polycystic ovary syndrome: Lifestyle modification, metformin for insulin resistance, and hormonal therapy for cycle/symptom control.
    • Endometrial carcinoma: Total hysterectomy with bilateral salpingo-oophorectomy ± adjuvant radiotherapy per staging.
    • Suspected ovarian carcinoma: Tumour markers and imaging, followed by surgical staging/debulking and chemotherapy.
    • Uterine prolapse: Pelvic floor exercises/pessary for mild disease; surgical repair for symptomatic prolapse.
    • Fibroadenoma: Observation with follow-up for typical small lesions; excision if large, symptomatic, or diagnostically uncertain.
    • Carcinoma breast: Multidisciplinary staging with surgery, ± chemotherapy/radiotherapy/endocrine therapy per subtype and stage.
    • Vaginitis: Empirical or culture-guided antifungal/antibiotic therapy for candidiasis/trichomoniasis/bacterial vaginosis.
    • Pelvic inflammatory disease: Empirical broad-spectrum antibiotics covering gonorrhoea/chlamydia and anaerobes; treat partners.
    • Perimenopausal syndrome: Lifestyle measures and hormone replacement therapy if symptomatic, weighed against individual risk.
  15. Paediatrics & Neonatology
    • Down syndrome (Trisomy 21): Multidisciplinary supportive care, screening for associated cardiac/endocrine/haematological anomalies.
    • Spina bifida (meningomyelocele): Early surgical closure of the defect and multidisciplinary management of associated hydrocephalus/neurological deficit.
    • Anencephaly: Palliative, comfort-focused care as the condition is incompatible with survival.
    • Failure to thrive: Systematic evaluation for organic causes plus nutritional and psychosocial support.
    • Developmental delay: Formal developmental assessment and early intervention/therapy referral.
    • Attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder: Behavioural therapy first-line, with stimulant medication for moderate-severe impairment.
    • ADHD: Behavioural interventions with stimulant medication added for significant functional impairment.
    • Autism spectrum disorder: Early behavioural and educational intervention, with management of any co-occurring conditions.
    • Kawasaki disease: IV immunoglobulin and high-dose aspirin, with echocardiographic surveillance for coronary aneurysms.
    • Acute rheumatic fever: Penicillin for the streptococcal infection, anti-inflammatory therapy for carditis/arthritis, and secondary prophylaxis.
    • Non-accidental injury (child abuse): Ensure child safety with safeguarding referral, document findings carefully, and provide multidisciplinary support.
    • Suspected inborn error of metabolism: Stop feeds, correct metabolic derangement, and urgent metabolic work-up/specialist referral.
    • Inborn error of metabolism: Acute correction of metabolic derangement (stop protein/fat intake as appropriate) and long-term dietary/enzyme management.
    • Congenital talipes equinovarus (clubfoot): Ponseti serial casting followed by bracing.
  16. Psychiatry
    • Major depressive disorder: Psychotherapy (e.g. CBT) and/or antidepressant medication depending on severity.
    • Major depressive episode: Antidepressant therapy and/or psychotherapy, with safety assessment for suicide risk.
    • Manic episode (bipolar disorder): Mood stabiliser or antipsychotic therapy, with hospitalisation if risk to self/others.
    • Schizophrenia: Antipsychotic medication combined with psychosocial support and rehabilitation.
    • Brief psychotic disorder: Short-term antipsychotic therapy with close follow-up, as symptoms often resolve within a month.
    • Panic disorder: Cognitive behavioural therapy and/or SSRIs; short-term benzodiazepines used cautiously for acute attacks.
    • Somatic symptom disorder: Regular scheduled follow-up, limiting unnecessary investigations, and psychotherapy for underlying distress.
    • Antisocial personality disorder: Psychotherapy focused on behaviour management; pharmacotherapy for specific comorbid symptoms.
    • Borderline personality disorder: Dialectical behaviour therapy as first-line psychotherapy; medication for comorbid symptoms only.
    • Opioid dependence syndrome: Opioid substitution therapy (methadone/buprenorphine) combined with psychosocial support.
    • Neuroleptic malignant syndrome: Stop the causative antipsychotic immediately, cool and hydrate the patient, and give dantrolene/bromocriptine if severe.
  17. Surgery & Perioperative
    • Malignant hyperthermia: Stop the triggering anaesthetic agent immediately, give IV dantrolene, and actively cool the patient.
    • Local anesthetic systemic toxicity: Stop the local anaesthetic, secure the airway, and give IV lipid emulsion therapy.
    • Intraoperative awareness: Immediately deepen anaesthesia, and provide postoperative psychological support/debriefing.
    • Possible esophageal intubation or cardiac arrest: Immediately confirm tube placement (capnography) and reintubate/start CPR as needed — needs immediate evaluation.
  18. Emergency, Toxicology & Envenomation
    • Anaphylaxis: Immediate intramuscular adrenaline, high-flow oxygen, IV fluids, and removal of the trigger.
    • Heat stroke: Rapid active cooling and aggressive fluid resuscitation — a medical emergency.
    • Severe dehydration: Rapid IV fluid resuscitation followed by correction of ongoing losses and electrolyte derangement.
    • Organophosphate poisoning: Atropine to control secretions plus pralidoxime, with airway support as needed.
    • Organophosphorus poisoning: High-dose atropine and pralidoxime, with early airway protection.
    • Suspected opioid poisoning: Naloxone reversal, with airway/breathing support and monitoring for re-sedation.
    • Viper envenomation with coagulopathy: Antivenom therapy guided by clotting studies, plus supportive care.
    • Neurotoxic snake envenomation: Urgent antivenom and airway support given the risk of respiratory paralysis.
    • Pyrexia of unknown origin: Systematic evaluation (history, exam, staged investigation) rather than empirical treatment before a cause is found.
    • Contrast-induced anaphylactoid reaction: Stop the contrast infusion, give adrenaline for severe reactions, with antihistamines/steroids and supportive care.
Management+
  1. Cardiovascular
    • Stable angina (ischemic heart disease): Beta-blocker or calcium channel blocker plus antiplatelet and statin first-line; add a second antianginal agent (long-acting nitrate/ranolazine) if symptoms persist, and proceed to coronary angiography with revascularisation for high-risk anatomy or refractory symptoms; monitor heart rate/BP on titration and repeat lipid panel at 4-12 weeks on statin; in renal impairment adjust statin dose and prefer agents with non-renal clearance, and avoid non-dihydropyridine CCBs with concurrent beta-blockade in reduced ejection fraction.
    • Essential hypertension: Lifestyle modification plus a single agent (ACE-I/ARB, CCB, or thiazide-like diuretic) started first-line, adding a second and then third agent from different classes if target not met within 4-6 weeks per visit; add a mineralocorticoid antagonist (spironolactone) for resistant hypertension after excluding secondary causes; monitor renal function and potassium 1-2 weeks after starting/uptitrating ACE-I/ARB or spironolactone; avoid ACE-I/ARB in pregnancy (use labetalol/methyldopa/nifedipine instead) and start with lower doses in the elderly given orthostasis risk.
    • Atrial fibrillation (rate control): Beta-blocker or rate-limiting CCB first-line, adding digoxin for inadequate control especially in heart failure or sedentary patients; anticoagulate per CHA2DS2-VASc score (DOAC preferred over warfarin unless mechanical valve or significant mitral stenosis); monitor renal function at baseline and at least annually on DOACs since dosing is renally adjusted; reduce DOAC dose or avoid in severe renal impairment (CrCl<15-30 depending on agent), and use warfarin with target INR 2-3 in valvular AF or antiphospholipid syndrome.
    • Valvular heart disease (general): Serial echocardiographic surveillance (interval depends on valve/severity) with intervention once criteria for severe symptomatic disease or LV dysfunction are met; escalate to earlier intervention if exercise testing reveals symptoms or falling BP; monitor for endocarditis risk and provide antibiotic prophylaxis only for high-risk procedures in high-risk lesions per current guidance; in pregnancy, severe stenotic lesions may require pre-conception intervention given poor tolerance of the physiological volume load.
    • Cardiogenic shock: Inotropic/vasopressor support (norepinephrine first-line, dobutamine for inotropy) with treatment of the precipitant (urgent revascularisation if MI-related); escalate to mechanical circulatory support (IABP, Impella, ECMO) if refractory to pharmacological support; monitor lactate clearance, urine output, and invasive haemodynamics/arterial line; in the elderly or frail, weigh mechanical support risk-benefit carefully given high complication rates.
    • Rheumatic heart disease: Secondary penicillin prophylaxis (monthly IM benzathine penicillin) continued for years to decades depending on severity and carditis history, escalating to valve surgery for haemodynamically significant damage; monitor for recurrent carditis and progressive valve dysfunction with periodic echocardiography; in pregnancy, significant mitral stenosis often decompensates in the second trimester and may need balloon valvotomy before delivery.
    • Empyema: Chest tube drainage plus targeted IV antibiotics, escalating to intrapleural fibrinolytics or surgical decortication if loculated/organised or drainage fails; monitor clinical response, repeat imaging, and inflammatory markers to guide duration; in immunocompromised or diabetic patients broaden empirical cover and lower the threshold for early surgical referral.
    • Bronchiectasis: Airway clearance physiotherapy and treatment of infective exacerbations first-line, escalating to long-term macrolide therapy (azithromycin) in frequent exacerbators and inhaled antibiotics for chronic Pseudomonas colonisation; monitor sputum culture, lung function, and QTc/LFTs on chronic macrolide therapy; avoid macrolides in patients with significant QT prolongation or non-tuberculous mycobacterial infection until excluded.
    • Acute ST-elevation myocardial infarction: Primary PCI within 90-120 minutes is preferred; give thrombolysis if PCI unavailable within that window, followed by transfer for rescue/routine PCI if thrombolysis fails or as protocol dictates; dual antiplatelet therapy (aspirin plus a P2Y12 inhibitor) and anticoagulation alongside high-intensity statin and beta-blocker/ACE-I once stable; monitor for reperfusion arrhythmias, mechanical complications (VSD, papillary muscle rupture, free wall rupture) and troponin trend; in the elderly or those with high bleeding risk, consider shorter DAPT duration and a less potent P2Y12 agent.
    • NSTEMI/unstable angina: Risk stratify (GRACE score) to guide timing of invasive angiography (immediate for very high-risk, within 24-72h for high/intermediate risk); dual antiplatelet therapy and anticoagulation started on admission, with early invasive strategy for high-risk patients; monitor serial troponin and ECG for evolving changes; in CKD, adjust anticoagulant dosing and consider the bleeding-ischaemia trade-off of an invasive strategy.
    • Acute pericarditis: NSAIDs plus colchicine as first-line therapy; treat the underlying cause if identified.
