    • Cardiac tamponade: Urgent pericardiocentesis or surgical drainage is definitive; IV fluids are only a temporising bridge and should not delay drainage; monitor haemodynamics continuously and send fluid for cytology/culture/TB workup; in malignant effusion consider a pericardial window to prevent recurrence.
    • Constrictive pericarditis: Diuretics for symptom control of congestion first-line; pericardiectomy is the definitive treatment for refractory or progressive disease, ideally before severe myocardial atrophy develops; monitor for the transient/reversible constrictive phase which may resolve with anti-inflammatory therapy before committing to surgery; surgical risk is elevated in those with long-standing calcific constriction and reduced ejection fraction.
    • Infective endocarditis: Prolonged targeted IV antibiotics (typically 4-6 weeks) guided by blood cultures and organism sensitivities, empirical cover broadened while awaiting cultures in acutely unwell patients; valve surgery indicated for heart failure, uncontrolled infection, large mobile vegetations, or recurrent emboli, ideally not delayed once indicated; monitor for new murmurs, emboli, and repeat echocardiography; adjust antibiotic dosing in renal impairment and consider outpatient parenteral therapy only once stable and source controlled.
    • Dilated cardiomyopathy: Standard heart-failure therapy (ACE-I/ARB or ARNI, beta-blocker, mineralocorticoid antagonist, SGLT2 inhibitor) titrated to target doses; escalate to device therapy (ICD/CRT) once on optimal medical therapy with persistent low EF, and to transplant/LVAD for advanced refractory disease; monitor renal function/potassium on ACE-I/ARNI/spironolactone combination and repeat echocardiography to reassess EF; avoid ARNI within 36h of ACE-I switch (washout period) and reduce diuretic doses cautiously in pregnancy-associated cardiomyopathy.
    • Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy: Beta-blockers or non-dihydropyridine CCBs first-line for symptoms, avoiding vasodilators/high-dose diuretics that worsen outflow obstruction; escalate to septal reduction therapy (myectomy or alcohol ablation) for drug-refractory obstructive symptoms, and ICD implantation for patients meeting sudden death risk criteria; monitor for arrhythmia and outflow gradient on serial echocardiography; screen first-degree relatives given autosomal dominant inheritance, and counsel against competitive sports in high-risk patients.
    • Restrictive cardiomyopathy: Diuretics for congestion as symptomatic therapy; treat the underlying infiltrative cause where identifiable (e.g. disease-specific therapy for amyloidosis, iron chelation for haemochromatosis); monitor for progression to advanced heart failure, since restrictive physiology carries a generally poor prognosis without disease-modifying therapy; anticoagulation is often needed given high atrial fibrillation/thrombus risk.
    • Wolff-Parkinson-White syndrome: Catheter ablation of the accessory pathway is curative and recommended for symptomatic or high-risk asymptomatic patients (short refractory period on EP study); avoid AV-nodal blocking agents (digoxin, verapamil) in pre-excited atrial fibrillation as they can accelerate conduction down the accessory pathway and precipitate VF; monitor asymptomatic patients with periodic risk stratification, particularly athletes.
    • Ventricular tachycardia (sustained): Immediate synchronised cardioversion if haemodynamically unstable; antiarrhythmic therapy (amiodarone/procainamide) and correction of reversible causes (electrolytes, ischaemia) if stable, with ICD evaluation for structural heart disease; monitor QTc on antiarrhythmic therapy and electrolytes (K+, Mg2+) closely; catheter ablation considered for recurrent monomorphic VT despite device therapy.
    • Ventricular fibrillation: Immediate defibrillation integrated with high-quality CPR per ACLS algorithm; escalate to amiodarone/lidocaine for refractory VF and treat reversible causes (H's and T's); post-resuscitation, targeted temperature management and urgent coronary angiography if ischaemic cause suspected; ICD implantation is indicated in survivors without a clearly reversible cause.
    • Bradyarrhythmia with syncope: Temporary pacing if haemodynamically unstable; permanent pacemaker for persistent symptomatic bradycardia once reversible causes (drugs, electrolytes, hypothyroidism) are excluded; monitor for pacemaker-related complications (lead displacement, infection) post-implant; review and hold AV-nodal blocking medications that may be contributing before committing to a device.
    • Complete heart block: Permanent pacemaker implantation is definitive for persistent complete heart block; temporary pacing (transcutaneous or transvenous) is used to bridge unstable patients; monitor for underlying reversible causes (inferior MI, Lyme disease, medication toxicity) which may allow recovery without a permanent device; in congenital complete heart block, pacing thresholds differ and paediatric cardiology input is needed.
    • Deep vein thrombosis: Anticoagulation (DOAC or LMWH/warfarin) for at least 3 months, with compression stockings for symptom relief.
    • Pulmonary embolism (massive): Systemic thrombolysis (or catheter-directed therapy if bleeding risk precludes systemic lysis) for haemodynamic instability, plus therapeutic anticoagulation and haemodynamic/vasopressor support; monitor for reperfusion bleeding and right heart strain on echocardiography; in recent surgery or high bleeding risk, favour catheter-directed or surgical embolectomy over systemic thrombolysis.
    • Pulmonary embolism (non-massive): Therapeutic anticoagulation (DOAC preferred in most patients) guided by risk stratification (PESI score) to determine inpatient versus outpatient management; escalate to thrombolysis only if signs of right heart strain progress to instability; monitor for recurrent VTE and consider thrombophilia workup if unprovoked and young; use LMWH instead of DOACs in active cancer (though DOACs now acceptable in many low-GI-bleed-risk cancers) and in pregnancy.
    • Aortic dissection (type A): Emergency surgical repair is definitive given high mortality with delay; blood pressure and heart rate control (beta-blocker first, then vasodilator) while awaiting theatre to reduce shear stress; monitor for malperfusion syndromes (coronary, cerebral, mesenteric, renal, limb) which change urgency and approach; in Marfan/connective tissue disease, root replacement is favoured over repair given recurrence risk.
    • Aortic dissection (type B, uncomplicated): Medical management with aggressive heart rate and blood pressure control (beta-blocker plus vasodilator) as first-line; escalate to endovascular repair (TEVAR) for complications such as malperfusion, rupture, refractory pain, or rapid expansion; monitor with serial imaging to detect progression; long-term surveillance imaging is required indefinitely given chronic dissection risk.
    • Abdominal aortic aneurysm (unruptured, large): Elective surgical or endovascular repair (EVAR preferred where anatomically suitable) once size threshold (~5.5cm, or smaller with rapid growth/symptoms) is met; surveillance ultrasound at intervals based on size for smaller aneurysms; monitor for endoleak on follow-up imaging after EVAR; in women, intervention is often considered at a smaller diameter given higher rupture risk per size.
    • Peripheral arterial disease (claudication): Supervised exercise therapy, antiplatelet and high-intensity statin therapy as first-line, with cilostazol for symptom relief if not contraindicated; escalate to revascularisation (endovascular or surgical) for lifestyle-limiting claudication refractory to medical therapy or for critical limb ischaemia; monitor ankle-brachial index and foot skin integrity, especially in diabetics; avoid cilostazol in heart failure.
    • Acute limb ischaemia: Emergency revascularisation (embolectomy, catheter-directed thrombolysis, or bypass depending on cause/extent) plus immediate therapeutic anticoagulation; monitor for reperfusion injury (compartment syndrome, hyperkalaemia, myoglobinuria) after revascularisation; time to intervention is critical - irreversible ischaemia after roughly 6 hours often necessitates primary amputation.
    • Varicose veins (symptomatic): Compression stockings and leg elevation first-line; endovenous thermal ablation or foam sclerotherapy for symptomatic truncal reflux refractory to conservative measures, with surgical stripping reserved for select anatomy; monitor for superficial thrombophlebitis and skin changes (lipodermatosclerosis, venous ulceration) suggesting need for earlier intervention.
    • Cardiac syncope (structural cause): Treat the underlying structural abnormality definitively (e.g. valve replacement, revascularisation); device therapy (ICD) if syncope relates to a malignant arrhythmia risk; monitor with prolonged rhythm monitoring (implantable loop recorder) if the cause remains unclear despite initial workup; driving restrictions apply until the cause is identified and treated per local regulations.
    • Prinzmetal (vasospastic) angina: Calcium channel blockers first-line, with long-acting nitrates as adjunct; avoid non-selective beta-blockers which may worsen spasm by leaving alpha-mediated vasoconstriction unopposed; monitor for coexisting fixed atherosclerotic disease requiring separate management; smoking cessation is essential as smoking is a major trigger.
    • Coronary artery ectasia: Antiplatelet/anticoagulant therapy to reduce thrombotic risk within the dilated segments; manage concurrent atherosclerosis as per standard secondary prevention; monitor for aneurysm progression on serial imaging; surgical exclusion considered only for very large or symptomatic aneurysmal segments.
    • Left ventricular aneurysm (post-MI): Standard heart-failure and antithrombotic therapy (anticoagulation if mural thrombus present); surgical resection reserved for refractory arrhythmia or heart failure not controlled medically; monitor for thrombus formation and arrhythmia on echocardiography and rhythm monitoring; avoid early high-intensity exercise post-MI until aneurysm risk is assessed.
    • Mitral regurgitation (severe, symptomatic): Surgical repair (preferred over replacement where feasible) or replacement for severe symptomatic disease; transcatheter edge-to-edge repair (MitraClip) for high-risk surgical candidates with suitable anatomy; monitor LV size/function on serial echocardiography since intervention timing depends on early signs of ventricular decompensation; in pregnancy, severe MR is generally better tolerated than stenotic lesions but still needs specialist monitoring.
    • Mitral stenosis (severe): Percutaneous balloon valvotomy for favourable (non-calcified, non-regurgitant) anatomy; surgical valve replacement otherwise; monitor for atrial fibrillation and thromboembolism, requiring anticoagulation; in pregnancy, severe MS often decompensates in the second/third trimester and balloon valvotomy may be needed before delivery.
    • Aortic stenosis (severe, symptomatic): Surgical aortic valve replacement for lower-risk patients; transcatheter aortic valve replacement (TAVR) for intermediate-high surgical risk or elderly patients; avoid vasodilators and excessive diuresis which can precipitate hypotension in fixed-output states; monitor for rapid symptomatic progression, since symptomatic severe AS carries high mortality without intervention.
    • Aortic regurgitation (severe, symptomatic): Surgical valve replacement is definitive for symptomatic severe AR or asymptomatic disease with LV dysfunction; vasodilator therapy as a bridge in those not yet meeting surgical criteria or as a surgical risk-reduction measure; monitor LV size/function serially as intervention timing depends on early ventricular changes before irreversible dysfunction develops.
    • Tricuspid regurgitation (severe): Treat the underlying cause first (e.g. pulmonary hypertension, left-sided valve disease); surgery reserved for severe primary TR or when performed concurrently with left-sided valve surgery; monitor right heart size/function and signs of right heart failure (oedema, hepatomegaly, ascites); diuretics for symptomatic congestion in the interim.
    • Prosthetic valve thrombosis: Urgent thrombolysis or surgical thrombectomy depending on severity, valve position, and thrombus burden; monitor INR closely as subtherapeutic anticoagulation is the usual precipitant; in pregnancy, mechanical valve thrombosis risk is elevated and requires careful anticoagulation bridging strategy.
    • Prosthetic valve endocarditis: Prolonged targeted IV antibiotics with early surgical evaluation given high complication risk (abscess, dehiscence, persistent bacteraemia); monitor closely for new conduction abnormalities suggesting perivalvular extension; early-onset (<1 year post-surgery) disease often reflects perioperative contamination and typically requires more aggressive surgical management.
    • Atrial flutter: Rate or rhythm control with anticoagulation per stroke risk score, similar to atrial fibrillation; catheter ablation of the cavotricuspid isthmus is often curative and preferred over long-term antiarrhythmic therapy; monitor for conversion to atrial fibrillation which is common; cardiovert only after adequate anticoagulation or TOE exclusion of atrial thrombus if duration >48h or unknown.
    • Paroxysmal supraventricular tachycardia: Vagal manoeuvres first-line, then IV adenosine for acute termination; catheter ablation for recurrent episodes is generally curative and preferred over long-term antiarrhythmic drugs; monitor for pre-excitation on baseline ECG which changes drug choice (avoid AV-nodal blockers if WPW-associated); in pregnancy, adenosine remains safe for acute termination.
    • Long QT syndrome: Avoidance of QT-prolonging drugs, beta-blocker therapy, and ICD for high-risk patients.
    • Brugada syndrome: Avoidance of triggering drugs and prompt fever management (fever can unmask the ECG pattern and precipitate arrhythmia); ICD implantation for high-risk patients (aborted sudden death, syncope with type 1 pattern); monitor asymptomatic patients with periodic risk stratification; screen first-degree relatives given autosomal dominant inheritance.
    • Sick sinus syndrome: Permanent pacemaker for symptomatic bradycardia; review and discontinue AV-nodal blocking medications that may be unmasking or worsening the syndrome before committing to a device where feasible; monitor for tachy-brady syndrome requiring concurrent rate control and anticoagulation for the tachyarrhythmic component.
    • Acute limb ischaemia (embolic): Emergency embolectomy and immediate anticoagulation; identify the embolic source (atrial fibrillation, mural thrombus, valvular disease) to guide secondary prevention; monitor for reperfusion injury post-revascularisation; long-term anticoagulation is usually required once the source is identified.
    • Superior vena cava syndrome (malignant): Urgent stenting for rapid symptom relief in severe/threatening presentations, with radiotherapy or chemotherapy targeting the underlying tumour as definitive therapy; monitor airway and cerebral oedema symptoms in severe cases; avoid upper limb IV access/central lines on the affected side.
    • Raynaud's phenomenon (secondary): Calcium channel blockers and cold avoidance first-line; escalate to PDE5 inhibitors or IV prostacyclin analogues for severe/refractory digital ischaemia; treat the underlying connective tissue disease; monitor for digital ulceration requiring more aggressive vasodilator therapy and wound care.
    • Takayasu arteritis: Corticosteroids for induction, with immunosuppression (methotrexate, tocilizumab) for relapsing or steroid-refractory disease; monitor inflammatory markers and vascular imaging (MR/CT angiography) for disease activity, as symptoms alone can under-represent ongoing vasculitis; surgical/endovascular intervention for critical stenoses once inflammation is controlled.
    • Buerger's disease (thromboangiitis obliterans): Complete and permanent smoking cessation is essential and the only measure proven to alter the disease course; vasodilator therapy and wound care for symptom relief; monitor for digital ulceration/gangrene requiring amputation if smoking continues; revascularisation options are limited given the distal small-vessel pattern.
    • Cardiac amyloidosis: Disease-specific therapy (tafamidis for transthyretin type, chemotherapy/autologous transplant for AL type) alongside heart-failure symptom management; monitor for arrhythmia and conduction disease requiring pacing; avoid standard heart-failure agents like ACE-I/ARB and beta-blockers which are often poorly tolerated in restrictive amyloid physiology; anticoagulate for atrial fibrillation given high intracardiac thrombus risk even at low CHA2DS2-VASc scores.
    • Constrictive pericarditis (tuberculous): Anti-tubercular therapy plus corticosteroids in the acute phase to reduce progression to constriction; pericardiectomy for established persistent constriction; monitor liver function on anti-TB therapy and for drug interactions (rifampicin with other cardiac medications); TB pericarditis is a common cause of constriction in endemic regions.
    • Left bundle branch block with heart failure: Cardiac resynchronisation therapy (CRT) added to standard heart-failure medication for patients with reduced EF and wide LBBB QRS; monitor EF and functional class response to CRT at 3-6 months; ensure guideline-directed medical therapy is optimised before/alongside device implantation rather than as a substitute.
    • Coarctation of the aorta: Surgical repair or balloon angioplasty/stenting depending on age at presentation (surgery generally preferred in infancy, stenting favoured in older children/adults); monitor for re-coarctation and hypertension long-term even after successful repair; screen for associated bicuspid aortic valve and berry aneurysms.
    • Patent foramen ovale (cryptogenic stroke): Percutaneous closure considered in select patients under 60 with a cryptogenic stroke and high-risk PFO features, alongside antiplatelet therapy; anticoagulation preferred over closure if a separate indication for anticoagulation exists; monitor for recurrent embolic events and reassess for alternative stroke aetiology if events continue post-closure.
    • Atrial septal defect (haemodynamically significant): Percutaneous or surgical closure for significant left-to-right shunt (RV volume overload) before irreversible pulmonary vascular disease develops; monitor pulmonary artery pressures, as closure is contraindicated once Eisenmenger physiology is established; in pregnancy, unrepaired large ASDs carry increased risk and closure is ideally done pre-conception.
    • Ventricular septal defect (large, symptomatic): Surgical closure to prevent progression to pulmonary hypertension and heart failure, timed in infancy for large defects with heart failure/failure to thrive; monitor for spontaneous closure of small defects which may avoid surgery; assess pulmonary vascular resistance before closure to exclude Eisenmenger physiology where closure would be contraindicated.
    • Tetralogy of Fallot: Complete surgical repair, typically performed in infancy; palliative shunt procedures used first in select unstable neonates before definitive repair; monitor long-term for pulmonary regurgitation and right ventricular dysfunction requiring pulmonary valve replacement in adulthood; lifelong cardiology follow-up is required.
    • Hypertensive emergency: IV antihypertensive therapy (agent chosen by end-organ involvement, e.g. labetalol/nicardipine) to gradually lower blood pressure by no more than 25% in the first hour to avoid hypoperfusion; monitor for evolving end-organ injury (renal function, neurological status, cardiac markers); in aortic dissection, target heart rate and BP more aggressively and rapidly than in other hypertensive emergencies; in pregnancy (severe pre-eclampsia), use labetalol/hydralazine/nifedipine and add magnesium sulfate for seizure prophylaxis.
    • Renovascular hypertension: Angioplasty/stenting for fibromuscular dysplasia (often curative in younger patients); medical therapy (ACE-I/ARB with caution) preferred over revascularisation for atherosclerotic disease unless refractory to medical therapy or declining renal function; monitor renal function and potassium closely after starting ACE-I/ARB given risk of acute kidney injury in bilateral disease; avoid ACE-I/ARB in bilateral renal artery stenosis or stenosis to a solitary kidney.
    • Cardiac contusion (traumatic): Cardiac monitoring for arrhythmia in the first 24-48 hours; supportive care as most cases are self-limiting; monitor troponin trend and echocardiography if there is haemodynamic instability or significant ECG changes; treat arrhythmias as they arise per standard protocols.
    • Myocarditis (viral): Supportive heart-failure therapy (as tolerated) forms the mainstay; immunosuppression reserved for specific biopsy-proven aetiologies (giant cell myocarditis, eosinophilic myocarditis, sarcoid) rather than routine viral myocarditis; monitor for fulminant presentation requiring mechanical circulatory support, and for arrhythmia; avoid strenuous exercise for 3-6 months post-diagnosis given risk of sudden cardiac death.
  2. Respiratory
    • Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (stable): Long-acting bronchodilator (LAMA/LABA) first-line stepped up to triple therapy (LAMA/LABA/ICS) if frequent exacerbations or blood eosinophils raised; add roflumilast or azithromycin prophylaxis for recurrent exacerbators despite triple therapy; monitor spirometry annually, inhaler technique, and exacerbation frequency; assess for long-term oxygen if resting SpO2 low, and avoid ICS in those with recurrent pneumonia or low eosinophils given limited benefit and infection risk.
    • Pulmonary arterial hypertension: Risk-stratify at diagnosis to guide initial mono- or combination PAH-specific therapy (endothelin receptor antagonist, PDE5 inhibitor, prostacyclin pathway agent); escalate to triple therapy or IV prostacyclin for high-risk features or inadequate response; monitor via repeat right heart catheterisation, 6-minute walk, BNP, and functional class at 3-6 months; refer for lung transplant assessment if refractory, and avoid pregnancy given very high maternal mortality.
    • Bronchiolitis: Supportive care (oxygen, fluids, nasal suction) is mainstay as it is a self-limiting viral illness; escalate to high-flow nasal cannula or CPAP for increasing work of breathing/hypoxia, with ICU/ventilation for impending respiratory failure; monitor feeding, respiratory rate, and oxygen saturation closely in infants under 3 months or with comorbidities; avoid routine bronchodilators, steroids, or antibiotics as they lack proven benefit.
    • Allergic rhinitis: Intranasal corticosteroid first-line, adding a non-sedating antihistamine for breakthrough symptoms; escalate to allergen immunotherapy for severe or poorly controlled disease with confirmed sensitisation; monitor symptom control and adherence, as technique errors commonly limit intranasal steroid efficacy; avoid prolonged topical decongestant use due to rebound rhinitis medicamentosa.
    • Chronic sinusitis: Intranasal corticosteroids and saline irrigation first-line, with a prolonged antibiotic course or short oral steroid course for refractory symptoms; escalate to endoscopic sinus surgery for structural obstruction, nasal polyps, or failure of medical therapy; monitor with nasal endoscopy and CT if surgery being considered; screen for aspirin-exacerbated respiratory disease if associated with asthma and nasal polyps.
    • Community-acquired pneumonia (moderate severity): Empirical beta-lactam plus macrolide (or respiratory fluoroquinolone) guided by CURB-65 severity; narrow therapy once culture/sensitivity available and switch IV to oral once clinically stable; monitor vitals, oxygenation, and inflammatory markers with reassessment at 48-72 hours if not improving; adjust dosing in renal impairment and consider atypical/aspiration cover in the elderly or those with swallowing difficulty.
    • Hospital-acquired pneumonia: Empirical broad-spectrum antibiotics covering resistant Gram-negatives and MRSA per local resistance patterns, de-escalated once cultures return; escalate cover for late-onset disease (>5 days) or prior antibiotic exposure given higher resistant-organism risk; monitor clinical response and repeat cultures if deteriorating; in ICU/ventilated patients apply ventilator-associated pneumonia bundles to prevent recurrence.
    • Aspiration pneumonia: Antibiotics covering oral anaerobes and Gram-negatives (e.g. amoxicillin-clavulanate) alongside airway protection and treatment of the underlying aspiration risk (dysphagia, reduced consciousness); escalate to drainage for complicating lung abscess/empyema; monitor swallowing function with formal assessment before resuming oral intake; in stroke or neurodegenerative disease arrange speech-language therapy review to reduce recurrence.
    • Lung abscess: Prolonged antibiotics (typically 3-6 weeks, often clindamycin or beta-lactam/beta-lactamase inhibitor) covering anaerobes as first-line, with postural drainage; escalate to percutaneous or surgical drainage for large abscesses, failure to improve, or suspected malignant obstruction; monitor with serial chest imaging to confirm resolution; investigate for underlying bronchial obstruction (tumour, foreign body) in non-resolving or atypical cases.
    • Pneumothorax (primary spontaneous, small): Observation with high-flow oxygen alone is appropriate for small, minimally symptomatic pneumothoraces; escalate to needle aspiration or chest tube for larger or symptomatic pneumothoraces; monitor with repeat chest X-ray until resolution and advise against air travel/diving until fully resolved; refer for pleurodesis or surgery after a first recurrence or in high-risk occupations (pilots, divers).
    • Tension pneumothorax: Immediate needle decompression followed by chest tube insertion.
    • Acute exacerbation of asthma: Nebulised salbutamol/ipratropium plus systemic corticosteroids first-line, escalating to IV magnesium sulfate or aminophylline for severe/life-threatening features; ICU referral and consideration of intubation for silent chest, exhaustion, or rising CO2; monitor peak flow, oxygen saturation, and work of breathing serially, with arterial blood gas in severe cases; in pregnancy treat as aggressively as in non-pregnant patients since hypoxia poses greater fetal risk than medication.
    • Acute exacerbation of COPD: Bronchodilators, systemic corticosteroids, and antibiotics (if increased sputum purulence) first-line; escalate to non-invasive ventilation for hypercapnic respiratory failure with acidosis, and invasive ventilation if NIV fails or is contraindicated; monitor arterial blood gases serially and titrate oxygen to avoid hypercapnia in chronic retainers; use controlled oxygen therapy targeting SpO2 88-92% in known CO2 retainers.
    • Interstitial lung disease (idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis): Antifibrotic therapy (pirfenidone or nintedanib) first-line to slow progression, as no therapy reverses established fibrosis; escalate to lung transplant assessment for progressive disease despite antifibrotics; monitor pulmonary function tests (FVC, DLCO) every 3-6 months and treat acute exacerbations with high-dose steroids while excluding infection; avoid long-term prednisone/immunosuppression monotherapy, which lacks benefit and may cause harm in IPF specifically.
    • Sarcoidosis (symptomatic pulmonary): Observation alone for asymptomatic stage I disease; oral corticosteroids first-line for symptomatic pulmonary or extrapulmonary disease, adding a steroid-sparing agent (methotrexate/azathioprine) for relapsing or steroid-refractory disease; monitor pulmonary function, chest imaging, and calcium (risk of hypercalcaemia from granuloma-mediated vitamin D activation); screen for cardiac and neurological involvement given risk of arrhythmia and sudden death.
    • Obstructive sleep apnoea: Weight loss and CPAP first-line for moderate-severe disease based on polysomnography severity; mandibular advancement devices as an alternative for mild-moderate disease or CPAP intolerance; escalate to upper airway surgery in select anatomical cases; monitor adherence and symptom/daytime sleepiness response, and screen for cardiovascular risk (hypertension, arrhythmia) given strong association.
    • Pulmonary tuberculosis (drug-sensitive): Standard 6-month regimen (2 months HRZE intensive phase, 4 months HR continuation) first-line under directly observed therapy where possible; extend continuation phase for cavitary disease with positive 2-month culture; monitor sputum smear/culture conversion, liver function (hepatotoxicity risk), and visual acuity on ethambutol; adjust doses in pregnancy (avoid streptomycin) and in renal/hepatic impairment.
    • Pleural effusion (malignant): Therapeutic thoracentesis for symptom relief and diagnosis first-line; escalate to indwelling pleural catheter or pleurodesis (talc) for recurrent effusions in patients with reasonable prognosis; monitor for re-accumulation and trapped lung (failure of full re-expansion) which limits pleurodesis success; choice between IPC and pleurodesis depends on expected survival and performance status.
    • Chylothorax: Dietary modification (medium-chain triglycerides) or bowel rest with total parenteral nutrition first-line alongside drainage; escalate to octreotide/somatostatin analogue for persistent leak, and surgical thoracic duct ligation or embolisation for high-output or refractory chylothorax; monitor triglyceride level of pleural fluid to confirm diagnosis and daily output to gauge response; correct nutritional and immunological deficits from chronic lymph loss.
    • Cystic fibrosis (pulmonary): Airway clearance therapy and CFTR modulator therapy (if genotype-eligible) form the backbone of care, with inhaled mucolytics (dornase alfa, hypertonic saline); treat exacerbations with targeted IV antibiotics against chronic colonising organisms (Pseudomonas); monitor lung function, sputum culture, and nutritional status regularly; CFTR modulators have transformed prognosis but require monitoring of liver function and cataracts.
    • Acute respiratory distress syndrome: Lung-protective ventilation (low tidal volume ~6mL/kg predicted body weight, plateau pressure <30cmH2O) is first-line; escalate to prone positioning for moderate-severe hypoxaemia and neuromuscular blockade or ECMO for refractory cases; monitor driving pressure, oxygenation trends, and fluid balance (conservative strategy preferred once haemodynamically stable); treat the underlying precipitant (sepsis, aspiration, pancreatitis) concurrently.
    • Pulmonary alveolar proteinosis: Whole-lung lavage is definitive first-line therapy for symptomatic autoimmune PAP; inhaled or subcutaneous GM-CSF is an alternative for milder disease; monitor with serial CT and pulmonary function, as spontaneous remission can occur; investigate for secondary causes (haematological malignancy, dust exposure, immunodeficiency) which change management from lavage-focused to treating the underlying cause.
    • Hypersensitivity pneumonitis: Antigen avoidance is the essential first-line intervention; add corticosteroids for symptomatic or fibrosing disease, with antifibrotics considered for progressive fibrotic HP; monitor pulmonary function and HRCT for progression to fibrosis; occupational cases require environmental/workplace modification and, in some, mandatory redeployment.
    • Occupational asthma: Complete removal from the causative workplace exposure is the definitive first-line measure, as continued exposure worsens long-term prognosis even with medical therapy; standard asthma pharmacotherapy is used adjunctively; monitor serial peak flow at work versus away from work to confirm the diagnosis; early recognition and workplace change substantially improve the chance of full recovery.
    • Pneumoconiosis (coal worker's): No disease-modifying therapy exists; management is supportive (bronchodilators, pulmonary rehabilitation) with further dust exposure avoidance to prevent progression; escalate to lung transplant assessment in progressive massive fibrosis with respiratory failure; monitor for progression to progressive massive fibrosis and screen for tuberculosis and connective tissue disease association (Caplan syndrome).
    • Asbestosis: Supportive management (smoking cessation, pulmonary rehabilitation, oxygen if hypoxic) as no specific therapy reverses fibrosis; further asbestos exposure must be eliminated; monitor with serial pulmonary function and chest imaging, and screen for mesothelioma and lung cancer given markedly increased risk, particularly with concurrent smoking; occupational compensation assessment is often relevant.
    • Mesothelioma: Multimodal therapy (chemotherapy +/- surgery +/- radiotherapy) for surgically resectable disease in fit patients; palliative chemotherapy/immunotherapy for unresectable/metastatic disease; monitor with imaging response assessment and manage recurrent pleural effusions with pleurodesis/indwelling catheter; overall prognosis remains poor and early palliative care involvement is appropriate.
    • Pulmonary hypertension (group 1): See pulmonary arterial hypertension management; risk-stratified PAH-specific therapy escalated to combination or parenteral prostacyclin for high-risk disease; monitor via right heart catheterisation and functional testing; distinguish carefully from other PH groups (left heart disease, lung disease, chronic thromboembolic) as management differs substantially and misclassification leads to inappropriate therapy.
    • Chronic thromboembolic pulmonary hypertension: Pulmonary endarterectomy is potentially curative and first-line for surgically accessible disease in operable patients; balloon pulmonary angioplasty or PAH-specific therapy (riociguat) for inoperable or residual disease; lifelong anticoagulation is required regardless of intervention; monitor with V/Q scan, right heart catheterisation, and functional class; refer early to an expert CTEPH centre as operability assessment is specialised.
    • Empyema necessitans: Chest tube drainage of the underlying empyema plus targeted IV antibiotics and drainage/debridement of the extrathoracic collection; escalate to surgical decortication if loculated or if drainage is inadequate; monitor clinical response and repeat imaging; consider underlying tuberculosis or actinomycosis as causes, particularly in endemic settings, as these require prolonged specific therapy.
    • Bronchopleural fistula: Chest tube drainage and infection control first-line, with nutritional optimisation; escalate to surgical closure (with muscle flap) or bronchoscopic sealant/valve placement for persistent fistulae; monitor for ongoing air leak and signs of empyema; ventilator strategies should minimise positive pressure across the fistula where mechanical ventilation is required.
    • Mediastinitis (acute): Urgent surgical debridement plus broad-spectrum IV antibiotics (covering mixed aerobic/anaerobic flora, especially post-oesophageal perforation or sternotomy) is first-line given high mortality if delayed; monitor haemodynamics closely as septic shock is common; identify and control the source (oesophageal perforation, descending cervical infection, sternal wound) as source control determines outcome.
    • Diaphragmatic paralysis (unilateral): Observation is appropriate for asymptomatic unilateral paralysis; symptomatic patients benefit from diaphragmatic plication to improve dyspnoea and lung mechanics; monitor pulmonary function and symptoms, and investigate for an underlying cause (phrenic nerve injury, malignancy, cervical spine disease); bilateral involvement is more likely to need nocturnal non-invasive ventilation.
    • Chronic cough (unexplained): Stepwise evaluation and empirical treatment trials for the common treatable causes (upper airway cough syndrome, asthma/cough-variant asthma, GORD) first-line; escalate to specialist referral and consideration of neuromodulator therapy (gabapentin, low-dose morphine) or speech therapy for refractory cough hypersensitivity; monitor response to each sequential trial rather than treating empirically in combination; exclude ACE-inhibitor-induced cough by medication review.
    • Acute bronchitis: Supportive care (analgesia, fluids) is first-line as it is usually viral and self-limiting; avoid routine antibiotics unless pertussis or bacterial superinfection is suspected; monitor for red flags (high fever, focal signs, hypoxia) suggesting evolving pneumonia; in COPD patients treat as an exacerbation if increased sputum purulence is present.
    • Whooping cough (pertussis): Macrolide antibiotics first-line, most effective if started early (catarrhal phase) to reduce transmission though less so once paroxysmal cough is established; supportive care and close monitoring of infants for apnoea/cyanotic spells, who are at highest risk of severe disease; contact prophylaxis with macrolides for close contacts, especially unimmunised infants and pregnant women near term.
    • Pulmonary nodule (indeterminate, low risk): Serial CT surveillance at intervals guided by nodule size and risk (Fleischner criteria) is first-line for low-risk nodules; escalate to PET-CT or biopsy for growing nodules or high-risk features; monitor growth rate as the key discriminator of malignancy; in high-risk smokers with larger nodules, earlier tissue diagnosis is warranted rather than prolonged surveillance.
    • Bronchogenic cyst (symptomatic): Surgical excision is first-line for symptomatic cysts (infection, compression, haemoptysis) given risk of recurrent infection and rare malignant transformation; asymptomatic incidental cysts may be observed with imaging follow-up; monitor for secondary infection which can complicate surgical planning; thoracoscopic resection is preferred where anatomically feasible.
    • Massive haemoptysis: Airway protection (lateral decubitus positioning with bleeding side down, selective intubation if needed) and urgent bronchial artery embolisation is first-line definitive treatment; surgery reserved for embolisation failure or an anatomically resectable localised source; monitor airway patency and haemodynamics continuously as asphyxiation, not exsanguination, is the primary cause of death; identify and treat the underlying cause (TB, bronchiectasis, malignancy, aspergilloma).
    • Tracheal stenosis (post-intubation): Endoscopic dilation with or without laser/stenting first-line for short-segment stenosis; surgical tracheal resection and reconstruction for longer-segment or recurrent stenosis in suitable candidates; monitor for restenosis after endoscopic therapy, which is common; prevention through cuff pressure monitoring during prolonged intubation reduces incidence.
    • Chronic respiratory failure (home oxygen dependent): Long-term oxygen therapy (>15 hours/day) improves survival in chronic hypoxaemic respiratory failure meeting criteria; add home non-invasive ventilation for hypercapnic failure, particularly in COPD with persistent hypercapnia post-exacerbation; monitor oxygen saturation, arterial blood gases, and adherence; avoid excessive oxygen flow in CO2 retainers to prevent worsening hypercapnia.
    • Eosinophilic pneumonia: Oral corticosteroids first-line, with rapid and often dramatic clinical response distinguishing this from most other ILDs; taper slowly given risk of relapse, particularly in chronic eosinophilic pneumonia; monitor peripheral eosinophil count and chest imaging for resolution; identify and remove any causative drug or exposure where relevant.
  3. Gastrointestinal
    • Gastroesophageal reflux disease: Lifestyle measures plus PPI first-line, stepping down to lowest effective dose once controlled; escalate to endoscopy for alarm features (dysphagia, weight loss, bleeding, age >55 with new symptoms) and to anti-reflux surgery (fundoplication) for refractory symptoms or large hiatus hernia; monitor for Barrett's oesophagus in long-standing disease; avoid long-term high-dose PPI without reassessment given bone/renal/infection signals in observational data.
    • Gastric carcinoma: Surgical resection with lymphadenectomy is curative-intent first-line for resectable disease, with perioperative chemotherapy for locally advanced tumours; palliative chemotherapy or best supportive care for metastatic disease; monitor nutritional status and for anastomotic leak/dumping syndrome post-gastrectomy; screen relatives in hereditary diffuse gastric cancer (CDH1) families.
    • Hemorrhoids and fistula-in-ano: Fibre, fluids, and topical therapy first-line for grade I-II haemorrhoids, escalating to rubber band ligation or haemorrhoidectomy for higher grade/refractory disease; fistula-in-ano requires surgical management (fistulotomy for simple, seton for complex/high tracts) to avoid incontinence; monitor for recurrence and for underlying Crohn's disease in complex or recurrent fistulae.
    • Abdominal wall hernia: Elective mesh repair first-line for symptomatic reducible hernias; emergency surgery for irreducible, obstructed, or strangulated hernia given risk of bowel ischaemia; monitor for recurrence and mesh-related complications (infection, chronic pain); watchful waiting is reasonable for minimally symptomatic inguinal hernia in low-risk patients.
    • Anorectal abscess: Urgent incision and drainage is definitive, with antibiotics reserved for surrounding cellulitis, immunocompromise, or systemic sepsis rather than as a substitute for drainage; monitor for evolution into a chronic fistula-in-ano, which occurs in a substantial minority; underlying Crohn's disease should be considered in recurrent or complex abscesses.
    • Urinary tract infection: Empirical antibiotics guided by local resistance patterns, adjusted once culture sensitivities are available.
    • Urinary tract calculi: Analgesia and hydration for small stones; lithotripsy or ureteroscopy for larger/obstructing stones.
    • IgA nephropathy: ACE-inhibitor/ARB for proteinuria control; corticosteroids for progressive disease with preserved renal function.
    • Lupus nephritis: Induction immunosuppression (corticosteroids plus cyclophosphamide or mycophenolate) followed by maintenance therapy.
    • Membranous nephropathy: ACE-inhibitor/ARB for proteinuria, with immunosuppression (e.g. rituximab) for high-risk or progressive disease.
    • Acute appendicitis: Appendicectomy (laparoscopic preferred) first-line, with antibiotics alone as a selective alternative for uncomplicated appendicitis in appropriate patients; broad-spectrum IV antibiotics plus urgent surgery for perforated/complicated disease; monitor for post-operative collection; in pregnancy, surgery should not be delayed given higher complication rates with expectant management.
    • Acute cholecystitis: Early laparoscopic cholecystectomy (within 72h) first-line; percutaneous cholecystostomy for those too unstable for surgery; IV antibiotics and analgesia as supportive care throughout; monitor for progression to gangrenous cholecystitis or perforation; in pregnancy, surgery is preferred in the second trimester when feasible.
    • Choledocholithiasis: ERCP with sphincterotomy and stone extraction first-line, followed by cholecystectomy (same admission where possible) to prevent recurrence; monitor liver function and for post-ERCP pancreatitis/cholangitis; in pregnancy, ERCP with abdominal shielding is preferred over surgery for symptomatic stones.
    • Acute pancreatitis (mild): Aggressive IV fluid resuscitation, analgesia, and early oral feeding as tolerated (rather than prolonged fasting) first-line; identify and address the cause (cholecystectomy for gallstone pancreatitis during the same admission); monitor for organ dysfunction using severity scoring; avoid prophylactic antibiotics in the absence of infected necrosis.
    • Acute pancreatitis (severe/necrotising): ICU-level supportive care with aggressive fluid resuscitation and organ support first-line; delay intervention on necrosis (drainage/necrosectomy) until walled-off (typically >4 weeks) unless infected necrosis with clinical deterioration mandates earlier action; monitor for infected necrosis (fever, rising inflammatory markers) and multi-organ failure; nutritional support (enteral preferred over parenteral) should start early.
    • Chronic pancreatitis: Analgesia, pancreatic enzyme replacement, and alcohol/smoking cessation first-line; escalate to endoscopic or surgical decompression for ductal obstruction/refractory pain; monitor for diabetes, malabsorption/steatorrhoea, and pancreatic cancer risk (particularly hereditary pancreatitis); coeliac plexus block can help refractory pain unresponsive to medical therapy.
    • Peptic ulcer disease: PPI first-line with H. pylori eradication therapy if positive (triple/quadruple regimen); stop or minimise NSAIDs/aspirin where possible, adding PPI co-prescription if they must continue; monitor for healing endoscopically in gastric ulcers to exclude malignancy; test-of-cure for H. pylori is recommended given eradication failure rates.
    • Perforated peptic ulcer: Urgent surgical repair (omental patch) is definitive, with non-operative management (NG decompression, IV PPI, antibiotics) reserved for selected stable patients with sealed perforation on imaging; monitor for sepsis and intra-abdominal collection; H. pylori eradication and NSAID cessation post-recovery reduce recurrence.
    • Upper gastrointestinal bleeding (variceal): Vasoactive drugs (terlipressin/octreotide) plus prophylactic antibiotics started immediately, with endoscopic band ligation within 12h as definitive therapy; escalate to TIPS or balloon tamponade for refractory bleeding; monitor haemoglobin, for hepatic encephalopathy, and rebleeding; beta-blockers for secondary prophylaxis once stabilised.
    • Upper gastrointestinal bleeding (non-variceal): IV PPI and endoscopy within 24h first-line, with endoscopic haemostasis (clips, thermal, injection) for high-risk stigmata; risk-stratify with Glasgow-Blatchford/Rockall scores to guide admission and timing; monitor haemoglobin and haemodynamics; interventional radiology embolisation or surgery for failed endoscopic control.
    • Lower gastrointestinal bleeding: Resuscitation first, then colonoscopy once stabilised (or CT angiography if actively bleeding and unstable) to localise and treat the source; escalate to interventional radiology embolisation or surgery if endoscopic control fails; monitor haemoglobin trend; most cases (diverticular, angiodysplasia) settle spontaneously with supportive care.
    • Acute diverticulitis (uncomplicated): Oral antibiotics and liquid diet on an outpatient basis for mild uncomplicated disease (or observation alone in selected low-risk patients per some guidelines); escalate to admission and IV antibiotics for systemic upset or comorbidity; monitor for progression to abscess/perforation; colonoscopy after resolution to exclude malignancy, avoided during the acute phase given perforation risk.
    • Diverticular abscess: IV antibiotics plus percutaneous drainage for abscesses >3-4cm first-line; emergency surgery (Hartmann's procedure) for generalised peritonitis or drainage failure; monitor for recurrence, which may prompt elective resection after recovery; interval colonoscopy is required to exclude underlying malignancy.
    • Bowel obstruction (small bowel, uncomplicated): NG decompression, IV fluids, and correction of electrolytes first-line ('drip and suck'), which resolves most adhesional obstructions; escalate to surgery for failure to improve within 48-72h, or immediately for signs of strangulation/ischaemia; monitor for peritonism and lactate as markers of compromised bowel.
    • Volvulus (sigmoid): Endoscopic decompression first-line for uncomplicated sigmoid volvulus, followed by elective resection given high recurrence risk; emergency surgery for signs of ischaemia, perforation, or failed endoscopic detorsion; monitor for recurrence, particularly common before definitive resection; caecal volvulus generally requires surgery rather than endoscopic reduction.
    • Ulcerative colitis (moderate flare): Oral/topical corticosteroids and 5-ASA first-line for induction, adding biologic therapy (anti-TNF, vedolizumab) for steroid-refractory or steroid-dependent disease; monitor CRP, faecal calprotectin, and for toxic megacolon in severe flares; screen for colorectal cancer with regular colonoscopic surveillance in long-standing extensive disease.
    • Crohn's disease (active): Corticosteroids for induction with early introduction of immunomodulator (azathioprine) or biologic (anti-TNF) for moderate-severe or steroid-dependent disease; surgery for stricturing/fistulising disease refractory to medical therapy; monitor nutritional status, CRP/calprotectin, and screen for TB/hepatitis B before biologics; smoking cessation is a key modifiable factor worsening course.
    • Irritable bowel syndrome: Dietary modification (low FODMAP trial) and reassurance first-line; add antispasmodics, laxatives, or antidiarrhoeals targeted to the predominant symptom pattern, with low-dose tricyclics or gut-directed psychotherapy for refractory pain; monitor for red flags (weight loss, bleeding, nocturnal symptoms, age >50 new onset) prompting investigation to exclude organic disease.
    • Celiac disease: Strict lifelong gluten-free diet is the only effective treatment; monitor coeliac serology and symptom response, and repeat duodenal biopsy if response is inadequate to exclude refractory disease; screen for osteoporosis, iron/B12/folate deficiency, and associated autoimmune conditions (type 1 diabetes, thyroid disease); avoid empirical gluten withdrawal before serology/biopsy as this reduces diagnostic accuracy.
    • Acute viral gastroenteritis: Oral rehydration therapy first-line, escalating to IV fluids for significant dehydration or intractable vomiting; antiemetics may help in children/adults with ongoing vomiting; monitor hydration status and electrolytes in severe cases; antibiotics are not indicated and antimotility agents are avoided if bloody diarrhoea/high fever suggests bacterial cause.
    • Clostridioides difficile colitis: Oral vancomycin or fidaxomicin first-line (metronidazole now reserved for mild disease where the others are unavailable); escalate to oral vancomycin plus IV metronidazole for fulminant disease, with surgery (colectomy) for toxic megacolon/perforation; monitor for recurrence, which is common and may need fidaxomicin or faecal microbiota transplant; stop the precipitating antibiotic where possible.
    • Colorectal carcinoma (resectable): Surgical resection with lymphadenectomy first-line, with neoadjuvant chemoradiotherapy for locally advanced rectal cancer and adjuvant chemotherapy for node-positive disease; monitor with surveillance colonoscopy and CEA/imaging post-resection for recurrence; screen first-degree relatives given familial risk, and test for Lynch syndrome in appropriate cases.
    • Hepatocellular carcinoma: Resection or liver transplantation for early-stage disease within transplant criteria (Milan criteria); locoregional therapy (ablation, TACE) for unresectable disease confined to the liver, and systemic therapy (tyrosine kinase inhibitors/immunotherapy) for advanced disease; monitor AFP and imaging surveillance every 6 months in cirrhotic patients for early detection; manage underlying cirrhosis/hepatitis concurrently.
    • Liver abscess (amoebic): Metronidazole first-line, which is effective for most cases without drainage; add percutaneous aspiration/drainage for large abscesses (>5cm), diagnostic uncertainty, or failure to respond within 3-5 days; monitor clinical and radiological resolution, which can lag behind symptomatic improvement; luminal agent (paromomycin) after treatment to eradicate intestinal colonisation.
    • Acute cholangitis: Urgent IV antibiotics plus biliary decompression (ERCP first-line) is definitive, with the timing of ERCP guided by severity (emergency for septic shock); percutaneous transhepatic drainage if ERCP fails/unavailable; monitor for sepsis and organ dysfunction; cholecystectomy after recovery if gallstones are the cause.
    • Portal hypertension with ascites: Sodium restriction and diuretics (spironolactone plus furosemide) first-line; escalate to large-volume paracentesis with albumin replacement for refractory/tense ascites, and TIPS for recurrent refractory ascites; monitor renal function and electrolytes on diuretics; avoid NSAIDs and excessive diuresis, which risk hepatorenal syndrome.
    • Spontaneous bacterial peritonitis: Empirical IV antibiotics (third-generation cephalosporin) started promptly on diagnostic paracentesis (neutrophils >250/mm3), plus IV albumin to reduce hepatorenal syndrome risk; monitor renal function and repeat paracentesis if not improving; secondary antibiotic prophylaxis (norfloxacin) after an episode given high recurrence risk.
    • Hepatic encephalopathy: Lactulose first-line, titrated to 2-3 soft stools/day, adding rifaximin for recurrent/refractory episodes; identify and treat precipitants (infection, GI bleeding, electrolyte disturbance, constipation); monitor mental status and ammonia trend (though ammonia correlates imperfectly with severity); avoid sedatives which can precipitate or worsen encephalopathy.
    • Achalasia: Pneumatic dilatation or laparoscopic Heller myotomy first-line (comparable efficacy), with peroral endoscopic myotomy (POEM) an emerging alternative; botulinum toxin injection reserved for those unfit for definitive therapy given limited duration of effect; monitor for reflux post-myotomy, which is common and may need long-term PPI; barium swallow/manometry confirms diagnosis and excludes pseudoachalasia.
    • Anal fissure: Topical GTN or diltiazem plus fibre/stool softeners first-line; escalate to botulinum toxin injection or lateral internal sphincterotomy for chronic fissures refractory to topical therapy; monitor healing at 6-8 weeks; sphincterotomy carries a small risk of incontinence, weighed against persistent symptoms.
    • Zenker's diverticulum: Endoscopic or open surgical myotomy with diverticulectomy/diverticulopexy for symptomatic disease; small asymptomatic diverticula can be observed; monitor for aspiration risk pre-intervention and recurrence post-procedure; regurgitation of undigested food and halitosis are characteristic presenting symptoms guiding diagnosis.
    • Barrett's oesophagus (no dysplasia): PPI therapy and surveillance endoscopy at intervals guided by segment length (typically 3-5 yearly) first-line; no endoscopic ablation indicated without dysplasia; monitor for progression to dysplasia on surveillance biopsies; lifestyle reflux measures should continue alongside acid suppression.
    • Barrett's oesophagus (high-grade dysplasia): Endoscopic eradication therapy (radiofrequency ablation with or without endoscopic mucosal resection of visible lesions) first-line given high progression risk to cancer; oesophagectomy reserved for those with invasive cancer or endoscopically unresectable disease; monitor with intensive surveillance post-ablation for recurrence.
    • Oesophageal carcinoma (resectable): Neoadjuvant chemoradiotherapy followed by oesophagectomy first-line for locally advanced resectable disease; endoscopic resection alone may suffice for early T1a disease; monitor nutritional status and for anastomotic leak post-operatively; palliative stenting/chemotherapy for unresectable or metastatic disease.
    • Oesophageal varices (primary prophylaxis): Non-selective beta-blockers or endoscopic variceal band ligation first-line for medium/large varices, chosen based on patient factors and local expertise; monitor for beta-blocker tolerance (heart rate, blood pressure) or band ligation-related ulceration; repeat surveillance endoscopy for those not on prophylaxis with small varices.
    • Mallory-Weiss tear: Supportive care as most bleeds are self-limiting; endoscopic haemostasis (clips, injection) for active bleeding at endoscopy; monitor haemoglobin and haemodynamics; identify and address the precipitant (forceful vomiting/retching, often alcohol-related).
    • Boerhaave syndrome (oesophageal rupture): Urgent surgical repair or oesophagectomy is first-line for most cases given high mortality if untreated, with covered stenting/drainage as an alternative in selected stable patients with contained perforation; broad-spectrum antibiotics and NPO status alongside; monitor for mediastinitis and sepsis; delay in diagnosis significantly worsens outcome, so maintain high suspicion after vomiting with chest pain.
    • Eosinophilic oesophagitis: Proton pump inhibitor trial first-line (a subset respond, termed PPI-responsive oesophageal eosinophilia), escalating to topical swallowed corticosteroids or dietary elimination for non-responders; monitor with repeat biopsy to confirm histological remission, as symptoms correlate imperfectly with inflammation; oesophageal dilatation for stricturing complications.
    • Gastric outlet obstruction (benign): Endoscopic balloon dilatation first-line for peptic stricture-related obstruction, with surgery (gastrojejunostomy) for recurrent/refractory cases; treat underlying peptic ulcer disease/H. pylori; monitor for recurrence and nutritional status; malignancy must be excluded before assuming a benign cause.
    • Zollinger-Ellison syndrome (gastrinoma): High-dose PPI first-line for acid hypersecretion control; localise and surgically resect the gastrinoma where feasible, particularly in sporadic disease, as this offers potential cure; monitor fasting gastrin and secretin stimulation test; screen for MEN1 syndrome given association, which alters surgical approach.
    • Gastroparesis: Dietary modification (small frequent low-fat, low-fibre meals) and prokinetics (metoclopramide, domperidone) first-line; escalate to gastric electrical stimulation or venting/feeding jejunostomy for refractory severe disease; monitor glycaemic control in diabetic gastroparesis, as poor control worsens motility; limit metoclopramide duration given extrapyramidal risk.
    • Small bowel bacterial overgrowth: Antibiotic course (rifaximin or other broad-spectrum agent) first-line, often cycled given high recurrence; treat the underlying predisposing cause (stricture, dysmotility, prior surgery) where identifiable; monitor for nutritional deficiencies (B12, fat-soluble vitamins) and symptom recurrence; hydrogen/methane breath testing supports diagnosis.
    • Mesenteric ischaemia (acute): Urgent surgical or endovascular revascularisation plus resection of non-viable bowel is definitive, with anticoagulation started promptly once diagnosis suspected; broad-spectrum antibiotics given bacterial translocation risk; monitor lactate and for peritonism indicating bowel infarction; high mortality reflects the need for rapid diagnosis and intervention.
    • Chronic mesenteric ischaemia: Endovascular angioplasty/stenting first-line for suitable anatomy, with open bypass reserved for extensive disease or endovascular failure; monitor for symptom recurrence (postprandial pain, weight loss) and in-stent restenosis; address cardiovascular risk factors given shared atherosclerotic aetiology.
    • Ischaemic colitis: Supportive care (fluids, bowel rest, antibiotics if signs of infection) first-line for most cases, which are transient and resolve without intervention; escalate to surgery for peritonitis, perforation, or gangrenous colitis; monitor for stricture formation as a late complication; identify and correct any precipitating hypoperfusion event.
    • Toxic megacolon: IV corticosteroids (or antibiotics if C. difficile-related) plus bowel rest and NG decompression first-line, with surgical colectomy for deterioration or failure to improve within 24-72h; monitor abdominal girth, vital signs, and for perforation; avoid antimotility agents and opioids which can precipitate the condition.
    • Microscopic colitis: Budesonide first-line for induction of remission, which has the strongest evidence base; stop implicated drugs (NSAIDs, PPIs, SSRIs) where possible as they are associated triggers; monitor symptom response and relapse, which is common on stopping therapy; distinguish from IBD on biopsy given normal endoscopic appearance.
    • Rectal prolapse: Surgical repair (rectopexy, abdominal or perineal approach depending on fitness) is definitive for full-thickness prolapse; pelvic floor exercises/biofeedback for mucosal prolapse or as an adjunct; monitor for recurrence, more common with perineal approaches; assess for coexisting pelvic floor dysfunction and incontinence.
    • Anal fistula (complex): Seton drainage first-line to control sepsis while preserving continence, with definitive repair (advancement flap, LIFT procedure) once inflammation settles; avoid fistulotomy for high/complex tracts given incontinence risk; monitor for underlying Crohn's disease, which changes management priorities toward medical control first.
    • Pilonidal sinus disease: Incision and drainage for acute abscess first-line, with definitive excision (with or without flap closure) for chronic/recurrent disease; hygiene and hair removal reduce recurrence; monitor wound healing, as recurrence and delayed healing are common with midline closure techniques.
    • Perianal abscess: Prompt incision and drainage is definitive; antibiotics as an adjunct only in immunocompromise, systemic sepsis, or extensive cellulitis; monitor for fistula formation on follow-up; recurrent or complex abscesses warrant assessment for underlying Crohn's disease.
    • Acute mesenteric adenitis: Supportive care (analgesia, hydration) first-line, as this is typically a self-limiting viral-associated condition; monitor for resolution and exclude appendicitis as the key differential, particularly in children; imaging (ultrasound) helps distinguish the two and avoids unnecessary surgery.
    • Cholangiocarcinoma (resectable): Surgical resection (with liver resection/transplant for select hilar tumours) first-line for early localised disease; biliary drainage (ERCP/PTC stenting) for obstructive jaundice pre-operatively or in unresectable disease; monitor liver function and CA 19-9; systemic chemotherapy for unresectable/metastatic disease.
    • Gallbladder carcinoma (early): Simple or extended cholecystectomy with lymphadenectomy first-line depending on tumour depth (often found incidentally after routine cholecystectomy); monitor for recurrence with surveillance imaging; screen for and address risk factors (porcelain gallbladder, large polyps) proactively in at-risk populations.
    • Primary sclerosing cholangitis: No proven disease-modifying medical therapy; manage complications (dominant stricture dilatation via ERCP, cholestatic symptom control) and monitor for cholangiocarcinoma and colorectal cancer (annual colonoscopy given strong IBD association) and progression to cirrhosis; liver transplantation for end-stage disease, though recurrence can occur post-transplant.
    • Primary biliary cholangitis: Ursodeoxycholic acid first-line, improving biochemical response and slowing progression in most patients; add obeticholic acid or fibrates for inadequate biochemical response; monitor liver biochemistry (alkaline phosphatase response predicts outcome) and screen for associated autoimmune conditions and osteoporosis; pruritus managed with cholestyramine or other antipruritics.
    • Autoimmune hepatitis: Corticosteroids (with or without azathioprine) first-line for induction, tapering to the lowest dose maintaining remission with a steroid-sparing agent; monitor liver biochemistry and consider repeat biopsy before stopping treatment given high relapse risk; liver transplantation for acute severe presentation or end-stage disease unresponsive to therapy.
    • Non-alcoholic fatty liver disease: Weight loss (7-10% of body weight) and management of metabolic risk factors first-line, which improves steatohepatitis and fibrosis; pioglitazone or GLP-1 agonists/newer agents for biopsy-proven NASH with fibrosis; monitor liver fibrosis non-invasively (elastography) and cardiovascular risk, the leading cause of mortality in this population.
    • Alcoholic hepatitis (severe): Corticosteroids (with Lille score reassessment at day 7 to guide continuation) first-line for severe disease (Maddrey score ≥32); alcohol abstinence support is essential alongside; monitor for infection before and during steroid therapy, and renal function given hepatorenal syndrome risk; nutritional support with adequate calorie/protein intake improves outcomes.
    • Wilson's disease (hepatic presentation): Chelation therapy (penicillamine or trientine) first-line, or zinc for pre-symptomatic/maintenance therapy; liver transplantation for acute liver failure or decompensated cirrhosis unresponsive to medical therapy; monitor copper studies (24h urinary copper, caeruloplasmin) and liver function; screen siblings given autosomal recessive inheritance.
    • Acute liver failure: Supportive ICU-level care addressing coagulopathy, encephalopathy, and cerebral oedema first-line, with N-acetylcysteine for paracetamol-induced cases regardless of cause suspected; urgent liver transplantation assessment using validated prognostic criteria (King's College criteria); monitor ammonia, coagulation, and neurological status closely given rapid deterioration risk.
    • Budd-Chiari syndrome: Anticoagulation first-line once diagnosed, escalating to angioplasty/stenting or TIPS for symptomatic outflow obstruction, and liver transplantation for fulminant or decompensated cirrhotic disease; monitor for an underlying prothrombotic disorder (myeloproliferative neoplasm is common) requiring separate long-term management; screen for JAK2 mutation.
    • Short bowel syndrome: Nutritional optimisation (enteral where possible, parenteral nutrition if inadequate absorption) first-line, with antidiarrhoeal/antisecretory agents to reduce output; teduglutide (GLP-2 analogue) for those dependent on parenteral nutrition to promote adaptation; monitor fluid/electrolyte balance and for parenteral nutrition-associated liver disease; intestinal transplantation reserved for irreversible intestinal failure with life-threatening complications.
    • Familial adenomatous polyposis: Prophylactic colectomy (typically in late teens/early twenties) is definitive given near-universal cancer risk if untreated; regular endoscopic surveillance (upper and lower GI) before and after surgery for polyp progression and duodenal/ampullary polyps; genetic testing and counselling for at-risk relatives given autosomal dominant inheritance.
    • Lynch syndrome (surveillance): Regular colonoscopic surveillance from a young age (typically every 1-2 years) is the mainstay rather than prophylactic surgery; consider risk-reducing hysterectomy/salpingo-oophorectomy after childbearing given endometrial/ovarian cancer risk; genetic testing and cascade testing of relatives given autosomal dominant inheritance; aspirin chemoprevention has evidence for colorectal cancer risk reduction.
    • Anal carcinoma: Chemoradiotherapy (Nigro protocol) first-line, achieving good cure rates while preserving sphincter function, with surgery reserved for residual/recurrent disease; monitor treatment response and for late radiation toxicity; HIV-positive patients have higher incidence and should be screened for HPV-related disease.
    • Carcinoid tumour (gastrointestinal): Surgical resection first-line for localised disease; somatostatin analogues (octreotide) for symptom control of carcinoid syndrome and disease control in unresectable/metastatic disease; monitor 5-HIAA levels and for carcinoid heart disease with echocardiography; avoid precipitants of carcinoid crisis (anaesthesia, tumour manipulation) without pre-treatment octreotide.
    • Coeliac crisis: IV fluid and electrolyte resuscitation plus corticosteroids first-line for this rare severe presentation of coeliac disease with profound diarrhoea/malabsorption; strict gluten-free diet established once stabilised; monitor electrolytes closely given risk of refeeding syndrome and severe dehydration; distinguish from other causes of acute severe diarrhoea.
    • Whipple's disease: Prolonged antibiotic therapy (initial IV ceftriaxone/penicillin followed by extended oral co-trimoxazole, typically 1 year) first-line given high relapse risk with shorter courses; monitor for neurological involvement, which may require CNS-penetrating regimens and closer surveillance; relapse can present atypically, requiring a high index of suspicion.
    • Protein-losing enteropathy: Treat the underlying cause (cardiac, inflammatory, lymphatic) first-line, as this determines definitive management; supportive nutritional support (high-protein diet, medium-chain triglycerides for lymphatic causes) alongside; monitor serum albumin and alpha-1-antitrypsin clearance to track response; diuretics for associated oedema.
    • Intestinal tuberculosis: Standard anti-tubercular therapy (4-drug regimen) first-line, generally for 6 months (may be extended); surgery reserved for complications (obstruction, perforation, bleeding) unresponsive to medical therapy; monitor clinical/radiological response; distinguishing from Crohn's disease can be difficult and is critical since immunosuppression would worsen TB.
  4. Endocrine & Metabolic
    • Type 2 diabetes mellitus: Metformin first-line alongside lifestyle modification, adding SGLT2 inhibitor or GLP-1 agonist early for those with established cardiovascular/renal disease or obesity regardless of glycaemic control; escalate to insulin for symptomatic hyperglycaemia or inadequate control on combination oral therapy; monitor HbA1c 3-6 monthly, renal function, and annual screening for retinopathy/nephropathy/neuropathy; adjust metformin dose or hold in significant renal impairment or acute illness risking lactic acidosis.
    • Metabolic syndrome and obesity: Lifestyle modification (diet, exercise, weight loss) first-line targeting each component (glycaemia, lipids, blood pressure, waist circumference); add pharmacotherapy (GLP-1 agonists) or bariatric surgery for inadequate response with significant obesity; monitor components individually with periodic reassessment of cardiovascular risk; address components together rather than in isolation, as they synergistically increase cardiovascular risk.
    • Dyslipidemia: Statin therapy first-line, intensity guided by cardiovascular risk category; add ezetimibe or PCSK9 inhibitor for inadequate LDL reduction on maximally tolerated statin, particularly in very high-risk patients; monitor lipid panel and liver function on initiation/uptitration; check creatine kinase only if myalgia occurs, as routine monitoring is not required, and use caution combining statins with fibrates given myopathy risk.
    • Cushing syndrome: Surgical resection of the causative source (pituitary adenoma, adrenal tumour, or ectopic ACTH source) is first-line definitive treatment; medical therapy (ketoconazole, metyrapone, or osilodrostat) used to control hypercortisolism pre-operatively or when surgery is not curative/feasible; monitor cortisol levels post-operatively for adrenal insufficiency requiring glucocorticoid replacement; screen for and manage cardiovascular risk factors, which remain elevated even after biochemical cure.
    • Hyperparathyroidism: Parathyroidectomy is definitive first-line for symptomatic primary hyperparathyroidism or those meeting surgical criteria (marked hypercalcaemia, renal/bone involvement, age <50); cinacalcet or bisphosphonates for those unfit for or declining surgery; monitor calcium, PTH, and bone density; localise the abnormal gland pre-operatively with sestamibi scan/ultrasound to guide minimally invasive surgery.
    • Type 1 diabetes mellitus: Basal-bolus insulin regimen (or insulin pump) first-line with carbohydrate counting education; escalate to continuous glucose monitoring and automated insulin delivery systems for suboptimal control or recurrent hypoglycaemia; monitor HbA1c, time-in-range on CGM, and annual screening for microvascular complications and autoimmune comorbidities (thyroid, coeliac disease); educate on sick-day rules to prevent DKA during intercurrent illness.
    • Diabetic ketoacidosis: IV fluid resuscitation, fixed-rate IV insulin infusion, and potassium replacement first-line, following a structured protocol; do not stop insulin once glucose normalises, but add dextrose-containing fluids instead to allow continued ketone clearance; monitor capillary ketones/blood glucose hourly and venous bicarbonate/pH regularly until resolution; identify and treat the precipitant (infection, insulin omission, new diagnosis) concurrently.
    • Hyperosmolar hyperglycaemic state: IV fluid resuscitation first-line with cautious, slower correction than DKA given very high osmolality and older, often frailer patient population; insulin started at lower rates or delayed until fluids alone have begun lowering glucose, to avoid precipitous osmotic shifts; monitor osmolality, glucose, and neurological status closely, and consider prophylactic anticoagulation given high VTE risk from hyperviscosity; identify and treat the precipitant, often infection or a new cardiovascular event.
    • Hypoglycaemia (severe): IV dextrose or IM glucagon first-line for severe hypoglycaemia with impaired consciousness where oral intake is unsafe; oral fast-acting carbohydrate for conscious patients able to swallow; monitor capillary glucose every 15 minutes until stable and recurrence risk (particularly with long-acting insulin or sulfonylureas, which may need prolonged observation/dextrose infusion); review and adjust the precipitating medication regimen before discharge.
    • Primary hypothyroidism: Levothyroxine replacement first-line, started at full dose in younger healthy patients but low-dose with gradual titration in the elderly or those with cardiac disease given risk of precipitating angina/arrhythmia; monitor TSH 6-8 weeks after dose changes, then annually once stable; increase dose in pregnancy (typically ~30% increment) as early as pregnancy is confirmed, and separate dosing from calcium/iron supplements which impair absorption.
    • Hyperthyroidism (Graves' disease): Antithyroid drugs (carbimazole/methimazole, or propylthiouracil in first trimester pregnancy) first-line, with beta-blockers for symptomatic control; escalate to radioactive iodine or thyroidectomy for relapse after drug withdrawal or patient preference; monitor thyroid function 4-6 weekly during titration and full blood count for agranulocytosis (advise urgent review for sore throat/fever); avoid radioactive iodine in active moderate-severe Graves' orbitopathy, which it can worsen.
    • Thyroid storm: High-dose antithyroid drugs, iodine solution (given at least an hour after antithyroid drugs to avoid fuelling synthesis), beta-blockade, and corticosteroids together first-line, alongside aggressive supportive care (cooling, fluids); treat the precipitant (infection, surgery, non-compliance); monitor cardiac rhythm, temperature, and mental status continuously in an ICU setting; this is a medical emergency with significant mortality if treatment is delayed.
    • Myxoedema coma: IV levothyroxine (with or without liothyronine) plus stress-dose IV hydrocortisone first-line, as unrecognised coexisting adrenal insufficiency can be unmasked by thyroid hormone replacement; supportive care for hypothermia, hypoventilation, and hypotension, often requiring ICU admission; monitor mental status, temperature, and cardiac rhythm closely; identify and treat the precipitant (infection, cold exposure, sedative drugs) concurrently.
    • Thyroid nodule (suspicious): Fine-needle aspiration cytology first-line for nodules with suspicious ultrasound features or size criteria; surgical excision (lobectomy or total thyroidectomy) for indeterminate/malignant cytology or compressive symptoms; monitor benign nodules with periodic ultrasound; molecular testing can help risk-stratify indeterminate cytology results and avoid unnecessary surgery in select cases.
    • Addison's disease (adrenal insufficiency): Glucocorticoid (hydrocortisone) and mineralocorticoid (fludrocortisone) replacement first-line, with patient education on sick-day dose doubling/tripling during illness; monitor for adequacy of replacement clinically (weight, blood pressure, electrolytes) rather than by cortisol level; issue a steroid emergency card/bracelet and IM hydrocortisone kit for emergency self-administration; increase doses substantially for surgery or severe illness to prevent adrenal crisis.
    • Adrenal crisis: Immediate IV hydrocortisone and aggressive IV fluid resuscitation first-line, given without waiting for confirmatory cortisol results if clinical suspicion is high, as delay is life-threatening; identify and treat the precipitant (infection, missed doses, surgery); monitor blood pressure, electrolytes (hyponatraemia/hyperkalaemia), and glucose closely; transition to oral maintenance replacement once stable, with mineralocorticoid resumed once hydrocortisone dose is tapered toward physiological levels.
    • Primary hyperaldosteronism: Mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist (spironolactone/eplerenone) first-line for bilateral adrenal hyperplasia or those unfit for/declining surgery; adrenalectomy is first-line and potentially curative for unilateral aldosterone-producing adenoma confirmed by adrenal vein sampling; monitor blood pressure and potassium; screen all resistant hypertension and hypertension with hypokalaemia for this treatable secondary cause via aldosterone-renin ratio.
    • Phaeochromocytoma: Alpha-blockade (phenoxybenzamine/doxazosin) first-line for at least 1-2 weeks pre-operatively, with beta-blockade added only after adequate alpha-blockade to avoid unopposed alpha-mediated hypertensive crisis; surgical resection is definitive once adequately blocked; monitor blood pressure and heart rate closely during blockade titration and peri-operatively; genetic testing is recommended given significant association with hereditary syndromes (MEN2, VHL, SDH mutations).
    • Prolactinoma: Dopamine agonist (cabergoline first-line, preferred over bromocriptine for efficacy and tolerability) normalises prolactin and shrinks most tumours, avoiding surgery in the majority; surgery reserved for resistant/intolerant cases or apoplexy; monitor prolactin levels and visual fields/MRI for macroadenomas; dopamine agonists are generally continued safely through pregnancy if needed for tumour control, particularly with macroadenomas.
    • Acromegaly: Trans-sphenoidal surgery is first-line definitive treatment for resectable pituitary somatotroph adenomas; medical therapy (somatostatin analogues, pegvisomant, or cabergoline) for residual/recurrent disease or those unfit for surgery; monitor IGF-1 and growth hormone suppression on OGTT to assess biochemical control; screen for and manage cardiovascular, sleep apnoea, and colonic polyp complications given increased risk.
