    • Diabetes insipidus (central): Desmopressin (DDAVP) replacement first-line, titrated to urine output and thirst; monitor serum sodium closely, particularly on initiation, given risk of dilutional hyponatraemia if fluid intake is not moderated; identify the underlying cause (pituitary surgery, trauma, infiltrative disease) as it may guide additional management; distinguish from nephrogenic DI via desmopressin response testing, as management differs substantially.
    • SIADH (hyponatraemia): Fluid restriction first-line for chronic, asymptomatic SIADH-related hyponatraemia; add a vaptan (tolvaptan) or demeclocycline for refractory cases; use hypertonic saline for severe symptomatic hyponatraemia instead; monitor sodium correction rate closely (no faster than 8-10 mmol/L/24h) to avoid osmotic demyelination; identify and treat the underlying cause (malignancy, CNS pathology, drugs, pulmonary disease).
    • Osteoporosis: Oral bisphosphonate first-line alongside calcium/vitamin D optimisation and weight-bearing exercise; escalate to denosumab, or anabolic agents (teriparatide/romosozumab) for severe/high-risk fracture or bisphosphonate-refractory disease; monitor bone density (DEXA) every 1-2 years and renal function before bisphosphonate dosing; consider drug holidays after prolonged bisphosphonate use to reduce atypical femoral fracture/osteonecrosis of the jaw risk.
    • Paget's disease of bone: Bisphosphonate therapy first-line for symptomatic disease or disease at high-risk sites (weight-bearing bones, skull base); monitor alkaline phosphatase to guide treatment response and need for retreatment; escalate to surgery for pathological fracture, severe deformity, or nerve compression; screen for rare malignant transformation (osteosarcoma) if new bone pain or rapidly rising alkaline phosphatase occurs.
    • Hypercalcaemia of malignancy: IV fluid resuscitation first-line followed by IV bisphosphonate (zoledronic acid) for sustained calcium lowering; denosumab as an alternative in renal impairment where bisphosphonates are contraindicated; monitor calcium, renal function, and for bisphosphonate-related hypocalcaemia/renal toxicity; calcitonin provides rapid but short-lived effect useful as a bridge while awaiting bisphosphonate onset.
    • Hypocalcaemia (symptomatic): IV calcium gluconate first-line for symptomatic/severe hypocalcaemia (tetany, seizures, QT prolongation), followed by oral calcium and vitamin D/calcitriol; monitor calcium, magnesium (correct concurrent hypomagnesaemia, which impairs PTH response), and ECG during acute correction; identify the underlying cause (post-surgical hypoparathyroidism, vitamin D deficiency) to guide longer-term therapy.
    • Familial hypercholesterolaemia: High-intensity statin first-line, started early given lifelong LDL exposure; add ezetimibe or PCSK9 inhibitor for inadequate LDL reduction, given very high cardiovascular risk; monitor LDL levels aggressively toward target and screen first-degree relatives (cascade testing) given autosomal dominant inheritance; consider LDL apheresis for homozygous or severe treatment-refractory disease.
    • Type 2 diabetes with established cardiovascular disease: SGLT2 inhibitor or GLP-1 receptor agonist first-line regardless of HbA1c, given proven cardiovascular benefit in this population, alongside metformin and standard secondary prevention (statin, antiplatelet, blood pressure control); monitor HbA1c, renal function, and volume status on SGLT2 inhibitors; avoid agents with neutral/adverse cardiovascular signal as second-line choices in this high-risk group.
    • Diabetic peripheral neuropathy: Optimise glycaemic control first-line to slow progression, plus a first-line neuropathic pain agent (pregabalin, gabapentin, duloxetine, or amitriptyline) for painful symptoms; monitor for progression with regular foot examination given loss of protective sensation risk; screen for foot ulceration risk and provide footwear/education, as neuropathy is the key driver of diabetic foot complications.
    • Diabetic foot ulcer: Offloading, wound debridement, and infection control (targeted antibiotics if infected) first-line, with multidisciplinary diabetic foot team involvement; escalate to vascular assessment/revascularisation if significant peripheral arterial disease coexists, and surgical debridement or amputation for deep infection/osteomyelitis; monitor wound healing, perfusion, and glycaemic control; regular offloading footwear and podiatry follow-up reduce recurrence.
    • Charcot foot (neuroarthropathy): Total contact casting and strict offloading first-line during the acute phase to prevent progressive bony destruction and deformity; escalate to surgical correction for severe deformity, instability, or recurrent ulceration once the acute phase resolves; monitor with serial imaging and skin temperature (differential warmth signals ongoing activity); distinguishing acute Charcot from infection/cellulitis is critical as management differs substantially.
    • Subacute (de Quervain's) thyroiditis: NSAIDs first-line for mild pain, with corticosteroids for more severe pain or marked thyrotoxic symptoms; beta-blockers for symptomatic thyrotoxic phase (antithyroid drugs are not used as this is not driven by increased synthesis); monitor thyroid function through the classic triphasic course (thyrotoxic, hypothyroid, recovery); most cases resolve spontaneously over weeks to months.
    • Hashimoto's thyroiditis: Levothyroxine replacement once overt hypothyroidism develops, following standard hypothyroidism dosing/monitoring principles; observation alone is appropriate for subclinical disease with normal/mildly elevated TSH and no symptoms in most patients; monitor TSH periodically given progression to overt hypothyroidism over time; screen for coexisting autoimmune conditions (coeliac disease, type 1 diabetes, pernicious anaemia).
    • Multinodular goitre (toxic): Radioactive iodine or antithyroid drugs first-line for medical control of thyrotoxicosis; surgery (thyroidectomy) for compressive symptoms, suspicious nodules, or patient preference; monitor thyroid function and, for large goitres, airway/oesophageal compression symptoms; radioactive iodine may cause transient worsening of thyrotoxicosis and is used cautiously in significant compressive symptoms.
    • Thyroid eye disease (Graves' orbitopathy): Smoking cessation and lubrication/supportive eye care first-line for mild disease; high-dose IV corticosteroids or teprotumumab for moderate-severe active disease; orbital decompression surgery for sight-threatening compressive optic neuropathy; monitor visual acuity, colour vision, and disease activity score; achieve stable euthyroidism, as both hyper- and hypothyroidism can worsen orbitopathy, and avoid radioactive iodine in active moderate-severe disease.
    • Medullary thyroid carcinoma: Total thyroidectomy with central neck dissection is first-line, as this tumour does not respond to radioactive iodine (non-follicular cell origin); escalate to tyrosine kinase inhibitors (selpercatinib, vandetanib) for advanced/metastatic disease; monitor serum calcitonin and CEA as tumour markers; screen for MEN2 syndrome and coexisting phaeochromocytoma (which must be excluded/treated before thyroid surgery) given strong genetic association.
    • Papillary thyroid carcinoma: Total or partial thyroidectomy first-line depending on tumour size/risk features, with radioactive iodine ablation for higher-risk disease; monitor thyroglobulin (as a tumour marker after total thyroidectomy) and neck ultrasound for recurrence; TSH suppression with levothyroxine is used post-operatively in higher-risk disease to reduce recurrence risk; excellent prognosis overall, allowing more conservative management (lobectomy, active surveillance) in low-risk small tumours.
    • Parathyroid carcinoma: En-bloc surgical resection (including ipsilateral thyroid lobe) is first-line, as this rare malignancy is often diagnosed intra-operatively or on suspicion of invasive features; monitor calcium and PTH post-operatively for recurrence; cinacalcet for symptomatic hypercalcaemia in recurrent/metastatic disease where further surgery is not feasible; radiotherapy has a limited role given relative radioresistance.
    • Hypoparathyroidism (post-surgical): Oral calcium and active vitamin D (calcitriol) first-line, titrated to maintain calcium in the low-normal range; add recombinant PTH for refractory cases requiring very high calcium/vitamin D doses; monitor calcium, phosphate, and 24-hour urinary calcium (to avoid nephrocalcinosis from over-replacement); most post-surgical cases are transient, resolving within days to weeks, but a minority become permanent.
    • Pseudohypoparathyroidism: Calcium and active vitamin D supplementation first-line, similar to hypoparathyroidism, but PTH levels remain elevated due to end-organ resistance rather than deficiency; monitor calcium, phosphate, and PTH; screen for associated features (Albright hereditary osteodystrophy phenotype, other hormone resistance) and for associated skeletal/developmental abnormalities depending on subtype.
    • Cushing's disease (pituitary): Trans-sphenoidal pituitary surgery is first-line definitive treatment; medical therapy (ketoconazole, metyrapone, pasireotide) or bilateral adrenalectomy for surgical failure or unfit patients; monitor post-operative cortisol for cure (low/undetectable cortisol indicates biochemical remission requiring glucocorticoid replacement); monitor long-term for recurrence, which can occur years after apparent cure.
    • Nelson's syndrome: Trans-sphenoidal surgical resection of the enlarging pituitary tumour is first-line following bilateral adrenalectomy for Cushing's disease; radiotherapy for residual/recurrent tumour; monitor ACTH levels and pituitary imaging regularly after bilateral adrenalectomy to detect early Nelson's syndrome; prophylactic pituitary radiotherapy at the time of adrenalectomy may reduce incidence in high-risk patients.
    • Hypopituitarism: Hormone replacement tailored to each deficient axis, with glucocorticoid replacement prioritised first (before thyroxine, to avoid precipitating adrenal crisis by increasing metabolic clearance of cortisol); monitor each axis individually (cortisol, thyroid function, sex hormones, growth hormone as indicated); identify and treat the underlying cause (pituitary tumour, apoplexy, Sheehan syndrome, radiotherapy) and provide a steroid emergency card if glucocorticoid-dependent.
    • Sheehan's syndrome: Hormone replacement across affected axes (glucocorticoid, thyroid, and others as needed) first-line, analogous to other causes of hypopituitarism; monitor for evolving deficiencies, which may present acutely (failure to lactate, amenorrhoea) or subtly over years; a history of severe post-partum haemorrhage with subsequent failure to lactate should prompt pituitary function testing.
    • Craniopharyngioma: Surgical resection (often with adjuvant radiotherapy for residual/recurrent tumour) is first-line; monitor for panhypopituitarism and diabetes insipidus, both common post-operatively, requiring hormone replacement; monitor visual fields given proximity to the optic chiasm; long-term follow-up for hypothalamic dysfunction (obesity, temperature dysregulation) and tumour recurrence is required, particularly in children.
    • Ectopic ACTH syndrome: Treat the underlying malignancy (often small cell lung cancer or carcinoid tumour) first-line where identifiable and resectable; medical cortisol-lowering therapy (ketoconazole, metyrapone, or etomidate in severe acute cases) for rapid control while source is localised or in unresectable disease; monitor cortisol, potassium (severe hypokalaemia common), and glucose; bilateral adrenalectomy considered if the source cannot be found or controlled.
    • Insulinoma: Surgical enucleation or resection is first-line and often curative for localised, sporadic insulinoma; diazoxide or somatostatin analogues for symptom control pre-operatively or in unresectable/metastatic disease; monitor for hypoglycaemia with supervised fasting test used diagnostically; screen for MEN1 syndrome, particularly with multifocal or recurrent tumours.
    • Glucagonoma: Surgical resection is first-line for localised disease; somatostatin analogues (octreotide) for symptom control (necrolytic migratory erythema, hyperglycaemia) in unresectable/metastatic disease; monitor glucose, weight, and for venous thromboembolism, which occurs at markedly increased frequency in glucagonoma; nutritional support and VTE prophylaxis are important adjuncts given catabolic state.
    • MEN 1 syndrome (surveillance/management): Manage each component tumour individually as it arises (parathyroidectomy for hyperparathyroidism, surgery/somatostatin analogues for pancreatic neuroendocrine tumours, trans-sphenoidal surgery for pituitary adenomas); lifelong biochemical and imaging surveillance is essential given near-universal, multifocal, and recurrent tumour development; screen first-degree relatives with genetic testing given autosomal dominant inheritance.
    • MEN 2 syndrome: Prophylactic thyroidectomy timed by RET mutation risk category (often in early childhood for highest-risk mutations) is first-line to prevent medullary thyroid carcinoma; screen for and treat phaeochromocytoma before any surgery given risk of intra-operative hypertensive crisis; monitor calcitonin, calcium/PTH (MEN2A), and catecholamines regularly; genetic testing and counselling for at-risk relatives is essential given autosomal dominant inheritance with high penetrance.
    • Obesity (class II-III): Structured lifestyle intervention first-line, adding pharmacotherapy (GLP-1/GIP agonists) for inadequate response, particularly with obesity-related comorbidity; bariatric surgery for class III obesity or class II with significant comorbidity refractory to medical therapy; monitor weight trajectory, metabolic comorbidities, and nutritional status post-surgery (vitamin/mineral deficiencies); comprehensive comorbidity screening (sleep apnoea, NAFLD, diabetes) should accompany management.
    • Metabolic acidosis due to lactic acidosis: Treat the underlying cause (fluid resuscitation and source control for sepsis/hypoperfusion, stopping metformin in metformin-associated lactic acidosis) first-line rather than bicarbonate alone; escalate to renal replacement therapy for severe refractory acidosis, particularly with metformin accumulation in renal failure; monitor lactate clearance and arterial blood gas trend as markers of response; distinguish type A (hypoperfusion) from type B (drug/metabolic) lactic acidosis as this guides specific treatment.
    • Refeeding syndrome (risk): Cautious, gradual reintroduction of nutrition first-line in at-risk patients (prolonged starvation, low BMI, minimal intake >10 days), starting well below full caloric requirement and increasing gradually over days; pre-emptive thiamine and electrolyte (phosphate, potassium, magnesium) supplementation before and during feeding; monitor electrolytes daily for the first week, as refeeding hypophosphataemia can cause cardiac/respiratory failure; identify high-risk patients proactively rather than reacting after electrolyte derangement occurs.
  5. Hematology & Oncology
    • Chronic myeloid leukaemia: Tyrosine kinase inhibitor (imatinib first-line, or a second-generation TKI) is first-line and has transformed prognosis; escalate to alternate TKI or allogeneic transplant for resistance/intolerance or progression to accelerated/blast phase; monitor BCR-ABL transcript levels by PCR at defined intervals to assess molecular response; screen for cardiovascular risk before second-generation TKIs given differing toxicity profiles.
    • Chronic lymphocytic leukaemia: Observation ('watch and wait') first-line for asymptomatic early-stage disease, as early treatment does not improve survival; targeted therapy (BTK inhibitor or venetoclax-based regimen) for symptomatic/progressive disease, largely replacing chemoimmunotherapy; monitor blood counts, lymph node size, and for Richter transformation (aggressive lymphoma transformation); screen for TP53/17p deletion before treatment selection as it predicts chemoimmunotherapy resistance.
    • Tumor lysis syndrome: IV hydration and rasburicase (or allopurinol for lower-risk disease) first-line, started pre-emptively in high-risk malignancies before chemotherapy; escalate to renal replacement therapy for severe refractory hyperkalaemia, hyperphosphataemia, or renal failure; monitor electrolytes, uric acid, and renal function frequently (every 4-6 hours) during the highest-risk period; avoid rasburicase in G6PD deficiency due to haemolysis risk.
    • Febrile neutropenia: Empirical broad-spectrum IV antibiotics (anti-pseudomonal beta-lactam) within one hour of presentation is first-line, regardless of identified source; escalate to antifungal cover for persistent fever beyond 4-7 days despite antibiotics; monitor temperature, neutrophil count recovery, and cultures; risk-stratify (e.g. MASCC score) to identify low-risk patients potentially suitable for oral antibiotics/outpatient management.
    • Thalassemia minor (trait): No specific treatment is required for thalassaemia trait itself, as it is typically asymptomatic with mild microcytic anaemia; genetic counselling is important, particularly for reproductive planning given risk of thalassaemia major in offspring if both partners carry trait; monitor to distinguish from iron deficiency (normal/high ferritin, disproportionately low MCV for degree of anaemia); avoid inappropriate iron supplementation, which does not help and may cause overload.
    • Iron deficiency anaemia: Oral iron replacement first-line, continued for 3 months after normalisation to replenish stores; IV iron for intolerance, malabsorption, or ongoing significant losses; monitor haemoglobin response at 2-4 weeks and ferritin to confirm repletion; investigate the underlying cause (particularly GI blood loss) in adults, especially post-menopausal women and men, given risk of underlying malignancy.
    • Megaloblastic anaemia (B12 deficiency): IM/high-dose oral B12 replacement first-line, with more frequent initial dosing for neurological involvement; monitor haemoglobin, MCV, and neurological symptoms, which may take longer to resolve than haematological parameters; check for pernicious anaemia (intrinsic factor antibodies) as the underlying cause requires lifelong replacement; replace B12 before folate if both are deficient, as folate alone can precipitate subacute combined degeneration.
    • Folate deficiency anaemia: Oral folate replacement first-line; always check and correct B12 status first or concurrently, as isolated folate replacement can mask B12 deficiency and precipitate neurological damage; monitor haemoglobin response over several weeks; identify the underlying cause (poor intake, malabsorption, increased demand in pregnancy/haemolysis) as it guides duration of therapy.
    • Aplastic anaemia (severe): Allogeneic stem cell transplant is first-line curative therapy for younger patients with a matched donor; immunosuppressive therapy (antithymocyte globulin plus ciclosporin) for those unsuitable for transplant; monitor blood counts closely and provide supportive transfusion/infection prophylaxis while awaiting response; eltrombopag may be added to immunosuppression to improve response rates.
    • Sickle cell crisis (vaso-occlusive): IV fluids, opioid analgesia, and oxygen (if hypoxic) first-line, with early aggressive pain control; escalate to exchange transfusion for acute chest syndrome, stroke, or multi-organ failure; monitor oxygen saturation, pain scores, and for evolving acute chest syndrome (new infiltrate plus respiratory symptoms), a leading cause of death; hydroxyurea reduces crisis frequency long-term and should be reviewed as maintenance therapy after recovery.
    • Thalassaemia major: Regular lifelong blood transfusion first-line to maintain haemoglobin and suppress ineffective erythropoiesis, combined with iron chelation therapy to prevent transfusional iron overload; allogeneic stem cell transplant is curative in eligible children with a matched donor; monitor ferritin/cardiac and hepatic iron (MRI T2*) and endocrine function (growth, pubertal development) given chelation-preventable organ damage from iron overload.
    • Haemophilia A (bleeding episode): Factor VIII replacement first-line, dosed according to bleed severity and target site; escalate to higher-dose/continuous infusion for major bleeds or surgery; monitor factor VIII levels and for inhibitor development (reduced response to replacement) which requires bypassing agents (activated factor VII or FEIBA); prophylactic factor replacement or emicizumab reduces long-term joint damage in severe disease.
    • Immune thrombocytopenic purpura: Corticosteroids first-line for significant bleeding/very low platelet count, with IVIG for rapid platelet rise when urgent (active bleeding, pre-procedure); escalate to thrombopoietin receptor agonists, rituximab, or splenectomy for chronic/refractory disease; monitor platelet count response; avoid platelet transfusion alone except for life-threatening bleeding, as transfused platelets are rapidly destroyed by the same antibody process.
    • Thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura: Urgent plasma exchange plus corticosteroids first-line, started on clinical suspicion without waiting for ADAMTS13 confirmation given rapid mortality if untreated; add caplacizumab and rituximab for refractory/relapsing disease; monitor platelet count, LDH, and schistocytes daily to assess response; avoid platelet transfusion unless life-threatening bleeding, as it can worsen microvascular thrombosis.
    • Disseminated intravascular coagulation: Treat the underlying cause, with blood product support (FFP, platelets, cryoprecipitate) guided by bleeding and labs.
    • Deep venous thrombosis with malignancy: Low-molecular-weight heparin or a DOAC (in appropriate cancer types without significant GI mucosal involvement) first-line, generally continued long-term/indefinitely while active malignancy persists given high recurrence risk; monitor for bleeding, particularly with DOACs in GI malignancies; reassess anticoagulation choice if the patient develops significant renal impairment or is undergoing procedures requiring interruption.
    • Hodgkin lymphoma: Combination chemotherapy (ABVD-based) first-line, with radiotherapy added for early-stage/bulky disease; escalate to salvage chemotherapy and autologous stem cell transplant for relapsed/refractory disease; monitor with interim PET-CT to guide treatment intensity/response-adapted therapy; screen for and monitor late treatment effects (secondary malignancy, cardiac/pulmonary toxicity) given generally excellent long-term survival and long follow-up horizon.
    • Non-Hodgkin lymphoma (diffuse large B-cell): R-CHOP (rituximab plus combination chemotherapy) first-line with curative intent; escalate to CAR-T cell therapy or autologous transplant for relapsed/refractory disease; monitor with interim and end-of-treatment PET-CT; screen for tumour lysis syndrome risk before first cycle given rapid tumour bulk reduction, and for CNS relapse risk factors guiding prophylactic intrathecal therapy.
    • Multiple myeloma: Combination therapy (proteasome inhibitor, immunomodulatory drug, and corticosteroid) first-line, followed by autologous stem cell transplant in eligible patients; escalate to CAR-T cell therapy or bispecific antibodies for relapsed/refractory disease; monitor serum free light chains/paraprotein, renal function, and calcium; bone-protective therapy (bisphosphonate/denosumab) is given alongside systemic therapy given high skeletal complication risk.
    • Acute myeloid leukaemia: Intensive induction chemotherapy, with allogeneic transplant for high-risk disease.
    • Acute lymphoblastic leukaemia: Risk-stratified multi-phase chemotherapy with CNS-directed prophylaxis.
    • Polycythaemia vera: Low-dose aspirin and therapeutic phlebotomy first-line to target haematocrit below threshold, reducing thrombotic risk; add cytoreductive therapy (hydroxyurea, or ruxolitinib for resistant/intolerant disease) for high-risk patients (age >60, prior thrombosis); monitor haematocrit, platelet count, and for progression to myelofibrosis or acute leukaemia; JAK2 mutation testing confirms diagnosis and does not typically change first-line management.
    • Essential thrombocythaemia: Low-dose aspirin first-line for low-risk disease; add cytoreductive therapy (hydroxyurea, or anagrelide as an alternative) for high-risk patients (age >60, prior thrombosis, very high platelet count); monitor platelet count and for thrombotic/haemorrhagic complications (acquired von Willebrand syndrome can occur at very high platelet counts, paradoxically increasing bleeding risk); avoid aspirin alone in this scenario given bleeding risk.
    • Myelodysplastic syndrome: Supportive care (transfusion support, growth factors) first-line for lower-risk disease; hypomethylating agents (azacitidine) for higher-risk disease, with allogeneic transplant considered in eligible fit patients as the only curative option; monitor blood counts and for progression to AML; risk-stratify using validated scoring systems (e.g. IPSS-R) to guide the intensity of intervention.
    • Breast carcinoma (early stage): Surgery (breast-conserving surgery with radiotherapy, or mastectomy) first-line, with adjuvant systemic therapy (endocrine therapy for hormone-receptor-positive, chemotherapy/HER2-targeted therapy as indicated by subtype) tailored to receptor status and risk; monitor with regular clinical and mammographic surveillance post-treatment; genomic assays can help select which hormone-receptor-positive patients benefit from adjuvant chemotherapy versus endocrine therapy alone.
    • Lung carcinoma (non-small cell, resectable): Surgical resection is first-line for early-stage disease in fit patients, with adjuvant chemotherapy (+/- immunotherapy) for higher-stage resected disease; stereotactic body radiotherapy is an alternative for those unfit for surgery; monitor with surveillance imaging post-resection; molecular testing (EGFR, ALK, PD-L1) guides targeted/immunotherapy selection even at earlier disease stages increasingly.
    • Prostate carcinoma (localised, low-risk): Active surveillance is first-line for low-risk disease, avoiding overtreatment given generally indolent behaviour; radical prostatectomy or radiotherapy for higher-risk localised disease or patient preference against surveillance; monitor PSA and repeat biopsy/MRI at defined intervals during surveillance to detect progression; discuss functional side effects (continence, erectile function) when comparing definitive treatment options.
    • Cervical carcinoma (early stage): Radical hysterectomy with lymphadenectomy or definitive chemoradiotherapy first-line depending on stage and fertility wishes; fertility-sparing surgery (radical trachelectomy) is an option in very early disease for appropriate candidates; monitor with regular clinical follow-up and cervical cytology/HPV testing; HPV vaccination and screening are the primary prevention strategies reducing incidence.
    • Ovarian carcinoma: Cytoreductive surgery plus platinum-based chemotherapy first-line, with the sequence (upfront surgery vs neoadjuvant chemotherapy then interval surgery) determined by resectability; maintenance PARP inhibitor therapy for BRCA-mutated or homologous-recombination-deficient disease after response to chemotherapy; monitor CA-125 and imaging for response/recurrence; genetic testing (BRCA1/2) should be offered to all patients given both treatment and familial implications.
    • Testicular germ cell tumour: Radical orchiectomy is first-line for both diagnosis and initial treatment; adjuvant chemotherapy or surveillance depending on stage and histology (seminoma vs non-seminoma), as both have excellent cure rates even in metastatic disease; monitor tumour markers (AFP, beta-hCG, LDH) and imaging during surveillance/follow-up; sperm banking should be offered before chemotherapy given fertility impact.
    • Bladder carcinoma (muscle-invasive): Neoadjuvant chemotherapy followed by radical cystectomy first-line in fit patients; trimodality therapy (maximal TURBT plus chemoradiotherapy) as a bladder-preserving alternative in selected patients; monitor with cystoscopic/imaging surveillance; adjuvant immunotherapy is considered for high-risk residual disease after cystectomy.
    • Renal cell carcinoma (localised): Partial or radical nephrectomy is first-line for localised disease, with partial nephrectomy preferred where technically feasible to preserve renal function; active surveillance is an option for small, low-risk tumours in older/comorbid patients; monitor renal function and with surveillance imaging for recurrence; adjuvant immunotherapy is considered in high-risk resected disease.
    • Pancreatic adenocarcinoma (resectable): Surgical resection (Whipple procedure for head tumours) first-line in resectable disease, with peri-operative (neoadjuvant and/or adjuvant) chemotherapy given high recurrence risk even after complete resection; monitor CA19-9 and imaging post-resection for recurrence; multidisciplinary review is essential to confirm resectability given proximity to major vessels, and borderline-resectable disease often benefits from neoadjuvant therapy first.
    • Anaemia of chronic disease: Treat the underlying inflammatory/chronic disease first-line, as the anaemia typically improves with disease control rather than iron supplementation alone; erythropoiesis-stimulating agents may be used in select settings (chronic kidney disease, chemotherapy-associated anaemia); monitor haemoglobin alongside disease activity markers; distinguish from coexisting iron deficiency (which can occur simultaneously) via ferritin/transferrin saturation, as this changes management.
    • Autoimmune haemolytic anaemia: Corticosteroids first-line for warm-antibody AIHA; escalate to rituximab or splenectomy for steroid-refractory/relapsing disease; cold-antibody (cold agglutinin) disease responds poorly to steroids and is instead managed with cold avoidance and rituximab-based therapy; monitor haemoglobin, reticulocyte count, and haemolysis markers (LDH, haptoglobin, bilirubin); identify underlying secondary causes (lymphoproliferative disease, infection, drugs) which may need separate treatment.
    • Paroxysmal nocturnal haemoglobinuria: Complement inhibitor therapy (eculizumab/ravulizumab) first-line, which markedly reduces haemolysis and thrombotic risk; allogeneic stem cell transplant is curative and considered for severe/refractory disease or associated bone marrow failure; monitor for breakthrough haemolysis and meningococcal infection risk (vaccination required before starting complement inhibitors given mechanism of action); anticoagulation is considered given high thrombosis risk, particularly at unusual sites.
    • Glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase deficiency (haemolytic crisis): Removal of the oxidative trigger (drug, infection, fava beans) first-line, alongside supportive care (hydration, transfusion if severe); monitor haemoglobin and for acute kidney injury from haemoglobinuria in severe crises; screen and counsel regarding drugs/foods to avoid long-term (certain antimalarials, sulfonamides, dapsone); neonatal screening is relevant given risk of severe neonatal jaundice/kernicterus.
    • Hereditary spherocytosis: Folic acid supplementation and monitoring for mild/moderate disease first-line, as many patients are well-compensated; splenectomy for severe/transfusion-dependent disease, ideally delayed until after age 5-6 in children given post-splenectomy infection risk, with pneumococcal/meningococcal/Hib vaccination beforehand; monitor haemoglobin, reticulocyte count, and gallstone development (chronic haemolysis predisposes to pigment gallstones).
    • Von Willebrand disease: Desmopressin (DDAVP) first-line for mild type 1 disease with adequate response on trial dosing, to release stored vWF; von Willebrand factor concentrate for more severe disease, type 2/3, or inadequate DDAVP response, particularly before surgery; monitor factor levels and bleeding time/response to therapy; avoid antiplatelet agents and NSAIDs which worsen bleeding tendency.
    • Factor V Leiden thrombophilia (with VTE): Standard anticoagulation for the acute VTE first-line, following usual duration guidelines rather than extending solely because of heterozygous Factor V Leiden, which confers only modest additional risk; extended/indefinite anticoagulation is considered for homozygous mutation, combined thrombophilias, or recurrent unprovoked VTE; monitor for bleeding and reassess ongoing anticoagulation need at defined intervals; routine thrombophilia testing is not required to guide most VTE management decisions.
    • Antiphospholipid syndrome (thrombotic): Long-term warfarin (target INR 2-3) first-line, as DOACs show inferior efficacy, particularly in triple-antibody-positive disease; monitor INR closely given labile anticoagulation control in some patients; screen for other autoimmune disease (SLE) and monitor for recurrent thrombosis despite therapeutic anticoagulation, which may require higher-intensity anticoagulation or addition of low-dose aspirin.
    • Heparin-induced thrombocytopenia: Immediate cessation of all heparin products first-line, with substitution by a non-heparin anticoagulant (argatroban, fondaparinux, or a DOAC once stable) given ongoing high thrombosis risk despite thrombocytopenia; do not give platelet transfusion for low platelet count alone, as it can worsen thrombosis; monitor platelet count recovery and confirm diagnosis with functional/immunoassay testing; warfarin should not be started until platelets have substantially recovered, given skin necrosis risk.
    • Warfarin-associated major bleeding: Immediate cessation of warfarin plus 4-factor prothrombin complex concentrate and IV vitamin K first-line for major/life-threatening bleeding, given rapid reversal compared to FFP alone; monitor INR after reversal and clinical bleeding response; identify and control the bleeding source; reassess the ongoing indication for anticoagulation and timing of resumption once haemostasis is secured.
    • Direct oral anticoagulant-associated major bleeding: Specific reversal agent (idarucizumab for dabigatran, andexanet alfa for factor Xa inhibitors where available) first-line for life-threatening bleeding, with 4-factor prothrombin complex concentrate as an alternative if specific reversal is unavailable; supportive measures (local haemostasis, transfusion) alongside; monitor renal function (affects drug clearance/timing of last dose relevance) and clinical bleeding response; consider activated charcoal if very recent ingestion.
    • Splenic rupture (traumatic): Haemodynamically unstable patients require urgent splenectomy or splenic artery embolisation first-line; stable patients with lower-grade injury may be managed with non-operative observation (serial haemoglobin, imaging) in a monitored setting; monitor haemodynamics and haemoglobin closely, as delayed rupture/bleeding can occur; vaccinate against encapsulated organisms if splenectomy is performed, given post-splenectomy sepsis risk.
    • Hypersplenism: Treat the underlying cause (e.g. portal hypertension, haematological malignancy) first-line rather than the splenomegaly itself where possible; splenectomy reserved for severe symptomatic cytopenias or massive symptomatic splenomegaly refractory to treating the cause; monitor blood counts and for complications of the underlying disease; vaccinate against encapsulated organisms before elective splenectomy.
    • Waldenström macroglobulinaemia: Observation for asymptomatic disease first-line, as early treatment does not improve survival; targeted therapy (BTK inhibitor-based regimen) for symptomatic disease (hyperviscosity, cytopenias, organomegaly); plasmapheresis for acute hyperviscosity syndrome as an urgent bridging measure before systemic therapy takes effect; monitor IgM paraprotein level and blood viscosity.
    • Hairy cell leukaemia: Purine analogue (cladribine) is first-line, achieving durable remission in most patients with a single course; escalate to rituximab or targeted therapy (BRAF inhibitor, given near-universal BRAF V600E mutation) for relapsed/refractory disease; monitor blood counts for recovery and relapse; infection risk is heightened both from the disease (monocytopenia) and treatment-related immunosuppression.
    • Chronic myelomonocytic leukaemia: Supportive care and hydroxyurea for cytoreduction in proliferative disease first-line; hypomethylating agents for higher-risk disease, with allogeneic transplant considered in eligible fit patients as the only curative option; monitor blood counts and for transformation to AML; this overlap myelodysplastic/myeloproliferative neoplasm has variable behaviour requiring individualised risk stratification.
    • Langerhans cell histiocytosis: Observation or local therapy (curettage, low-dose radiotherapy) first-line for single-system, single-site bone disease; systemic chemotherapy for multisystem or high-risk organ involvement; monitor for diabetes insipidus (pituitary involvement) and organ dysfunction; long-term surveillance is needed given risk of late reactivation and neurodegenerative complications.
    • Amyloidosis (AL, systemic): Chemotherapy targeting the underlying clonal plasma cell population (bortezomib-based regimen) first-line, often followed by autologous stem cell transplant in eligible patients; monitor free light chains and organ-specific markers (cardiac biomarkers, proteinuria) to assess haematological and organ response; cardiac involvement drives prognosis and requires careful assessment before intensive therapy given risk of decompensation.
    • Graft-versus-host disease (acute): Systemic corticosteroids first-line for grade II or higher acute GVHD; escalate to second-line agents (ruxolitinib) for steroid-refractory disease; monitor organ-specific staging (skin, gut, liver) to guide treatment intensity; balance immunosuppression against infection risk and relapse of the underlying malignancy, given the beneficial graft-versus-leukaemia effect that accompanies mild GVHD.
    • Tumour lysis syndrome (prevention): Pre-emptive IV hydration and prophylactic allopurinol (or rasburicase for high-risk disease) first-line, started before initiating chemotherapy in at-risk malignancies (high-bulk, rapidly proliferating tumours); monitor electrolytes and renal function closely during the highest-risk 24-72 hour window after treatment initiation; risk-stratify by tumour type/bulk and baseline renal function to determine prophylaxis intensity.
    • Febrile neutropenia (low-risk): Oral broad-spectrum antibiotics (e.g. ciprofloxacin plus amoxicillin-clavulanate) with outpatient management may be appropriate first-line for carefully selected low-risk patients (MASCC score-defined) with reliable follow-up; monitor closely for clinical deterioration requiring admission; ensure clear safety-netting and rapid access back to care, as risk stratification tools are imperfect and reassessment is essential.
    • Cancer-associated venous thromboembolism: Low-molecular-weight heparin or an appropriate DOAC first-line, generally continued long-term while active malignancy persists given high recurrence risk off anticoagulation; monitor for bleeding, particularly with DOACs in GI/genitourinary malignancies; reassess choice and duration periodically, and consider IVC filter only for absolute anticoagulation contraindication with acute proximal DVT/PE.
    • Superior vena cava obstruction (haematological malignancy): Urgent chemotherapy/radiotherapy targeting the underlying lymphoma is first-line, as haematological malignancies are typically highly chemo/radiosensitive, obviating need for stenting in most cases; endovascular stenting for rapid symptom relief in severe/threatening airway compromise while awaiting treatment response; monitor airway and neurological status closely; tissue diagnosis should be obtained urgently but should not unduly delay treatment in life-threatening presentations.
    • Malignant pleural effusion: Therapeutic thoracentesis first-line for symptom relief and diagnosis; indwelling pleural catheter or talc pleurodesis for recurrent effusions depending on expected survival and lung re-expansion (trapped lung favours IPC); monitor for re-accumulation and treat the underlying malignancy systemically where feasible; assess for trapped lung before committing to pleurodesis given lower success if the lung cannot fully re-expand.
    • Malignant ascites: Therapeutic paracentesis first-line for symptomatic relief; diuretics may help in ascites with a significant portal hypertensive component; peritoneovenous shunt or catheter drainage for recurrent, refractory ascites in appropriate candidates; monitor electrolytes and renal function, particularly with large-volume paracentesis or diuretic use; treat the underlying malignancy systemically where feasible.
    • Brain metastases (multiple): Stereotactic radiosurgery or whole-brain radiotherapy first-line depending on number/size of lesions and systemic disease control, alongside corticosteroids for peritumoural oedema/mass effect; targeted/systemic therapy for the primary tumour where CNS-penetrant options exist; monitor neurological status and repeat imaging for response/progression; anticonvulsants are used only if seizures have occurred, not as routine prophylaxis.
    • Bone marrow failure (post-chemotherapy): Supportive care (transfusion support, growth factors, infection prophylaxis) first-line while awaiting marrow recovery, which is expected with most standard chemotherapy regimens; monitor blood counts closely for recovery trajectory and evolving cytopenia patterns; investigate for secondary myelodysplasia/leukaemia if recovery is delayed or atypical, particularly after certain chemotherapy classes (alkylating agents, topoisomerase inhibitors).
    • Hepatoblastoma (paediatric): Surgical resection combined with chemotherapy (neoadjuvant to shrink tumour, or adjuvant post-resection) first-line, with excellent cure rates in localised disease; liver transplantation for unresectable disease confined to the liver; monitor alpha-fetoprotein as a sensitive tumour marker for response and recurrence; multidisciplinary paediatric oncology input is essential given the rarity of this tumour.
    • Wilms tumour (nephroblastoma): Nephrectomy combined with chemotherapy (timing varies by protocol/region, either upfront surgery or neoadjuvant chemotherapy first) is first-line, with excellent overall cure rates; radiotherapy added for higher-stage/unfavourable histology disease; monitor with imaging surveillance for recurrence and screen contralateral kidney for bilateral disease; genetic/syndromic association (WAGR, Beckwith-Wiedemann) should prompt broader surveillance.
    • Neuroblastoma (paediatric): Risk-stratified therapy (surgery alone for low-risk, combination chemotherapy/surgery/radiotherapy plus immunotherapy for high-risk disease) is first-line, with treatment intensity guided by age, stage, and biological markers (MYCN amplification); monitor urinary catecholamine metabolites and imaging for response; high-risk disease requires intensive multimodal therapy including autologous stem cell transplant and anti-GD2 immunotherapy.
    • Retinoblastoma: Eye-preserving therapy (intra-arterial/intravitreal chemotherapy, focal laser/cryotherapy) is first-line where feasible to preserve vision and the eye; enucleation for advanced intraocular disease or when eye-preserving therapy fails; monitor with regular examination under anaesthesia during active treatment; genetic testing and counselling are important given hereditary forms carrying risk of bilateral disease and secondary malignancy.
    • Gastrointestinal stromal tumour (GIST): Surgical resection is first-line for localised, resectable disease; tyrosine kinase inhibitor (imatinib) as neoadjuvant/adjuvant therapy for high-risk resected disease or as primary therapy for unresectable/metastatic disease, given characteristic KIT/PDGFRA mutation sensitivity; monitor with imaging response assessment; mutation testing guides choice of TKI, as some genotypes (e.g. PDGFRA D842V) are imatinib-resistant.
    • Merkel cell carcinoma: Wide local excision with sentinel lymph node biopsy is first-line for localised disease, given high metastatic potential even in small tumours; adjuvant radiotherapy for high-risk features; immunotherapy (checkpoint inhibitor) for advanced/metastatic disease, given notable responsiveness; monitor closely for recurrence given aggressive behaviour, and consider immunosuppression status as a risk factor requiring closer surveillance.
  6. Neurology
    • Transient ischemic attack: Urgent risk stratification (ABCD2 score) and dual antiplatelet therapy (aspirin plus clopidogrel for 21 days) first-line for high-risk TIA, followed by long-term single antiplatelet and statin; escalate to carotid endarterectomy/stenting for significant symptomatic carotid stenosis, ideally within 2 weeks; monitor for recurrent events and complete rapid vascular imaging (carotid Doppler/CT angiogram) and cardiac rhythm monitoring for AF; treat as a medical emergency given high early stroke risk.
    • Tension-type headache: Simple analgesia (paracetamol/NSAIDs) first-line for episodic attacks, limited to avoid medication overuse; add amitriptyline for chronic/frequent tension-type headache prophylaxis; monitor headache frequency/analgesic use with a headache diary; address contributing factors (stress, poor sleep, posture) as part of a non-pharmacological approach.
    • Cerebral palsy: Multidisciplinary management (physiotherapy, occupational therapy, speech therapy) first-line focused on function and development; add botulinum toxin injections or oral antispasmodics for spasticity, with selective dorsal rhizotomy or intrathecal baclofen for severe refractory spasticity; monitor growth, hip surveillance (risk of dislocation), and seizure control where epilepsy coexists; early intervention improves functional outcomes.
    • Focal epilepsy: A single first-line anti-seizure medication appropriate to focal epilepsy (e.g. lamotrigine, levetiracetam, carbamazepine) is started after a second unprovoked seizure (or after the first in high-recurrence-risk cases); escalate to combination therapy or epilepsy surgery evaluation for drug-resistant disease (failure of two appropriate drugs); monitor seizure frequency, drug levels/side effects, and adherence; MRI is required to exclude a structural lesion amenable to surgical cure.
    • Dementia with Lewy bodies: Cholinesterase inhibitors first-line, often with a good response given prominent cholinergic deficit; avoid antipsychotics where possible given marked neuroleptic sensitivity risking severe, sometimes fatal reactions; monitor cognition, hallucinations, and parkinsonism; manage fluctuating cognition, visual hallucinations, and REM sleep behaviour disorder as core features distinguishing it from Alzheimer's disease.
    • Acute ischaemic stroke (within window): IV thrombolysis (alteplase/tenecteplase) within the licensed time window first-line, with mechanical thrombectomy added for large vessel occlusion within an extended window based on perfusion imaging; monitor neurological status (NIHSS), blood pressure (kept below thrombolysis thresholds), and for haemorrhagic transformation with repeat imaging; door-to-needle time targets drive protocolised rapid assessment.
    • Acute ischaemic stroke (outside window): Aspirin (started within 24-48 hours) first-line as thrombolysis is no longer an option; mechanical thrombectomy may still be appropriate for large vessel occlusion with favourable perfusion imaging beyond standard windows; monitor for progression/complications and initiate secondary prevention (antiplatelet, statin, blood pressure control) alongside early rehabilitation; investigate stroke aetiology (cardioembolic, large/small vessel) to guide long-term prevention.
    • Intracerebral haemorrhage: Blood pressure lowering (target systolic <140mmHg acutely) and reversal of any anticoagulation first-line; escalate to neurosurgical evacuation for select cases (cerebellar haemorrhage with deterioration, large lobar haematoma with mass effect); monitor neurological status and repeat imaging for haematoma expansion; avoid routine surgery for most supratentorial haemorrhages, where evidence for benefit is limited outside specific indications.
    • Subarachnoid haemorrhage (aneurysmal): Urgent aneurysm securing (endovascular coiling preferred where feasible, or surgical clipping) first-line to prevent rebleeding; nimodipine is given routinely to reduce vasospasm-related delayed cerebral ischaemia; monitor for vasospasm (transcranial Doppler, clinical deterioration) typically days 4-14, hydrocephalus, and hyponatraemia (cerebral salt wasting or SIADH); external ventricular drain for acute hydrocephalus.
    • Status epilepticus: IV benzodiazepine first-line, followed promptly by a second-line IV anti-seizure medication (levetiracetam, valproate, or phenytoin) if seizures continue; escalate to anaesthetic agents and intubation for refractory status epilepticus unresponsive to first- and second-line therapy; monitor continuous EEG in refractory/super-refractory cases and treat the underlying cause (electrolytes, infection, non-adherence); avoid delay between treatment steps, as prolonged status worsens outcome.
    • Generalised tonic-clonic epilepsy: Sodium valproate is historically first-line but is avoided in women of childbearing potential given teratogenicity, with lamotrigine or levetiracetam preferred instead in this group; monitor seizure frequency and drug levels/side effects; screen for and counsel on pregnancy planning in women of reproductive age given the significant teratogenic risk of several anti-seizure medications; avoid abrupt medication withdrawal, which risks rebound seizures/status epilepticus.
    • Migraine (acute attack): Triptan plus NSAID first-line for moderate-severe attacks, taken early in the attack for best efficacy; escalate to prophylactic therapy (beta-blocker, topiramate, or CGRP monoclonal antibody) for frequent/disabling attacks; monitor attack frequency and medication use, screening for medication overuse headache; avoid triptans in significant cardiovascular/cerebrovascular disease given vasoconstrictive mechanism.
    • Cluster headache: High-flow oxygen and subcutaneous sumatriptan first-line for acute attacks, given rapid onset and severity; verapamil is first-line prophylaxis during a cluster bout, requiring ECG monitoring given dose-dependent cardiac conduction effects; monitor attack frequency and response, and consider short-course corticosteroids as a bridge while prophylaxis takes effect; occipital nerve block is an option for refractory cases.
    • Trigeminal neuralgia: Carbamazepine first-line, often with dramatic response supporting the diagnosis; escalate to microvascular decompression or stereotactic radiosurgery for drug-refractory disease or intolerable side effects; monitor sodium (carbamazepine-induced hyponatraemia) and drug levels; investigate for an underlying cause (vascular compression, multiple sclerosis) with MRI, particularly in younger patients or bilateral symptoms.
    • Bell's palsy: Oral corticosteroids started within 72 hours of onset first-line to improve recovery; antivirals may be added for severe presentations though evidence for benefit alone is weaker; eye protection (lubricants, taping at night) is essential given risk of exposure keratopathy from incomplete eye closure; monitor for recovery over weeks to months, and reconsider diagnosis (stroke, tumour, Lyme disease) if atypical features or non-recovery.
    • Guillain-Barré syndrome: IVIG or plasma exchange first-line, started as soon as possible after diagnosis, with equal efficacy between the two; monitor respiratory function (forced vital capacity) closely given risk of rapid progression to respiratory failure requiring ventilation, and autonomic function (arrhythmia, blood pressure lability); avoid corticosteroids, which lack proven benefit in GBS unlike CIDP; early ICU involvement is warranted for rapidly progressive or bulbar-involved disease.
    • Myasthenia gravis (crisis): Plasma exchange or IVIG first-line for myasthenic crisis with respiratory/bulbar compromise; monitor respiratory function (forced vital capacity, negative inspiratory force) closely given risk of rapid deterioration requiring ventilation; avoid or use cautiously drugs that worsen neuromuscular transmission (aminoglycosides, certain beta-blockers, magnesium); identify and treat any precipitant (infection, medication change).
    • Myasthenia gravis (stable): Pyridostigmine first-line for symptomatic control; add corticosteroids or a steroid-sparing immunosuppressant/biologic for inadequate control; consider thymectomy in generalised disease, particularly with thymoma or in younger patients with acetylcholine receptor antibody-positive disease; monitor symptom control and for medication side effects; educate on crisis-precipitating drugs and situations (infection, surgery).
    • Multiple sclerosis (relapse): High-dose IV (or oral) corticosteroids first-line to shorten relapse duration, though this does not alter long-term disability; ensure the patient is established on an appropriate disease-modifying therapy for long-term relapse/progression prevention, escalating therapy for breakthrough disease activity; monitor with MRI for new lesion activity and clinical relapse rate; plasma exchange is considered for severe steroid-refractory relapses.
    • Parkinson's disease: Levodopa (with a peripheral decarboxylase inhibitor) first-line for most patients, particularly older patients, with dopamine agonists or MAO-B inhibitors as alternatives in younger patients to delay levodopa-related motor complications; escalate to deep brain stimulation for motor fluctuations refractory to optimised medical therapy; monitor for motor complications (wearing-off, dyskinesia) and non-motor symptoms (cognitive decline, autonomic dysfunction, depression) which significantly affect quality of life.
    • Alzheimer's disease: Cholinesterase inhibitors first-line for mild-moderate disease, with memantine added or used alone in moderate-severe disease; monitor cognitive trajectory (standardised testing) and function; manage behavioural and psychological symptoms non-pharmacologically first-line given antipsychotic risks in dementia; newer anti-amyloid monoclonal antibodies are an option in select early, biomarker-confirmed disease under specialist supervision given infusion reaction and ARIA (amyloid-related imaging abnormality) monitoring requirements.
    • Normal pressure hydrocephalus: Large-volume lumbar puncture (or extended lumbar drain trial) first-line to predict response before committing to surgery; ventriculoperitoneal shunting is definitive for those with a positive trial response, particularly gait improvement; monitor gait, cognition, and urinary symptoms (the classic triad) pre- and post-intervention; shunt complications (infection, over-drainage, subdural haematoma) require ongoing surveillance.
    • Idiopathic intracranial hypertension: Weight loss and acetazolamide first-line for medical management; escalate to optic nerve sheath fenestration or CSF diversion (shunt) for progressive visual loss despite medical therapy; monitor visual fields and acuity closely, as visual loss can be irreversible if untreated; therapeutic lumbar puncture provides temporary relief while definitive management is arranged.
    • Cerebral abscess: Empirical broad-spectrum antibiotics plus neurosurgical aspiration/drainage first-line for most abscesses, particularly those >2.5cm or causing mass effect; smaller abscesses may respond to antibiotics alone with close monitoring; monitor with serial imaging to confirm resolution and neurological status for deterioration; identify and treat the source (sinusitis, otitis, endocarditis, haematogenous spread) concurrently.
    • Bacterial meningitis: Empirical IV antibiotics (covering the likely pathogens by age/risk factors) started immediately, without waiting for lumbar puncture if this would delay treatment, plus dexamethasone (particularly for pneumococcal meningitis) to reduce neurological sequelae; monitor neurological status, and repeat imaging/LP if not improving as expected; contact prophylaxis for close contacts in meningococcal disease; this is a medical emergency where delay materially worsens outcome.
    • Viral encephalitis (HSV): Empirical IV aciclovir started immediately on clinical suspicion, without waiting for PCR confirmation, given high mortality/morbidity if treatment is delayed; monitor neurological status, repeat CSF HSV PCR if initially negative but suspicion remains high, and repeat imaging for complications; continue treatment for the full course (typically 14-21 days) even if early PCR is negative, if clinical suspicion remains high.
    • Spinal cord compression (malignant): Urgent high-dose corticosteroids plus emergency MRI whole spine first-line on clinical suspicion, given time-critical risk of irreversible paralysis; escalate to urgent radiotherapy or surgical decompression depending on tumour radiosensitivity, spinal stability, and prior radiotherapy; monitor neurological status (motor/sensory level, bladder/bowel function) closely, as this is a neuro-oncological emergency requiring same-day assessment.
    • Cervical spondylotic myelopathy: Surgical decompression is first-line for moderate-severe or progressive myelopathy, as conservative management alone is unlikely to reverse cord dysfunction; conservative management (physiotherapy, collar) may be considered for mild, non-progressive disease with close monitoring; monitor neurological function (gait, hand dexterity, bladder function) for progression, which should prompt surgical referral; earlier surgery before severe deficits develop is associated with better recovery.
    • Essential tremor: Propranolol or primidone first-line for symptomatic action tremor affecting function; escalate to deep brain stimulation or focused ultrasound thalamotomy for severe, medication-refractory, functionally disabling tremor; monitor tremor severity and functional impact; distinguish from Parkinsonian rest tremor, as management and prognosis differ substantially.
    • Restless legs syndrome: Iron studies and correction of iron deficiency first-line, as this is a common and reversible contributor; dopamine agonists or alpha-2-delta ligands (gabapentin, pregabalin) for persistent symptomatic disease; monitor for augmentation (worsening/earlier symptom onset) with long-term dopamine agonist use, which may require switching drug class; review medications (antihistamines, antidepressants) that can exacerbate symptoms.
    • Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis: Riluzole first-line, providing modest survival benefit; supportive multidisciplinary management (respiratory support with NIV, nutritional support including gastrostomy, communication aids) is central to care given the incurable, progressive nature of the disease; monitor respiratory function (forced vital capacity) regularly to time NIV initiation; early palliative care involvement and advance care planning discussions are appropriate given prognosis.
    • Transverse myelitis: High-dose IV corticosteroids first-line, with plasma exchange added for severe or steroid-refractory presentations; investigate for an underlying cause (multiple sclerosis, neuromyelitis optica, infection, autoimmune disease) as this guides longer-term disease-modifying therapy; monitor neurological function (motor, sensory, bladder/bowel) and MRI for lesion evolution; distinguishing idiopathic from NMOSD/MS-associated disease is important given differing long-term treatment and relapse risk.
    • Acute disseminated encephalomyelitis: High-dose IV corticosteroids first-line, typically following a recent infection or vaccination; escalate to IVIG or plasma exchange for steroid-refractory disease; monitor neurological status and MRI, as ADEM is typically monophasic with a generally favourable prognosis in children; distinguish from multiple sclerosis (which requires different long-term management) based on monophasic course and multifocal presentation.
    • Neuromyelitis optica spectrum disorder: High-dose IV corticosteroids plus plasma exchange first-line for acute attacks, given typically severe attacks with poor spontaneous recovery; long-term immunosuppression (rituximab or complement/IL-6 pathway inhibitors) is essential for relapse prevention, as this differs fundamentally from MS management; monitor aquaporin-4 antibody status and attack frequency; avoid certain MS disease-modifying therapies (interferon-beta, natalizumab), which can worsen NMOSD.
    • Chronic inflammatory demyelinating polyneuropathy: IVIG, plasma exchange, or corticosteroids first-line, each with roughly comparable efficacy allowing individualised choice; escalate to combination therapy or other immunosuppressants for refractory/relapsing disease; monitor strength, function, and nerve conduction studies to assess and guide ongoing treatment need; unlike GBS, corticosteroids are effective and often used given the chronic relapsing/progressive course.
    • Diabetic autonomic neuropathy: Optimise glycaemic control first-line to slow progression, with symptomatic treatment targeted to the affected system (fludrocortisone/midodrine for orthostatic hypotension, prokinetics for gastroparesis); monitor blood pressure (lying/standing), cardiac autonomic testing, and gastrointestinal/genitourinary symptoms; screen for silent myocardial ischaemia given blunted anginal warning symptoms from autonomic denervation.
    • Charcot-Marie-Tooth disease: Supportive management (physiotherapy, orthotics/ankle-foot orthoses, occupational therapy) is the mainstay, as no disease-modifying pharmacotherapy currently exists; monitor for progressive weakness, foot deformity, and functional decline; genetic testing confirms subtype and informs family counselling given variable inheritance patterns; avoid neurotoxic drugs (vincristine and certain others) which can precipitate acute deterioration in susceptible subtypes.
    • Wernicke's encephalopathy: Immediate high-dose IV thiamine first-line, given before or alongside any glucose administration, as glucose without thiamine can precipitate or worsen the condition in at-risk patients; treat empirically on clinical suspicion without waiting for confirmatory testing, given risk of irreversible progression to Korsakoff syndrome if delayed; monitor the classical triad (confusion, ataxia, ophthalmoplegia), though all three are often not present together; screen at-risk patients (alcohol use disorder, malnutrition, bariatric surgery) proactively.
    • Korsakoff syndrome: High-dose thiamine continued alongside abstinence support first-line, though established amnestic syndrome often shows limited reversibility once fully developed, unlike the acute Wernicke phase; supportive cognitive rehabilitation and structured care given persistent anterograde/retrograde amnesia and confabulation; monitor cognitive function and nutritional status long-term; prevention through prompt thiamine treatment of Wernicke's encephalopathy is the most effective strategy.
    • Cerebral venous sinus thrombosis: Anticoagulation (typically low-molecular-weight heparin, even in the presence of associated haemorrhagic infarction) first-line, as this is the definitive treatment reducing propagation and improving outcome; escalate to endovascular thrombectomy for severe/deteriorating cases despite anticoagulation; monitor neurological status and for raised intracranial pressure (papilloedema); identify and address prothrombotic risk factors (oral contraceptives, pregnancy, thrombophilia).
    • Chronic subdural haematoma: Surgical burr-hole drainage first-line for symptomatic haematomas causing significant mass effect or neurological deficit; conservative management with serial imaging is reasonable for small, asymptomatic haematomas, particularly in patients on anticoagulation where reversal risk must be weighed; monitor neurological status and repeat imaging for re-accumulation, which is not uncommon after drainage; identify and manage anticoagulation/antiplatelet use, a common contributing factor especially in the elderly.
    • Acute subdural haematoma: Urgent surgical evacuation (craniotomy) first-line for significant haematomas with mass effect or neurological deterioration; conservative management with close monitoring for small, minimally symptomatic haematomas; monitor neurological status (GCS) closely and repeat imaging for expansion; reverse any anticoagulation immediately given time-critical risk of herniation.
    • Extradural haematoma: Urgent surgical evacuation is first-line and often life-saving, particularly with a lucid interval followed by rapid deterioration, a classic presentation; small, asymptomatic extradural haematomas without mass effect may be observed closely with serial imaging; monitor neurological status (GCS) very closely, as deterioration can be rapid; this is a neurosurgical emergency given the classically arterial (middle meningeal artery) bleeding source.
    • Traumatic brain injury (moderate-severe): Airway protection, ICP monitoring, and control of secondary brain injury (avoiding hypoxia/hypotension) first-line per established protocols; escalate to decompressive craniectomy for refractory raised intracranial pressure; monitor ICP, cerebral perfusion pressure, and neurological status continuously in a critical care setting; avoid routine prophylactic hyperventilation/hypothermia, which lack proven outcome benefit and carry risk.
    • Diffuse axonal injury: Supportive critical care management (ICP control, avoidance of secondary insults) is the mainstay, as there is no specific reversal treatment for the primary axonal injury; monitor neurological status closely, recognising that clinical severity may be disproportionate to initial imaging findings; MRI (particularly susceptibility-weighted sequences) is more sensitive than CT for detecting the extent of injury; prognosis is variable and long-term rehabilitation needs should be anticipated early.
    • Vestibular schwannoma (acoustic neuroma): Observation with serial MRI first-line for small, asymptomatic or minimally symptomatic tumours, particularly in older patients, given typically slow growth; stereotactic radiosurgery or microsurgical resection for growing/symptomatic tumours, chosen based on tumour size and patient factors; monitor hearing, facial nerve function, and tumour size on serial imaging; balance treatment choice against hearing preservation and facial nerve preservation goals.
    • Meningioma (symptomatic): Surgical resection is first-line for symptomatic or growing meningiomas amenable to safe resection; stereotactic radiosurgery for smaller tumours, surgically inaccessible locations, or incomplete resection; observation with serial imaging is appropriate for small, asymptomatic, incidentally found meningiomas given typically slow growth; monitor for recurrence with periodic imaging, particularly for higher-grade or incompletely resected tumours.
    • Glioblastoma: Maximal safe surgical resection followed by concurrent chemoradiotherapy (temozolomide-based) and adjuvant chemotherapy is first-line (the Stupp protocol); tumour-treating fields may be added as adjunctive therapy; monitor with serial MRI for progression, recognising pseudoprogression can mimic true progression early after chemoradiotherapy; prognosis remains poor overall, and early palliative care/advance care planning discussion is appropriate given typical disease trajectory.
    • Low-grade glioma: Maximal safe surgical resection first-line, with the extent of resection significantly influencing prognosis; adjuvant radiotherapy and chemotherapy for higher-risk features (residual tumour, older age, certain molecular markers); monitor with serial MRI for transformation to higher-grade disease, which can occur over years; molecular profiling (IDH mutation, 1p/19q co-deletion) guides both prognosis and treatment intensity.
    • Pituitary apoplexy: Urgent stress-dose IV corticosteroids first-line given risk of coexisting acute adrenal insufficiency, even before confirming the diagnosis if clinical suspicion is high; escalate to urgent surgical decompression for significant visual loss, altered consciousness, or severe ophthalmoplegia; monitor visual fields, consciousness level, and pituitary hormone axes; conservative management with close monitoring is reasonable for mild presentations without significant visual/neurological compromise.
    • Chiari malformation (symptomatic): Surgical posterior fossa decompression is first-line for symptomatic Chiari malformation (headache, syrinx, brainstem/cerebellar signs); asymptomatic, incidentally found malformations are typically observed without intervention; monitor symptoms and for syrinx development/progression on MRI; assess for associated syringomyelia, which often improves with successful decompression.
    • Syringomyelia (symptomatic): Treat the underlying cause first-line where identifiable (Chiari decompression, tumour resection, tethered cord release), as syrinx often improves once the causative obstruction to CSF flow is addressed; direct syrinx shunting is reserved for cases without an identifiable/treatable underlying cause or persistent symptoms after treating the cause; monitor neurological function and syrinx size on serial MRI.
    • Cervical radiculopathy: Conservative management (physiotherapy, analgesia, short course of oral corticosteroids for severe pain) first-line, as most cases improve without surgery; escalate to surgical decompression for progressive motor deficit, severe unremitting pain, or failure of conservative management; monitor neurological function (specific myotome/dermatome) for progression; imaging correlation with clinical findings is important given high rates of asymptomatic disc abnormalities on MRI.
    • Peripheral facial nerve palsy (traumatic): Early surgical decompression is considered for complete paralysis with evidence of nerve transection or rapidly progressive/complete palsy following significant trauma (particularly temporal bone fracture), as outcomes are better with earlier intervention; conservative management with eye protection for incomplete or gradually evolving palsy; monitor facial nerve function serially (electroneurography can help predict recovery/guide surgical timing); protect the cornea given risk of exposure keratopathy.
    • Tension headache (chronic): Amitriptyline first-line prophylaxis for chronic tension-type headache; limit acute analgesic use to avoid medication overuse headache, which commonly complicates chronic headache disorders; monitor headache frequency and analgesic consumption via a headache diary; address contributing lifestyle/psychological factors (stress, sleep, posture) as part of a comprehensive management plan.
    • Medication overuse headache: Withdrawal of the overused acute medication is the essential first-line treatment, as continued use perpetuates the cycle; bridging therapy (short corticosteroid course, or a preventive agent) can ease the withdrawal period; monitor headache frequency during and after withdrawal, which often worsens transiently before improving; address the underlying primary headache disorder with appropriate prophylaxis once overuse is resolved.
    • Cervicogenic headache: Physiotherapy targeting cervical spine mobility and posture first-line; add cervical facet or occipital nerve blocks for diagnostic confirmation and symptomatic relief in refractory cases; monitor headache frequency and cervical range of motion; distinguish carefully from migraine/tension-type headache, as management differs and misdiagnosis is common given overlapping features.
    • Autonomic dysreflexia (spinal cord injury): Immediate removal of the noxious stimulus (bladder distension, faecal impaction, tight clothing) is the essential first-line action, as this is the usual trigger and its removal often resolves the episode; sit the patient upright and treat severe hypertension with a short-acting antihypertensive if the stimulus cannot be immediately identified/removed; monitor blood pressure closely given risk of hypertensive emergency (stroke, seizure); this is a medical emergency in patients with spinal cord injury at or above T6.
    • Spinal muscular atrophy: Disease-modifying therapy (nusinersen, onasemnogene abeparvovec gene therapy, or risdiplam) started as early as possible, ideally pre-symptomatically via newborn screening, first-line, as these have transformed the natural history of the disease; supportive multidisciplinary care (respiratory support, nutrition, orthopaedic management of scoliosis) remains essential alongside; monitor motor function with standardised scales and respiratory function; genetic counselling for families given autosomal recessive inheritance.
    • Duchenne muscular dystrophy: Corticosteroids first-line, started once motor decline begins, to slow disease progression and prolong ambulation; exon-skipping or gene-targeted therapies are available for eligible genetic subtypes; monitor cardiac (echocardiography, as dilated cardiomyopathy is near-universal) and respiratory function (which drive mortality) regularly, with proactive respiratory support and cardioprotective medication; multidisciplinary care (physiotherapy, orthopaedic management of scoliosis/contractures) is essential.
    • Periodic limb movement disorder: Treatment is generally reserved for cases causing significant sleep disruption or daytime symptoms, using similar agents to restless legs syndrome (dopamine agonists, alpha-2-delta ligands) first-line; check and correct iron deficiency as a contributing/reversible factor; monitor with polysomnography to confirm diagnosis and assess treatment response; distinguish from restless legs syndrome, with which it frequently coexists but is a distinct diagnosis based on the movement pattern during sleep.
    • Narcolepsy: Scheduled daytime naps and stimulant medication (modafinil first-line) for excessive daytime sleepiness; add sodium oxybate or a specific anti-cataplexy agent for narcolepsy with cataplexy; monitor symptom control (sleepiness, cataplexy frequency) and, given cardiovascular stimulant effects, cardiovascular status on stimulant therapy; polysomnography plus multiple sleep latency testing confirms diagnosis and helps exclude other causes of hypersomnolence.
  7. Infectious Disease
    • Multibacillary leprosy: Multidrug therapy (rifampicin, dapsone, clofazimine) for 12 months is first-line per WHO protocol; escalate corticosteroids for reversal (type 1) reactions or erythema nodosum leprosum (type 2, sometimes needing thalidomide where available); monitor for nerve function impairment at each visit, as early detection and steroid treatment of neuritis prevents permanent disability; screen household contacts and provide single-dose rifampicin prophylaxis where indicated.
    • Secondary syphilis: Single dose IM benzathine penicillin G is first-line; use an alternative regimen (doxycycline) only if genuinely penicillin-allergic, as penicillin desensitisation is preferred in pregnancy given no proven alternative; monitor non-treponemal titres (RPR/VDRL) at 6 and 12 months to confirm serological response; screen for and treat sexual partners, and test for HIV and other STIs concurrently given shared risk factors.
    • Genital herpes: Oral aciclovir/valaciclovir first-line for the initial episode, shortening duration and severity; suppressive daily therapy for frequent recurrences (>6/year) or to reduce transmission risk to partners; monitor recurrence frequency; in pregnancy, suppressive therapy from 36 weeks reduces risk of neonatal transmission, and active lesions at delivery warrant caesarean section.
    • Chlamydial urethritis/cervicitis: Doxycycline first-line (azithromycin as an alternative, particularly in pregnancy where doxycycline is avoided); treat presumptively for gonorrhoea co-infection if local prevalence is high or testing unavailable; monitor for and treat sexual partners; test of cure is not routinely needed except in pregnancy, where re-testing is recommended given consequences of treatment failure.
    • Gonorrhoea: Single-dose IM ceftriaxone first-line, with dual therapy historically added but increasingly ceftriaxone monotherapy per updated resistance-pattern guidance; treat presumptively for chlamydia co-infection; monitor for treatment failure given rising antimicrobial resistance, with test of cure recommended in pharyngeal infection or if symptoms persist; screen and treat sexual partners and test for other STIs including HIV.
    • Genital tract tuberculosis: Standard multidrug anti-tuberculous therapy (as for pulmonary TB) is first-line, typically for 6 months; surgery is reserved for complications (tubo-ovarian abscess, persistent pelvic mass) not responding to medical therapy; monitor treatment response clinically and with imaging; counsel on infertility risk, as genital TB is a significant cause of tubal factor infertility even after successful treatment.
    • Enteric (typhoid) fever: Empirical antibiotics (ceftriaxone or a fluoroquinolone depending on local resistance patterns) first-line, narrowed once sensitivities available; escalate to high-dose dexamethasone alongside antibiotics for severe disease with delirium/shock; monitor for complications (intestinal perforation, GI bleeding) particularly in the third week of untreated illness; identify chronic carriers (positive stool culture beyond 12 months) who pose ongoing public health risk.
    • Malaria (uncomplicated P. falciparum): Artemisinin-based combination therapy (ACT) first-line per local resistance patterns; monitor parasitaemia clearance and clinical response over 48-72 hours, escalating to treatment for severe malaria if deterioration occurs; in pregnancy, ACTs are preferred in the second/third trimester with quinine-based regimens historically used in the first, though current guidance increasingly supports ACTs throughout; ensure primaquine (with G6PD testing first) for radical cure if P. vivax/ovale co-infection is possible.
    • Severe/complicated malaria: IV artesunate is first-line, superior to quinine for reducing mortality in severe malaria; monitor for complications (cerebral malaria, acute kidney injury, severe anaemia, hypoglycaemia) with close haemodynamic and neurological monitoring, often requiring ICU care; check for post-artesunate delayed haemolysis over the following weeks; treat hypoglycaemia aggressively as it is both a complication and a marker of severity.
