    • Dengue fever (without warning signs): Supportive care (paracetamol for fever/pain, adequate oral hydration) is first-line, as there is no specific antiviral therapy; avoid NSAIDs and aspirin given bleeding risk; monitor for warning signs (abdominal pain, persistent vomiting, mucosal bleeding, lethargy) that signal progression to severe dengue, particularly around defervescence (days 3-7); daily full blood count to track haematocrit and platelet trend guides need for escalation.
    • Dengue haemorrhagic fever/shock: Careful, protocolised IV fluid resuscitation first-line, titrated closely to avoid both under-resuscitation (shock) and over-resuscitation (fluid overload, a major cause of mortality); monitor haematocrit, platelet count, and vital signs frequently (often hourly during critical phase); platelet transfusion is reserved for significant active bleeding rather than low count alone; the critical phase (plasma leakage) typically occurs around defervescence and requires the closest monitoring.
    • Leptospirosis: IV penicillin or ceftriaxone first-line for moderate-severe disease, with doxycycline for milder disease/prophylaxis; monitor renal function (Weil's disease causing renal failure), liver function, and for pulmonary haemorrhage, a major cause of mortality; supportive dialysis for renal failure; occupational/environmental exposure history (water contact, rodent exposure) supports diagnosis in endemic settings.
    • Rabies (post-exposure): Immediate wound washing with soap and water first-line, followed promptly by rabies vaccine series and rabies immunoglobulin (infiltrated around the wound) for category III exposures; there is no effective treatment once symptoms develop, making prompt post-exposure prophylaxis essential; monitor wound healing and completion of the full vaccine schedule; category of exposure (contact, broken skin, mucous membrane/deep bite) determines whether immunoglobulin is needed.
    • Tetanus: Wound debridement plus human tetanus immunoglobulin and metronidazole first-line, alongside completing/boosting active tetanus vaccination; escalate to muscle relaxants/sedation and ventilatory support in an ICU for severe spasms/autonomic instability; monitor for autonomic dysfunction (labile blood pressure, arrhythmia) and respiratory compromise from laryngospasm; a dark, quiet environment reduces spasm-triggering stimuli.
    • Cholera: Aggressive oral or IV rehydration (per severity) first-line, as fluid loss is the primary driver of mortality; add a single-dose antibiotic (doxycycline or azithromycin) to shorten duration and reduce transmission in moderate-severe cases; monitor hydration status and stool output closely to guide ongoing fluid replacement; zinc supplementation reduces duration/severity in children.
    • Amoebic dysentery: Metronidazole (or tinidazole) first-line for invasive disease, followed by a luminal agent (paromomycin) to eradicate intestinal cyst carriage and prevent relapse/transmission; monitor clinical response and consider imaging for liver abscess if fever/right upper quadrant pain persist; distinguish from bacterial dysentery via stool microscopy/antigen testing, as management differs.
    • Giardiasis: Metronidazole or tinidazole first-line; monitor symptom resolution, as post-infectious lactose intolerance can cause persistent symptoms after successful treatment; investigate for and treat all household/close contacts with symptoms given faecal-oral transmission; consider an alternative agent for treatment failure, as resistance is increasingly recognised.
    • HIV infection (newly diagnosed): Immediate initiation of combination antiretroviral therapy first-line regardless of CD4 count, as 'treat all' improves outcomes and reduces transmission; monitor CD4 count and viral load at baseline and periodically to confirm viral suppression; screen for opportunistic infection risk (TB, cryptococcal antigen if CD4 low) and coinfections (hepatitis B/C, syphilis) before/at initiation; counsel on adherence, as resistance develops rapidly with suboptimal adherence.
    • Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia: High-dose co-trimoxazole, with corticosteroids for moderate-severe hypoxaemia.
    • Cryptococcal meningitis: Induction with IV amphotericin B plus flucytosine first-line, followed by consolidation and maintenance fluconazole; carefully manage raised intracranial pressure with therapeutic lumbar puncture, as this significantly affects survival; monitor opening pressure at each LP and clinical response; delay antiretroviral therapy initiation by several weeks in HIV-associated disease to reduce risk of immune reconstitution inflammatory syndrome.
    • Toxoplasmosis (cerebral, HIV-related): Pyrimethamine plus sulfadiazine (with folinic acid) first-line; monitor clinical and radiological response at 2 weeks, with brain biopsy considered for non-response given differential diagnosis of primary CNS lymphoma; start antiretroviral therapy after a short delay to reduce IRIS risk; secondary prophylaxis is continued until sustained CD4 recovery on ART.
    • Latent tuberculosis infection: Isoniazid for 6-9 months, or shorter rifamycin-based regimens (3-4 months), first-line depending on drug interactions and adherence considerations; monitor liver function periodically, particularly with isoniazid or in those with pre-existing liver disease; exclude active TB before starting treatment (chest X-ray, symptom screen) to avoid inadequate monotherapy of active disease; prioritise treatment in high-risk contacts (HIV, young children, immunosuppressed).
    • Multidrug-resistant tuberculosis: All-oral bedaquiline-based regimens (with linezolid and other second-line agents guided by resistance testing) are now first-line, replacing longer injectable-containing regimens; treatment duration is typically 6-9 months for standardised shorter regimens where eligible; monitor for drug toxicity (QT prolongation with bedaquiline, peripheral neuropathy/myelosuppression with linezolid) and sputum culture conversion; directly observed therapy improves adherence given the complexity and duration of treatment.
    • Hepatitis B (chronic, active): Tenofovir or entecavir first-line for active chronic hepatitis B with elevated viral load/ALT or evidence of liver disease; monitor HBV DNA, ALT, and for cirrhosis/hepatocellular carcinoma development with regular surveillance (ultrasound +/- AFP); screen and vaccinate close contacts; in pregnancy, tenofovir is used in the third trimester for high viral load to reduce mother-to-child transmission alongside infant immunoprophylaxis.
    • Hepatitis C (chronic): Direct-acting antiviral combination therapy (typically 8-12 weeks) is first-line, achieving cure (sustained virological response) in the great majority of patients regardless of genotype with modern pan-genotypic regimens; monitor HCV RNA at end of treatment and 12 weeks after to confirm cure; screen for and manage cirrhosis (ongoing HCC surveillance is required even after cure if cirrhosis is present); assess for drug interactions before starting DAAs.
    • Herpes zoster: Antiviral therapy (aciclovir/valaciclovir) started early, plus analgesia for pain.
    • Chickenpox (varicella, uncomplicated): Supportive care (antihistamines for itch, paracetamol for fever, avoiding aspirin given Reye syndrome risk) is first-line in healthy children, as the disease is usually self-limiting; oral aciclovir for adults, adolescents, or those with risk factors for severe disease given higher complication rates in these groups; monitor for secondary bacterial skin infection and, rarely, pneumonia/encephalitis; varicella-zoster immunoglobulin for susceptible high-risk contacts (immunocompromised, pregnant, neonates) after significant exposure.
    • Measles: Supportive care and vitamin A supplementation; no specific antiviral therapy.
    • Mumps: Supportive care (analgesia, hydration) is first-line, as no specific antiviral therapy exists; monitor for complications (orchitis, pancreatitis, meningitis, rare permanent deafness); testicular support and analgesia for orchitis, which can cause testicular atrophy though rarely infertility; isolate during the infectious period and ensure MMR vaccination status is addressed in contacts.
    • Infectious mononucleosis: Supportive care (rest, analgesia, hydration) is first-line, as antivirals lack proven benefit; avoid amoxicillin/ampicillin, which characteristically causes a widespread rash in EBV infection and can cause diagnostic confusion; monitor for splenomegaly and advise against contact sports for 3-4 weeks given splenic rupture risk; consider corticosteroids only for severe complications (airway obstruction from tonsillar swelling, severe haemolytic anaemia).
    • Scrub typhus: Doxycycline first-line, with rapid clinical response typically supporting the diagnosis; azithromycin is an alternative in pregnancy or children where doxycycline is relatively contraindicated (though short courses in children are now often considered acceptable); monitor for multi-organ involvement (ARDS, myocarditis, meningoencephalitis) in severe/delayed presentations; look for the characteristic eschar, which aids diagnosis in endemic areas.
    • Brucellosis: Combination doxycycline plus rifampicin (or streptomycin) for at least 6 weeks first-line, as monotherapy carries high relapse rates; monitor clinical response and inflammatory markers, with longer courses/combination therapy for focal complications (spondylitis, endocarditis); investigate exposure history (unpasteurised dairy, livestock contact) which supports diagnosis and informs public health/occupational counselling.
    • Candidaemia: Echinocandin first-line empirical therapy pending speciation/susceptibility, particularly in critically ill or recently azole-exposed patients; step down to fluconazole for susceptible species once stable; remove/exchange central venous catheters where feasible, as source control significantly affects outcome; monitor with repeat blood cultures to confirm clearance and screen for endophthalmitis with dilated fundoscopy given risk of ocular seeding.
    • Invasive aspergillosis: Voriconazole first-line for invasive pulmonary/disseminated disease; isavuconazole is an alternative with a more favourable toxicity/interaction profile; monitor voriconazole levels (narrow therapeutic window, significant interactions) and galactomannan/imaging response; reduce immunosuppression where feasible, and consider surgical resection for localised refractory disease or before further chemotherapy in haematology patients.
    • Sepsis with unknown source: Early broad-spectrum empirical antibiotics (within 1 hour) plus IV fluid resuscitation and source identification first-line, following sepsis bundle principles; escalate to vasopressor support for fluid-refractory hypotension; monitor lactate clearance, urine output, and haemodynamics closely; narrow antibiotics once a source/organism is identified, and actively search for an occult source (abscess, endocarditis) if initial work-up is unrevealing.
    • Septic shock: IV fluid resuscitation and early broad-spectrum antibiotics first-line, with noradrenaline as first-line vasopressor for persistent hypotension despite fluids; add vasopressin or corticosteroids for refractory shock; monitor lactate clearance, mean arterial pressure, and end-organ perfusion continuously in a critical care setting; identify and achieve source control (drainage, debridement) as a priority alongside pharmacological therapy.
    • Necrotising fasciitis: Urgent surgical debridement is first-line and time-critical, as antibiotics alone cannot control this rapidly progressive infection; broad-spectrum IV antibiotics (including clindamycin for antitoxin effect against toxin-producing organisms) started immediately alongside; monitor for the need for repeat debridement (often required within 24-48 hours) and for septic shock; this is a surgical emergency where delayed debridement is strongly associated with mortality.
    • COVID-19 (severe/hypoxic): Supplemental oxygen and dexamethasone first-line for hypoxic disease; add remdesivir and/or an IL-6 inhibitor (tocilizumab) for progressive severe disease per current evidence-based protocols; monitor oxygenation trend and inflammatory markers, escalating respiratory support (high-flow, NIV, invasive ventilation) as needed; avoid corticosteroids in non-hypoxic disease given lack of benefit and potential harm.
    • Influenza (high-risk patient): Oseltamivir started as early as possible (ideally within 48 hours of symptom onset) first-line in high-risk patients (elderly, pregnant, comorbid, immunocompromised); monitor for secondary bacterial pneumonia, a leading complication; treatment may still provide benefit beyond 48 hours in hospitalised/severe cases; annual vaccination remains the primary preventive strategy for future seasons.
    • Toxic shock syndrome: Aggressive fluid resuscitation, source control (removal of tampon/packing, debridement of infected tissue), and antibiotics (covering toxin-producing Staphylococcus/Streptococcus, including clindamycin for antitoxin effect) first-line; escalate to IVIG for refractory shock, particularly streptococcal TSS; monitor haemodynamics and multi-organ function closely given rapid progression to multi-organ failure; this is a medical emergency requiring ICU-level monitoring.
    • Staphylococcus aureus bacteraemia: IV antibiotics (flucloxacillin for MSSA, vancomycin or an alternative for MRSA) for a minimum duration (typically 2 weeks uncomplicated, 4-6 weeks for complicated/endocarditis) first-line; mandatory echocardiography to assess for endocarditis given significant risk regardless of clinical suspicion; monitor with repeat blood cultures to confirm clearance and identify/remove any removable source (line, abscess); infectious disease consultation improves outcomes in this condition.
    • Osteomyelitis (chronic): Surgical debridement of necrotic/infected bone plus prolonged targeted IV then oral antibiotics (often 6+ weeks) first-line, as chronic osteomyelitis with sequestrum rarely resolves with antibiotics alone; monitor inflammatory markers and imaging response; consider local antibiotic delivery (beads, cement) and staged reconstruction for extensive bone loss; recurrence is common and long-term follow-up is needed.
    • Diabetic foot infection (moderate-severe): Broad-spectrum IV antibiotics plus surgical debridement first-line for moderate-severe infection, with imaging (MRI) to assess for osteomyelitis; vascular assessment/revascularisation where significant peripheral arterial disease coexists, as perfusion critically affects healing; monitor wound response, glycaemic control, and offloading adherence; multidisciplinary diabetic foot team involvement improves limb salvage rates.
    • Actinomycosis: Prolonged high-dose penicillin (often IV then oral, for weeks to months) first-line, given the characteristically indolent, fibrosing nature of infection requiring extended treatment; surgical debridement/drainage for abscess or extensive tissue involvement; monitor clinical and radiological response over the prolonged treatment course; consider the diagnosis in chronic, mass-like infections mimicking malignancy, particularly with characteristic sulfur granules on histology.
    • Nocardiosis: Co-trimoxazole first-line (or combination therapy for severe/disseminated disease, particularly CNS involvement) for a prolonged course (months); monitor clinical and radiological response and screen for CNS involvement with brain imaging given tendency for dissemination, particularly in immunocompromised hosts; assess and address underlying immunosuppression as a contributing factor.
    • Q fever: Doxycycline first-line for acute disease; prolonged combination therapy (doxycycline plus hydroxychloroquine) for months to years for chronic Q fever (endocarditis), given risk of relapse with shorter courses; monitor serology (phase I/II antibody titres) to distinguish acute from chronic disease and guide treatment duration; occupational/animal exposure history (livestock, especially parturition) supports diagnosis.
    • Lyme disease (early localised): Oral doxycycline (or amoxicillin as an alternative, including in young children/pregnancy) for 10-14 days first-line for early localised disease (erythema migrans); monitor for progression to disseminated disease (neurological, cardiac, or joint involvement) if untreated or inadequately treated; single-dose post-exposure prophylaxis is considered after a high-risk tick bite in endemic areas under specific criteria.
    • Lyme neuroborreliosis: IV ceftriaxone (or in some cases oral doxycycline for milder facial palsy alone) for 2-4 weeks first-line for CNS/neurological involvement; monitor neurological symptoms for resolution, which may be gradual even after adequate treatment; distinguish post-treatment symptoms (which do not indicate ongoing infection) from true treatment failure, as prolonged/repeated antibiotic courses are not supported by evidence and carry harm.
    • Chikungunya fever: Supportive care (analgesia, hydration) is first-line, as there is no specific antiviral treatment; avoid NSAIDs/aspirin until dengue is excluded given overlapping presentation and bleeding risk in dengue; monitor for persistent/chronic arthralgia, which can last months to years and may need longer-term analgesic/anti-inflammatory management; vector control and mosquito avoidance are key preventive measures.
    • Zika virus infection: Supportive care (analgesia, hydration) is first-line, as no specific antiviral treatment exists; avoid NSAIDs/aspirin until dengue is excluded; in pregnancy, monitor closely with serial ultrasound given risk of congenital Zika syndrome (microcephaly) and refer to specialist fetal medicine services; counsel on sexual transmission risk and appropriate precautions for a defined period after infection/exposure.
    • Yellow fever: Supportive care (fluid/haemodynamic support, management of coagulopathy and renal/hepatic failure) is the mainstay, as no specific antiviral treatment exists; monitor liver function, coagulation, and renal function closely given risk of severe hepatorenal failure in the toxic phase; vaccination is the cornerstone of prevention and is highly effective, required for travel to/from endemic areas per international health regulations.
    • Japanese encephalitis: Supportive care (ICU-level management of raised intracranial pressure, seizures, and respiratory support as needed) is the mainstay, as no specific antiviral treatment exists; monitor neurological status closely given risk of significant long-term neurological sequelae in survivors; vaccination is the primary prevention strategy in endemic/at-risk travellers; vector control reduces transmission at a population level.
    • Filariasis (lymphatic): Diethylcarbamazine (or ivermectin/albendazole combinations in areas co-endemic for onchocerciasis, where DEC is avoided) first-line for active infection; supportive lymphoedema management (skin hygiene, compression, exercise) for established lymphatic damage, as this does not reverse with antifilarial treatment alone; monitor for allergic reactions to treatment from dying microfilariae; mass drug administration is a key public health control strategy in endemic regions.
    • Onchocerciasis: Ivermectin first-line, given periodically (typically annually) as microfilaricidal treatment; monitor for ocular involvement (river blindness) with regular ophthalmological screening, as this drives the most serious long-term morbidity; avoid diethylcarbamazine in co-endemic areas given risk of severe ocular/systemic reactions; mass drug administration is the primary public health control strategy.
    • Schistosomiasis: Praziquantel first-line, effective against the adult worm stage; monitor for chronic complications (hepatosplenic disease, bladder pathology/urothelial carcinoma risk with S. haematobium) with appropriate imaging surveillance in endemic exposure; repeat treatment may be needed as praziquantel has limited efficacy against immature stages; address water contact/exposure reduction as part of public health control.
    • Ascariasis: Single-dose albendazole or mebendazole first-line; monitor for complications of heavy worm burden (intestinal obstruction, biliary/pancreatic duct obstruction) which may require surgical/endoscopic intervention in addition to antihelminthic therapy; mass deworming programmes in endemic areas reduce community burden, particularly in children where growth/nutritional impact is greatest.
    • Hookworm infection: Single-dose albendazole or mebendazole first-line; treat associated iron deficiency anaemia with iron supplementation, as chronic blood loss from hookworm is a major cause of anaemia in endemic areas; monitor haemoglobin response; mass deworming and improved sanitation are key public health prevention strategies.
    • Taeniasis (tapeworm): Single-dose praziquantel or niclosamide first-line for intestinal tapeworm infection; monitor for passage of segments confirming treatment success; counsel on prevention (thorough cooking of meat) and screen for neurocysticercosis risk in T. solium carriers, as autoinfection can lead to this more serious tissue-invasive form.
    • Neurocysticercosis (viable cysts): Antihelminthic therapy (albendazole, sometimes with praziquantel) combined with corticosteroids first-line for viable parenchymal cysts, as treatment can provoke inflammation around dying cysts; anti-seizure medication for associated seizures, which are the most common presentation; monitor with serial imaging and neurological status; antihelminthic therapy is avoided in ocular or heavily calcified/degenerating cysts with significant surrounding oedema until inflammation is controlled, and hydrocephalus may require neurosurgical intervention.
    • Hydatid disease (echinococcosis): Surgical resection (or PAIR - puncture, aspiration, injection, re-aspiration - for suitable cysts) is first-line for symptomatic or large cysts, combined with peri-procedural albendazole to reduce recurrence/seeding risk; albendazole alone for small, inoperable, or multiple cysts; monitor with serial imaging for recurrence; avoid unprotected percutaneous drainage without antihelminthic cover given anaphylaxis risk from cyst fluid leakage.
    • Leishmaniasis (visceral): Liposomal amphotericin B first-line in most settings given superior efficacy/safety over pentavalent antimonials; monitor blood counts (pancytopenia is characteristic and should improve with treatment), renal function, and clinical response (resolution of fever, splenomegaly); screen for HIV coinfection, which significantly alters treatment response and relapse risk; relapse surveillance is important in immunocompromised patients.
    • Leishmaniasis (cutaneous): Treatment approach depends on species/region and lesion characteristics; options include local therapy (intralesional antimonials, cryotherapy) for simple lesions or systemic therapy (antimonials, miltefosine) for complex/multiple lesions or those at risk of mucosal spread; monitor lesion healing and scarring; species identification helps predict risk of mucocutaneous spread, which changes management toward systemic therapy.
    • African trypanosomiasis: Stage-specific therapy is essential: early haemolymphatic stage treated with pentamidine (T.b. gambiense) or suramin (T.b. rhodesiense); late CNS-stage disease requires CNS-penetrant therapy (fexinidazole or NECT combination therapy), as haemolymphatic-stage drugs do not adequately cross the blood-brain barrier; monitor CSF for staging (cell count, trypanosomes) to guide treatment choice; lumbar puncture is essential before treatment selection.
    • Chagas disease (acute): Benznidazole (or nifurtimox) first-line, most effective when given in the acute phase; monitor for cardiac involvement (acute myocarditis) and treatment tolerability, as side effects are common and may limit the full course; screen for chronic Chagas cardiomyopathy/megacolon/megaoesophagus years later even after apparently successful acute treatment, as these complications can still develop; treatment in chronic phase has more debated benefit but is generally still offered, particularly in younger patients.
    • Diphtheria: Diphtheria antitoxin first-line, given as early as possible (ideally on clinical suspicion, without waiting for culture confirmation) as it neutralises only unbound toxin; add antibiotics (penicillin or erythromycin) to eliminate the organism and prevent transmission; monitor airway (risk of obstruction from pseudomembrane) and cardiac status (myocarditis is a major cause of death); isolate and provide close contact prophylaxis given high transmissibility.
    • Pertussis (infant, severe): Macrolide antibiotics first-line, most beneficial if started early though may not shorten established paroxysmal cough; supportive care with close monitoring for apnoea, cyanosis, and feeding difficulty, often requiring hospitalisation and sometimes ICU care in young infants; monitor oxygen saturation and for secondary bacterial pneumonia; prophylaxis for close contacts and maternal vaccination in pregnancy are key prevention strategies given highest severity/mortality in young unvaccinated infants.
    • Botulism: Botulinum antitoxin first-line, given as early as possible to neutralise circulating unbound toxin, as it cannot reverse toxin already bound to nerve terminals; supportive respiratory care (often requiring prolonged ventilation) as recovery depends on nerve terminal regeneration over weeks to months; monitor respiratory function closely given risk of progressive descending paralysis leading to respiratory failure; identify the source (food-borne, wound, infant) as this affects specific management (wound debridement for wound botulism).
    • Gas gangrene (clostridial myonecrosis): Urgent, extensive surgical debridement is first-line and time-critical, alongside high-dose IV penicillin plus clindamycin (for antitoxin effect); hyperbaric oxygen may be considered as an adjunct where available, though should not delay surgery; monitor for rapidly progressive systemic toxicity and shock; this is a surgical emergency where delayed debridement carries very high mortality.
    • Anthrax (cutaneous): Oral ciprofloxacin or doxycycline first-line for uncomplicated cutaneous anthrax; escalate to IV combination antibiotic therapy for systemic involvement or evidence of toxaemia; monitor for progression to systemic disease, which is uncommon but serious; avoid incision/manipulation of the characteristic eschar, which can promote systemic spread; report to public health authorities given bioterrorism implications in unexplained clusters.
    • Plague: IV or IM streptomycin (or gentamicin/doxycycline as alternatives) first-line, started as early as possible given high mortality if treatment is delayed, particularly in pneumonic plague; strict respiratory isolation for pneumonic plague given high person-to-person transmissibility; monitor for septicaemic spread and disseminated intravascular coagulation; report to public health authorities given outbreak and bioterrorism implications.
    • Melioidosis: IV ceftazidime or a carbapenem for an intensive initial phase (2+ weeks) first-line, followed by a prolonged oral eradication phase (co-trimoxazole, typically 3-6 months) to prevent relapse, given intracellular organism persistence; monitor for abscess formation (multiple organs, including prostate/spleen) requiring drainage alongside antibiotics; screen for underlying diabetes, a major risk factor; relapse is common without completing the full eradication phase.
    • Listeriosis: IV ampicillin (often with gentamicin for synergy in severe disease, particularly meningitis/bacteraemia) first-line, as Listeria is intrinsically resistant to cephalosporins, a common empirical meningitis choice that must be specifically supplemented; monitor for CNS involvement, which carries significant mortality; in pregnancy, treat promptly given risk of fetal loss/neonatal sepsis, and consider the diagnosis in unexplained febrile illness in pregnancy or immunocompromised/elderly patients.
    • Toxoplasmosis (congenital): Combination pyrimethamine, sulfadiazine, and folinic acid for an extended course (typically 12 months) is first-line for confirmed congenital infection; monitor visual (chorioretinitis surveillance), auditory, and neurodevelopmental outcomes long-term given risk of delayed manifestations; maternal treatment during pregnancy (spiramycin, or pyrimethamine-sulfadiazine if fetal infection confirmed) reduces transmission/severity; screening programmes vary by region.
    • Congenital rubella syndrome: Supportive, multidisciplinary management of the affected organ systems (cardiac, ophthalmological, audiological) is the mainstay, as there is no treatment for established congenital rubella syndrome; monitor hearing, vision, cardiac status, and developmental progress long-term; prevention through maternal rubella immunity/vaccination before pregnancy is the primary strategy, as this condition is now rare where vaccination coverage is high; infected infants shed virus for prolonged periods and require appropriate isolation precautions.
    • Congenital cytomegalovirus infection: Oral valganciclovir (or IV ganciclovir) for symptomatic congenital CMV, particularly with CNS/hearing involvement, first-line, shown to improve hearing and neurodevelopmental outcomes; monitor blood counts (myelosuppression is a common side effect) and hearing/neurodevelopment long-term, as CMV-related hearing loss can be progressive; asymptomatic infection is generally not treated but still requires audiological follow-up given delayed-onset hearing loss risk.
    • Herpes zoster ophthalmicus: Oral (or IV for severe/immunocompromised) antiviral therapy first-line, started promptly to reduce ocular complications; urgent ophthalmology referral given risk of keratitis, uveitis, and vision-threatening complications, particularly with nasociliary involvement (Hutchinson's sign); monitor visual acuity and for late complications (post-herpetic neuralgia, chronic ocular inflammation); topical corticosteroids for ocular inflammation are used under ophthalmology guidance alongside systemic antivirals.
    • Fungal keratitis: Topical antifungal therapy (natamycin for filamentous fungi, or an azole/amphotericin for yeast) first-line, often requiring intensive frequent dosing; escalate to therapeutic keratoplasty for progressive disease despite medical therapy or impending perforation; monitor closely with serial slit-lamp examination given risk of rapid progression; avoid topical corticosteroids, which can worsen fungal keratitis; identify risk factors (trauma with vegetative matter, contact lens use) which support diagnosis.
    • Mucormycosis (rhino-orbital-cerebral): Urgent surgical debridement combined with high-dose IV liposomal amphotericin B first-line, as this is a rapidly progressive, angioinvasive infection where delay is strongly associated with mortality; correct underlying predisposing factors (control hyperglycaemia/ketoacidosis, reduce immunosuppression where possible) alongside; monitor extent of disease with imaging and repeated surgical debridement as needed; this is a medical and surgical emergency requiring multidisciplinary (ENT, ophthalmology, infectious disease) input.
  8. Rheumatology
    • ANCA-associated vasculitis: Induction with high-dose corticosteroids plus rituximab or cyclophosphamide first-line for organ- or life-threatening disease, adding plasma exchange for severe pulmonary haemorrhage or rapidly progressive renal failure; maintenance with rituximab or azathioprine after remission; monitor ANCA titre trend, renal function, and for treatment-related infection/cytopenias; avotermin/avacopan can reduce steroid exposure in induction per current guidance.
    • Takayasu arteritis: High-dose corticosteroids first-line, adding a steroid-sparing immunosuppressant (methotrexate, tocilizumab) for relapsing or refractory disease; escalate to vascular intervention (angioplasty/bypass) for critical stenosis once inflammation is controlled; monitor inflammatory markers (often unreliable alone) alongside vascular imaging (MR/CT angiography) for disease activity; blood pressure should be measured in all four limbs given differential vessel involvement.
    • Antiphospholipid syndrome: Long-term anticoagulation (warfarin, target INR 2-3) first-line for thrombotic APS, as DOACs are less effective particularly in triple-positive disease; low-dose aspirin plus prophylactic-dose heparin for obstetric APS; monitor INR closely and for recurrent thrombosis despite therapeutic anticoagulation; catastrophic APS requires anticoagulation plus corticosteroids and plasma exchange/IVIG given high mortality.
    • Rheumatoid arthritis: Methotrexate first-line conventional DMARD, escalating to biologic or targeted synthetic DMARD (TNF inhibitor, JAK inhibitor) for inadequate response at 3-6 months; monitor disease activity score, and full blood count/liver function on methotrexate; screen for latent TB and hepatitis B/C before biologics; use JAK inhibitors cautiously in older patients or those with cardiovascular/VTE risk factors given boxed warnings.
    • Ankylosing spondylitis: NSAIDs first-line with regular physiotherapy/exercise programme; escalate to TNF inhibitor or IL-17 inhibitor biologic for inadequate response to NSAIDs (conventional DMARDs are ineffective for axial disease); monitor spinal mobility, inflammatory markers, and for extra-articular manifestations (uveitis, IBD); screen for and manage cardiovascular risk given increased association.
    • Psoriatic arthritis: NSAIDs and conventional DMARD (methotrexate) first-line for peripheral disease, escalating to biologic therapy (TNF, IL-17, or IL-23 inhibitor) for inadequate response or axial/enthesitis-predominant disease; monitor joint counts, skin involvement, and nail changes; avoid IL-17 inhibitors in active inflammatory bowel disease given risk of exacerbation, preferring TNF or IL-23 inhibitors instead.
    • Reactive arthritis: NSAIDs first-line for symptomatic joint control; add a short corticosteroid course or intra-articular injection for severe mono/oligoarthritis; treat any identified triggering infection (chlamydia, enteric pathogens) with appropriate antibiotics though this does not reliably alter arthritis course; monitor for chronicity, which occurs in a minority and may require DMARD therapy similar to other spondyloarthropathies.
    • Polymyalgia rheumatica: Low-dose oral corticosteroids first-line with rapid dramatic symptomatic response supporting the diagnosis; taper slowly over 1-2 years to minimise relapse; monitor inflammatory markers and symptoms at each taper step, and screen for coexisting or evolving giant cell arteritis (visual symptoms, headache) which requires much higher-dose steroids; consider methotrexate as a steroid-sparing agent for relapsing or prolonged-course disease.
    • Giant cell arteritis: Immediate high-dose corticosteroids first-line (started on clinical suspicion, without waiting for biopsy) to prevent irreversible visual loss; add tocilizumab for relapsing disease or as a steroid-sparing strategy; monitor inflammatory markers, visual symptoms, and temporal artery biopsy/ultrasound to confirm diagnosis; screen for aortitis/aneurysm given long-term risk, particularly in those with a prolonged disease course.
    • Systemic sclerosis: Organ-specific therapy is the mainstay (ACE-I for renal crisis, PDE5 inhibitors/endothelin antagonists for pulmonary hypertension and Raynaud's, immunosuppression for progressive skin/lung fibrosis); monitor annual pulmonary function/echocardiography for early pulmonary hypertension and ILD detection; avoid high-dose corticosteroids, which can precipitate scleroderma renal crisis; nifedipine and avoidance of cold are first-line for Raynaud's phenomenon.
    • Sjögren's syndrome: Symptomatic management (artificial tears/saliva, pilocarpine/cevimeline for secretomotor stimulation) first-line for sicca symptoms; add hydroxychloroquine or immunosuppression for systemic/extraglandular involvement; monitor for lymphoma risk (parotid swelling, cytopenias, low complement) given significantly increased incidence; screen for associated autoimmune conditions and congenital heart block risk in pregnancy if anti-Ro/La positive.
    • Granulomatosis with polyangiitis: Induction with high-dose corticosteroids plus rituximab or cyclophosphamide first-line, as for other severe ANCA-associated vasculitis; add plasma exchange for severe renal failure or pulmonary haemorrhage; monitor ANCA titre, renal function, and for relapse, which is common and often requires re-induction; screen for chronic sinonasal and subglottic disease requiring separate ENT management.
    • Polyarteritis nodosa: High-dose corticosteroids first-line, adding cyclophosphamide for severe or organ-threatening disease; treat associated hepatitis B (if present) with antivirals alongside immunosuppression, as HBV-associated PAN has a distinct management pathway prioritising antiviral therapy; monitor for mesenteric, renal, and neurological (mononeuritis multiplex) involvement; angiography may reveal characteristic microaneurysms guiding diagnosis.
    • Behçet's disease: Colchicine first-line for mucocutaneous and mild joint disease; escalate to immunosuppression (azathioprine, TNF inhibitors) for major organ involvement (uveitis, vascular, neurological, GI); monitor for ocular involvement requiring urgent ophthalmology input given sight-threatening potential; anticoagulation for venous thrombosis is used cautiously given coexisting risk of pulmonary artery aneurysm and haemorrhage.
    • Fibromyalgia: Patient education, graded exercise, and CBT first-line, as pharmacotherapy alone has modest effect; add low-dose amitriptyline, duloxetine, or pregabalin for inadequate response to non-pharmacological measures; monitor functional outcomes rather than relying solely on symptom scores; avoid opioids, which lack evidence of benefit and carry dependence risk in this population.
    • Osteoarthritis (knee): Weight loss, exercise/physiotherapy, and topical/oral NSAIDs first-line; intra-articular corticosteroid injection for symptomatic flares refractory to first-line measures; escalate to total knee replacement for severe symptomatic disease impacting quality of life despite conservative therapy; monitor functional status and pain, and use NSAIDs cautiously in renal impairment, cardiovascular disease, or the elderly.
    • Septic arthritis: Urgent joint aspiration for diagnosis followed by empirical IV antibiotics (covering Staphylococcus aureus) and surgical/arthroscopic washout, particularly for large joints or prosthetic joint infection; narrow antibiotics once organism identified, typically for 2-4 weeks; monitor clinical response, inflammatory markers, and repeat aspiration if not improving; treat as an orthopaedic emergency given risk of rapid cartilage destruction.
    • Pseudogout (CPPD arthropathy): NSAIDs or intra-articular corticosteroid injection first-line for acute attacks, with colchicine as an alternative; low-dose colchicine or NSAID prophylaxis for recurrent attacks; monitor for underlying metabolic associations (hyperparathyroidism, haemochromatosis, hypomagnesaemia) particularly in younger or atypical presentations; joint aspiration with polarised microscopy confirms diagnosis and excludes septic arthritis, which must always be considered concurrently.
    • Systemic sclerosis renal crisis: Urgent ACE-inhibitor therapy is first-line and disease-specific (uniquely, ACE-I are used even with rising creatinine, unlike most renal emergencies), titrated aggressively to control blood pressure; escalate to dialysis if renal failure progresses despite ACE-I therapy, with a meaningful chance of later renal recovery; monitor blood pressure and renal function closely, ideally with hospital admission; avoid high-dose corticosteroids, which are a recognised precipitant.
    • Mixed connective tissue disease: Treatment is directed at the dominant clinical feature (arthritis, myositis, sclerodermatous, or lupus-like) using the corresponding disease-specific therapy; monitor for pulmonary arterial hypertension, which is a leading cause of mortality and warrants regular echocardiographic screening; monitor anti-U1-RNP titres and organ-specific serologies; overlap features may evolve over time, requiring reassessment of the predominant phenotype.
    • Adult-onset Still's disease: High-dose corticosteroids first-line for systemic symptoms (fever, rash, arthritis); escalate to IL-1 or IL-6 inhibitor biologic therapy for steroid-refractory or relapsing disease; monitor ferritin (often markedly elevated and a useful activity marker), liver function, and for macrophage activation syndrome, a life-threatening complication requiring prompt recognition and treatment; exclude infection and malignancy before committing to immunosuppression given overlapping presentation.
    • Relapsing polychondritis: Corticosteroids first-line for active inflammation, with dose guided by severity of organ involvement; escalate to immunosuppression (methotrexate, biologics) for relapsing or corticosteroid-refractory disease; monitor for airway involvement (tracheal/laryngeal chondritis), which can be life-threatening and may require stenting or tracheostomy; screen for associated haematological malignancy (myelodysplastic syndrome) particularly in older patients.
    • IgG4-related disease: Corticosteroids first-line, with generally good initial response across affected organs; escalate to rituximab for relapsing or steroid-refractory disease, or as a steroid-sparing strategy; monitor serum IgG4 levels (imperfectly correlate with activity), organ imaging, and for fibrotic organ damage which may not reverse with treatment; biopsy with characteristic histopathology (storiform fibrosis, IgG4+ plasma cells) supports diagnosis and excludes malignancy mimics.
    • Eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis: High-dose corticosteroids first-line, adding cyclophosphamide for severe organ-threatening (cardiac, GI, renal, neurological) disease, or mepolizumab (anti-IL-5) for milder/relapsing disease allowing steroid reduction; monitor eosinophil count, ANCA (positive in a subset), and cardiac assessment given risk of eosinophilic myocarditis; asthma control often needs to be managed alongside systemic therapy.
    • Microscopic polyangiitis: Induction with high-dose corticosteroids plus rituximab or cyclophosphamide first-line, as for other ANCA-associated vasculitides; add plasma exchange for severe pulmonary haemorrhage or rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis; monitor renal function, ANCA titre, and pulmonary status; maintenance immunosuppression reduces relapse risk after induction remission is achieved.
    • Cryoglobulinaemic vasculitis: Treat the underlying cause first-line (antiviral therapy for hepatitis C-associated disease, which is the most common cause); add corticosteroids plus rituximab or plasma exchange for severe organ-threatening disease (renal, neurological, cutaneous ulceration); monitor cryoglobulin levels, complement, and renal function; avoid prolonged immunosuppression alone without addressing underlying HCV, as this risks viral flare.
    • Juvenile idiopathic arthritis: NSAIDs and intra-articular corticosteroids first-line for oligoarticular disease; escalate to methotrexate or biologic DMARD for polyarticular or systemic-onset disease, or inadequate response; monitor growth, joint damage, and screen regularly for asymptomatic uveitis (particularly ANA-positive oligoarticular disease) given risk of silent sight-threatening inflammation; multidisciplinary input (ophthalmology, physiotherapy) is essential given growth and development considerations.
    • Familial Mediterranean fever: Colchicine first-line, both to control attack frequency and to prevent amyloidosis, the most serious long-term complication; escalate to IL-1 inhibitor biologics (anakinra, canakinumab) for colchicine-resistant disease; monitor for adherence (the major cause of treatment failure), inflammatory markers between attacks, and urine protein to detect evolving amyloid nephropathy; genetic counselling is relevant given autosomal recessive inheritance.
    • Gout (acute flare): NSAIDs, colchicine, or corticosteroids first-line depending on comorbidity (NSAIDs avoided in renal impairment/heart failure, colchicine dose-reduced in renal impairment); do not start or stop urate-lowering therapy during an acute flare; monitor renal function and response; joint aspiration to exclude septic arthritis is warranted in atypical or first presentations, particularly with fever.
    • Gout (chronic tophaceous): Urate-lowering therapy (allopurinol first-line, titrated to target serum urate <6mg/dL, or <5mg/dL with visible tophi) alongside flare prophylaxis (colchicine or low-dose NSAID) during initiation; escalate allopurinol dose gradually to reduce flare risk, using febuxostat as an alternative if intolerant; monitor serum urate to target and renal/liver function; screen for HLA-B*5801 before allopurinate in high-risk populations (e.g. Han Chinese, Thai) given severe hypersensitivity risk.
    • Osteoarthritis (hip): Weight loss, exercise/physiotherapy, and analgesia (paracetamol/topical or oral NSAIDs) first-line; escalate to total hip replacement for severe symptomatic disease with functional impairment refractory to conservative measures; monitor pain and function, and use NSAIDs cautiously in renal impairment, cardiovascular disease, or older patients; intra-articular injection has a more limited role in the hip than the knee.
    • Chronic low back pain (non-specific): Education, activity/exercise encouragement, and simple analgesia first-line, avoiding prolonged bed rest which worsens outcomes; escalate to structured physiotherapy programme, and consider CBT for pain-related disability; monitor for red flags (progressive neurological deficit, cauda equina symptoms, weight loss, fever) requiring urgent imaging, which are rare in non-specific back pain; avoid routine imaging in the absence of red flags, as it does not improve outcomes and increases inappropriate intervention.
    • Spondylolisthesis (symptomatic): Physiotherapy and activity modification first-line for mild, stable, low-grade slips; escalate to surgical decompression/fusion for high-grade slips, progressive neurological deficit, or symptoms refractory to conservative management; monitor for progression on serial imaging, particularly in adolescents with growth remaining; sports restriction may be needed in symptomatic athletes with high-grade slips.
    • Enteropathic arthritis: Treatment of the underlying inflammatory bowel disease (biologic therapy) often improves peripheral arthritis; sulfasalazine or TNF inhibitors first-line for peripheral joint disease, while axial disease is managed as for ankylosing spondylitis; avoid NSAIDs where possible given risk of exacerbating IBD; monitor both gut and joint activity concurrently as they may not always parallel each other; IL-17 inhibitors are avoided given risk of worsening IBD.
    • Sarcoid arthropathy: NSAIDs first-line for acute sarcoid arthritis (often part of Löfgren syndrome with erythema nodosum and hilar lymphadenopathy), which is typically self-limiting; add corticosteroids for more persistent or destructive joint involvement; monitor for resolution, as Löfgren syndrome carries an excellent prognosis; distinguish acute self-limiting presentations from chronic sarcoid arthropathy, which may need steroid-sparing immunosuppression.
  9. Dermatology
    • Contact dermatitis: Identify and avoid the trigger allergen/irritant first-line, with topical corticosteroids for acute flares; emollients form the maintenance backbone in all cases; monitor for chronicity/lichenification with repeated exposure; patch testing helps confirm allergic contact dermatitis and identify specific allergens when the trigger is unclear.
    • Bullous pemphigoid: Potent topical corticosteroids first-line (as effective as systemic steroids with fewer side effects in localised/moderate disease), escalating to systemic corticosteroids plus a steroid-sparing agent (azathioprine, mycophenolate) for widespread/refractory disease; monitor for infection of eroded skin and steroid-related complications, particularly in the elderly who predominate; doxycycline is a lower-toxicity alternative for milder disease.
    • Molluscum contagiosum: Observation is first-line in immunocompetent children, as most lesions resolve spontaneously within months; physical destruction (cryotherapy, curettage) or topical agents (cantharidin, potassium hydroxide) for persistent, cosmetically bothersome, or widespread lesions; monitor for widespread/atypical presentation as a marker of underlying immunosuppression (e.g. HIV) in adults.
    • Viral warts: Topical salicylic acid or cryotherapy first-line, with combination/repeated treatment often needed given modest single-session cure rates; escalate to more aggressive destructive methods (curettage, laser) for refractory lesions; monitor for spontaneous resolution, which occurs in the majority over 1-2 years, particularly in children; genital warts warrant screening for other STIs.
    • Pediculosis (lice infestation): Topical pediculicide (permethrin or malathion) first-line, with a repeat application after 7-9 days to kill newly hatched lice; wet-combing as an adjunct/alternative; monitor for treatment failure/resistance, and treat close contacts to prevent reinfestation; environmental measures (washing bedding/clothing) have a supportive role.
    • Squamous cell carcinoma (skin): Surgical excision with clear margins first-line, with Mohs micrographic surgery for high-risk sites (face) or high-risk histology; radiotherapy for non-surgical candidates or as adjuvant therapy for high-risk features (perineural invasion, positive margins); monitor for local recurrence and regional nodal metastasis, more common than with basal cell carcinoma; sun protection counselling to reduce further lesions.
    • Seborrhoeic keratosis: Reassurance and observation first-line, as these are benign and require no treatment; cryotherapy or curettage for symptomatic (itchy, irritated) or cosmetically bothersome lesions; monitor for atypical features (rapid growth, bleeding, irregular pigmentation) which warrant biopsy to exclude melanoma, given occasional diagnostic overlap.
    • Psoriasis (plaque, moderate-severe): Topical corticosteroid plus vitamin D analogue first-line for localised disease, escalating to phototherapy or systemic therapy (methotrexate, biologics) for moderate-severe/widespread disease; monitor for psoriatic arthritis (joint symptoms), cardiovascular risk factors, and liver/renal function on systemic agents; screen for latent TB and hepatitis before biologics.
    • Atopic dermatitis (eczema): Emollients and topical corticosteroids first-line for flares, with topical calcineurin inhibitors as steroid-sparing options for sensitive sites; escalate to phototherapy or systemic immunomodulators (dupilumab, ciclosporin) for severe refractory disease; monitor for secondary bacterial/viral infection and growth in children on prolonged topical steroids; identify and avoid trigger factors (irritants, allergens).
    • Acne vulgaris (moderate): Topical retinoid plus benzoyl peroxide or topical antibiotic first-line, escalating to oral antibiotics (doxycycline) for moderate inflammatory disease, and oral isotretinoin for severe/scarring or treatment-resistant disease; monitor liver function/lipids and pregnancy status (strict contraception required) on isotretinoin given teratogenicity; hormonal therapy (combined oral contraceptive, spironolactone) is an option in females.
    • Cellulitis: Oral antibiotics (flucloxacillin or equivalent) first-line for uncomplicated disease, escalating to IV antibiotics for systemic upset, rapid progression, or failure of oral therapy; mark the margin to monitor progression; elevate the affected limb and manage any portal of entry (tinea pedis, wounds); consider necrotising fasciitis if pain is disproportionate to signs, which requires urgent surgical assessment.
    • Impetigo: Topical antibiotic (fusidic acid/mupirocin) first-line for localised disease, escalating to oral antibiotics for widespread or bullous impetigo; monitor for spread given high contagiousness, and exclude from school/childcare until lesions crust/dry or 48h after starting treatment; hygiene measures reduce transmission to close contacts.
    • Herpes simplex (recurrent labial/genital): Episodic oral aciclovir/valaciclovir at prodrome first-line for infrequent recurrences; suppressive daily antiviral therapy for frequent recurrences (>6/year) or to reduce transmission risk in genital disease; monitor for atypical/disseminated disease in immunocompromised patients requiring IV therapy; counsel on transmission risk and safe sex practices for genital disease.
    • Scabies: Topical permethrin 5% applied to the whole body first-line, repeated after 7 days; treat all close contacts simultaneously regardless of symptoms to prevent reinfestation, and wash bedding/clothing; oral ivermectin for crusted (Norwegian) scabies or outbreak settings; monitor for persistent itch post-treatment (post-scabetic pruritus), which does not necessarily indicate treatment failure.
    • Tinea corporis: Topical antifungal (terbinafine or azole) first-line, continued for 1-2 weeks beyond clinical resolution; escalate to oral antifungal (terbinafine) for extensive, recalcitrant, or immunocompromised-host disease; monitor for spread to close contacts and animal source if applicable (zoophilic dermatophytes); avoid topical corticosteroid monotherapy which can mask/worsen the infection (tinea incognito).
    • Melanoma (localised): Wide local excision with margins guided by Breslow thickness is definitive first-line; sentinel lymph node biopsy offered for intermediate/high-risk tumours to stage disease; monitor with regular skin surveillance and clinical follow-up for recurrence/new primaries; adjuvant immunotherapy or targeted therapy (BRAF-mutant disease) considered for high-risk resected disease per current guidance.
    • Basal cell carcinoma: Surgical excision with clear margins first-line, with Mohs micrographic surgery for high-risk sites (face) or recurrent tumours; topical therapy (imiquimod) or radiotherapy for superficial lesions or non-surgical candidates; monitor for local recurrence, which is the main risk (metastasis is rare); sun protection counselling given field cancerisation risk of further lesions.
    • Squamous cell carcinoma (cutaneous): Surgical excision with clear margins first-line, with Mohs surgery for high-risk sites/histology; radiotherapy as adjuvant for high-risk features or as primary treatment in non-surgical candidates; monitor regional lymph nodes given metastatic potential exceeding basal cell carcinoma; immunosuppressed patients (transplant recipients) need closer surveillance given higher incidence and more aggressive behaviour.
    • Urticaria (chronic): Non-sedating H1-antihistamine first-line, up-titrating to 4x standard dose before considering treatment failure; escalate to omalizumab or ciclosporin for refractory disease; monitor for angioedema/anaphylaxis features requiring emergency management; investigate for underlying autoimmune or physical triggers in atypical or treatment-refractory presentations.
    • Stevens-Johnson syndrome/TEN: Immediate withdrawal of the causative drug is the single most important step, alongside supportive care in a burns/ICU setting (fluid, wound, and temperature management); consider IVIG or ciclosporin though evidence is mixed; monitor mucosal involvement (ocular, genital) closely given long-term scarring risk, with early ophthalmology input; mortality correlates with body surface area involved (SCORTEN score).
    • Pemphigus vulgaris: Systemic corticosteroids first-line, with early addition of rituximab now favoured over steroid monotherapy given superior remission rates and steroid-sparing effect; monitor for secondary infection of denuded skin/mucosa and steroid-related complications; oral involvement is often the presenting and most refractory feature, requiring dedicated mouth care.
    • Bullous pemphigoid: Topical or systemic corticosteroids, with steroid-sparing agents for chronic disease.
    • Vitiligo: Topical corticosteroids or calcineurin inhibitors first-line for localised disease, escalating to phototherapy (narrowband UVB) for widespread disease; monitor treatment response, which can take months, and screen for associated autoimmune conditions (thyroid disease); camouflage and psychological support are important given cosmetic and quality-of-life impact.
    • Alopecia areata: Intralesional or topical corticosteroids first-line for limited patchy disease; escalate to systemic immunosuppression (JAK inhibitors are increasingly used) for extensive or refractory disease; monitor for spontaneous regrowth, which is common in limited disease, and for progression to alopecia totalis/universalis; screen for associated autoimmune conditions (thyroid disease, vitiligo).
    • Rosacea: Topical metronidazole or azelaic acid first-line for mild-moderate papulopustular disease, escalating to oral doxycycline for more severe/refractory disease; avoid triggers (sun exposure, alcohol, spicy food, heat) which vary by individual; monitor for ocular rosacea (dryness, blepharitis) needing separate management, and phymatous changes which may need laser/surgical treatment.
    • Contact dermatitis (allergic): Allergen identification via patch testing and strict avoidance is central to management, alongside topical corticosteroids for acute flares; emollients maintain the skin barrier; monitor for chronic relapsing disease with ongoing occult exposure; occupational contact dermatitis may require workplace modification or, rarely, change of occupation.
    • Contact dermatitis (irritant): Avoidance of the irritant and barrier protection (gloves, emollients) first-line, as no allergen is involved and patch testing is not diagnostic; topical corticosteroids for acute inflammation; monitor for chronicity with ongoing occupational/repeated exposure; distinguishing from allergic contact dermatitis (patch test negative) guides whether allergen avoidance counselling is needed.
    • Seborrhoeic dermatitis: Topical antifungal (ketoconazole) shampoo/cream first-line, targeting the associated Malassezia yeast overgrowth; add low-potency topical corticosteroid for inflammatory flares; monitor for chronic relapsing course requiring intermittent maintenance therapy; more extensive/severe disease can be a marker of HIV infection, warranting consideration in atypical presentations.
    • Lichen planus: Topical corticosteroids first-line for cutaneous/mucosal disease, escalating to systemic corticosteroids or other immunosuppression for widespread, erosive, or refractory disease; monitor oral lichen planus for malignant transformation risk with periodic review; identify and stop implicated drugs in lichenoid drug eruptions, which mimic idiopathic disease.
    • Pityriasis rosea: Reassurance and observation first-line, as this is a self-limiting condition resolving over 6-8 weeks without treatment; symptomatic relief (emollients, antihistamines for itch) as needed; monitor for atypical presentation, and consider secondary syphilis as a differential in sexually active patients with a similar rash pattern.
    • Erythema multiforme: Identify and treat/remove the trigger (most commonly HSV, sometimes drugs) first-line, with symptomatic treatment (antihistamines, topical corticosteroids) for the rash; suppressive antiviral therapy for recurrent HSV-associated disease; monitor for progression to more severe mucocutaneous involvement, distinguishing from Stevens-Johnson syndrome which carries a different prognosis and management intensity.
    • Erythema nodosum: Treat the underlying cause (infection, sarcoidosis, IBD, drugs) first-line, alongside symptomatic management (NSAIDs, leg elevation, rest); monitor for resolution over weeks, as most cases are self-limiting once the trigger resolves; investigate for underlying systemic disease particularly if recurrent or associated with systemic symptoms.
    • Pyoderma gangrenosum: Systemic corticosteroids first-line for significant disease, with ciclosporin as an alternative/steroid-sparing agent; avoid surgical debridement where possible, as pathergy can worsen ulceration; monitor for and treat the associated systemic disease (IBD, rheumatoid arthritis, haematological malignancy) present in a substantial proportion; wound care requires a specialised, non-aggressive approach.
    • Hidradenitis suppurativa: Topical/oral antibiotics and lifestyle measures (weight loss, smoking cessation) first-line for mild disease, escalating to biologic therapy (adalimumab) for moderate-severe disease, and surgery (wide excision) for chronic scarred/tunnelling disease; monitor disease severity (Hurley staging) to guide escalation; psychological support is important given significant quality-of-life impact.
    • Actinic keratosis: Cryotherapy for isolated lesions first-line, with field-directed topical therapy (5-fluorouracil, imiquimod, or photodynamic therapy) for multiple lesions/field change; monitor for progression to squamous cell carcinoma, the main rationale for treatment; sun protection counselling to prevent new lesions in this chronically sun-damaged population.
    • Keloid scar: Intralesional corticosteroid injection first-line, often combined with silicone gel sheeting/pressure therapy; escalate to surgical excision combined with adjuvant therapy (steroid injection or radiotherapy) for refractory lesions, as excision alone carries high recurrence; monitor for recurrence, which is common; counsel at-risk individuals (darker skin types, family history) before elective procedures in keloid-prone sites.
    • Molluscum contagiosum: Often self-limiting; cryotherapy or curettage for persistent/extensive lesions.
    • Warts (verrucae): Topical salicylic acid or cryotherapy first-line.
    • Herpetic whitlow: Oral aciclovir first-line, particularly if started early in the course; avoid incision and drainage, which can worsen/spread the infection and delay healing; monitor for recurrence, which is common given viral latency; healthcare workers should avoid direct patient contact during active lesions given transmission risk.
    • Necrobiosis lipoidica: Topical or intralesional corticosteroids first-line for active inflammatory lesions; optimise glycaemic control given strong diabetes association, though this does not reliably improve the skin lesions; monitor for ulceration, a recognised complication requiring wound care; biopsy may be needed to exclude other granulomatous conditions if atypical.
    • Erythroderma: Admission for supportive care (fluid/electrolyte balance, temperature regulation, infection surveillance) first-line given risk of high-output cardiac failure and sepsis; identify and treat the underlying cause (psoriasis, eczema, drug reaction, lymphoma); monitor fluid balance and skin barrier function closely; systemic therapy targeted to the underlying dermatosis once stabilised.
    • Toxic epidermal necrolysis: Immediate causative drug withdrawal and transfer to a burns/ICU unit for supportive care is first-line, mirroring major burn management; IVIG or ciclosporin considered though evidence is mixed; monitor for sepsis and multi-organ involvement (ocular, mucosal, pulmonary) with early specialist input across disciplines; mortality is high and correlates with body surface area involved.
    • Drug reaction with eosinophilia and systemic symptoms (DRESS): Immediate withdrawal of the causative drug first-line, with systemic corticosteroids for significant organ involvement; monitor liver, renal, and cardiac function closely given risk of delayed/relapsing multi-organ involvement over weeks; screen for HHV-6 reactivation, which is associated with the condition and relapse; recovery can be protracted with slow steroid tapering required.
    • Fixed drug eruption: Identify and avoid the causative drug is definitive, as rechallenge reliably reproduces lesions at the same site; topical corticosteroids for symptomatic relief of the acute lesion; monitor for the generalised bullous variant, which can mimic more severe reactions and needs closer supportive care; patch testing at the lesion site can help confirm the causative agent.
    • Cutaneous T-cell lymphoma (mycosis fungoides): Skin-directed therapy (topical corticosteroids, phototherapy) first-line for early-stage limited disease; escalate to systemic therapy (retinoids, interferon, or targeted agents) for advanced/refractory disease; monitor staging with skin examination and, in advanced disease, blood/nodal involvement; multidisciplinary review is recommended given the range of stage-adapted treatment options.
  10. Ophthalmology
    • Congenital cataract: Early surgical extraction (ideally within the first 6-8 weeks for dense unilateral cataract) is first-line to prevent irreversible amblyopia, followed by intensive optical correction and amblyopia therapy (patching); monitor visual development closely with paediatric ophthalmology follow-up, as the window for visual rehabilitation is narrow; screen for associated syndromic/metabolic causes (TORCH infections, galactosaemia) in bilateral cases.
    • Dacryocystitis: Systemic antibiotics first-line for acute infection, with incision and drainage for abscess formation; definitive treatment of the underlying nasolacrimal duct obstruction (dacryocystorhinostomy) once acute infection has settled; monitor for orbital cellulitis as a complication of untreated/spreading infection; probing is first-line for congenital nasolacrimal duct obstruction in infants before considering DCR.
    • Corneal foreign body/abrasion: Topical anaesthetic for examination, foreign body removal, and topical antibiotic first-line, with cycloplegic drops for pain from ciliary spasm; avoid topical anaesthetic for ongoing home use given corneal toxicity risk; monitor for infection (increasing pain, discharge) and healing at follow-up; contact lens wearers need cover for Pseudomonas given higher keratitis risk.
    • Hypertensive retinopathy: Blood pressure control is the definitive treatment, as retinal changes are a marker of systemic disease rather than a target for direct ocular therapy; monitor retinal findings (grade of retinopathy) as a marker of hypertensive severity/end-organ damage and cardiovascular risk; screen for accelerated/malignant hypertension (grade 3-4 changes with papilloedema) requiring urgent blood pressure reduction and hospital assessment.
    • Age-related macular degeneration: AREDS2 vitamin/mineral supplementation first-line for intermediate dry AMD to slow progression; anti-VEGF intravitreal injection for wet (neovascular) AMD, which requires prompt initiation to preserve vision; monitor with an Amsler grid for symptomatic changes prompting urgent review, and regular OCT imaging on anti-VEGF therapy; low vision aids/support for advanced disease with irreversible vision loss.
    • Ptosis: Observation for mild, stable, non-amblyogenic ptosis first-line; urgent assessment and surgical correction for congenital ptosis obstructing the visual axis given amblyopia risk; investigate acquired ptosis for underlying cause (myasthenia gravis, Horner syndrome, third nerve palsy) before elective surgical correction; monitor visual development in children with any degree of visual axis occlusion.
    • Exotropia (divergent squint): Observation for intermittent, well-controlled exotropia first-line; surgical correction (extraocular muscle surgery) for constant, large-angle, or progressively worsening deviation; monitor for amblyopia and stereopsis loss with orthoptic assessment; patching/optical correction addresses any associated refractive amblyopia before or alongside surgical planning.
    • Ocular chemical injury: Immediate copious irrigation (before formal visual acuity testing) is the single most important step, continued until pH normalises; alkali burns cause more severe damage than acid and need especially prolonged irrigation; escalate to specialist ophthalmology assessment for grading (Roper-Hall) and management of ongoing epithelial defect/limbal ischaemia; monitor for glaucoma and corneal scarring as long-term sequelae requiring extended follow-up.
    • Open-angle glaucoma: Topical prostaglandin analogue first-line to lower intraocular pressure, adding beta-blocker or other agents for inadequate control; escalate to laser trabeculoplasty or surgical trabeculectomy for progression despite maximal medical therapy; monitor intraocular pressure, optic disc appearance, and visual fields regularly to detect progression; screen first-degree relatives given hereditary risk.
    • Acute angle-closure glaucoma: Immediate IV acetazolamide plus topical agents (pilocarpine, beta-blocker, alpha-agonist) to rapidly lower intraocular pressure is first-line, as this is an ophthalmic emergency threatening permanent vision loss; laser peripheral iridotomy is definitive once the acute attack settles, performed on the fellow eye prophylactically too; monitor intraocular pressure response closely and escalate to surgical iridectomy if laser is not accessible/effective.
    • Cataract (visually significant): Phacoemulsification with intraocular lens implantation is definitive and highly effective, offered once visual impairment affects daily function/quality of life; no effective medical therapy delays progression; monitor for post-operative complications (endophthalmitis, posterior capsule opacification); biometry planning targets the desired post-operative refraction based on patient preference/lifestyle.
    • Diabetic retinopathy (proliferative): Panretinal photocoagulation laser first-line to induce regression of neovascularisation; anti-VEGF intravitreal injection as an alternative/adjunct, particularly with concurrent macular oedema; vitrectomy for vitreous haemorrhage or tractional retinal detachment; monitor with regular retinal screening in all diabetics, and optimise glycaemic/blood pressure control which slows progression across all stages.
    • Age-related macular degeneration (wet): Anti-VEGF intravitreal injection (loading doses then individualised regimen) first-line, started promptly as delay worsens visual outcomes; monitor with OCT imaging and Amsler grid self-monitoring for recurrence between injections; treatment is typically long-term/indefinite given high recurrence off therapy; combination or newer longer-acting agents are options for suboptimal responders.
    • Retinal detachment: Urgent surgical repair (vitrectomy, scleral buckle, or pneumatic retinopexy depending on detachment type/location) is time-critical, particularly with macula-threatened or macula-off detachment where visual outcome worsens with delay; monitor for proliferative vitreoretinopathy as a cause of re-detachment; identify and treat retinal breaks in the fellow eye prophylactically if high-risk features are present.
    • Central retinal artery occlusion: Immediate measures (ocular massage, IOP-lowering therapy) attempted if presenting within the narrow treatment window, though evidence for benefit is limited; treat as a stroke equivalent with urgent stroke/cardiovascular workup, as CRAO shares aetiology with cerebral stroke; monitor for giant cell arteritis in older patients (urgent ESR/CRP and temporal artery assessment) given need for emergency high-dose steroids if present; visual prognosis is generally poor once established.
    • Bacterial keratitis: Intensive topical broad-spectrum antibiotics (often fortified) first-line, guided by corneal scrape culture in significant cases; escalate therapy/frequency for non-response or progression; monitor closely given risk of rapid progression to corneal perforation; contact lens wearers should stop lens use and are managed with Pseudomonas cover given higher risk of this aggressive organism.
    • Bacterial conjunctivitis: Topical antibiotic (chloramphenicol) first-line, though many cases are self-limiting and antibiotics mainly shorten duration/prevent spread; hygiene measures reduce transmission; monitor for red flags (reduced vision, photophobia, significant pain) suggesting keratitis/more serious pathology requiring urgent ophthalmology referral rather than simple conjunctivitis management.
    • Allergic conjunctivitis: Topical antihistamine/mast cell stabiliser first-line, alongside allergen avoidance where identifiable; cold compresses provide symptomatic relief; escalate to topical corticosteroids (short courses, ophthalmology-supervised) for severe/refractory disease given steroid-related glaucoma/cataract risk with prolonged use; monitor for atopic keratoconjunctivitis features in chronic severe disease, which carries corneal complication risk.
    • Uveitis (anterior): Topical corticosteroids plus cycloplegic first-line to reduce inflammation and pain/prevent synechiae; investigate for an underlying systemic association (HLA-B27-related disease, sarcoidosis, infection) particularly with recurrent or bilateral disease; monitor intraocular pressure on steroid therapy and for complications (cataract, glaucoma, synechiae); escalate to systemic immunosuppression for chronic/recurrent or posterior segment involvement.
    • Orbital cellulitis: IV broad-spectrum antibiotics first-line given risk of intracranial spread and vision loss; urgent surgical drainage for abscess formation, proptosis with visual compromise, or failure to improve on IV antibiotics; monitor visual acuity, colour vision, and pupillary response closely as markers of optic nerve compromise; distinguish from preseptal cellulitis, which is managed less aggressively as it lacks orbital involvement.
    • Optic neuritis: High-dose IV corticosteroids considered to hasten visual recovery (though final visual outcome is largely unaffected) first-line for significant vision loss; investigate with MRI brain/orbits, as this often heralds multiple sclerosis, particularly with white matter lesions; monitor visual recovery, which is usually good even without treatment; discuss disease-modifying therapy referral if MRI suggests high conversion risk to MS.
    • Retinopathy of prematurity: Regular screening examinations in at-risk premature infants first-line to detect treatable disease before irreversible damage; laser photocoagulation or anti-VEGF injection for threshold/high-risk pre-threshold disease; monitor for progression at defined screening intervals until vascularisation is complete or disease regresses; long-term follow-up for myopia, strabismus, and amblyopia even after successful treatment.
    • Strabismus (childhood): Optical correction of any refractive error and amblyopia treatment (patching) first-line, as these often coexist and treating amblyopia is time-critical; surgical correction of the deviation for persistent/large-angle strabismus once optical/patching measures are optimised; monitor visual development and binocular function; early assessment is important as the window for reversing amblyopia narrows with age.
    • Corneal abrasion: Topical antibiotic ointment/drops and analgesia first-line, with most abrasions healing within 24-48h; avoid topical anaesthetic for ongoing use given delayed healing/toxicity risk; monitor for infection and non-healing, particularly in contact lens wearers who need Pseudomonas cover; bandage contact lens or patching has limited evidence and is used selectively.
    • Chemical eye injury (alkali): Immediate copious irrigation before any other assessment is the critical first step, continued until pH returns to normal (typically 7.0); alkali injury penetrates deeper than acid and requires more aggressive/prolonged irrigation; escalate to specialist ophthalmology management (topical steroids, ascorbate, tectonic support) for significant limbal/corneal involvement; monitor for delayed complications (glaucoma, corneal melt, symblepharon) over weeks to months.
    • Hyphaema (traumatic): Bed rest with head elevation and eye shield first-line to reduce rebleed risk, avoiding NSAIDs/aspirin which increase bleeding; monitor intraocular pressure closely (raised IOP is common and needs treatment) and for rebleeding, typically within the first 3-5 days; escalate to surgical evacuation for uncontrolled IOP, corneal blood staining, or non-clearing hyphaema; sickle cell status should be checked given higher complication risk in sickle trait/disease.
    • Globe rupture (traumatic): Immediate protection with a rigid shield (avoiding any pressure on the globe) and urgent surgical repair is definitive, as this is a vision- and globe-threatening emergency; avoid further examination/manipulation that could extrude intraocular contents; broad-spectrum IV antibiotics and tetanus prophylaxis alongside; monitor for endophthalmitis and sympathetic ophthalmia risk in the fellow eye with severe injury.
    • Endophthalmitis (post-surgical): Urgent intravitreal antibiotic injection (with vitreous tap for culture) is first-line and time-critical, as delay significantly worsens visual outcome; escalate to vitrectomy for severe presentation (light perception vision or worse); monitor visual acuity and inflammation response closely, often requiring repeat intravitreal dosing; this is an ophthalmic emergency requiring same-day specialist management.
    • Dacryocystitis (acute): Systemic antibiotics first-line for the acute infective episode, with drainage of any associated abscess; definitive surgical correction (dacryocystorhinostomy) of the underlying duct obstruction deferred until acute inflammation resolves; monitor for spread to orbital cellulitis, particularly in more severe or delayed-presentation cases.
    • Chalazion: Warm compresses and lid massage first-line, as many resolve spontaneously over weeks; intralesional corticosteroid injection or surgical incision/curettage for persistent lesions; monitor for recurrence and, in atypical or recurrent unilateral lesions in older patients, consider biopsy to exclude sebaceous gland carcinoma, an important mimic.
    • Blepharitis: Lid hygiene (warm compresses, lid margin cleaning) first-line as the mainstay of chronic management; add topical antibiotic or short-course topical steroid for acute exacerbations; monitor for associated dry eye and meibomian gland dysfunction requiring concurrent management; this is typically a chronic relapsing condition requiring ongoing maintenance rather than a curable one-off treatment.
    • Entropion/ectropion: Lubrication and taping as temporising measures first-line while surgery is arranged; definitive surgical correction (lid tightening/repositioning procedures) is the mainstay for symptomatic disease; monitor for corneal exposure/abrasion complications, particularly with entropion where lashes rub the cornea, which can threaten vision if prolonged.
