    • Pterygium (symptomatic): Lubricants and sun protection (UV-blocking sunglasses) first-line for mild irritation; surgical excision with conjunctival autograft for visually significant, rapidly growing, or persistently symptomatic pterygium; monitor for recurrence, which is reduced but not eliminated by autografting compared to bare sclera technique; astigmatism/visual axis involvement is an indication to expedite surgery.
    • Keratoconus: Spectacle or rigid contact lens correction first-line for visual rehabilitation in early/stable disease; corneal collagen cross-linking to halt progression in documented progressive disease, ideally before significant thinning; corneal transplantation (DALK or penetrating keratoplasty) for advanced disease with contact lens intolerance or central scarring; monitor corneal topography serially to detect progression warranting cross-linking.
    • Optic disc oedema (papilloedema): Urgent neuroimaging to identify and treat the underlying cause (raised intracranial pressure from mass lesion, venous sinus thrombosis, idiopathic intracranial hypertension) is first-line, as papilloedema itself is a sign rather than a primary diagnosis; monitor visual fields and acuity closely, as prolonged untreated papilloedema causes irreversible optic atrophy; lumbar puncture with opening pressure once mass lesion excluded, both diagnostic and sometimes therapeutic in IIH.
    • Retinal vein occlusion (central): Anti-VEGF intravitreal injection first-line for macular oedema with vision loss; investigate for underlying risk factors (hypertension, glaucoma, hyperviscosity states) and manage these concurrently; monitor for neovascular complications (neovascular glaucoma) requiring panretinal photocoagulation; visual prognosis is variable and depends on ischaemic versus non-ischaemic subtype.
    • Amblyopia: Correction of the underlying cause (refractive error, strabismus, or visual deprivation such as cataract) first-line, followed by occlusion therapy (patching the better eye) to stimulate the amblyopic eye; monitor visual acuity response to patching regularly, adjusting patching regimen accordingly; treatment is most effective when started early, as efficacy declines with increasing age at presentation.
    • Congenital cataract: Early surgical removal to prevent amblyopia.
    • Thyroid-associated orbitopathy (compressive): Manage thyroid dysfunction and smoking cessation first-line (smoking significantly worsens orbitopathy severity/progression); high-dose IV corticosteroids for active moderate-severe inflammatory disease, with orbital decompression surgery for sight-threatening compressive optic neuropathy; monitor visual acuity, colour vision, and optic nerve function closely for signs of compression requiring urgent decompression; strabismus/eyelid surgery is deferred until the disease is quiescent.
    • Orbital fracture (blowout): Conservative management (ice, avoid nose-blowing, analgesia) first-line for fractures without significant entrapment or enophthalmos; urgent surgical repair for muscle entrapment with the oculocardiac reflex (bradycardia, nausea on gaze) or significant diplopia/enophthalmos; monitor for infraorbital nerve numbness and diplopia on serial examination; CT imaging defines fracture extent and guides the urgency of surgical referral.
  11. ENT
    • Tympanic membrane perforation: Watchful waiting first-line, as most traumatic/post-infective perforations heal spontaneously within weeks; keep the ear dry and avoid topical aminoglycosides (ototoxic through a perforation) unless infected; escalate to surgical repair (myringoplasty) for perforations persisting beyond 6-12 weeks or causing recurrent infection/significant hearing loss; monitor hearing and for cholesteatoma development in chronic perforations.
    • Sensorineural hearing loss: Identify and treat any reversible cause (ototoxic drug withdrawal, treat underlying infection) first-line, though most causes are irreversible; hearing aids or cochlear implantation (for severe-profound loss with inadequate hearing aid benefit) for rehabilitation; monitor progression with serial audiometry, particularly important in sudden or asymmetric loss to exclude a retrocochlear cause (vestibular schwannoma) with MRI.
    • Adenoid hypertrophy: Watchful waiting first-line in mild cases, as adenoids often regress with age; adenoidectomy for significant nasal obstruction, recurrent otitis media with effusion, or obstructive sleep apnoea features; monitor for associated conductive hearing loss and speech/behavioural impact; assess for coexisting tonsillar hypertrophy, as combined adenotonsillectomy is often performed together for sleep-disordered breathing.
    • Laryngitis: Voice rest, hydration, and avoidance of irritants (smoking) first-line, as most cases are viral and self-limiting; antibiotics only for evidence of bacterial superinfection or in the immunocompromised; monitor for persistent hoarseness beyond 3 weeks, which warrants laryngoscopy to exclude malignancy, particularly in smokers/heavy alcohol users.
    • Vocal cord nodules: Voice therapy (speech and language therapy) first-line, addressing the vocal misuse/overuse pattern that causes them, with good response in most cases; surgical excision reserved for persistent nodules despite adequate voice therapy; monitor vocal quality and adherence to therapy; occupational voice users (singers, teachers) benefit from targeted vocal hygiene education to prevent recurrence.
    • Nasopharyngeal carcinoma: Radiotherapy (with concurrent chemotherapy for locally advanced disease) is first-line given radiosensitivity and difficult surgical access; monitor treatment response with imaging and EBV DNA levels (a useful biomarker in EBV-associated disease) where available; screen for and manage radiotherapy-related complications (xerostomia, hypothyroidism); regional variation in incidence (endemic in parts of East/Southeast Asia) should raise suspicion in at-risk populations presenting with neck mass/nasal symptoms.
    • Sialadenitis: IV or oral antibiotics first-line for acute bacterial sialadenitis, alongside hydration, sialagogues (to stimulate salivary flow), and gland massage; escalate to surgical drainage for abscess formation; monitor for an obstructing stone as the underlying cause, particularly with recurrent episodes, which may need duct exploration/removal.
    • Benign paroxysmal positional vertigo: Epley (or appropriate canalith repositioning) manoeuvre is first-line and often curative in a single session; vestibular sedatives have limited role and may delay central compensation if used long-term; monitor for recurrence, which is common and may need repeat manoeuvres; exclude central causes (posterior circulation stroke) if atypical features (other neurological signs, non-fatiguing nystagmus) are present.
    • Acute otitis media: Analgesia first-line with a watchful-waiting approach for 48-72h in most children given high spontaneous resolution rates; antibiotics (amoxicillin) for children under 2 with bilateral disease, those with otorrhoea, or if symptoms persist/worsen; monitor for complications (mastoiditis, persistent effusion) and recurrent episodes which may warrant grommet insertion.
    • Chronic suppurative otitis media: Aural toilet plus topical antibiotic (quinolone, safe with a perforated drum) first-line for active discharge; definitive surgical management (myringoplasty/tympanoplasty, or mastoidectomy for cholesteatoma) once infection is controlled; monitor for cholesteatoma, which requires more aggressive surgical management given erosive/destructive potential; avoid ototoxic topical agents (aminoglycosides) through a perforation for prolonged periods where alternatives exist.
    • Acute tonsillitis (bacterial): Analgesia and supportive care first-line, as most cases are viral; penicillin (or macrolide if allergic) for confirmed/suspected group A streptococcal infection, guided by clinical scoring (Centor/FeverPAIN); monitor for peritonsillar abscess (quinsy) as a complication; recurrent tonsillitis meeting frequency criteria may warrant tonsillectomy referral.
    • Peritonsillar abscess (quinsy): Needle aspiration or incision and drainage plus IV antibiotics is first-line and usually curative; monitor airway patency closely given risk of obstruction with large abscesses; interval tonsillectomy considered for recurrent quinsy; steroids may hasten symptomatic improvement as an adjunct to drainage and antibiotics.
    • Acute sinusitis (bacterial): Analgesia, nasal saline irrigation, and intranasal corticosteroids first-line, as most acute sinusitis is viral and self-limiting; antibiotics reserved for symptoms persisting beyond 10 days, severe symptoms, or a 'double-sickening' pattern suggesting bacterial superinfection; monitor for orbital or intracranial complications (periorbital swelling, severe headache, neurological signs) requiring urgent imaging and escalation.
    • Epistaxis (anterior): Direct pressure (pinching the soft cartilaginous part of the nose) for 10-15 minutes first-line, with topical vasoconstrictor/cautery for persistent bleeding; nasal packing if cautery fails; monitor blood pressure and for underlying coagulopathy/anticoagulant use in recurrent or difficult-to-control bleeds; most anterior bleeds arise from Little's area and respond well to first-line measures.
    • Epistaxis (posterior, severe): Posterior nasal packing (balloon or ribbon gauze) plus resuscitation first-line, given greater blood loss and airway risk than anterior bleeds; escalate to sphenopalatine artery ligation or embolisation for bleeding refractory to packing; monitor haemoglobin and haemodynamics closely, admitting for observation given aspiration and hypoxia risk with posterior packs; correct any coagulopathy concurrently.
    • Obstructive sleep apnoea (paediatric): Adenotonsillectomy is first-line and highly effective when tonsillar/adenoidal hypertrophy is the cause, which is the case in most paediatric OSA; monitor growth, behaviour, and cardiovascular signs (right heart strain in severe untreated disease); polysomnography confirms diagnosis/severity and guides urgency of intervention; CPAP is reserved for those with persistent OSA post-surgery or when surgery is contraindicated.
    • Vocal cord nodules: Voice therapy first-line; surgery reserved for refractory cases.
    • Laryngeal carcinoma (early): Radiotherapy or transoral laser surgery, both with comparable cure rates and organ (voice) preservation, are first-line for early-stage glottic disease; monitor with regular laryngoscopic surveillance for recurrence; smoking and alcohol cessation counselling is essential given shared risk factors and impact on treatment outcomes; voice outcomes should be discussed when choosing between modalities.
    • Head and neck squamous cell carcinoma (locally advanced): Concurrent chemoradiotherapy or surgery followed by adjuvant (chemo)radiotherapy first-line, chosen based on site/resectability and functional considerations; monitor treatment response and for treatment-related toxicity (mucositis, xerostomia, dysphagia) requiring supportive care (feeding tube, speech/swallow therapy); multidisciplinary team input is essential given the complexity of functional/cosmetic trade-offs.
    • Foreign body in the ear/nose/airway: Prompt removal under direct visualisation (or endoscopically for airway/deep foreign bodies) is first-line, avoiding blind instrumentation which risks pushing objects deeper; airway foreign bodies causing respiratory distress require emergency management (back blows/Heimlich, or rigid bronchoscopy); monitor for complications (mucosal injury, infection) and button battery ingestion specifically requires urgent removal given rapid tissue necrosis risk.
    • Meniere's disease: Low-salt diet and betahistine first-line for symptom control between attacks; intratympanic steroid or gentamicin injection for refractory vertigo attacks despite medical therapy; monitor hearing (typically fluctuating, progressing to permanent loss over time) and vertigo frequency/severity; surgical options (endolymphatic sac surgery, labyrinthectomy) reserved for severe refractory disease given more definitive but higher-risk nature.
    • Sudden sensorineural hearing loss: High-dose oral (or intratympanic) corticosteroids started as soon as possible (ideally within 2 weeks) is first-line, as this is an otological emergency where delay reduces recovery chance; urgent MRI to exclude vestibular schwannoma as an underlying cause; monitor hearing recovery with serial audiometry; most cases remain idiopathic despite investigation.
    • Nasal septal deviation (symptomatic): Nasal saline and intranasal corticosteroids first-line for mild obstructive symptoms; septoplasty for significant deviation causing persistent obstruction refractory to medical therapy; monitor symptom improvement and nasal airflow; assess for coexisting rhinitis/polyps contributing to obstruction, as these need separate/concurrent management.
    • Nasal polyposis: Intranasal (and short-course oral) corticosteroids first-line, often with long-term maintenance given high recurrence; escalate to biologic therapy (anti-IL-4/13, anti-IgE) or endoscopic sinus surgery for refractory disease; monitor symptom control and screen for associated asthma and aspirin-exacerbated respiratory disease (Samter's triad); recurrence is common even after surgery without ongoing medical maintenance therapy.
    • Juvenile nasopharyngeal angiofibroma: Surgical excision (often with pre-operative embolisation to reduce intraoperative bleeding given high vascularity) is first-line and curative for resectable disease; radiotherapy reserved for unresectable/recurrent disease; monitor for recurrence with surveillance imaging; this occurs almost exclusively in adolescent males, an important diagnostic clue for recurrent unilateral epistaxis in this demographic.
    • Nasopharyngeal carcinoma: Chemoradiotherapy as primary treatment.
    • Salivary gland stone (sialolithiasis): Conservative measures (hydration, sialagogues, gland massage, warm compresses) first-line for small distal stones, which may pass spontaneously; sialendoscopy or surgical removal for larger/proximal stones or recurrent obstruction; monitor for secondary infection (sialadenitis) requiring antibiotics; gland excision reserved for recurrent symptomatic stones unresponsive to duct-preserving approaches.
    • Parotitis (bacterial): IV antibiotics (covering Staphylococcus aureus) plus hydration and sialagogues first-line; surgical drainage for abscess formation; monitor for risk factors (dehydration, poor oral hygiene, post-surgical) and address these to prevent recurrence; distinguish from viral parotitis (mumps), which is managed supportively and has different infection control implications.
    • Pleomorphic adenoma (parotid): Superficial or partial parotidectomy with a cuff of normal tissue (avoiding enucleation, which risks recurrence) is definitive; monitor for facial nerve function post-operatively, as the nerve runs through the gland and is at risk during surgery; monitor for recurrence, and note the (rare but recognised) risk of malignant transformation in long-standing untreated lesions, supporting surgical excision even though the tumour is benign.
    • Cholesteatoma: Surgical excision (mastoidectomy) is definitive, as cholesteatoma is locally destructive and does not resolve with medical therapy alone; topical antibiotics may control secondary infection/discharge pre-operatively; monitor for recurrence, which requires long-term follow-up given risk of residual/recurrent disease; untreated disease risks progressive hearing loss, facial nerve palsy, and intracranial complications (abscess, meningitis).
    • Otosclerosis: Hearing aids first-line for symptomatic conductive hearing loss, as a non-surgical option; stapedectomy/stapedotomy offers definitive surgical correction with good hearing outcomes for suitable candidates; monitor hearing progression, which is often bilateral and progressive; sodium fluoride has historically been used to slow progression though evidence is limited and it is now rarely used.
    • Mastoiditis (acute): IV antibiotics first-line, with urgent surgical drainage (cortical mastoidectomy) for abscess formation, subperiosteal collection, or failure to improve on IV antibiotics; monitor closely for intracranial complications (meningitis, sigmoid sinus thrombosis, brain abscess) given proximity, which mandate imaging if there are red flag features; this typically arises as a complication of inadequately treated acute otitis media.
    • Retropharyngeal abscess: IV antibiotics first-line for small abscesses/cellulitis, with urgent surgical drainage for larger collections or airway compromise; monitor airway closely given risk of sudden obstruction, particularly in young children where this is more common; imaging (CT neck) confirms extent and guides the decision for surgical versus medical management alone.
    • Ludwig's angina: IV broad-spectrum antibiotics plus urgent airway assessment/securing (often requiring awake fibreoptic intubation or surgical airway) is first-line, as rapid bilateral submandibular space swelling can cause life-threatening airway obstruction; surgical drainage for abscess formation or failure to improve; monitor airway continuously given the rapidity with which obstruction can develop; dental source control (extraction of the causative tooth) is often needed once stabilised.
    • Laryngomalacia (infant): Reassurance and observation first-line, as most cases are mild and resolve spontaneously by 12-18 months as laryngeal cartilage matures; escalate to supraglottoplasty for severe disease with feeding difficulty, failure to thrive, or apnoeic episodes; monitor growth and respiratory status, as these guide the need for surgical intervention; positional/feeding advice can help mild-moderate symptoms.
    • Vocal cord paralysis (unilateral): Voice therapy first-line, as many patients compensate adequately, particularly with unilateral paralysis; escalate to injection medialisation or surgical thyroplasty for persistent significant dysphonia/aspiration; investigate the underlying cause (thyroid surgery, malignancy, idiopathic) with imaging of the nerve's course; monitor for aspiration risk, particularly relevant with bilateral involvement which additionally threatens the airway.
    • Tinnitus (subjective, chronic): Reassurance, hearing aid provision (if hearing loss present), and sound therapy/tinnitus retraining first-line, as most chronic subjective tinnitus has no curable underlying cause; cognitive behavioural therapy for tinnitus-related distress; monitor for red flags (unilateral pulsatile tinnitus, asymmetric hearing loss) warranting imaging to exclude vestibular schwannoma or vascular cause; manage coexisting anxiety/depression, which commonly amplifies perceived severity.
    • Presbycusis: Hearing aid fitting first-line once hearing loss impacts communication/quality of life, as this is a progressive age-related sensorineural process without a reversible cause; monitor hearing with serial audiometry to track progression and guide aid adjustment; address associated social isolation and cognitive impact, given recognised links between untreated hearing loss and cognitive decline.
    • Otitis externa: Topical antibiotic-corticosteroid combination drops first-line, with aural toilet (cleaning debris) to improve drug penetration and speed resolution; keep the ear dry during treatment and avoid cotton buds/instrumentation; monitor for progression to malignant otitis externa in diabetic/immunocompromised patients, which needs a markedly different, more aggressive approach.
    • Malignant (necrotising) otitis externa: Prolonged IV/oral antipseudomonal antibiotics (often 6-8 weeks or longer) first-line, as this is an aggressive skull-base osteomyelitis typically in diabetic or immunocompromised patients; surgical debridement for progression or abscess despite antibiotics; monitor with inflammatory markers and imaging (CT/MRI, or nuclear medicine scanning) to track disease resolution, as clinical appearance can lag; cranial nerve involvement (particularly facial nerve) indicates more extensive disease and worse prognosis.
  12. Orthopaedics & Trauma
    • Elbow fracture-dislocation: Closed reduction under sedation first-line for simple dislocations, followed by early mobilisation once stable; open reduction and internal fixation (with ligament/capsule repair) for fracture-dislocations with instability (e.g. terrible triad injury); monitor neurovascular status closely before and after reduction given ulnar/median nerve and brachial artery proximity; splint in a position avoiding stiffness (avoid prolonged immobilisation beyond 1-2 weeks) as elbow stiffness is the dominant long-term complication.
    • Wrist and hand fractures: Closed reduction and cast immobilisation first-line for stable, minimally displaced fractures; surgical fixation (K-wires, plates, or external fixation) for unstable, intra-articular, or open injuries; monitor for compartment syndrome and median nerve function (acute carpal tunnel) in high-energy wrist injuries; in children, remodelling potential allows more angulation to be accepted, reducing the threshold for non-operative management.
    • Spinal compression fracture (traumatic): Analgesia and bracing first-line for stable fractures without neurological compromise; escalate to surgical stabilisation (instrumented fusion) for unstable fracture patterns, significant kyphosis, or neurological deficit; monitor neurological status serially and reassess stability with flexion-extension imaging once acute pain settles; in osteoporotic compression fractures, consider vertebroplasty/kyphoplasty for refractory pain and address underlying bone health.
    • Spondylolisthesis: Physiotherapy (core stabilisation) and activity modification first-line for low-grade, asymptomatic-to-mild disease; surgical fusion (with or without decompression) for progressive slip, high-grade disease, or neurological compromise; monitor slip progression with serial imaging in growing adolescents; avoid high-impact sports until symptoms and slip grade are controlled.
    • Knee dislocation: Emergency reduction is time-critical, followed immediately by careful assessment for popliteal vascular injury (ankle-brachial index, CT angiography if abnormal) given high risk of limb-threatening ischaemia; staged ligament reconstruction (multi-ligament repair) once swelling settles; monitor distal pulses and compartments closely for 24-48 hours post-reduction even with a normal initial vascular exam, as delayed vascular injury can occur; low threshold for vascular surgery referral.
    • Tuberculosis of bone and joint: Standard anti-TB therapy (typically extended to 6-12 months for osteoarticular disease) as first-line, with surgical debridement reserved for abscess, significant deformity, or neurological compromise (Pott's disease with cord compression); monitor clinical and radiological response, and liver function on therapy; screen for HIV and multidrug-resistant TB in poor responders; joint destruction may still need reconstructive surgery after infection is controlled.
    • Septic arthritis: Emergency joint washout/drainage (arthroscopic or open) plus empirical then targeted IV antibiotics is first-line, as delay risks rapid cartilage destruction; escalate to repeat washout if clinical response is inadequate at 48-72 hours; monitor inflammatory markers and clinical response serially, with a low threshold to re-image/re-aspirate; in prosthetic joint infection, staged revision arthroplasty is usually required rather than washout alone.
    • Diabetic foot ulcer: Glycaemic optimisation, offloading (total contact casting), and regular debridement first-line; escalate to targeted antibiotics for infected ulcers (guided by deep tissue culture, not swab) and vascular assessment/revascularisation if ischaemic component present; monitor for osteomyelitis with imaging (MRI) if the ulcer probes to bone; amputation is reserved for extensive necrosis, uncontrolled sepsis, or failed revascularisation; multidisciplinary foot clinic follow-up reduces recurrence.
    • Open fracture (Gustilo-Anderson I-II): Urgent IV antibiotics (within an hour of injury) and tetanus prophylaxis first-line, followed by thorough surgical debridement and irrigation within 24 hours, with fracture stabilisation appropriate to the fracture pattern; monitor for infection and compartment syndrome; delay definitive soft-tissue closure if contamination is significant, favouring staged management.
    • Meniscal tear: Physiotherapy and activity modification first-line for degenerative or stable peripheral tears, particularly in older patients where surgery offers limited benefit; arthroscopic partial meniscectomy or repair for symptomatic mechanical tears (locking, giving way) in younger patients, favouring repair over resection where the tear is repairable to preserve meniscal function; monitor for early osteoarthritis progression after meniscectomy given loss of shock absorption.
    • Open fracture (Gustilo-Anderson III): Urgent broad-spectrum IV antibiotics, aggressive debridement, and provisional external fixation first-line given high contamination/soft-tissue loss; escalate to staged definitive fixation and flap coverage once the wound is clean, often requiring plastic surgery input; monitor closely for compartment syndrome and deep infection; amputation may be considered early in mangled-extremity scores predicting non-salvageable limbs.
    • Closed long bone fracture (displaced): Reduction with internal fixation (intramedullary nailing or plating) or external fixation first-line depending on fracture pattern, soft-tissue condition, and patient factors; monitor for compartment syndrome in the acute period and for delayed union/non-union on serial imaging; in polytrauma, damage-control external fixation followed by delayed definitive fixation reduces complication risk in the physiologically unstable patient.
    • Hip fracture (elderly): Early surgical fixation (intramedullary nail/sliding hip screw) or arthroplasty (hemiarthroplasty/total hip replacement, guided by fracture pattern and pre-injury mobility) within 24-48 hours is first-line to enable early mobilisation and reduce mortality; escalate to orthogeriatric co-management for perioperative optimisation; monitor for delirium, VTE, and pressure injury proactively; delay beyond 48 hours without medical justification is associated with worse outcomes.
    • Compartment syndrome: Emergency fasciotomy is definitive and time-critical (ideally within 6 hours of onset) once diagnosed clinically or by compartment pressure measurement; monitor the wound for delayed primary closure or skin grafting once swelling resolves; avoid elevating the limb above heart level (worsens perfusion) and avoid tight dressings/casts pending fasciotomy; missed diagnosis risks irreversible muscle necrosis and limb loss.
    • Septic arthritis (native joint): Urgent surgical washout plus targeted IV antibiotics (initially empirical covering Staphylococcus aureus, narrowed to culture) is first-line; escalate to repeat arthroscopic or open washout if clinical/inflammatory markers fail to improve within 48-72 hours; monitor joint function and inflammatory markers serially; consider gonococcal arthritis in sexually active young adults, which often responds to antibiotics alone without repeat washout.
    • Osteomyelitis (acute): Prolonged targeted IV antibiotics (typically 4-6 weeks, often with oral step-down) first-line, with surgical debridement reserved for abscess, sequestrum, or failure of medical therapy; monitor inflammatory markers and clinical response, with imaging (MRI) to assess extent and guide surgical planning; in children, haematogenous osteomyelitis usually responds well to antibiotics alone if treated early, whereas adults and diabetics more often need surgical debridement.
    • Rotator cuff tear (full-thickness): Physiotherapy first-line for partial/degenerative tears in lower-demand or older patients; surgical repair (arthroscopic or open) for acute traumatic full-thickness tears in active individuals, ideally within weeks to limit muscle retraction and fatty infiltration; monitor functional recovery post-repair with structured rehabilitation; massive irreparable tears in the elderly may be managed with reverse total shoulder arthroplasty if pain/function are significantly impaired.
    • Anterior cruciate ligament rupture: Physiotherapy (quadriceps strengthening, neuromuscular training) first-line for low-demand patients or those willing to modify activity; surgical reconstruction for active/high-demand patients or those with instability affecting daily function; monitor for meniscal/chondral injury at the time of reconstruction, as concurrent injuries affect long-term outcome; delaying surgery for a period of pre-habilitation improves post-operative range of motion.
    • Achilles tendon rupture: Functional bracing in equinus with early controlled mobilisation is an effective non-operative option with re-rupture rates approaching surgical repair in appropriately selected patients; surgical repair preferred for active individuals, delayed presentation, or re-rupture; monitor for re-rupture and wound complications (higher with open repair); early weight-bearing and structured rehabilitation improve functional outcomes regardless of treatment choice.
    • Carpal tunnel syndrome: Wrist splinting (especially night-time) and corticosteroid injection first-line for mild-moderate disease; surgical decompression for refractory symptoms, significant sensory loss, or thenar wasting; monitor nerve conduction studies to grade severity and guide urgency of surgery; pregnancy-associated carpal tunnel syndrome often resolves post-partum and can usually be managed conservatively.
    • Frozen shoulder (adhesive capsulitis): Physiotherapy and intra-articular corticosteroid injection first-line, particularly effective in the early painful "freezing" phase; capsular release (arthroscopic or manipulation under anaesthesia) for refractory disease persisting beyond 6-12 months; monitor range of motion serially, as the condition is often self-limiting over 1-3 years; diabetics have higher incidence and slower resolution, warranting earlier/more aggressive intervention.
    • Scoliosis (adolescent idiopathic, progressive): Bracing first-line for moderate curves (typically 25-40°) in a skeletally immature patient to prevent progression; surgical correction (instrumented spinal fusion) for severe curves (>45-50°) or progression despite bracing; monitor curve progression with serial standing radiographs during growth spurts (Risser staging); screen for underlying neuromuscular or syndromic causes if atypical curve pattern or early onset.
    • Developmental dysplasia of the hip: Pavlik harness first-line in infants under 6 months with reducible dysplasia; closed or open reduction with spica casting for failed harness treatment or late-presenting cases; monitor with serial ultrasound (under 6 months) or X-ray (over 6 months) to confirm reduction and acetabular development; delayed diagnosis increases risk of avascular necrosis and long-term osteoarthritis, underscoring the value of newborn screening.
    • Slipped capital femoral epiphysis: Urgent surgical pinning (in-situ fixation) is first-line to prevent further slippage once diagnosed, regardless of severity, given risk of avascular necrosis with delay; monitor the contralateral hip closely, as bilateral involvement is common (prophylactic pinning is considered in high-risk endocrine/metabolic disorders); avoid weight-bearing until stabilised; screen for underlying endocrinopathy (hypothyroidism, growth hormone disorders) in atypical age or weight presentations.
    • Osteosarcoma: Neoadjuvant chemotherapy followed by limb-sparing surgical resection with wide margins is first-line where feasible; amputation reserved for tumours not amenable to limb salvage (extensive neurovascular involvement); monitor chemotherapy response histologically (percentage necrosis) to guide adjuvant chemotherapy intensity and prognosis; screen for pulmonary metastases at diagnosis and on surveillance, as the lung is the dominant site of spread.
    • Bone metastases with pain: Radiotherapy first-line for localised painful metastases; surgical stabilisation (prophylactic fixation) for impending pathological fracture (per Mirels' scoring) or actual fracture; monitor for hypercalcaemia and further skeletal-related events; bisphosphonates/denosumab reduce skeletal-related event frequency and are used alongside definitive local treatment; multidisciplinary input balances life expectancy against surgical morbidity.
    • Spinal stenosis (lumbar, symptomatic): Physiotherapy and analgesia first-line, with epidural steroid injection as an adjunct for radicular pain; decompressive laminectomy (with or without fusion) for refractory neurogenic claudication or progressive neurological deficit; monitor for cauda equina symptoms (saddle anaesthesia, bladder/bowel dysfunction) which mandate urgent surgical referral; in the elderly, weigh surgical risk against expected functional benefit.
    • Herniated lumbar disc with radiculopathy: Conservative management (analgesia, physiotherapy, activity modification) first-line, as most cases improve within 6-12 weeks; discectomy for persistent radicular pain despite conservative therapy beyond 6-12 weeks, or for progressive/severe neurological deficit; monitor for cauda equina syndrome red flags requiring emergency MRI and decompression; epidural steroid injection can be considered as an interim measure.
    • Clavicle fracture (midshaft, minimally displaced): Sling immobilisation first-line, as most heal without surgery; surgical fixation (plating) for significant displacement, shortening (>2cm), or comminution given higher non-union/malunion risk with these patterns; monitor for neurovascular compromise (subclavian vessels, brachial plexus) at presentation; early gentle mobilisation once pain allows reduces stiffness.
    • Scaphoid fracture: Cast immobilisation first-line for non-displaced, stable fractures; surgical fixation (percutaneous screw) for displaced fractures, proximal pole fractures, or those at high non-union risk (e.g. athletes wanting faster return); monitor with repeat imaging (including MRI/CT if X-ray equivocal) given the risk of missed fracture and subsequent avascular necrosis of the proximal pole due to retrograde blood supply.
    • Colles' fracture: Closed reduction and cast immobilisation first-line for stable, acceptably aligned fractures; surgical fixation (volar locking plate) for unstable/displaced fractures or those failing to maintain reduction in cast; monitor for median nerve compression (acute carpal tunnel) and, in the longer term, for malunion affecting wrist function; in elderly low-demand patients, some residual deformity is often accepted given lower functional requirements.
    • Supracondylar humerus fracture (paediatric): Closed reduction and percutaneous pinning first-line for displaced (Gartland type II-III) fractures, with careful neurovascular monitoring given proximity of the brachial artery and median/radial/ulnar nerves; open reduction if closed reduction fails or the vessel is entrapped; monitor the pulse and hand perfusion closely post-reduction, as a pink pulseless hand requires urgent vascular reassessment; compartment syndrome is a recognised complication requiring vigilance.
    • Ankle fracture (unstable): Surgical open reduction and internal fixation is first-line for unstable patterns (bimalleolar, trimalleolar, or with syndesmotic disruption); monitor soft-tissue swelling before surgery (may require staged approach with temporary splinting) and post-operative wound healing, particularly in diabetics/smokers where complication rates are higher; early weight-bearing protocols post-fixation are increasingly favoured where fixation is stable.
    • Calcaneal fracture: Conservative management (immobilisation, elevation, delayed weight-bearing) first-line for minimally displaced, extra-articular fractures; surgical fixation (ORIF) for displaced intra-articular fractures to restore joint congruity, ideally once swelling settles; monitor for wound complications (notably high with the lateral extensile approach) and subtalar joint stiffness/arthritis long-term; smokers have markedly higher wound complication rates and should be counselled accordingly.
    • Pelvic fracture (unstable): Haemodynamic stabilisation with a pelvic binder is immediate first-line in the trauma bay, followed by definitive fixation (external or internal) once the patient is stable; escalate to angioembolisation or preperitoneal packing for ongoing haemorrhage despite binder and resuscitation; monitor haemodynamic status and associated injuries (urogenital, rectal, neurological) closely, as pelvic fractures are frequently part of high-energy polytrauma.
    • Spinal fracture (stable, non-neurological): Bracing and analgesia with early mobilisation as tolerated is first-line; monitor for delayed instability or neurological change with follow-up imaging and clinical review; encourage early mobilisation to prevent deconditioning and VTE, particularly in elderly patients where prolonged bed rest carries significant risk.
    • Spinal fracture with neurological deficit: Urgent surgical decompression and stabilisation is first-line, ideally within 24 hours of injury where feasible, as earlier decompression is associated with better neurological recovery; maintain spinal precautions and mean arterial pressure targets (typically >85mmHg) to optimise cord perfusion pending surgery; monitor neurological status closely (ASIA scale) and manage complications of spinal cord injury (autonomic dysreflexia, neurogenic bladder/bowel, VTE prophylaxis) proactively.
    • Flail chest: Analgesia (including regional techniques such as epidural or paravertebral blocks) and respiratory support (including non-invasive ventilation or mechanical ventilation if respiratory failure develops) first-line; surgical rib fixation considered for severe flail segments, particularly if ventilator-dependent or undergoing thoracotomy for another indication; monitor respiratory status closely given risk of underlying pulmonary contusion worsening over 24-48 hours; aggressive pulmonary toilet and early mobilisation reduce pneumonia risk.
    • Femoral shaft fracture: Intramedullary nailing is first-line definitive fixation in most adults, ideally within 24 hours in the stable patient; damage-control external fixation first in the physiologically unstable polytrauma patient, with delayed conversion to nailing; monitor for fat embolism syndrome (respiratory distress, petechiae, confusion) particularly in the first 24-72 hours, and for blood loss which can be substantial even in closed fractures.
    • Pathological fracture (metastatic): Surgical stabilisation (often with adjuvant cement/prosthetic reconstruction) first-line to enable pain relief and mobilisation, followed by radiotherapy to the fixation site; monitor for further skeletal-related events and consider bisphosphonates/denosumab; investigate for the primary tumour if not already known, as management (and prognosis) depends heavily on tumour type; multidisciplinary oncological input guides fixation choice and post-operative systemic therapy.
    • Chronic osteomyelitis (with sequestrum): Surgical debridement with sequestrectomy is first-line, as chronic infection with dead bone rarely resolves with antibiotics alone; prolonged targeted antibiotics (often with local antibiotic-eluting beads/spacers) as an adjunct; monitor for recurrence, which is common, and for the resulting bone defect requiring staged reconstruction (bone grafting, distraction osteogenesis); malignant transformation (Marjolin's ulcer) is a rare but recognised long-term complication of chronic sinus tracts.
    • Avascular necrosis of the femoral head: Core decompression first-line for early-stage disease (pre-collapse) to relieve intraosseous pressure and potentially preserve the joint; total hip arthroplasty for advanced disease with collapse and secondary osteoarthritis; monitor disease progression with serial imaging (MRI is most sensitive) as early intervention offers the best chance of joint preservation; address underlying risk factors (corticosteroid use, alcohol excess, sickle cell disease) where modifiable.
    • Tennis elbow (lateral epicondylitis): Activity modification, physiotherapy (eccentric strengthening), and bracing first-line; corticosteroid injection can give short-term relief but is associated with higher recurrence than physiotherapy alone, so is used judiciously; surgical debridement for refractory disease beyond 6-12 months of conservative therapy; monitor functional improvement, as most cases resolve spontaneously over 1-2 years even without intervention.
    • Plantar fasciitis: Stretching, orthotics, and activity modification first-line; corticosteroid injection or extracorporeal shockwave therapy for refractory pain beyond several months; surgical release reserved for cases failing extensive conservative management (typically 6-12 months); monitor for plantar fascia rupture risk with repeated corticosteroid injections; most cases resolve within a year with conservative measures alone.
    • De Quervain's tenosynovitis: Splinting and activity modification first-line; corticosteroid injection into the first dorsal compartment for persistent symptoms; surgical release for refractory cases; monitor for recurrence, particularly in new mothers (repetitive lifting/gripping) where symptoms often improve as the causative activity changes.
    • Trigger finger: Splinting and activity modification first-line for mild disease; corticosteroid injection into the flexor tendon sheath is highly effective and typically first-line for more established disease; surgical release (percutaneous or open) for cases failing one or two injections; higher recurrence and lower injection response rates are seen in diabetics, who often need earlier surgical referral.
    • Dupuytren's contracture: Observation for early disease without functional limitation; collagenase injection or needle fasciotomy for cords causing mild-moderate contracture; surgical fasciectomy for more severe or recurrent contracture affecting hand function; monitor for disease recurrence, which is common regardless of treatment modality, and counsel on the genetic/familial and alcohol-related associations.
    • Ganglion cyst: Observation first-line, as many resolve spontaneously; aspiration for symptomatic relief, though recurrence is common; surgical excision for persistent, painful, or functionally limiting cysts, or where diagnosis is uncertain; monitor for recurrence post-excision and reassure patients that ganglions are benign.
    • Osgood-Schlatter disease: Activity modification and analgesia first-line, as the condition is self-limiting and typically resolves with skeletal maturity; physiotherapy (quadriceps stretching) as an adjunct; surgical excision of a persistent ossicle rarely needed only if symptoms persist into adulthood; monitor growth and reassure families about the benign, self-limiting course.
    • Perthes disease: Containment bracing or activity modification first-line for younger children with mild disease and good femoral head coverage; surgical containment (osteotomy) for older children, more severe disease, or poor femoral head coverage on imaging; monitor femoral head shape and hip range of motion with serial imaging through the disease course (which spans years); prognosis is better with younger age at onset and less femoral head involvement.
    • Talipes equinovarus (recurrence): Repeat Ponseti casting and bracing first-line for recurrence after initial correction, as non-compliance with the foot abduction brace is the most common cause; tendon transfer surgery for recurrent relapse despite good brace compliance; monitor brace adherence closely, as recurrence is strongly linked to inconsistent bracing use during the maintenance phase.
    • Rickets (nutritional): Vitamin D and calcium supplementation first-line, with correction of dietary/sunlight exposure deficiencies; monitor bone deformity and biochemical markers (calcium, phosphate, alkaline phosphatase, vitamin D level) for response to therapy; surgical correction of residual bony deformity (e.g. bowing) is reserved for cases persisting after biochemical normalisation and skeletal maturity; screen for and address underlying malabsorption or renal causes if response to standard therapy is poor.
    • Charcot arthropathy (non-diabetic): Immobilisation and offloading (total contact casting) first-line to prevent progressive joint destruction during the acute phase; surgical reconstruction/fusion for established deformity causing instability or recurrent ulceration; monitor with serial imaging for progression, as the joint remains at risk of further collapse until the acute inflammatory phase resolves; identify and manage the underlying neuropathic cause (e.g. syphilis, syringomyelia, alcohol-related neuropathy).
    • Compartment syndrome (chronic exertional): Activity modification and physiotherapy first-line for mild disease; fasciotomy (open or endoscopic) for symptoms significantly limiting activity/sport despite conservative measures; confirm the diagnosis with intracompartmental pressure testing before and after exercise, as this is a clinical/physiological diagnosis distinct from acute compartment syndrome; monitor for recurrence post-fasciotomy, which occurs in a minority.
  13. Obstetrics & Gynaecology
    • Obstructed/prolonged labour: Close partographic monitoring with oxytocin augmentation first-line where contractions are inadequate and the pelvis is adequate; emergency caesarean section if obstruction is confirmed (cephalopelvic disproportion, malposition) or fetal distress develops; monitor fetal heart rate continuously and maternal vitals for signs of uterine rupture (especially in a scarred uterus); prolonged obstructed labour risks obstetric fistula, underscoring the importance of timely escalation.
    • Umbilical cord prolapse: Immediate manual elevation of the presenting part to relieve cord compression, with the mother positioned in knee-chest or Trendelenburg, followed by emergency caesarean section as definitive management; monitor fetal heart rate continuously during transfer to theatre; if full dilatation and criteria are met, an assisted vaginal delivery may be faster than caesarean; bladder filling can be used as an adjunct to relieve compression during transfer.
    • Placental abruption (moderate-severe): Maternal resuscitation (IV fluids, blood products as needed) and continuous fetal monitoring first-line, with expedited delivery (caesarean if fetal distress or significant abruption, vaginal if stable and progressing) depending on severity and gestation; monitor closely for disseminated intravascular coagulation and postpartum haemorrhage, both common complications; anti-D prophylaxis in Rh-negative mothers.
    • Uterine fibroids (asymptomatic/incidental): Observation first-line for asymptomatic fibroids not affecting fertility; medical therapy (GnRH agonists, ulipristal) for symptom control or pre-operative shrinkage; myomectomy or hysterectomy for large/symptomatic fibroids depending on fertility wishes; monitor growth in pregnancy (rapid growth/red degeneration can cause pain) and reassess if new symptoms develop.
    • Ovarian cyst: Observation with interval ultrasound first-line for simple, small (<5cm), asymptomatic cysts, as most resolve spontaneously; surgical removal (cystectomy, preserving ovarian tissue where possible) for large, symptomatic, complex, or suspicious cysts; monitor tumour markers (CA-125) and imaging features for malignant potential, particularly in postmenopausal women where the threshold for surgical removal is lower.
    • Cervical carcinoma: Staging (clinical/imaging) determines treatment: radical hysterectomy with lymphadenectomy first-line for early-stage disease, or definitive chemoradiotherapy for locally advanced disease; fertility-sparing radical trachelectomy an option in very early disease for appropriate candidates; monitor with regular clinical follow-up and cervical cytology/HPV testing post-treatment; HPV vaccination and screening remain the primary prevention strategies.
    • Amenorrhea: Systematic evaluation (pregnancy test first, then hormonal panel, pelvic imaging) to identify the underlying cause (hypothalamic-pituitary, ovarian, uterine/outflow tract) before targeted treatment; monitor bone health in hypoestrogenic states (functional hypothalamic amenorrhoea) given fracture risk; address the underlying driver (weight, stress, exercise in hypothalamic causes; hormone replacement in ovarian insufficiency) rather than treating amenorrhoea symptomatically alone.
    • Preterm premature rupture of membranes: Antibiotics (typically erythromycin-based regimens) to prolong latency and reduce infection, plus antenatal corticosteroids for fetal lung maturity, first-line; magnesium sulfate for neuroprotection if very preterm; monitor closely for chorioamnionitis (fever, tachycardia, uterine tenderness) and fetal wellbeing, balancing infection risk against prematurity risk when timing delivery.
    • Transverse lie: External cephalic version if feasible and no contraindication, attempted from around 36-37 weeks; caesarean section for delivery if version fails, contraindicated, or in labour, as vaginal delivery in transverse lie risks cord prolapse and obstructed labour; monitor for spontaneous version, which can occur up to term.
    • Ectopic pregnancy (unruptured, stable): Methotrexate first-line for eligible small unruptured ectopics (low beta-hCG, no fetal cardiac activity, no significant pain) with serial beta-hCG monitoring to confirm resolution; surgical (laparoscopic) management otherwise; monitor for treatment failure or rupture requiring conversion to surgery; anti-D prophylaxis in Rh-negative women.
    • Ectopic pregnancy (ruptured): Emergency surgical management (laparoscopic or open salpingectomy/salpingostomy) with concurrent resuscitation is definitive and time-critical given risk of haemorrhagic shock; monitor haemodynamic status closely and cross-match blood; anti-D prophylaxis in Rh-negative women; counsel on future ectopic risk and fertility.
    • Miscarriage (incomplete): Expectant, medical (misoprostol), or surgical evacuation first-line depending on patient preference, bleeding severity, and clinical status; monitor for heavy bleeding or infection with expectant/medical management, with a low threshold to proceed to surgical evacuation if either occurs; anti-D prophylaxis in Rh-negative women undergoing surgical management or with significant bleeding.
    • Preeclampsia (severe): Blood pressure control (labetalol/nifedipine/hydralazine) and magnesium sulfate seizure prophylaxis first-line, with timed delivery once maternal/fetal condition dictates; escalate antihypertensives if targets not met and monitor for HELLP syndrome/eclampsia; monitor renal function, liver function, and platelet count closely; delivery is the only definitive cure, with timing balancing maternal safety against fetal maturity.
    • Eclampsia: Magnesium sulfate to control and prevent further seizures is first-line, alongside blood pressure control and expedited delivery once the mother is stabilised; monitor for magnesium toxicity (reflexes, respiratory rate, urine output) especially in renal impairment; continue magnesium for 24 hours post-delivery/last seizure given ongoing seizure risk in this window.
    • Postpartum haemorrhage: Uterotonics (oxytocin first-line, adding ergometrine/carboprost/misoprostol for inadequate response) and uterine massage first-line; escalate to balloon tamponade, compression sutures, or surgical measures (uterine artery ligation, hysterectomy as a last resort) if bleeding continues; monitor for disseminated intravascular coagulation and transfuse early in a balanced ratio if blood loss is significant; identify and address the underlying cause (tone, tissue, trauma, thrombin).
    • Placenta praevia: Elective caesarean delivery timed to gestational age and bleeding risk is first-line, typically around 36-37 weeks for stable major praevia; emergency caesarean for significant bleeding; monitor for recurrent antepartum haemorrhage and consider antenatal corticosteroids if preterm delivery becomes likely; avoid vaginal examination once praevia is diagnosed given haemorrhage risk.
    • Placental abruption (severe, with fetal compromise): Urgent delivery, typically by emergency caesarean, with maternal resuscitation (fluids, blood products) run in parallel is first-line given the time-critical nature of fetal compromise; monitor for disseminated intravascular coagulation and postpartum haemorrhage aggressively, as abruption is a major cause of both; anti-D prophylaxis in Rh-negative mothers.
    • Gestational diabetes mellitus: Dietary and lifestyle modification first-line; escalate to insulin or metformin if glycaemic targets are not met on lifestyle measures alone; monitor fetal growth (macrosomia risk) with serial ultrasound and glycaemic control closely through pregnancy; screen for persistent diabetes with a postpartum oral glucose tolerance test, as gestational diabetes confers substantially increased future type 2 diabetes risk.
    • Premature rupture of membranes (preterm): Antibiotics and corticosteroids for fetal lung maturity first-line, with expectant management balanced against infection risk; escalate to delivery if chorioamnionitis, fetal distress, or advancing gestation makes expectant management no longer favourable; monitor maternal temperature, fetal heart rate, and inflammatory markers closely for signs of evolving infection.
    • Preterm labour: Tocolysis (nifedipine or atosiban) to allow time for corticosteroid administration first-line, plus magnesium sulfate for fetal neuroprotection if very preterm; monitor for contraindications to tocolysis (chorioamnionitis, significant bleeding, severe pre-eclampsia) where delivery should not be delayed; escalate to delivery if tocolysis fails or is contraindicated.
    • Chorioamnionitis: Broad-spectrum IV antibiotics and expedited delivery (regardless of gestation) is first-line, as this is an obstetric emergency with maternal sepsis and fetal risk; monitor maternal temperature, fetal heart rate, and inflammatory markers closely; neonatal team should be alerted for early-onset neonatal sepsis risk given in-utero exposure.
    • Polycystic ovary syndrome: Lifestyle modification (weight management) first-line, particularly given metabolic overlap; combined oral contraceptives for cycle regulation and hyperandrogenism in those not seeking pregnancy; metformin and/or ovulation induction (letrozole/clomiphene) for fertility; monitor metabolic parameters (glucose tolerance, lipids) given increased long-term cardiometabolic risk; screen for endometrial hyperplasia risk with prolonged amenorrhoea.
    • Endometriosis: NSAIDs and hormonal suppression (combined contraceptives, progestins, or GnRH agonists) first-line for pain control; surgical excision/ablation for refractory pain or fertility concerns, with assisted reproduction considered alongside or instead of repeat surgery; monitor symptom recurrence, which is common after stopping hormonal suppression; counsel on the chronic, relapsing nature of the disease.
    • Uterine fibroids (symptomatic): Hormonal therapy (levonorgestrel IUD, GnRH agonists, ulipristal) for symptom control first-line; myomectomy for those wishing to preserve fertility, or hysterectomy for definitive treatment in those who have completed childbearing; monitor haemoglobin given menorrhagia-related anaemia; uterine artery embolisation is an alternative for those wishing to avoid surgery, though less suitable if future fertility is desired.
    • Pelvic inflammatory disease: Empirical broad-spectrum antibiotics covering gonorrhoea, chlamydia, and anaerobes first-line, started promptly on clinical suspicion without waiting for swab results; escalate to IV antibiotics and consider surgical drainage for tubo-ovarian abscess or failure to respond to oral therapy within 72 hours; monitor for infertility, ectopic pregnancy risk, and chronic pelvic pain as long-term sequelae; partner treatment and contact tracing are essential.
    • Endometrial carcinoma (early stage): Total hysterectomy with bilateral salpingo-oophorectomy (with lymph node assessment) is first-line for early-stage disease; adjuvant radiotherapy for high-risk histological features; fertility-sparing progestin therapy an option in carefully selected young women with early, low-grade disease wishing to preserve fertility; monitor for recurrence with clinical follow-up; postmenopausal bleeding should always prompt urgent endometrial assessment given this diagnosis.
    • Preterm pre-eclampsia with severe features: Expedited delivery balanced against fetal maturity is first-line once severe features develop, with corticosteroids given if time allows; maternal stabilisation (blood pressure control, magnesium sulfate) takes priority over prolonging pregnancy; monitor maternal end-organ function (renal, hepatic, haematological) and fetal wellbeing closely, as deterioration can be rapid.
    • Molar pregnancy: Suction curettage evacuation is first-line, with tissue sent for histology to confirm diagnosis and exclude choriocarcinoma; serial beta-hCG monitoring post-evacuation until normalisation and beyond, given risk of persistent gestational trophoblastic disease; escalate to chemotherapy if beta-hCG plateaus or rises; effective contraception is advised during the monitoring period to avoid confounding a new pregnancy with persistent disease.
    • Vaginal candidiasis: Topical or oral antifungal therapy (azoles) is first-line; escalate to longer courses for recurrent disease (4 or more episodes/year), with maintenance suppressive therapy considered; monitor for underlying predisposing factors (diabetes, immunosuppression, recent antibiotics) in recurrent cases; treatment of asymptomatic colonisation is not routinely recommended.
    • Hyperemesis gravidarum: IV fluids and electrolyte correction plus antiemetics (first-line agents such as cyclizine/promethazine, escalating to ondansetron or corticosteroids for refractory cases) first-line; thiamine supplementation before/alongside dextrose-containing fluids to prevent Wernicke's encephalopathy; monitor weight, ketones, and electrolytes; consider enteral or parenteral nutrition in severe, prolonged cases with significant weight loss.
    • Recurrent pregnancy loss: Systematic investigation for underlying cause (antiphospholipid syndrome, parental karyotype, uterine anomaly, thrombophilia) with targeted therapy where identified (e.g. aspirin/heparin for antiphospholipid syndrome); monitor closely in subsequent pregnancies with early reassurance scans; a significant proportion remain unexplained despite full work-up, and supportive care/psychological support is an important component of management.
    • Cervical incompetence: Cervical cerclage in a subsequent pregnancy (typically placed around 12-14 weeks) is first-line for women with a history of painless second-trimester loss consistent with cervical insufficiency; progesterone supplementation as an adjunct in high-risk pregnancies; monitor cervical length by transvaginal ultrasound serially where cerclage is not placed prophylactically, allowing rescue cerclage if shortening is detected.
    • Intrahepatic cholestasis of pregnancy: Ursodeoxycholic acid for symptom relief and to lower bile acid levels is first-line, alongside increased fetal surveillance; consideration of early delivery (around 37-38 weeks, earlier if bile acids very high) given increased stillbirth risk; monitor liver function tests and bile acid levels serially; pruritus typically resolves rapidly post-delivery, confirming the diagnosis retrospectively.
    • Amniotic fluid embolism: Immediate resuscitation (airway, oxygen, circulatory support with fluids/vasopressors, correction of coagulopathy with blood products) is first-line in this obstetric emergency; escalate to ICU-level supportive care, including consideration of ECMO in refractory cardiovascular collapse; monitor closely for disseminated intravascular coagulation, which is almost universal; perimortem caesarean section should be considered without delay if maternal cardiac arrest occurs and the fetus is viable.
    • Uterine rupture: Emergency laparotomy for delivery and uterine repair (or hysterectomy if repair is not feasible/further childbearing not desired) is first-line and time-critical given risk of catastrophic haemorrhage and fetal death; monitor haemodynamic status closely and resuscitate aggressively in parallel; risk factors (previous caesarean, uterine surgery) should prompt vigilance for scar dehiscence signs in labour.
    • Shoulder dystocia: Systematic manoeuvres (McRoberts positioning, suprapubic pressure, escalating to internal rotational manoeuvres or delivery of the posterior arm) to deliver the anterior shoulder is first-line, performed calmly and in sequence; monitor for neonatal brachial plexus injury and clavicular fracture, and for maternal postpartum haemorrhage/perineal trauma; document the time from head delivery to body delivery and manoeuvres used.
    • Cord prolapse: Immediate manual elevation of the presenting part and emergency caesarean delivery is first-line, as with earlier cord prolapse entries, given the shared time-critical pathophysiology; monitor fetal heart rate continuously during preparation for delivery; consider immediate vaginal delivery only if full dilatation and low station make it faster than caesarean.
    • Twin-to-twin transfusion syndrome: Fetoscopic laser ablation of communicating placental vessels is first-line for severe cases (typically Quintero stage II or above); serial amnioreduction as an alternative or adjunct where laser is unavailable; monitor both twins closely with serial ultrasound (fluid volumes, Doppler, growth) after treatment given ongoing risk of recurrence or single twin demise; refer promptly to a fetal medicine centre once diagnosed given the rapid disease trajectory.
    • Multiple pregnancy (uncomplicated twins): Increased-frequency antenatal surveillance (growth scans, chorionicity-based monitoring) is first-line, with monochorionic twins monitored more intensively than dichorionic given shared placental circulation risks; monitor for twin-specific complications (growth discordance, twin-to-twin transfusion in monochorionic pregnancies) and pre-eclampsia, which is more common in multiple pregnancy; plan delivery timing and mode based on chorionicity and presentation.
    • Rhesus isoimmunisation: Anti-D prophylaxis at sensitising events and routinely in the third trimester is first-line prevention in Rh-negative women; once isoimmunised, serial monitoring of maternal antibody titres and fetal middle cerebral artery Doppler for anaemia, escalating to intrauterine transfusion for significant fetal anaemia; monitor closely in subsequent pregnancies given risk of earlier and more severe disease; neonatal exchange transfusion may be required for severe haemolytic disease of the newborn.
    • Bacterial vaginosis: Oral or vaginal metronidazole (or clindamycin) is first-line; escalate to longer or alternative regimens for recurrent disease; monitor for recurrence, which is common; treat symptomatic pregnant women given an association with preterm birth, though routine screening/treatment of asymptomatic women is not universally recommended; partner treatment is not routinely required.
    • Trichomoniasis: Oral metronidazole (single dose or extended course) is first-line for both the patient and their partner(s), given it is a sexually transmitted infection; monitor for treatment failure and re-infection; screen for other sexually transmitted infections given frequent co-infection; treat in pregnancy given association with preterm birth and low birth weight.
    • Vulvar carcinoma (early): Wide local excision (with sentinel lymph node biopsy or inguinofemoral lymphadenectomy depending on depth of invasion) is first-line for early-stage disease; radiotherapy/chemoradiotherapy for locally advanced or unresectable disease; monitor for local recurrence with regular follow-up examination; associations with lichen sclerosus and HPV should prompt ongoing surveillance of the vulva.
    • Vaginal carcinoma: Radiotherapy or surgery (depending on stage and site) is first-line, with radiotherapy often preferred given anatomical constraints on surgical margins; monitor for local recurrence and treatment-related morbidity (vaginal stenosis); screen for a primary elsewhere (cervix, vulva) as primary vaginal carcinoma is rare and secondary involvement more common.
    • Adenomyosis: Hormonal therapy (levonorgestrel IUD, combined contraceptives, or progestins) first-line for symptom control; hysterectomy for definitive treatment in those who have completed childbearing and have refractory symptoms; monitor haemoglobin given associated menorrhagia; MRI can help distinguish from fibroids and guide conservative surgical options where fertility preservation is desired.
    • Asherman's syndrome: Hysteroscopic adhesiolysis is first-line, followed by measures to prevent re-adhesion (intrauterine balloon/device, oestrogen therapy); monitor menstrual return and fertility outcomes, with repeat hysteroscopy if symptoms recur; counsel on variable fertility outcomes even after successful adhesiolysis depending on adhesion severity.
    • Premature ovarian insufficiency: Hormone replacement therapy is first-line, both for symptom control and to protect long-term bone and cardiovascular health given the prolonged oestrogen-deficient period; fertility options (donor oocyte) discussed for those desiring pregnancy; monitor bone density and cardiovascular risk factors; investigate for underlying cause (autoimmune, genetic, iatrogenic) where relevant.
    • Menorrhagia (heavy menstrual bleeding): Levonorgestrel IUD or tranexamic acid/NSAIDs first-line depending on contraceptive needs; escalate to combined contraceptives or endometrial ablation for refractory symptoms in those who have completed childbearing; hysterectomy reserved for failure of less invasive measures; monitor haemoglobin and investigate for structural causes (fibroids, polyps) or coagulopathy, particularly in adolescents.
    • Dysmenorrhoea (primary): NSAIDs first-line, given their action against prostaglandin-mediated uterine contraction; combined oral contraceptives as an alternative or adjunct; monitor response and reconsider the diagnosis (screening for endometriosis) if symptoms are refractory to standard first-line therapy, as primary dysmenorrhoea should respond well to NSAIDs/hormonal suppression.
    • Female genital prolapse: Pelvic floor physiotherapy and pessary use first-line, particularly for those wishing to avoid or delay surgery; surgical repair (native tissue or mesh-based, per current guidance) for symptomatic prolapse refractory to conservative measures; monitor for pessary-related complications (erosion, discharge) with regular review; recurrence risk after surgical repair should be discussed pre-operatively.
    • Ovarian torsion: Emergency surgical detorsion (with ovarian conservation where the ovary appears viable) is first-line and time-critical to preserve ovarian function; oophorectomy reserved for clearly necrotic tissue; monitor post-operative ovarian function with follow-up ultrasound; a high index of suspicion is needed as clinical presentation can mimic other causes of acute abdomen.
    • Ovarian hyperstimulation syndrome: Supportive management (fluid balance, analgesia, thromboprophylaxis) first-line for mild-moderate disease; escalate to hospital admission, paracentesis for symptomatic ascites, and closer monitoring for severe disease; monitor haematocrit, renal function, and for thromboembolism given haemoconcentration risk; prevention (using a GnRH antagonist protocol and freeze-all strategy in high-risk patients) is key in assisted reproduction settings.
    • Postmenopausal bleeding: Urgent endometrial assessment (transvaginal ultrasound with endometrial biopsy if thickened) is first-line given the need to exclude endometrial carcinoma; hysteroscopy with targeted biopsy if focal pathology (polyp) is suspected; monitor for recurrent bleeding even after benign initial assessment, warranting further investigation; any postmenopausal bleeding should be treated as potential malignancy until proven otherwise.
  14. Paediatrics & Neonatology
    • Neonatal jaundice: Phototherapy guided by bilirubin nomograms (age-in-hours specific) first-line for significant hyperbilirubinaemia; exchange transfusion for severe, rapidly rising, or refractory levels approaching neurotoxic thresholds; monitor bilirubin trend closely (more frequently if rising rapidly or risk factors present) and feeding/hydration status; screen for haemolytic causes (ABO/Rh incompatibility, G6PD deficiency) if jaundice is early, severe, or rapidly progressive.
    • Cerebral palsy: Multidisciplinary rehabilitation (physiotherapy, occupational therapy, orthotics, speech therapy) tailored to the child's functional profile is first-line; escalate to botulinum toxin injections or oral antispasmodics for troublesome spasticity, with selective dorsal rhizotomy or intrathecal baclofen for severe refractory spasticity; monitor growth, hip surveillance (dislocation risk), and seizure control where epilepsy coexists; early intervention improves functional outcomes.
    • Type 1 diabetes mellitus in children: Basal-bolus insulin therapy with structured education for the child and family is first-line; escalate to insulin pump therapy for those with recurrent hypoglycaemia or suboptimal control on injections; monitor HbA1c, growth, and screen annually for microvascular complications and coeliac/thyroid autoimmunity from a defined age; involve the family in carbohydrate counting and sick-day rules to prevent diabetic ketoacidosis.
    • Acute glomerulonephritis in children: Supportive management of fluid balance and blood pressure first-line, with treatment of any underlying streptococcal infection (antibiotics if active infection present); monitor renal function, blood pressure, and urine output closely during the acute phase given risk of hypertensive emergency and fluid overload; most cases resolve fully, but persistent proteinuria/hypertension warrants nephrology follow-up.
    • Complex febrile seizure: Evaluate for an underlying CNS infection or structural cause (lumbar puncture/imaging as indicated by clinical features) first-line, given atypical features (prolonged, focal, or recurrent within 24 hours) raise concern beyond simple febrile seizures; antipyretics and seizure first-aid advice for the family; monitor for recurrence and consider EEG/further work-up if atypical features persist or recur; most children have a good long-term neurological outcome.
    • Congenital anomalies (general): Multidisciplinary assessment and coordinated surgical/medical management depending on the specific anomaly is first-line; monitor growth and development longitudinally, and screen for associated anomalies (cardiac, renal) given clustering in syndromic presentations; genetic counselling for the family regarding recurrence risk where a syndrome or inherited cause is identified.
    • Neonatal jaundice (physiological, significant): Phototherapy guided by bilirubin nomograms is first-line; exchange transfusion for severe/refractory hyperbilirubinaemia approaching exchange thresholds; monitor bilirubin trend and adequacy of feeding, as inadequate intake worsens physiological jaundice; reassure that physiological jaundice typically resolves within 1-2 weeks, with prolonged jaundice beyond this warranting further work-up.
    • Neonatal sepsis: Empirical broad-spectrum IV antibiotics started promptly on suspicion is first-line, given the rapid deterioration risk in neonates; narrow therapy once culture and sensitivity results are available; monitor closely for haemodynamic instability, respiratory distress, and evolving organ dysfunction; a low threshold for treatment is maintained given the difficulty of clinically excluding sepsis in this age group.
    • Respiratory distress syndrome (preterm): Antenatal corticosteroids first-line if preterm birth is anticipated, followed by surfactant therapy and respiratory support (CPAP or ventilation) after delivery; escalate respiratory support as needed while minimising ventilator-induced lung injury; monitor oxygenation, chest X-ray appearance, and for complications (pneumothorax, bronchopulmonary dysplasia) with prolonged ventilation.
    • Necrotising enterocolitis: Bowel rest (stopping enteral feeds) and IV antibiotics first-line for suspected/confirmed disease; surgical intervention (laparotomy or peritoneal drain) for perforation or clinical deterioration despite medical therapy; monitor abdominal exam, serial abdominal X-rays, and inflammatory markers closely; breast milk feeding and standardised feeding protocols reduce incidence in preterm infants.
    • Hypoxic-ischaemic encephalopathy: Therapeutic hypothermia initiated within 6 hours of birth for eligible moderate-severe cases is first-line, as earlier initiation improves neurodevelopmental outcome; supportive intensive care (seizure control, ventilation, multi-organ support) alongside; monitor with continuous EEG/aEEG for seizures (often subclinical) and serial neurological examination; MRI at day 5-14 helps predict long-term prognosis.
    • Patent ductus arteriosus (symptomatic, preterm): Pharmacological closure (indomethacin/ibuprofen/paracetamol) first-line for haemodynamically significant PDA; surgical or catheter-based ligation if medical therapy fails or is contraindicated; monitor for complications of medical therapy (renal impairment, gastrointestinal bleeding) and cardiorespiratory status; many small PDAs close spontaneously, so treatment is reserved for those causing significant compromise.
    • Congenital diaphragmatic hernia: Stabilisation and gentle ventilation (avoiding high pressures that worsen pulmonary hypoplasia) first-line, with surgical repair once the infant is haemodynamically stable, typically after 24-72 hours rather than as an emergency; monitor for persistent pulmonary hypertension, a major determinant of outcome; ECMO may be needed as a bridge in severe cases with inadequate gas exchange.
    • Pyloric stenosis: IV fluid and electrolyte correction (correcting the characteristic hypochloraemic hypokalaemic metabolic alkalosis) first-line before surgery, as anaesthetic risk is high if uncorrected; pyloromyotomy (open or laparoscopic) is definitive once electrolytes are normalised; monitor feeding tolerance and weight gain post-operatively; surgery is not an emergency and should wait for adequate resuscitation.
    • Intussusception: Air or contrast enema reduction is first-line, successful in most cases if performed early; surgery if reduction fails, there are signs of perforation/peritonitis, or a pathological lead point (e.g. Meckel's diverticulum) is suspected, particularly in older children; monitor for recurrence post-reduction (occurs in a minority) and for signs of bowel ischaemia/perforation during the procedure.
    • Croup: Oral dexamethasone is first-line for all severities, as it reduces symptom duration and hospitalisation; nebulised adrenaline for severe stridor at rest, with close monitoring afterward for rebound symptoms as the effect is short-lived; monitor respiratory status and consider admission for persistent severe symptoms or stridor at rest despite treatment.
    • Bronchiolitis (severe): Supportive oxygen and hydration (nasogastric or IV fluids if oral intake inadequate) first-line, as this is a self-limiting viral illness without specific antiviral treatment; escalate to high-flow nasal cannula or CPAP for increasing work of breathing/hypoxia, with ICU/ventilation for impending respiratory failure; monitor feeding, respiratory rate, and oxygen saturation closely, particularly in infants under 3 months or with comorbidities; avoid routine bronchodilators, steroids, or antibiotics, which lack proven benefit.
    • Acute otitis media (paediatric): Analgesia first-line, as many cases resolve spontaneously; antibiotics for young infants (under 6 months), bilateral disease in younger children, otorrhoea, or severe/persistent symptoms beyond 48-72 hours; monitor for treatment failure and complications (mastoiditis) which, though rare, require prompt escalation; recurrent otitis media may warrant ENT referral for grommet insertion.
    • Kawasaki disease: IVIG plus high-dose aspirin, started promptly (ideally within 10 days of fever onset) to reduce coronary aneurysm risk, is first-line; escalate to a second dose of IVIG or corticosteroids for IVIG-resistant disease; monitor with serial echocardiography for coronary artery involvement, extending surveillance long-term if aneurysms develop; low-dose aspirin is continued until platelet count and echocardiogram normalise.
    • Henoch-Schönlein purpura: Supportive care with analgesia is first-line, as the condition is usually self-limiting; corticosteroids for significant abdominal pain or renal involvement; monitor urinalysis and blood pressure for weeks to months after presentation given risk of delayed nephritis; most children recover fully, but a minority progress to chronic kidney disease requiring nephrology follow-up.
    • Nephrotic syndrome (childhood, steroid-sensitive): Oral corticosteroids are first-line for both induction and relapse therapy, with the majority of children responding well; escalate to steroid-sparing agents (calcineurin inhibitors, mycophenolate) for frequent relapsers or steroid dependence; monitor urine protein (dipstick), weight, and blood pressure at home to detect relapse early; screen for steroid side effects with prolonged/frequent courses.
    • Febrile seizure (simple): Reassurance and antipyretics is first-line, as antipyretics do not prevent recurrence but improve comfort; no long-term antiepileptic therapy is required given the excellent prognosis; monitor for recurrence (common but not harmful) and educate families on seizure first-aid; investigate further only if atypical features (focal, prolonged, recurrent within the same illness) are present.
    • Attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder: Behavioural therapy and parent training first-line for young children (under 6); stimulant medication (methylphenidate/amphetamines) for older children with significant functional impairment, alongside behavioural strategies; monitor growth, appetite, sleep, and cardiovascular parameters on stimulant therapy; non-stimulant options (atomoxetine, guanfacine) considered where stimulants are contraindicated or poorly tolerated.
