    • Autism spectrum disorder: Early behavioural and developmental intervention (applied behaviour analysis, speech/occupational therapy) is first-line, with earlier intervention associated with better outcomes; treat comorbid symptoms (irritability, sleep disturbance, anxiety) pharmacologically where significantly impairing; monitor developmental progress and family support needs; screen for co-occurring conditions (epilepsy, intellectual disability, GI symptoms) which are common.
    • Failure to thrive: Identify and treat the underlying cause (organic or non-organic) first-line, with nutritional rehabilitation and growth monitoring as the cornerstone; escalate to enteral feeding support for severe or refractory cases where oral intake alone is insufficient; monitor growth parameters (weight, length, head circumference) serially against standardised charts; involve a multidisciplinary team (dietitian, social work) where psychosocial factors contribute.
    • Acute rheumatic fever: Anti-inflammatory therapy (aspirin for arthritis, corticosteroids for significant carditis) first-line, alongside eradication of the streptococcal infection with penicillin; secondary penicillin prophylaxis (monthly IM benzathine penicillin) is essential and continued for years depending on carditis severity; monitor for evolving valvular disease with echocardiography; recurrence prevention through prophylaxis adherence is the key long-term determinant of outcome.
    • Congenital hypothyroidism: Immediate levothyroxine replacement, started as soon as possible after diagnosis (ideally identified through newborn screening), is first-line to prevent neurodevelopmental impairment; monitor thyroid function closely in the first years of life with dose adjustments as the child grows; delayed treatment beyond the first weeks of life is associated with irreversible cognitive impairment, underscoring the urgency of prompt initiation.
    • Cystic fibrosis (paediatric, new diagnosis): Multidisciplinary care including airway clearance physiotherapy, CFTR modulator therapy (if genotype-eligible), pancreatic enzyme replacement, and nutritional support is first-line from diagnosis; monitor growth, lung function, and sputum microbiology regularly; screen for CF-related diabetes and liver disease as the child grows; early comprehensive care significantly improves long-term prognosis.
    • Transient tachypnoea of the newborn: Supportive oxygen therapy is first-line, as the condition is usually self-limiting within 24-72 hours due to delayed clearance of fetal lung fluid; monitor respiratory rate and oxygen requirement, distinguishing from more serious causes of neonatal respiratory distress if symptoms persist beyond the expected timeframe or worsen.
    • Meconium aspiration syndrome: Respiratory support (oxygen, CPAP, or ventilation as needed) and monitoring for persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn is first-line; escalate to surfactant therapy or inhaled nitric oxide for severe hypoxaemia; monitor oxygenation and consider ECMO for refractory cases; suctioning practices at delivery have evolved and should follow current resuscitation guidelines rather than routine deep suctioning.
    • Persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn: Oxygen and inhaled nitric oxide is first-line to reduce pulmonary vascular resistance; ECMO for refractory cases failing to respond to nitric oxide and optimal ventilation; monitor oxygenation index and echocardiographic estimates of pulmonary pressures closely; treat the underlying cause (meconium aspiration, sepsis, congenital diaphragmatic hernia) concurrently.
    • Neonatal hypoglycaemia: IV dextrose is first-line for symptomatic or severe cases; early feeding for at-risk asymptomatic infants (infants of diabetic mothers, preterm, growth-restricted) as an initial measure before escalating to IV dextrose; monitor blood glucose serially according to risk-based protocols; investigate for persistent/recurrent hypoglycaemia (hyperinsulinism, metabolic disorders) if it does not resolve with standard measures.
    • Neonatal seizures: Identify and treat the underlying cause (hypoxic-ischaemic encephalopathy, metabolic disturbance, infection, structural abnormality) is first-line, alongside antiepileptic therapy (phenobarbitone first-line) for seizure control; monitor with continuous EEG/aEEG given the high rate of subclinical/electrographic-only seizures in neonates; correct treatable metabolic causes (hypoglycaemia, hypocalcaemia) promptly as these can mimic or cause seizures.
    • Congenital hip dislocation (late presenting): Closed or open reduction with spica casting is first-line, with the approach depending on age at diagnosis (closed reduction more successful in younger children, open reduction increasingly needed with delayed presentation); monitor acetabular development and femoral head position with serial imaging post-reduction; avascular necrosis is a recognised complication requiring careful surveillance.
    • Biliary atresia: Early Kasai portoenterostomy (ideally before 8 weeks of age) is first-line, as earlier surgery is associated with better bile drainage and native liver survival; liver transplant if Kasai fails or disease progresses to end-stage liver disease; monitor liver function, growth, and for cholangitis (a common post-Kasai complication) closely; early diagnosis (via conjugated bilirubin screening) is critical given the narrow surgical window.
    • Gastroschisis: Surgical closure (primary if feasible) is first-line; staged silo reduction for cases where primary closure would cause excessive abdominal pressure/compartment syndrome; monitor bowel function and nutrition closely, as prolonged ileus is common; unlike omphalocele, associated anomalies are less frequent, but bowel-related complications (atresia, dysmotility) require ongoing surveillance.
    • Omphalocele: Staged surgical closure is first-line, particularly for large defects where primary closure would compromise ventilation/circulation; assessment for associated anomalies (cardiac, chromosomal, Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome) is essential given frequent co-occurrence; monitor for these associated conditions proactively, as they often determine overall prognosis more than the abdominal wall defect itself.
    • Tracheo-oesophageal fistula: Surgical repair, typically in the neonatal period, is first-line, with staged repair for long-gap variants; monitor for anastomotic leak, stricture, and recurrent fistula post-operatively; screen for associated anomalies (VACTERL association) given frequent clustering; long-term follow-up for oesophageal dysmotility and reflux is often needed.
    • Anorectal malformation: Staged surgical reconstruction depending on the type and level of the malformation is first-line, often beginning with a colostomy in high/intermediate lesions followed by definitive pull-through surgery; monitor bowel and urinary function long-term, as continence outcomes vary with malformation complexity; screen for associated anomalies (spinal, renal, cardiac) given frequent co-occurrence.
    • Hirschsprung's disease: Surgical pull-through procedure (removing the aganglionic segment) is first-line definitive treatment, often preceded by decompression (rectal irrigation or colostomy) in the neonatal period if the child presents unwell; monitor for post-operative enterocolitis, a significant cause of morbidity, and for long-term bowel function/continence; a rectal biopsy confirming absent ganglion cells is required for diagnosis before surgery.
    • Congenital heart disease (duct-dependent): Prostaglandin E1 infusion to maintain ductal patency is first-line immediately on diagnosis/suspicion, as ductal closure can cause rapid haemodynamic collapse; definitive surgical or catheter-based repair once stabilised; monitor oxygen saturation, perfusion, and for apnoea (a recognised prostaglandin side effect); antenatal diagnosis allows planned delivery at a cardiac centre, improving outcomes.
    • Down syndrome with duodenal atresia: Surgical duodenoduodenostomy is first-line definitive treatment, usually performed once the infant is stabilised (nasogastric decompression, IV fluids pre-operatively); monitor for associated cardiac defects, which are common in Down syndrome and should be assessed with echocardiography before surgery where feasible; long-term follow-up addresses the broader Down syndrome care plan (thyroid, hearing, developmental surveillance).
    • Sudden infant death syndrome (risk reduction): Safe sleep counselling (supine sleeping position, firm sleep surface, room-sharing without bed-sharing, avoiding overheating and soft bedding) is first-line prevention, delivered proactively to all families; monitor and reinforce these messages at each well-child visit; smoking cessation counselling for parents further reduces risk, as maternal smoking is a major modifiable risk factor.
    • Breath-holding spells: Reassurance and parental education is first-line, as the condition is benign and self-limiting, typically resolving by school age; screen for and treat iron deficiency anaemia, which is associated with breath-holding spells and treatment of which can reduce frequency; monitor for atypical features (true seizure activity, cardiac cause) if episodes are prolonged or associated with abnormal movements.
    • Enuresis (nocturnal, primary): Reassurance, motivational therapy, and bedwetting alarms are first-line, particularly in younger children where spontaneous resolution is common; desmopressin as pharmacological adjunct for short-term control (e.g. for sleepovers) or when alarm therapy fails; monitor for underlying constipation (a common contributor) and treat concurrently; investigate further if daytime symptoms or secondary enuresis (after a dry period) are present.
    • Constipation (functional, paediatric): Dietary modification (fibre, fluid) and osmotic laxatives (e.g. macrogol) first-line, often requiring a disimpaction regimen before maintenance therapy; monitor for withholding behaviours and soiling (overflow incontinence), which respond to adequate laxative dosing and behavioural support; maintenance therapy is often needed for months and should not be stopped too early, as premature discontinuation risks relapse.
    • Coeliac disease (paediatric): Strict lifelong gluten-free diet is first-line and definitive treatment once diagnosis is confirmed (serology plus duodenal biopsy per current criteria); monitor growth, symptom resolution, and coeliac serology to confirm dietary adherence; screen at-risk groups (type 1 diabetes, Down syndrome, first-degree relatives) periodically given increased prevalence; involve a dietitian for long-term dietary support.
    • Type 1 diabetes (new diagnosis, paediatric): Insulin therapy (basal-bolus regimen) started promptly on diagnosis is first-line, with urgent assessment for diabetic ketoacidosis requiring IV fluid and insulin protocols if present; structured diabetes education for the family is essential from diagnosis; monitor blood glucose, growth, and psychosocial adjustment closely in the early period as the family adapts to a chronic condition.
    • Precocious puberty (central): GnRH agonist therapy is first-line to halt pubertal progression and preserve adult height potential; monitor growth velocity, bone age, and pubertal staging on treatment; investigate for an underlying CNS cause (particularly in younger children or boys, where the yield of finding a structural lesion is higher) with MRI; treatment is typically continued until an age-appropriate time for normal puberty to resume.
    • Growth hormone deficiency: Recombinant growth hormone replacement is first-line once diagnosis is confirmed (auxology plus stimulation testing); monitor growth velocity, bone age, and IGF-1 levels on treatment to guide dosing; investigate for other pituitary hormone deficiencies (panhypopituitarism) and structural causes (pituitary/hypothalamic lesion) with MRI; treatment is most effective when started early and continued through growth completion.
    • Juvenile dermatomyositis: Corticosteroids plus a steroid-sparing agent (methotrexate) is first-line; escalate to IVIG or biologic therapy for refractory disease or significant skin/muscle involvement; monitor muscle strength, skin findings, and for calcinosis (a long-term complication); screen for and manage the risk of interstitial lung disease and, rarely, malignancy association in specific antibody subtypes.
    • Immune thrombocytopenia (childhood): Observation alone is first-line for mild disease without significant bleeding, as most childhood ITP resolves spontaneously; corticosteroids or IVIG for significant bleeding or very low platelet counts; monitor platelet count and bleeding symptoms; escalate to second-line therapy (rituximab, splenectomy) only for chronic, refractory disease with clinically significant bleeding, which is uncommon in children.
    • Acute lymphadenitis (cervical, bacterial): Oral or IV antibiotics (depending on severity) covering typical skin/oral flora (Staphylococcus, Streptococcus) is first-line; drainage for abscess formation not responding to antibiotics alone; monitor clinical response over 48-72 hours, escalating imaging (ultrasound) and treatment if fluctuance develops or symptoms fail to improve; consider atypical causes (mycobacterial, cat-scratch disease) if the presentation is subacute or antibiotic-refractory.
    • Umbilical hernia (paediatric): Observation is first-line, as the large majority resolve spontaneously by 2-4 years of age; surgical repair reserved for hernias persisting beyond this age, those that are large, or complicated by incarceration (rare); monitor for signs of incarceration (irreducibility, pain, skin changes) which would prompt urgent surgical referral; reassure families about the generally benign, self-resolving course.
  15. Psychiatry
    • Generalized anxiety disorder: Cognitive behavioural therapy and/or SSRIs/SNRIs are first-line; escalate to a second agent or combined CBT plus pharmacotherapy for inadequate response; monitor for suicidality in the early weeks of antidepressant treatment, particularly in younger patients; taper pharmacotherapy slowly after sustained remission (typically 12 months or more) given relapse risk with abrupt discontinuation.
    • Post-traumatic stress disorder: Trauma-focused psychotherapy (e.g. CBT or EMDR) is first-line, with SSRIs for pharmacological support particularly where psychotherapy access is limited or comorbid depression is present; monitor for comorbid substance use and suicidality, both common; avoid benzodiazepines as routine treatment given limited efficacy and dependence risk; screen for and address safety in ongoing abusive situations before/alongside trauma-focused work.
    • Bipolar depression: A mood stabiliser or an agent with proven bipolar-depression efficacy (e.g. quetiapine, lamotrigine, lurasidone) is first-line; avoid antidepressant monotherapy given the risk of precipitating mania/mixed states or rapid cycling; monitor mood state closely for emergence of hypomanic/manic symptoms; screen for suicidality, which is elevated in bipolar depression; ensure an adequate mood stabiliser is in place before or alongside any antidepressant use.
    • Acute agitation/violent behaviour: De-escalation techniques are first-line; rapid tranquilisation with medication (oral or IM benzodiazepine/antipsychotic) only if safety is at risk and de-escalation fails; monitor sedation level, respiratory status, and vital signs closely after rapid tranquilisation given risk of over-sedation; identify and treat any underlying organic cause (delirium, intoxication, withdrawal) rather than assuming a primary psychiatric cause.
    • Suicidal patient (acute risk): Immediate safety assessment, removal of means, and admission or close supervision if risk is high is first-line; escalate to involuntary admission per local legal frameworks if the patient lacks capacity or refuses voluntary care and risk is imminent; monitor risk dynamically, as it can fluctuate rapidly; involve family/support systems where appropriate and ensure follow-up is arranged before any discharge, given the elevated risk period immediately post-discharge.
    • Generalised anxiety disorder (chronic, treatment-resistant): Combination CBT plus pharmacotherapy (SSRI/SNRI) is first-line for those failing monotherapy; escalate to augmentation strategies (buspirone, pregabalin) or a different antidepressant class for refractory cases; monitor functional impairment and comorbid depression, which frequently co-occurs; reassess the diagnosis if response remains poor, considering underlying medical causes of anxiety (thyroid disease, cardiac arrhythmia).
    • Obsessive-compulsive disorder: Cognitive behavioural therapy (exposure and response prevention) is first-line, with SSRIs at higher doses than typically used for depression as pharmacological first-line/adjunct; escalate to clomipramine or augmentation with an antipsychotic for refractory disease; monitor symptom severity with standardised scales and treatment adherence, as exposure-based therapy requires active patient engagement; longer treatment trials (12+ weeks) are often needed before concluding an agent has failed.
    • Social anxiety disorder: Cognitive behavioural therapy and/or SSRIs are first-line; monitor for comorbid substance use (particularly alcohol used for self-medication) and depression; escalate to an SNRI or augmentation strategy for inadequate response to an adequate SSRI trial; screen for avoidant personality traits which may need longer-term psychotherapeutic work beyond symptom-focused CBT.
    • Post-traumatic stress disorder (chronic): Trauma-focused psychotherapy is first-line, with pharmacotherapy (SSRIs) as adjunct, particularly for chronic or treatment-resistant presentations; monitor for treatment-resistant symptoms and comorbid conditions (depression, substance use, chronic pain) that commonly accompany chronic PTSD; escalate to specialist trauma services for complex or treatment-refractory presentations, particularly with a history of prolonged or repeated trauma.
    • Alcohol withdrawal syndrome: Benzodiazepine-based symptom-triggered therapy (using a validated withdrawal scale) is first-line, with thiamine given before/alongside glucose to prevent Wernicke's encephalopathy; escalate to fixed-dose or higher-dose benzodiazepine regimens for severe withdrawal or seizure history; monitor vital signs and withdrawal scores frequently in the acute phase; screen for and correct electrolyte abnormalities (magnesium, potassium) which can complicate withdrawal.
    • Delirium tremens: IV benzodiazepines (often high-dose, symptom-triggered) is first-line, with supportive care (fluid, electrolyte correction) and thiamine replacement given concurrently; escalate to ICU-level care and consider adjunctive agents (phenobarbitone, propofol) for refractory cases requiring intubation; monitor for autonomic instability, seizures, and hyperthermia closely given the significant mortality risk of untreated delirium tremens.
    • Anorexia nervosa: Nutritional rehabilitation and psychotherapy (family-based therapy in adolescents, CBT in adults) is first-line; escalate to inpatient/day-programme care for medical instability (bradycardia, electrolyte disturbance, rapid weight loss) or failure of outpatient treatment; monitor for refeeding syndrome closely when reintroducing nutrition after prolonged starvation, with gradual caloric increase and electrolyte/phosphate monitoring; involve a multidisciplinary team (medical, dietetic, psychiatric) throughout.
    • Bulimia nervosa: Cognitive behavioural therapy is first-line; SSRIs (particularly fluoxetine, at higher doses than for depression) as adjunct or for those declining/not responding to psychotherapy alone; monitor electrolytes (particularly potassium) given purging-related risk of hypokalaemia and cardiac arrhythmia; screen for dental erosion and oesophageal complications from recurrent vomiting, and address comorbid mood/anxiety disorders which are common.
    • Insomnia disorder: Cognitive behavioural therapy for insomnia is first-line, with superior long-term efficacy to pharmacotherapy; short-term pharmacotherapy (Z-drugs, or low-dose sedating antidepressants) as adjunct for acute exacerbations, avoiding long-term use given dependence and tolerance risk; monitor sleep patterns (sleep diary) and screen for underlying contributors (sleep apnoea, mood disorder, substance use) rather than treating insomnia in isolation.
    • Adjustment disorder: Supportive psychotherapy, generally time-limited, is first-line, focused on adaptive coping with the identified stressor; monitor for evolution into a more persistent mood or anxiety disorder if symptoms do not resolve within the expected timeframe (typically within 6 months of stressor resolution); pharmacotherapy is not routinely first-line but may be considered if symptoms are severe or the diagnosis evolves.
    • Delirium (acute confusional state): Identify and treat the underlying cause (infection, metabolic disturbance, medication, withdrawal) is first-line, alongside reorientation strategies and minimal use of sedatives; escalate to low-dose antipsychotic only if severe agitation poses safety risk and non-pharmacological measures fail; monitor cognitive status serially (e.g. CAM) and avoid benzodiazepines except in withdrawal-related delirium, as they can worsen confusion in other causes.
    • Catatonia: A benzodiazepine trial (typically lorazepam) is first-line, often producing rapid dramatic improvement that is also diagnostically confirmatory; electroconvulsive therapy for refractory cases or when rapid resolution is needed (e.g. malignant catatonia with autonomic instability); monitor for malignant catatonia features (fever, autonomic instability, rigidity) which constitute a medical emergency; identify and treat the underlying psychiatric or medical cause once stabilised.
    • Conduct disorder: Multimodal behavioural and family-based intervention (parent management training, multisystemic therapy) is first-line; pharmacotherapy targeted at specific comorbid symptoms (e.g. stimulants for comorbid ADHD, or mood stabilisers for severe aggression) as adjunct rather than primary treatment; monitor for evolution into antisocial personality patterns and screen for comorbid substance use, learning difficulties, and trauma history which often underlie presentations.
    • Substance use disorder (opioid): Opioid agonist therapy (methadone or buprenorphine) combined with psychosocial support is first-line, with strong evidence for reducing overdose mortality and illicit use; monitor for treatment retention and concurrent use of other substances (benzodiazepines, alcohol) which increase overdose risk when combined with opioid agonist therapy; provide naloxone and overdose education to patients and their contacts regardless of treatment engagement.
    • Gambling disorder: Cognitive behavioural therapy and support-group involvement (e.g. Gamblers Anonymous) is first-line; monitor for comorbid substance use and mood disorders, which frequently co-occur; screen for financial and relationship harms, and consider referral to financial counselling/legal support alongside psychological treatment given the practical consequences that often drive presentation.
    • Schizoaffective disorder: Combination antipsychotic and mood-stabilising/antidepressant therapy, tailored to the predominant mood component (manic or depressive), is first-line; monitor for metabolic side effects of antipsychotics (weight, glucose, lipids) with regular screening; escalate to clozapine for treatment-resistant psychotic symptoms; long-term maintenance therapy is usually required given the chronic, relapsing course.
    • Delusional disorder: Antipsychotic therapy is first-line, though engagement can be challenging given limited insight typical of this condition; build a therapeutic alliance gradually rather than confronting the delusion directly, which often worsens engagement; monitor functional impact, as many patients maintain relatively preserved functioning outside the delusional system; assess for risk to self or others related to the specific delusional content (e.g. persecutory beliefs).
    • Body dysmorphic disorder: Cognitive behavioural therapy and/or SSRIs at higher doses (similar to OCD dosing) is first-line; monitor for suicidality, which is notably elevated in this condition; screen for and discourage cosmetic/surgical procedures sought to address the perceived defect, as these rarely improve symptoms and may worsen distress; address comorbid depression and social withdrawal which commonly accompany the disorder.
    • Hoarding disorder: Specialised cognitive behavioural therapy targeting hoarding-specific behaviours (acquiring, discarding, organising) is first-line, distinct from standard OCD-focused CBT; monitor safety and functional impact of the home environment (fire risk, sanitation, fall hazards), particularly in elderly patients; involve family/community services where safety risks are significant; insight is often limited, requiring a patient, non-confrontational approach.
    • Trichotillomania: Habit reversal training is first-line behavioural therapy; monitor for comorbid anxiety and for medical complications (trichobezoar if hair-pulling is accompanied by hair-eating); pharmacotherapy has limited evidence but N-acetylcysteine or SSRIs are sometimes trialled for refractory cases; address underlying stress/anxiety triggers alongside the behavioural intervention.
    • Dissociative identity disorder: Long-term trauma-focused psychotherapy is first-line, working toward integration or cooperative functioning between identity states; monitor for self-harm and suicidality, which are common given the typically severe trauma history; ensure safety and stabilisation are established before deeper trauma processing work, as premature exploration can be destabilising; screen for comorbid PTSD and complex trauma presentations.
    • Depersonalisation-derealisation disorder: Psychotherapy addressing underlying anxiety/trauma is first-line; SSRIs as adjunct, though evidence is modest; monitor for comorbid anxiety and depression, which are common drivers/co-occurring conditions; reassure patients that the experience, while distressing, is not indicative of psychosis, as this reassurance itself can reduce secondary anxiety about the symptoms.
    • Conversion disorder (functional neurological symptom disorder): Multidisciplinary rehabilitation with physiotherapy and psychological therapy is first-line, alongside clear, non-judgemental communication of the diagnosis as a genuine condition rather than "faking"; monitor functional recovery with a rehabilitative rather than purely psychiatric framing; avoid unnecessary repeated investigations once organic causes have been reasonably excluded, as this can reinforce illness anxiety and delay rehabilitation engagement.
    • Factitious disorder: Non-confrontational psychiatric engagement, focusing on underlying psychological needs rather than direct confrontation about symptom fabrication, is first-line; monitor for harm from unnecessary medical procedures/investigations sought by the patient; involve a consistent care team and avoid excessive investigation to reduce iatrogenic harm; distinguish carefully from malingering, where external incentive (rather than psychological need) drives symptom fabrication.
    • Postpartum depression: Psychotherapy and/or antidepressants (selecting agents compatible with breastfeeding where relevant) is first-line, with attention to mother-infant bonding and safety; monitor for suicidal ideation and, importantly, any thoughts of harm toward the infant, which require urgent escalation; involve partners/family in support and screen using validated tools (e.g. Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale) at routine postnatal contacts.
    • Postpartum psychosis: Urgent psychiatric admission (mother-baby unit where available) is first-line given the high acuity and risk to both mother and infant; antipsychotic and/or mood-stabilising therapy alongside close monitoring; monitor closely for rapid symptom evolution and risk of harm to self or infant, as this is a psychiatric emergency; involve family and child protection services as appropriate to ensure infant safety during the acute episode.
    • Seasonal affective disorder: Light therapy is first-line, typically administered in the morning; antidepressants for more severe presentations or inadequate response to light therapy alone; monitor mood seasonally and consider prophylactic treatment initiation before the anticipated seasonal onset in patients with a clear recurrent pattern; screen for bipolar spectrum features, as seasonal mood episodes can occur in bipolar disorder.
    • Premenstrual dysphoric disorder: SSRIs (continuous or luteal-phase-only dosing) are first-line pharmacotherapy, with luteal-phase dosing offering similar efficacy with less cumulative exposure; combined oral contraceptives as an alternative/adjunct; monitor symptom timing with a prospective symptom diary across at least two cycles to confirm the diagnosis before committing to long-term treatment, as retrospective recall is unreliable.
    • Nicotine dependence: Behavioural support combined with nicotine replacement therapy or varenicline/bupropion is first-line; monitor quit attempts and relapse, providing repeated brief interventions as relapse is common and does not preclude eventual success; screen for and address comorbid mental health conditions, which are more prevalent among smokers and can complicate quit attempts if untreated.
    • Cannabis use disorder: Psychosocial interventions (motivational interviewing, CBT) are the mainstay, as no approved pharmacotherapy exists; monitor for cannabis-induced psychosis or exacerbation of underlying psychotic/mood disorders, particularly with high-potency products; screen for and address comorbid conditions, and provide education on risks of early-onset heavy use, particularly regarding psychosis risk in vulnerable individuals.
    • Stimulant use disorder: Psychosocial treatment (CBT, contingency management) is the mainstay, as no approved pharmacotherapy exists; manage acute complications (cardiovascular events, psychosis) symptomatically as they arise; monitor for stimulant-induced psychosis, which usually resolves with abstinence but may require short-term antipsychotic treatment; screen for cardiovascular risk given stimulant-related cardiac strain.
    • Sedative-hypnotic withdrawal: Gradual tapering of the sedative agent (using an equivalent long-acting benzodiazepine if needed) is first-line to avoid withdrawal seizures, which can occur with abrupt cessation particularly of high-dose or long-term use; monitor for withdrawal symptoms and seizure risk closely during the taper; inpatient management is warranted for high-dose dependence or history of severe withdrawal/seizures.
    • Intellectual disability (management of comorbidities): Individualised educational and behavioural support is first-line, with treatment of comorbid psychiatric/medical conditions (anxiety, depression, epilepsy) tailored to the individual's communication and cognitive level; monitor for underrecognised pain or distress presenting as behavioural change, as communication limitations can mask the underlying cause; involve family/carers closely in assessment and care planning given their detailed knowledge of the individual's baseline.
    • Tourette syndrome: Behavioural therapy (habit reversal training/comprehensive behavioural intervention for tics) is first-line; alpha-agonists (clonidine/guanfacine) or antipsychotics for significant tic burden not responding to behavioural therapy; monitor tic severity and functional/social impact rather than treating mild tics that do not significantly impair the child; screen for and address common comorbidities (ADHD, OCD) which often drive more functional impairment than the tics themselves.
    • Selective mutism: Behavioural therapy with gradual exposure is first-line, involving close collaboration with family and school to create a consistent, low-pressure approach to speaking situations; monitor progress across settings (home, school, community) as generalisation can be slow; screen for underlying social anxiety, which frequently coexists, and consider SSRIs as adjunct for more severe or refractory cases alongside behavioural intervention.
  16. Anaesthesia & Perioperative
    • Failed airway: Follow a structured failed-airway algorithm first-line, escalating stepwise from optimised laryngoscopy to a supraglottic device, then to front-of-neck access (surgical/cannula cricothyroidotomy) if oxygenation cannot be achieved; monitor oxygen saturation continuously throughout and declare "can't intubate, can't oxygenate" early rather than persisting with repeated intubation attempts; debrief and document fully afterward, and communicate the difficult airway to the patient and future anaesthetists.
    • Contrast reaction (general): Stop the infusion immediately and assess severity is first-line; treat mild reactions symptomatically with antihistamines, escalate to corticosteroids for moderate reactions, and give IM adrenaline promptly for anaphylaxis; monitor airway, breathing, and circulation closely for at least several hours after a significant reaction given risk of biphasic recurrence; document the reaction clearly and consider pre-medication or an alternative contrast agent for future imaging.
    • Aspiration during anaesthesia: Suction the airway and position head-down/lateral immediately is first-line, with respiratory support (oxygen, ventilation) as needed; escalate to bronchoscopy for particulate aspiration causing airway obstruction; antibiotics only if pneumonia develops rather than as routine prophylaxis; monitor oxygenation and chest X-ray for 24-48 hours given delayed chemical pneumonitis can evolve after the initial event.
    • Postoperative ileus: Bowel rest, nasogastric decompression if significantly distended, early mobilisation, and correction of electrolyte disturbance (particularly potassium) are first-line; escalate to imaging (to exclude mechanical obstruction) if ileus is prolonged beyond the expected 3-5 days; monitor bowel sounds, distension, and tolerance of oral intake, reintroducing feeding gradually as function returns; minimise opioid use where possible given its contribution to ileus.
    • Cancer-related chronic pain: WHO analgesic ladder approach first-line, starting with non-opioids and escalating stepwise through weak then strong opioids with adjuvant agents (neuropathic agents, corticosteroids for bone/nerve pain) as needed for adequate control; monitor pain scores regularly and titrate proactively rather than reactively; screen for opioid side effects (constipation, sedation) and co-prescribe a laxative routinely; reassess for disease progression if pain escalates unexpectedly.
    • Postoperative nausea and vomiting: Prophylactic antiemetics (e.g. ondansetron, dexamethasone, combined for high-risk patients) for at-risk patients (identified by validated risk scores) is first-line; rescue antiemetic therapy from a different class if prophylaxis fails or was not given; monitor hydration and electrolyte status with persistent vomiting; minimise opioid and volatile anaesthetic use in high-risk patients where feasible as part of a multimodal prevention strategy.
    • Difficult airway (anticipated): Awake fibreoptic intubation or a planned structured approach with backup airway equipment immediately available is first-line once a difficult airway is anticipated on assessment; escalate to a surgical airway plan discussed in advance if predicted difficulty is severe; monitor oxygenation continuously during any airway attempt and have a clear, rehearsed plan B/C before induction; communicate the anticipated difficulty to the full team and document findings for future anaesthetic encounters.
    • Postoperative hypoxaemia: Supplemental oxygen and identification of the cause (atelectasis, aspiration, opioid-induced hypoventilation, pulmonary oedema, pulmonary embolism) is first-line; escalate to non-invasive or invasive ventilatory support if hypoxaemia is severe or worsening; monitor respiratory rate, oxygen saturation, and work of breathing closely in the early postoperative period, particularly in high-risk patients (obesity, sleep apnoea, opioid-sparing not achieved); investigate promptly rather than simply increasing oxygen if the cause is unclear.
    • Postoperative delirium: Non-pharmacological reorientation strategies (consistent staff, orientation cues, sleep-wake cycle preservation, early mobilisation) are first-line; minimal use of sedatives, with low-dose antipsychotics reserved for severe agitation posing safety risk; monitor with a validated delirium screening tool serially, as delirium is frequently under-recognised, particularly the hypoactive subtype; identify and correct precipitants (pain, urinary retention, constipation, infection, medication side effects) actively rather than treating agitation alone.
    • Epidural haematoma (post-neuraxial): Emergency MRI and surgical decompression is first-line and time-critical, as delay beyond 8-12 hours from symptom onset significantly worsens neurological recovery; monitor neurological status (motor/sensory function, bladder function) closely after any neuraxial procedure, with a low threshold for imaging if new or worsening deficit, back pain, or bladder/bowel dysfunction develops; risk is higher with anticoagulation, and timing of neuraxial procedures relative to anticoagulant dosing should follow established safety intervals.
    • Local anaesthetic toxicity (mild): Stop the injection immediately and provide supportive monitoring is first-line; prepare lipid emulsion therapy proactively in case of progression to severe toxicity (seizures, cardiovascular collapse); monitor for evolving neurological (perioral tingling, tinnitus, agitation) and cardiovascular symptoms closely, as mild toxicity can progress rapidly; ensure lipid emulsion and resuscitation equipment are readily available wherever significant volumes of local anaesthetic are used.
    • Postoperative wound infection: Wound care with drainage of collections is first-line for superficial infection; antibiotics guided by culture for deeper or systemic infection, escalating to surgical debridement for necrotising infection or failure to improve; monitor wound appearance, systemic signs of sepsis, and inflammatory markers; risk factors (diabetes, obesity, smoking, immunosuppression) should prompt closer surveillance and optimisation where modifiable.
    • Perioperative myocardial infarction: Standard ACS management (antiplatelet therapy, anticoagulation, urgent revascularisation as indicated) adapted for the perioperative bleeding risk is first-line, with cardiology input essential given the competing risks of surgical bleeding versus ischaemia; monitor troponin and ECG serially in high-risk surgical patients, as presentation is often silent (masked by analgesia/sedation); balance antithrombotic intensity carefully against the surgical site and timing.
    • Malignant hyperthermia (established): Immediate dantrolene administration is first-line and life-saving, alongside stopping all triggering agents and active cooling; escalate supportive care (hyperkalaemia correction, arrhythmia management, renal protection from myoglobinuria) in parallel; monitor core temperature, end-tidal CO2, and electrolytes continuously during the acute episode; screen family members for susceptibility once the index case is confirmed, and flag the diagnosis clearly for all future anaesthetics.
    • Awareness under general anaesthesia (risk reduction): Depth-of-anaesthesia monitoring in high-risk cases (e.g. total intravenous anaesthesia, neuromuscular blockade, cardiac surgery) and adequate agent dosing is first-line prevention; monitor for risk factors (previous awareness, difficult airway requiring reduced induction dose, haemodynamic instability limiting anaesthetic depth) proactively; if awareness is reported postoperatively, take it seriously, document thoroughly, debrief the patient, and offer psychological support given the potential for post-traumatic symptoms.
    • Postoperative hypotension: Fluid bolus and identification of the cause (bleeding, sepsis, anaesthetic residual effect, cardiac dysfunction) is first-line; escalate to vasopressor support and targeted treatment of the underlying cause if fluid-refractory; monitor blood pressure trend, urine output, and for concealed bleeding (particularly after abdominal/pelvic/orthopaedic surgery) rather than attributing hypotension to anaesthetic effect alone without adequate reassessment.
    • Perioperative anaphylaxis: Immediate IM/IV adrenaline is first-line and time-critical, alongside stopping the likely triggering agent (commonly neuromuscular blockers, antibiotics, or chlorhexidine) and airway/circulatory support; escalate fluid resuscitation and consider glucagon if the patient is on beta-blockers and refractory to adrenaline; monitor closely for biphasic reactions over the following hours; send serum tryptase at the time of reaction and refer for allergy testing afterward to identify the culprit agent.
    • Postoperative urinary retention: Bladder scan and catheterisation if significant retention is first-line; monitor for recurrent retention after catheter removal, particularly in older men or those with pre-existing prostatic disease, opioid use, or neuraxial anaesthesia (all of which increase risk); consider a trial without catheter with a defined plan for recatheterisation if retention recurs, and review opioid dosing as a contributing factor.
    • Postoperative atelectasis: Incentive spirometry, early mobilisation, and adequate analgesia to allow deep breathing and coughing are first-line, particularly after upper abdominal or thoracic surgery; escalate to chest physiotherapy or non-invasive respiratory support for more significant atelectasis with hypoxaemia; monitor respiratory rate and oxygen saturation, and encourage sitting upright rather than supine positioning to aid lung expansion.
    • High spinal block (accidental): Immediate airway and haemodynamic support (oxygen, ventilatory support if needed, vasopressors and fluids for hypotension/bradycardia) is first-line until the block recedes; monitor respiratory function and level of consciousness closely, as ascending block can progress to apnoea and loss of consciousness; reassure the patient once stable, as the experience can be frightening, and document the event for future anaesthetic planning.
    • Perioperative hypothermia (prevention): Active warming (forced-air warmers, warmed IV fluids, and warmed theatre environment) throughout the perioperative period is first-line prevention, started before induction rather than only once hypothermia is established; monitor core temperature continuously in longer procedures; hypothermia increases bleeding, wound infection, and cardiac event risk, making proactive prevention more effective than reactive rewarming.
  17. Nephrology
    • Acute kidney injury (pre-renal): IV fluid resuscitation to restore renal perfusion first-line, with removal of nephrotoxic drugs (NSAIDs, ACE-I/ARB temporarily); escalate to renal replacement therapy for refractory hyperkalaemia, acidosis, or fluid overload; monitor urine output, creatinine trend, and volume status closely as over-resuscitation risks pulmonary oedema; in cirrhosis-related hepatorenal physiology, use albumin rather than crystalloid and consider terlipressin.
    • Acute kidney injury (intrinsic, ATN): Supportive care (avoid further nephrotoxins, optimise haemodynamics) as no specific pharmacotherapy reverses established ATN; escalate to renal replacement therapy for refractory hyperkalaemia, acidosis, fluid overload, or uraemic symptoms; monitor daily renal function and electrolytes, as most ATN is reversible over 1-3 weeks; adjust all renally cleared drug doses during the injury and recovery phases.
    • Acute kidney injury with hyperkalaemia: IV calcium gluconate for cardiac membrane stabilisation first-line if ECG changes present, alongside insulin-dextrose and salbutamol to shift potassium intracellularly; escalate to urgent dialysis for refractory or severe hyperkalaemia (>6.5-7 mmol/L) not responding to medical therapy; monitor ECG continuously and repeat potassium after each intervention, since shifting agents are temporising rather than eliminating; avoid potassium-retaining drugs until resolved.
    • Chronic kidney disease (stage 3-4): ACE-I/ARB and SGLT2 inhibitor first-line for proteinuric CKD to slow progression, alongside blood pressure control and management of anaemia/mineral bone disease; escalate to nephrology referral and dialysis-access planning as eGFR approaches stage 5; monitor eGFR, potassium, and proteinuria every 3-6 months; adjust drug dosing for renal clearance and avoid nephrotoxins (NSAIDs, contrast where avoidable).
    • End-stage renal disease: Renal replacement therapy (haemodialysis, peritoneal dialysis, or pre-emptive transplantation) is definitive, with transplantation preferred where feasible given superior survival and quality of life; monitor dialysis adequacy (Kt/V), anaemia, and mineral bone disease markers regularly; manage vascular access complications and cardiovascular risk aggressively, as cardiovascular disease is the leading cause of death; early transplant work-up improves access to pre-emptive transplantation.
    • Nephrotic syndrome (adult, minimal change): High-dose oral corticosteroids first-line, with the majority achieving remission; escalate to calcineurin inhibitor or cyclophosphamide for steroid-resistant or frequently relapsing disease; monitor for remission by urine protein, and for steroid-related complications (glucose, bone density) on prolonged courses; prophylactic anticoagulation is considered in severe hypoalbuminaemia given high thrombosis risk.
    • Focal segmental glomerulosclerosis: High-dose corticosteroids first-line, escalating to calcineurin inhibitors for steroid-resistant disease; consider rituximab or other steroid-sparing agents for relapsing/refractory disease; monitor proteinuria and renal function closely as FSGS has a higher risk of progression to ESRD than minimal change disease; genetic testing is warranted in familial or steroid-resistant paediatric-onset cases as it changes management away from immunosuppression.
    • Rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis: Urgent high-dose IV corticosteroids plus cyclophosphamide or rituximab first-line, as delay risks irreversible renal failure; add plasma exchange for anti-GBM disease or severe ANCA-vasculitis with pulmonary haemorrhage; monitor renal function daily during induction and serial ANCA/anti-GBM titres; renal biopsy should not be delayed as it guides both diagnosis and prognosis.
    • Post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis: Supportive care (fluid/salt restriction, antihypertensives, diuretics for oedema) is first-line, as the condition is generally self-limiting, especially in children; monitor renal function, complement (C3) normalisation, and blood pressure; treat any persistent streptococcal infection with antibiotics; adults have a less favourable prognosis and a lower threshold for renal biopsy if recovery is incomplete.
    • Diabetic nephropathy: ACE-I/ARB and SGLT2 inhibitor first-line for reno-protection regardless of blood pressure, with glycaemic and blood pressure optimisation; add a non-steroidal mineralocorticoid antagonist (finerenone) for residual albuminuria risk; monitor urine albumin-creatinine ratio and eGFR at least annually; avoid combining ACE-I and ARB together due to hyperkalaemia/AKI risk without added benefit.
    • Polycystic kidney disease: Blood pressure control (ACE-I/ARB first-line) and tolvaptan for select patients with rapidly progressive disease to slow cyst growth; escalate to renal replacement therapy/transplant as ESRD develops; monitor renal function, blood pressure, and screen for intracranial aneurysms in those with a family history of aneurysm/SAH; genetic counselling is relevant given autosomal dominant inheritance and screening of at-risk relatives.
    • Renal artery stenosis: Medical therapy (ACE-I/ARB with caution, statin, antiplatelet) is first-line for atherosclerotic disease unless refractory hypertension or declining renal function; angioplasty/stenting is preferred and often curative for fibromuscular dysplasia; monitor renal function and potassium closely after starting ACE-I/ARB given bilateral disease risk of AKI; avoid ACE-I/ARB in bilateral stenosis or stenosis to a solitary functioning kidney.
    • Renal tubular acidosis: Oral alkali replacement (sodium bicarbonate or citrate) is first-line across all types to correct acidosis and prevent bone/growth complications; potassium supplementation or restriction depends on subtype (hypokalaemic type 1/2 vs hyperkalaemic type 4); monitor serum bicarbonate, potassium, and growth in children; identify and treat the underlying cause (autoimmune, drug-induced, obstructive uropathy for type 4).
    • Hyponatraemia (severe, symptomatic): Hypertonic (3%) saline bolus first-line for severe symptomatic hyponatraemia (seizures, coma) to acutely raise sodium; correct no faster than 8-10 mmol/L in 24 hours to avoid osmotic demyelination syndrome; monitor sodium every 2-4 hours during active correction; in chronic hyponatraemia or alcoholism/malnutrition, correct even more cautiously given higher demyelination risk.
    • Hyperkalaemia (severe, ECG changes): IV calcium gluconate immediately for membrane stabilisation given ECG changes, followed by insulin-dextrose and salbutamol to shift potassium intracellularly; escalate to urgent dialysis if refractory or in severe AKI/ESRD; monitor continuous ECG and repeat potassium after each measure; identify and stop causative drugs (ACE-I/ARB, potassium-sparing diuretics, NSAIDs).
    • Metabolic acidosis (severe): Treat the underlying cause (fluid resuscitation for lactic acidosis, insulin for DKA, dialysis for toxin-related acidosis) rather than bicarbonate alone; reserve IV bicarbonate for severe acidaemia (pH <7.1-7.2) or specific indications (severe hyperkalaemia, TCA overdose); monitor arterial blood gas and anion gap trend to guide ongoing therapy; distinguish anion-gap from non-anion-gap causes early, as this directs the diagnostic work-up.
    • Contrast-induced nephropathy (prevention): IV isotonic saline hydration pre- and post-procedure is the primary preventive first-line measure in at-risk patients; minimise contrast volume and use iso-osmolar/low-osmolar agents; monitor renal function 48-96 hours post-procedure in high-risk patients (pre-existing CKD, diabetes, dehydration); hold nephrotoxic drugs (NSAIDs, and consider metformin) periprocedurally in significant renal impairment.
    • Renal transplant rejection (acute): Pulse IV corticosteroids first-line for acute cellular rejection, escalating to T-cell depleting antibody therapy (ATG) for steroid-resistant or severe rejection; antibody-mediated rejection requires plasma exchange, IVIG, and/or rituximab; monitor graft function (creatinine), donor-specific antibodies, and biopsy for classification which determines specific therapy; balance immunosuppression intensification against infection risk.
    • Hepatorenal syndrome: IV albumin plus a splanchnic vasoconstrictor (terlipressin, or noradrenaline in ICU) first-line to improve renal perfusion; renal replacement therapy as a bridge to liver transplant in appropriate candidates, since transplant is the only definitive cure; monitor renal function and haemodynamics closely; exclude other causes of AKI (hypovolaemia, intrinsic renal disease) before diagnosing HRS, as it is a diagnosis of exclusion.
    • Peritoneal dialysis-associated peritonitis: Empirical intraperitoneal antibiotics covering both Gram-positive and Gram-negative organisms first-line, narrowed once culture available; catheter removal is required for refractory infection, fungal peritonitis, or relapsing episodes; monitor dialysate clarity/cell count response at 48-72 hours; fungal peritonitis carries high catheter loss and mortality risk and warrants early surgical/nephrology involvement.
    • Membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis: Treat the underlying cause first (infection, monoclonal gammopathy, complement dysregulation, autoimmune disease), as classification now centres on immune-complex versus complement-mediated disease; immunosuppression (steroids +/- other agents) or complement inhibitors used for complement-mediated (C3 glomerulopathy) disease; monitor complement levels, renal function, and proteinuria; renal biopsy with immunofluorescence/EM is essential to guide subtype-specific therapy.
    • Alport syndrome: ACE-I/ARB first-line to reduce proteinuria and slow progression once microalbuminuria is detected, even before overt renal impairment; genetic counselling given X-linked/autosomal inheritance patterns; monitor renal function, hearing, and ophthalmological findings (lenticonus) as part of the syndromic triad; renal transplant is the definitive treatment for ESRD and is not complicated by disease recurrence in the graft.
    • Thin basement membrane disease: Reassurance and monitoring alone for isolated microscopic haematuria with normal renal function and no proteinuria, as prognosis is generally excellent; add ACE-I/ARB if proteinuria or hypertension develops; monitor renal function and proteinuria periodically, as a minority progress; distinguish from Alport syndrome via family history and genetic testing given differing prognosis and screening implications for relatives.
    • Fabry disease (renal): Enzyme replacement therapy (agalsidase) or oral chaperone therapy (migalastat, in amenable mutations) first-line to slow renal, cardiac, and cerebrovascular disease progression; ACE-I/ARB for proteinuria as adjunctive renoprotection; monitor renal function, proteinuria, cardiac MRI, and neuropathic pain symptoms; genetic counselling and family screening are important given X-linked inheritance.
    • Cast nephropathy (myeloma kidney): Urgent treatment of the underlying myeloma (bortezomib-based regimen) to rapidly reduce free light chains is first-line, alongside aggressive IV hydration; escalate to high-cut-off haemodialysis for severe AKI to remove light chains, particularly if renal recovery potential exists; monitor free light chain levels and renal function closely; avoid nephrotoxins and correct hypercalcaemia, which commonly coexists and worsens renal injury.
    • Analgesic nephropathy: Complete cessation of the causative analgesic (combination NSAID/phenacetin-type analgesics) is the essential first-line step, as continued use drives progression; supportive management of resulting CKD; monitor renal function and screen for urothelial malignancy given increased risk with phenacetin exposure; papillary necrosis may cause obstruction requiring urological intervention.
    • Acute interstitial nephritis (drug-induced): Prompt withdrawal of the causative drug (commonly antibiotics, NSAIDs, PPIs) is first-line and often sufficient for recovery; add corticosteroids for severe or persistent renal impairment despite drug withdrawal; monitor renal function for recovery over days to weeks; a careful drug history and eosinophilia/urinary eosinophils support diagnosis though absence does not exclude it.
    • Uric acid nephropathy: IV hydration and urine alkalinisation first-line, with rasburicase for tumour-lysis-associated hyperuricaemia given rapid and potent uric-acid-lowering effect; allopurinol used for prevention rather than acute treatment; monitor renal function and uric acid levels closely during tumour lysis risk periods; avoid rasburicase in G6PD deficiency due to haemolysis risk.
    • Nephrolithiasis (recurrent, metabolic evaluation): Increased fluid intake and dietary modification (sodium/oxalate restriction, adequate calcium) first-line for prevention after metabolic work-up (24-hour urine studies) identifies risk factors; add thiazides for hypercalciuria or citrate for hypocitraturia/uric acid stones based on stone composition; monitor recurrence with periodic imaging; underlying causes (hyperparathyroidism, RTA, bowel disease) should be specifically treated.
    • Medullary sponge kidney: Conservative management (hydration, treatment of any hypercalciuria/hypocitraturia) is first-line as most cases are asymptomatic or present with recurrent stones/UTI; escalate to standard stone management (lithotripsy, ureteroscopy) for symptomatic calculi; monitor for recurrent UTIs and nephrocalcinosis; renal function is usually preserved and prognosis is generally good.
    • Renal vein thrombosis: Anticoagulation is first-line, particularly in the setting of nephrotic syndrome (which predisposes via urinary antithrombin loss); thrombolysis or thrombectomy reserved for bilateral involvement with acute renal failure; monitor renal function and treat the underlying nephrotic syndrome to reduce ongoing thrombotic risk; screen for pulmonary embolism given high co-occurrence.
    • Cholesterol embolisation syndrome: Supportive management (renal support, blood pressure control, statin therapy) as no specific reversal treatment exists; avoid further vascular procedures/anticoagulation manipulation that may provoke further embolisation, though this must be balanced against the indication for anticoagulation; monitor renal function, skin findings (livedo reticularis, blue toe syndrome), and eosinophilia; typically follows vascular intervention or anticoagulation/thrombolysis in atherosclerotic patients.
    • Anti-GBM disease (Goodpasture syndrome): Urgent plasma exchange plus high-dose corticosteroids and cyclophosphamide is first-line, especially with pulmonary haemorrhage or severe renal failure, as rapid treatment improves renal and pulmonary outcomes; monitor anti-GBM titres, renal function, and for pulmonary haemorrhage; renal biopsy should not be delayed; unlike ANCA vasculitis, relapse is uncommon once treated, so long-term immunosuppression is usually not required after remission.
    • IgA vasculitis nephritis: Supportive care (ACE-I/ARB for proteinuria, blood pressure control) first-line for mild disease; escalate to corticosteroids +/- immunosuppression for crescentic or nephrotic-range proteinuria; monitor renal function and urinalysis, as most paediatric cases resolve while adult-onset carries higher renal risk; screen for other organ involvement (GI, joint, skin) as part of the systemic vasculitis.
    • Renal amyloidosis: Treat the underlying clonal/inflammatory process driving amyloid deposition (chemotherapy for AL amyloidosis, anti-inflammatory control for AA amyloidosis) as the primary first-line strategy; supportive ACE-I/ARB for proteinuria; monitor renal function, proteinuria, and cardiac involvement (which drives prognosis in AL amyloidosis); renal transplant may be considered once the underlying disease is controlled.
    • Bartter syndrome: Potassium and salt supplementation first-line, adding a potassium-sparing diuretic (amiloride/spironolactone) and NSAID (indomethacin) to reduce urinary potassium/prostaglandin-mediated losses; monitor electrolytes closely, particularly in infancy where growth and volume status must be tracked; genetic subtype influences severity and prognosis, with antenatal forms being most severe.
    • Gitelman syndrome: Oral magnesium and potassium supplementation first-line, as hypomagnesaemia often limits potassium correction; add potassium-sparing diuretics for refractory hypokalaemia; monitor electrolytes regularly, as this is generally a lifelong but milder condition than Bartter syndrome; distinguish from diuretic misuse/bulimia via urinary chloride and diuretic screening when the presentation is ambiguous.
    • Nephrogenic diabetes insipidus: Thiazide diuretics paradoxically reduce urine output first-line by inducing mild volume depletion that enhances proximal water reabsorption, combined with low-sodium/low-protein diet; identify and stop the causative drug (commonly lithium) where possible; monitor serum sodium and urine output, and ensure free access to water to prevent dangerous hypernatraemia; amiloride can reduce lithium-induced NDI specifically by blocking lithium entry into collecting duct cells.
    • Dialysis catheter-related bacteraemia: Empirical IV antibiotics covering Gram-positive organisms (including MRSA) plus Gram-negative cover, adjusted once cultures return; catheter removal is generally required for tunnelled catheter infection with severe sepsis, persistent bacteraemia, or fungal infection, though catheter salvage with antibiotic lock therapy may be tried in stable uncomplicated cases; monitor blood cultures for clearance and for metastatic infection (endocarditis, osteomyelitis) with persistent bacteraemia.
    • Renal transplant chronic rejection: Optimisation of maintenance immunosuppression and aggressive control of cardiovascular risk factors first-line, as chronic rejection has limited reversibility; monitor graft function trend, proteinuria, and donor-specific antibodies; biopsy to distinguish chronic antibody-mediated rejection from other causes of graft dysfunction (calcineurin inhibitor toxicity, recurrent disease) as management differs; early referral for re-transplant work-up if graft failure appears likely.
  18. Urology
    • Benign prostatic hyperplasia: Alpha-blocker first-line for symptomatic relief, adding a 5-alpha-reductase inhibitor for larger prostates or to reduce progression risk; escalate to surgery (TURP or newer minimally invasive techniques) for refractory symptoms, recurrent retention, or complications (renal impairment, recurrent haematuria/UTI); monitor PSA and renal function, and review for orthostatic hypotension on alpha-blockers, particularly in the elderly.
    • Acute urinary retention: Immediate urethral (or suprapubic if urethral catheterisation fails) catheterisation is first-line; start an alpha-blocker before a trial without catheter to improve success rate; monitor for post-obstructive diuresis and renal function after decompression; identify and treat the precipitant (BPH, constipation, medications, infection, neurological cause).
    • Testicular torsion: Immediate surgical exploration and detorsion is definitive, as testicular salvage is time-critical (best outcomes within 6h of symptom onset); manual detorsion can be attempted as a temporising measure while awaiting theatre but does not replace surgical fixation; bilateral orchidopexy is performed to prevent future torsion, given anatomical predisposition is often bilateral; monitor for testicular viability at surgery, with orchidectomy if non-viable.
    • Epididymo-orchitis: Empirical antibiotics targeting likely organism (chlamydia/gonorrhoea in young sexually active men, coliforms in older men/those with BPH) first-line, guided by local protocols and STI screening; analgesia and scrotal support as adjuncts; monitor for abscess formation or failure to improve, prompting imaging; testicular torsion must be excluded urgently in any acute scrotal pain given differing time-critical management.
    • Renal/ureteric colic: NSAIDs first-line for analgesia (as effective as opioids with fewer side effects); most stones <5-6mm pass spontaneously with medical expulsive therapy (alpha-blocker); escalate to urgent decompression (stent/nephrostomy) for infected obstructed system (surgical emergency) or refractory pain/renal impairment; monitor for fever suggesting infected obstruction, and stone size/position on imaging to guide need for intervention (lithotripsy, ureteroscopy).
    • Staghorn calculus: Percutaneous nephrolithotomy first-line given large stone burden, sometimes combined with shockwave lithotripsy for residual fragments; treat and prevent recurrent infection, as these stones are often infection-related (struvite); monitor renal function and for recurrence with metabolic stone workup; untreated staghorn calculi carry a significant risk of progressive renal damage and sepsis.
    • Bladder outlet obstruction: Catheterisation for acute decompression first-line, then treat the underlying cause (BPH, stricture, prolapse) definitively; monitor renal function and for post-obstructive diuresis after decompression; urodynamic studies help clarify the obstruction mechanism when the cause is unclear, guiding targeted intervention.
    • Priapism (ischaemic): Immediate aspiration of the corpora plus intracavernosal phenylephrine is first-line, as this is a urological emergency where delay risks permanent erectile dysfunction; escalate to surgical shunting if aspiration/phenylephrine fails; monitor for resolution and treat the underlying cause (sickle cell disease, medications); distinguish from non-ischaemic (high-flow) priapism, which is not an emergency and managed differently.
    • Paraphimosis: Manual reduction with analgesia/lubrication first-line, using techniques to reduce oedema (compression, granulated sugar) if simple reduction fails; escalate to emergency dorsal slit or circumcision if manual reduction is unsuccessful, given risk of glans ischaemia; monitor for recurrence and arrange elective circumcision to prevent it.
    • Fournier's gangrene: Urgent aggressive surgical debridement plus broad-spectrum IV antibiotics is definitive and time-critical, as delay significantly increases mortality; repeat debridement often needed until the infection is controlled; monitor for sepsis/multi-organ dysfunction and diabetes (a common predisposing factor requiring concurrent management); reconstructive surgery is deferred until infection has resolved.
    • Vesicoureteric reflux (paediatric): Prophylactic low-dose antibiotics and observation first-line for lower-grade reflux, as many children outgrow it; escalate to surgical/endoscopic correction for high-grade reflux, breakthrough infections, or renal scarring; monitor for recurrent UTIs and renal scarring with imaging (DMSA scan); most reflux resolves spontaneously with growth, favouring a conservative approach where possible.
    • Neurogenic bladder: Clean intermittent self-catheterisation is first-line for most cases to protect renal function and manage incontinence, alongside anticholinergics/beta-3 agonists for detrusor overactivity; escalate to botulinum toxin injection or surgical reconstruction for refractory cases; monitor renal function, bladder pressures (urodynamics), and for recurrent UTI given high renal complication risk if unmanaged.
    • Stress urinary incontinence: Pelvic floor muscle training first-line, which has good evidence particularly in postpartum women; escalate to surgical options (mid-urethral sling, colposuspension) for refractory symptoms significantly affecting quality of life; monitor symptom severity and impact on quality of life to guide escalation; weight loss and treatment of chronic cough (smoking cessation) are useful adjuncts.
    • Urethral stricture: Urethral dilatation or internal urethrotomy first-line for short simple strictures, though recurrence is common; urethroplasty (open reconstruction) offers higher long-term success for longer/recurrent strictures and is preferred where expertise allows; monitor for recurrence with flow rates/follow-up cystoscopy; intermittent self-dilatation may be used to maintain patency after initial treatment in select cases.
    • Hydrocele: Observation first-line in infants, as most congenital hydroceles resolve spontaneously by age 1-2; surgical repair (hydrocelectomy) for persistent, large, or symptomatic hydroceles, and in adults where secondary causes (tumour, infection) have been excluded; monitor for resolution in infants and exclude an underlying testicular pathology with ultrasound in adult-onset hydrocele.
    • Varicocele (symptomatic/infertility): Observation for asymptomatic varicoceles with normal semen parameters; surgical or radiological (embolisation) repair for pain, testicular atrophy, or infertility with abnormal semen parameters; monitor semen analysis and testicular size/growth in adolescents; repair does not guarantee improved fertility outcomes, and this should be discussed when counselling.
    • Interstitial cystitis/bladder pain syndrome: Patient education, dietary modification, and bladder training first-line, given the chronic relapsing nature of the condition; escalate to oral agents (amitriptyline, pentosan polysulfate) or intravesical therapy for refractory symptoms; monitor symptom severity and quality of life, as objective markers correlate poorly with symptoms; exclude other causes of chronic pelvic/bladder pain (infection, malignancy) before committing to this diagnosis.
    • Renal cell carcinoma (metastatic): Systemic therapy (tyrosine kinase inhibitors combined with immune checkpoint inhibitors) first-line for metastatic disease, representing a major shift from older cytokine-based therapy; cytoreductive nephrectomy considered in select patients with good performance status and oligometastatic disease; monitor treatment response with imaging and for immune-related adverse effects on checkpoint inhibitors; palliative radiotherapy for symptomatic metastases (bone, brain).
    • Urothelial carcinoma (non-muscle-invasive bladder): Transurethral resection of bladder tumour (TURBT) first-line, followed by intravesical therapy (BCG for high-risk, mitomycin for intermediate-risk) to reduce recurrence/progression; monitor with regular cystoscopic surveillance given high recurrence rates; escalate to cystectomy for BCG-refractory high-risk disease or progression to muscle-invasive disease.
    • Penile carcinoma: Organ-preserving surgery (wide local excision, glansectomy) first-line for early localised disease where feasible, with partial/total penectomy for more advanced disease; assess and manage inguinal lymph nodes based on risk stratification, as nodal status is the key prognostic factor; monitor for local and nodal recurrence; delayed presentation (often due to embarrassment) worsens prognosis, so early referral for any persistent penile lesion is important.
    • Peyronie's disease: Observation during the acute inflammatory phase (up to 12-18 months), as spontaneous improvement can occur; intralesional collagenase or traction therapy for the stable phase with bothersome curvature; surgical correction (plication or grafting) for stable, severe deformity causing functional impairment; monitor for progression during the acute phase before considering surgery, which is deferred until stability is reached.
    • Erectile dysfunction: Phosphodiesterase-5 inhibitors first-line, alongside treatment of underlying cardiovascular/metabolic risk factors which often coexist; escalate to intracavernosal injection, vacuum devices, or penile prosthesis for PDE5-inhibitor failure; monitor cardiovascular risk, as ED can be an early marker of systemic vascular disease warranting cardiovascular risk assessment; avoid PDE5 inhibitors with nitrates given risk of severe hypotension.
    • Male infertility (varicocele-related): Varicocelectomy considered for clinically significant varicocele with abnormal semen parameters and otherwise unexplained infertility; assisted reproduction (IVF/ICSI) as an alternative or adjunct, particularly with a female partner factor or severe male factor; monitor semen analysis post-repair, noting improvement can take several months given the sperm production cycle; counsel realistically on the variable evidence for fertility benefit from repair.
    • Cryptorchidism: Orchidopexy (ideally by age 12-18 months) is first-line to optimise fertility potential and allow surveillance, and to reduce (though not eliminate) malignancy risk; hormonal therapy has a limited/lower success rate compared to surgery; monitor for testicular malignancy risk long-term even after successful orchidopexy, with patient education on self-examination; spontaneous descent is unlikely after 6 months of age.
    • Posterior urethral valves: Immediate bladder catheterisation for decompression first-line in the newborn with suspected diagnosis, followed by endoscopic valve ablation once stable; monitor renal function closely, as this is a leading cause of paediatric renal failure if diagnosis/treatment is delayed; long-term surveillance for chronic kidney disease and bladder dysfunction is required even after successful ablation.
    • Pelvi-ureteric junction obstruction: Observation with serial imaging first-line for mild/stable hydronephrosis, particularly if asymptomatic and renal function preserved; pyeloplasty (open, laparoscopic, or robotic) for symptomatic obstruction, deteriorating renal function, or worsening hydronephrosis; monitor differential renal function with nuclear renography to guide the decision to intervene.
    • Urethral trauma (pelvic fracture-associated): Suprapubic catheterisation is first-line if urethral injury is suspected (blood at meatus, inability to void) rather than attempting urethral catheterisation, which risks converting a partial to complete injury; delayed urethroplasty once the patient is stable and pelvic injuries have healed is preferred over immediate primary realignment in most complete disruptions; monitor for stricture formation, a common long-term complication requiring surveillance.
    • Bladder trauma (extraperitoneal rupture): Conservative management with catheter drainage first-line, as most extraperitoneal ruptures heal without surgery; surgical repair reserved for concomitant pelvic fracture fixation, bone fragments in the bladder, or failure of conservative management; monitor with repeat cystogram before catheter removal to confirm healing; intraperitoneal rupture, by contrast, requires surgical repair given peritonitis risk.
    • Renal trauma (blunt, high-grade): Non-operative management with close monitoring first-line for haemodynamically stable patients, even with high-grade injury, given the kidney's capacity to heal; angioembolisation for ongoing bleeding/pseudoaneurysm; surgery (repair or nephrectomy) reserved for haemodynamic instability or failure of conservative/angiographic management; monitor haemoglobin, haemodynamics, and repeat imaging to assess for delayed complications (urinoma, delayed bleeding).
    • Urosepsis (obstructed, infected system): Emergency decompression (nephrostomy or ureteric stent) alongside broad-spectrum IV antibiotics and resuscitation is definitive, as source control is essential and antibiotics alone are inadequate with an obstructed infected system; monitor for septic shock requiring escalation to critical care; treat the underlying obstruction (stone, stricture, tumour) definitively once the acute sepsis has resolved.
    • Chronic pelvic pain syndrome (male): Multimodal management (alpha-blockers, pelvic floor physiotherapy, and pain management strategies) first-line, given the limited efficacy of antibiotics in most cases despite historical practice; a trial of antibiotics is reasonable if an infective component cannot be excluded; monitor symptom scores and quality of life, as objective findings are often absent; psychological support/counselling has a role given the chronic, often relapsing course.
    • Overactive bladder: Bladder training and lifestyle modification (fluid/caffeine reduction) first-line; escalate to antimuscarinics or beta-3 agonists (mirabegron, preferred in the elderly given lower anticholinergic burden) for inadequate response; further escalate to intravesical botulinum toxin or sacral neuromodulation for refractory symptoms; monitor for anticholinergic side effects (dry mouth, constipation, cognitive impact) particularly in older patients.
  19. Critical Care & Emergency
    • Anaphylactic shock: Immediate IM adrenaline (anterolateral thigh) is first-line and should not be delayed for IV access; high-flow oxygen, IV fluids, and removal of the trigger alongside; repeat adrenaline every 5 minutes for inadequate response, escalating to IV adrenaline infusion for refractory shock; monitor for biphasic reaction requiring observation for several hours after resolution; antihistamines/steroids are adjuncts only and must never substitute for adrenaline.
    • Cardiogenic pulmonary oedema: IV diuretics, nitrates, and non-invasive ventilation (CPAP) first-line; inotropic support for hypotensive patients unable to tolerate vasodilators; monitor oxygenation, urine output, and renal function on diuresis; identify and treat the precipitant (ACS, arrhythmia, valve failure), which guides definitive management beyond symptomatic decongestion.
    • Acute respiratory failure (type 1): High-flow oxygen (or non-invasive/high-flow nasal therapy) first-line to correct hypoxaemia; identify and treat the underlying cause (pneumonia, pulmonary oedema, PE, ARDS); escalate to invasive ventilation for failure of non-invasive support or deteriorating respiratory effort; monitor oxygen saturation, work of breathing, and arterial blood gases serially to guide escalation.
    • Acute respiratory failure (type 2): Controlled oxygen therapy (avoiding hyperoxia which worsens hypercapnia via V/Q mismatch and reduced hypoxic drive) plus non-invasive ventilation (BiPAP) first-line, particularly effective in COPD exacerbation; escalate to invasive ventilation for NIV failure or contraindication; monitor arterial CO2 and pH trend, and treat the underlying cause (COPD exacerbation, neuromuscular weakness, sedative overdose).
    • Multi-organ dysfunction syndrome: Source control and treatment of the underlying trigger (sepsis, trauma, pancreatitis) first-line, alongside organ-support therapies (ventilation, vasopressors, renal replacement therapy) tailored to the failing systems; monitor organ function scores (SOFA) serially to track trajectory; early, aggressive resuscitation and source control reduce progression risk; nutritional and glycaemic support are important supportive adjuncts.
    • Massive haemorrhage: Activation of a massive transfusion protocol with balanced blood product ratios (red cells, plasma, platelets) first-line, alongside rapid haemorrhage control (surgical, interventional radiology, or tranexamic acid if within the appropriate window for trauma); monitor coagulation status (including viscoelastic testing where available) to guide targeted product replacement; avoid excessive crystalloid, which worsens coagulopathy and dilution.
    • Burns (major, >20% TBSA): Aggressive fluid resuscitation (formula-guided, e.g. Parkland) titrated to urine output first-line, alongside airway assessment for inhalational injury which may need early intubation; early burns centre transfer and surgical debridement/grafting for full-thickness burns; monitor for compartment syndrome (escharotomy if needed), infection, and evolving fluid requirements; analgesia and nutritional support are critical given high catabolic demand.
