    • Electrical injury: Cardiac monitoring for arrhythmia first-line, as electrical injury can cause immediate or delayed cardiac events; assess for deep tissue/muscle injury (often underestimated from surface appearance) and check creatine kinase/myoglobin for rhabdomyolysis; monitor renal function and urine output given AKI risk from myoglobinuria; high-voltage injury requires more extensive monitoring and a lower threshold for admission than low-voltage domestic injury.
    • Drowning/near-drowning: Airway, breathing, and circulation support with oxygen/ventilation first-line, as hypoxia is the primary insult; observe for at least 4-6 hours even in asymptomatic patients given risk of delayed pulmonary complications; monitor for aspiration pneumonitis, ARDS, and hypothermia; cervical spine precautions if diving/trauma mechanism is possible.
    • Hypothermia (severe): Careful rewarming (active external for moderate, active internal/extracorporeal for severe with cardiac instability) first-line, handling the patient gently to avoid triggering ventricular fibrillation; withhold defibrillation/drugs below a core temperature threshold per ACLS hypothermia algorithm, as they are often ineffective until rewarmed; monitor core temperature and cardiac rhythm continuously; 'not dead until warm and dead' guides prolonged resuscitation efforts in cardiac arrest.
    • Heat exhaustion: Rapid cooling (evaporative/convective methods) and oral or IV rehydration first-line, as the core temperature is not as critically elevated as in heat stroke; monitor for progression to heat stroke (altered mental status, core temp >40°C) requiring more aggressive cooling; electrolyte correction (particularly sodium) as needed; rest and avoidance of further heat exposure until symptoms fully resolve.
    • Carbon monoxide poisoning: High-flow 100% oxygen first-line, which accelerates carboxyhaemoglobin dissociation; hyperbaric oxygen for severe poisoning (loss of consciousness, significant neurological/cardiac features, pregnancy) where available within the appropriate window; monitor carboxyhaemoglobin levels and neurological status, including for delayed neuropsychiatric sequelae; identify and address the source to prevent re-exposure/co-exposure of others.
    • Cyanide poisoning: Hydroxocobalamin (or sodium thiosulfate/nitrites as alternatives) first-line, given promptly on clinical suspicion (severe metabolic acidosis with disproportionately normal oxygen saturation) rather than awaiting confirmatory levels; 100% oxygen and supportive care alongside; monitor lactate (markedly elevated) as a useful marker of severity and response to treatment; consider co-exposure to carbon monoxide in smoke inhalation given overlapping presentations.
    • Tricyclic antidepressant overdose: IV sodium bicarbonate first-line for cardiotoxicity (widened QRS, arrhythmia, hypotension), which both reverses sodium channel blockade and drives serum alkalinisation; avoid class Ia/Ic antiarrhythmics which worsen sodium channel blockade; monitor ECG (QRS width predicts seizure/arrhythmia risk) continuously; benzodiazepines for seizures, and IV lipid emulsion considered for refractory cardiovascular collapse.
    • Paracetamol overdose: N-acetylcysteine first-line, guided by the paracetamol level plotted against a treatment nomogram (or given empirically for staggered/uncertain-time overdoses); activated charcoal if presenting within 1 hour of a significant ingestion; monitor liver function, INR, and renal function serially, as these guide need for liver unit referral; refer for transplant assessment using validated criteria (King's College) if acute liver failure develops.
    • Salicylate poisoning: IV fluids and urinary alkalinisation (sodium bicarbonate) first-line to enhance renal elimination; haemodialysis for severe poisoning (very high levels, renal failure, severe acidosis, altered mental status, pulmonary oedema); monitor salicylate levels serially (can continue rising after ingestion), acid-base status (classically mixed respiratory alkalosis/metabolic acidosis), and glucose; avoid intubation without careful ventilation strategy, as impaired compensatory hyperventilation can precipitate severe acidaemia.
    • Benzodiazepine overdose: Supportive care (airway protection, monitoring) first-line, as isolated benzodiazepine overdose is rarely fatal; flumazenil reversal used cautiously and selectively, avoided in chronic benzodiazepine users or mixed overdose (particularly with tricyclics) given seizure risk from precipitated withdrawal; monitor respiratory status and level of consciousness; co-ingestants (opioids, alcohol) significantly increase risk and change management priorities.
    • Digoxin toxicity: Digoxin-specific antibody fragments (DigiFab) first-line for significant toxicity (life-threatening arrhythmia, severe hyperkalaemia, high levels); correct electrolytes cautiously, avoiding calcium administration for hyperkalaemia in digoxin toxicity given historical concern for worsening cardiac toxicity; monitor ECG for characteristic arrhythmias (bidirectional VT, various blocks) and potassium level, a key severity marker in acute toxicity; renal impairment predisposes to toxicity at therapeutic doses given renal clearance.
    • Beta-blocker overdose: IV glucagon first-line (bypasses beta-receptor blockade to increase cAMP), alongside atropine and inotropic/vasopressor support for refractory bradycardia/hypotension; high-dose insulin-euglycaemia therapy for severe refractory cardiovascular toxicity; monitor ECG, glucose (on insulin therapy), and haemodynamics closely; distinguish from calcium channel blocker overdose where possible, as management overlaps but glucagon is more specific to beta-blockade.
    • Calcium channel blocker overdose: IV calcium first-line to help overcome blockade, alongside high-dose insulin-euglycaemia therapy which is now considered a mainstay for refractory shock; glucagon and vasopressors as adjuncts; monitor glucose and potassium closely on high-dose insulin therapy, and ECG/haemodynamics continuously; lipid emulsion therapy considered for cardiovascular collapse refractory to other measures.
    • Traumatic haemorrhagic shock: Damage control resuscitation (permissive hypotension until haemorrhage control, balanced blood products, minimising crystalloid) first-line, alongside rapid identification and control of the bleeding source (surgical or interventional radiology); tranexamic acid within 3 hours of injury reduces mortality; monitor for the lethal triad (coagulopathy, acidosis, hypothermia) and correct proactively; massive transfusion protocol activation for ongoing significant blood loss.
    • Tension pneumothorax (traumatic): Immediate needle decompression (or finger thoracostomy) is first-line and should not await imaging confirmation in a haemodynamically unstable patient, as this is a clinical diagnosis in extremis; definitive chest tube insertion follows decompression; monitor for re-accumulation and for the underlying injury requiring further management; bilateral tension pneumothorax should be considered in traumatic cardiac arrest with no response to initial measures.
    • Cardiac arrest (shockable rhythm): Immediate defibrillation integrated with high-quality CPR (minimal interruption) is first-line per ACLS algorithm for VF/pulseless VT; adrenaline and amiodarone given per protocol for refractory rhythms; identify and treat reversible causes (H's and T's) throughout; monitor end-tidal CO2 as a marker of CPR quality and return of spontaneous circulation; post-resuscitation care (targeted temperature management, urgent coronary angiography if cardiac cause suspected) follows ROSC.
    • Cardiac arrest (non-shockable rhythm): High-quality CPR with adrenaline every 3-5 minutes is first-line for PEA/asystole, with defibrillation not indicated; aggressively search for and treat reversible causes (H's and T's), as these carry a worse prognosis without identification and correction; monitor end-tidal CO2 and rhythm changes (may convert to a shockable rhythm requiring reassessment); post-resuscitation care follows the same principles as for shockable arrests once ROSC achieved.
    • Anaphylactoid transfusion reaction: Immediate cessation of the transfusion first-line, alongside supportive management as for anaphylaxis (IM adrenaline for significant reactions, antihistamines, fluids); monitor for progression and send the unit/patient sample back to the blood bank for investigation; distinguish from other transfusion reactions (febrile non-haemolytic, acute haemolytic, TRALI) as management and subsequent transfusion precautions differ significantly.
    • Acute mountain sickness: Halt ascent and allow acclimatisation first-line for mild symptoms, with descent for worsening or severe symptoms; acetazolamide for prophylaxis/treatment of mild-moderate symptoms; monitor for progression to high-altitude pulmonary or cerebral oedema (worsening dyspnoea at rest, ataxia, altered mental status), which mandate immediate descent regardless of other measures; graded ascent is the most effective preventive strategy.
    • High-altitude pulmonary oedema: Immediate descent is the definitive treatment and should not be delayed; supplemental oxygen and nifedipine as adjuncts/bridging measures if descent is delayed (e.g. by weather); portable hyperbaric bag as a temporising measure if descent is not immediately possible; monitor oxygen saturation and respiratory status closely; this can be rapidly fatal without prompt descent, so err on the side of early evacuation.
    • High-altitude cerebral oedema: Immediate descent plus dexamethasone is first-line, as this is a life-threatening emergency; supplemental oxygen and portable hyperbaric bag as bridging measures if descent is delayed; monitor neurological status (ataxia, confusion, progressing to coma) closely; this represents a progression of acute mountain sickness and mandates urgent evacuation regardless of other symptoms.
    • Decompression sickness: Immediate 100% oxygen and urgent recompression therapy (hyperbaric oxygen) is definitive, as delay worsens neurological outcomes; IV fluids for haemoconcentration; monitor neurological and joint symptoms closely, and avoid air travel/further altitude exposure until fully resolved; type II (neurological) decompression sickness requires the most urgent recompression given risk of permanent deficit.
    • Envenomation (scorpion sting, severe): Antivenom first-line for severe systemic envenomation (autonomic storm, cardiovascular/respiratory compromise), alongside supportive management of pain and autonomic symptoms; monitor for cardiac (myocarditis, arrhythmia) and pulmonary (oedema) complications, particularly in children who are at higher risk of severe envenomation; local wound care and tetanus status review as adjuncts.
    • Black widow spider envenomation: Analgesia and supportive care first-line, as most cases are self-limiting though painful; antivenom reserved for severe systemic symptoms (significant muscle cramping, autonomic instability) given anaphylaxis risk with antivenom itself; monitor for latrodectism (generalised muscle pain, cramping, autonomic symptoms) evolution over hours; benzodiazepines can help muscle spasm/pain in the interim.
    • Jellyfish sting (severe): Vinegar irrigation (for box jellyfish species) or hot water immersion (for many other species) first-line depending on the likely species, as treatment is species-specific and the wrong measure can worsen envenomation; antivenom for severe box jellyfish envenomation where available; monitor for systemic toxicity (cardiovascular, respiratory) in severe stings, particularly with Chironex/Irukandji syndrome; analgesia and wound care as supportive measures.
    • Methanol poisoning: Fomepizole (or ethanol if fomepizole unavailable) first-line to block alcohol dehydrogenase and prevent toxic metabolite formation; folinic acid as an adjunct to enhance formate metabolism; haemodialysis for severe poisoning (significant acidosis, visual symptoms, high levels, renal failure); monitor anion/osmolar gap, visual symptoms (a hallmark of methanol toxicity), and acid-base status closely.
    • Ethylene glycol poisoning: Fomepizole (or ethanol if unavailable) first-line to block alcohol dehydrogenase; IV calcium and correction of severe hypocalcaemia (from calcium oxalate crystal formation) as needed; haemodialysis for severe poisoning (significant acidosis, renal impairment, high levels); monitor renal function closely given risk of acute kidney injury from oxalate crystal deposition, and anion/osmolar gap trend.
    • Iron overdose (acute): IV desferrioxamine chelation first-line for significant systemic toxicity (based on clinical severity and/or serum iron level); whole bowel irrigation for large ingestions with radiopaque tablets visible on abdominal X-ray; monitor serum iron level, acid-base status, and for the biphasic course (initial GI toxicity, apparent recovery, then delayed shock/hepatic failure) which can mislead if surveillance is stopped too early.
    • Organophosphate poisoning (severe): IV atropine titrated to secretions (not heart rate) first-line, given in escalating doses until secretions dry; add pralidoxime early to reactivate acetylcholinesterase before ageing occurs; decontamination (remove clothing, wash skin) protects staff and patient; monitor for the intermediate syndrome (delayed respiratory muscle weakness) which can occur after apparent initial recovery, requiring close observation and ventilatory support readiness.
    • Lithium toxicity: IV fluids first-line to promote renal excretion (avoiding NSAIDs/ACE-I/diuretics which impair lithium clearance and are common precipitants); haemodialysis for severe toxicity (significant neurological symptoms, very high levels, renal failure, or inability to tolerate fluids); monitor lithium levels serially, as levels can continue rising after ingestion (chronic toxicity) or with slow-release preparations; distinguish acute-on-chronic toxicity, which correlates less well with level and more with clinical severity.
    • Serotonin syndrome: Immediate discontinuation of serotonergic agents first-line, alongside supportive care and benzodiazepines for agitation/muscle rigidity; cyproheptadine (serotonin antagonist) for moderate-severe cases; monitor for hyperthermia and autonomic instability, with active cooling for significant hyperthermia; distinguish from neuroleptic malignant syndrome by the drug history and clinical pattern (hyperreflexia/clonus favouring serotonin syndrome), as management differs.
    • Neuroleptic malignant syndrome (severe): Immediate discontinuation of the causative antipsychotic (or dopaminergic withdrawal management if precipitated by dopamine agonist cessation) first-line, alongside supportive cooling and hydration; dantrolene or bromocriptine for severe cases; monitor creatine kinase, renal function (rhabdomyolysis-related AKI), and temperature closely; benzodiazepines help agitation; this typically evolves over days, distinguishing it from the more rapid-onset serotonin syndrome.
    • Acute mesenteric ischaemia (post-cardiac surgery): Urgent CT angiography and surgical/endovascular revascularisation with resection of non-viable bowel first-line, given very high mortality if diagnosis is delayed in this high-risk post-operative population; maintain a high index of suspicion given atypical/subtle presentation in sedated, post-operative patients; monitor lactate and abdominal examination closely in at-risk patients (low cardiac output, vasopressor use, prolonged bypass); broad-spectrum antibiotics given bacterial translocation risk.
  20. Palliative Care & Pain Medicine
    • Cancer pain (moderate-severe): WHO analgesic ladder with regular (not solely as-needed) opioid titration and adjuvant agents (neuropathic agents, corticosteroids for nerve compression/raised intracranial pressure, bisphosphonates for bone pain) is first-line; escalate opioid dose steadily against response rather than under-dosing out of unfounded dependence concerns; monitor pain scores and side effects (sedation, constipation) regularly; co-prescribe a laxative routinely and reassess the underlying cause if pain pattern changes.
    • Breakthrough pain: Immediate-release opioid rescue dosing (typically 1/6th to 1/10th of the total daily background dose) alongside the regular background analgesic regimen is first-line; escalate the background dose if breakthrough episodes are frequent (more than 3-4 per day) rather than relying solely on rescue doses; monitor timing and triggers of breakthrough pain (incident vs spontaneous) to guide whether pre-emptive dosing before a triggering activity is more appropriate.
    • Opioid-induced constipation: Prophylactic stimulant laxative (e.g. senna) co-prescribed with opioid therapy from initiation is first-line, as constipation does not develop tolerance the way other opioid side effects do; escalate to combination laxatives or a peripherally acting mu-opioid receptor antagonist for refractory cases; monitor bowel habit proactively rather than waiting for the patient to report symptoms, as under-reporting is common.
    • Terminal dyspnoea: Low-dose opioids (titrated for symptom relief rather than respiratory depression) are first-line, with benzodiazepines added where anxiety is a significant contributor, alongside non-pharmacological measures (fan directed at the face, positioning, room ventilation); monitor comfort rather than respiratory rate or oxygen saturation as the primary target in the terminal phase; reassure family that opioid use for comfort in this setting does not hasten death when appropriately titrated.
    • Malignant bowel obstruction (inoperable): Antisecretory (octreotide/hyoscine butylbromide) and antiemetic medication with analgesia is first-line for symptom control where surgery is not appropriate; escalate to a venting gastrostomy for refractory vomiting significantly affecting quality of life; monitor symptom burden and titrate medications for comfort rather than pursuing bowel function restoration, which is often not achievable in this setting; involve the patient and family in goals-of-care discussions given the prognostic implications.
    • Terminal agitation: Anxiolytic/sedative medication (benzodiazepines, or antipsychotics if a delirium component is prominent) titrated for comfort is first-line, with attention to reversible causes (urinary retention, pain, constipation, medication effects) sought and addressed where feasible; monitor comfort and family understanding closely, as terminal agitation is distressing for those present; explain the approach and likely trajectory to family to support their coping.
    • Neuropathic pain: Gabapentinoids (gabapentin/pregabalin), tricyclic antidepressants, or SNRIs (duloxetine) are first-line agents, chosen based on comorbidities and side-effect tolerability; escalate to combination therapy or specialist pain referral for refractory pain; monitor for sedation and, in older patients, falls risk with gabapentinoids/tricyclics; allow an adequate trial period (several weeks) before concluding an agent has failed, as response is often gradual.
    • Complex regional pain syndrome: Multidisciplinary physiotherapy and pain management (with emphasis on early, graded mobilisation and desensitisation) is first-line; neuropathic agents and nerve blocks (sympathetic blocks) as adjuncts for refractory pain; monitor functional progress as the primary outcome measure rather than pain scores alone, as early mobilisation despite pain improves long-term function; early diagnosis and treatment initiation improve prognosis significantly.
    • Chronic non-cancer pain: Multimodal, function-focused management (physiotherapy, psychological therapy, non-opioid analgesics) minimising long-term opioid reliance is first-line, given limited long-term opioid efficacy evidence and dependence risk in this setting; monitor functional outcomes rather than pain scores alone as the primary treatment goal; if opioids are used, set clear functional goals and review regularly, tapering if benefit is not demonstrated.
    • Malignant spinal cord compression (palliative setting): Urgent high-dose corticosteroids and radiotherapy is first-line, started promptly on clinical suspicion given the time-critical nature of neurological outcome; surgical decompression for select patients with good performance status and a single level of compression, particularly with spinal instability; monitor neurological status (motor/sensory level, bladder/bowel function) closely, as this is an oncological emergency requiring same-day assessment and treatment initiation.
    • Cancer-related fatigue: Exercise therapy where tolerated (often counterintuitive to patients but evidence-supported) is first-line, alongside treatment of contributing factors (anaemia, hypothyroidism, sleep disturbance, depression, medication side effects); monitor for reversible contributors that are frequently overlooked as "just part of cancer"; psychostimulants have limited evidence and are reserved for specific refractory cases under specialist guidance.
    • Cachexia (cancer-related): Nutritional support and treatment of reversible contributing factors (pain limiting intake, nausea, mucositis, depression) is first-line; limited role for appetite stimulants (megestrol, corticosteroids for short-term use), which do not reverse the underlying catabolic process; monitor weight and functional status while setting realistic goals with the patient/family, as cachexia in advanced disease often does not fully reverse with nutritional intervention alone.
    • End-of-life secretions (death rattle): Anticholinergic medication (hyoscine hydrobromide/glycopyrronium) and repositioning (lateral position to aid drainage) is first-line; reassurance for family that this is a common, generally non-distressing (to the patient) part of the dying process, as it is often more distressing to observers than the patient; monitor comfort rather than aiming to eliminate the sound entirely, which is often not fully achievable.
    • Opioid-induced neurotoxicity: Opioid rotation (switching to an alternative opioid at a reduced equianalgesic dose) or dose reduction with adequate hydration is first-line for myoclonus, hyperalgesia, or confusion attributed to opioid accumulation; monitor renal function, as neurotoxicity is more common with renal impairment due to accumulation of active metabolites; distinguish from disease progression or other causes of confusion before attributing symptoms solely to opioids.
    • Bone pain (metastatic): Analgesia per the WHO ladder plus palliative radiotherapy (often single-fraction) for localised pain is first-line; escalate to bisphosphonates/denosumab to reduce skeletal-related events and, for widespread bone metastases, consider radioisotope therapy; monitor for pathological fracture risk (Mirels' score) and refer for prophylactic orthopaedic fixation if impending fracture is identified.
    • Existential/spiritual distress (palliative setting): Multidisciplinary psychosocial and spiritual care support alongside symptom management is first-line, recognising that physical symptom control alone does not address this dimension of suffering; monitor for coexisting depression or anxiety, which can present similarly but may benefit from additional targeted treatment; involve chaplaincy/spiritual care and psychology/counselling services proactively rather than only on request, as patients may not raise this distress unprompted.
  21. Forensic & Legal Medicine
    • Suspected physical child abuse: Safeguarding referral is first-line and mandatory once suspicion arises, alongside thorough, contemporaneous documentation of injuries (photography, body maps, injury dating where possible) and a multidisciplinary child protection assessment; escalate to admission for safety and further investigation (skeletal survey, ophthalmological exam) if abuse is significantly suspected; monitor the child's ongoing safety and involve child protection services regardless of parental reassurance, as the duty to report overrides diagnostic certainty.
    • Sexual assault (recent): Forensic examination with informed consent by a trained examiner is first-line, ideally within the recommended forensic window, alongside prophylaxis for sexually transmitted infections and emergency contraception where relevant; psychological support and referral to specialist services is essential alongside the medical/forensic assessment; monitor for delayed disclosure of injuries and psychological sequelae, and ensure the patient retains control over reporting to police, which should not be a precondition for receiving care.
    • Suspected domestic violence: Safety assessment (including a structured risk assessment tool) is first-line, alongside documentation of injuries and referral to support/legal services; escalate to safety planning and, where children are involved, child safeguarding assessment; monitor for escalating severity and recognise that leaving an abusive relationship carries elevated short-term risk, so safety planning should be individualised rather than assuming departure is always the safest immediate step.
    • Death in custody (unexplained): Mandatory medico-legal autopsy and full, independent documentation for inquest is first-line, given the heightened scrutiny and public interest in custodial deaths; escalate to involvement of an independent forensic pathologist rather than the treating institution's own staff to preserve impartiality; monitor chain-of-custody of the body and all evidence meticulously, as procedural integrity is essential for legal proceedings.
    • Suspected poisoning (forensic): Preservation of samples (blood, urine, gastric contents) for toxicological analysis alongside clinical management is first-line, taken before treatment where feasible without delaying life-saving care; escalate to police/forensic toxicology involvement where criminal intent is suspected; monitor and clearly document the clinical course and sample chain-of-custody, as toxicological findings may later be scrutinised in legal proceedings.
    • Fitness to stand trial assessment: Structured psychiatric/psychological evaluation against the relevant legal criteria (understanding of charges, ability to instruct counsel, understanding of court proceedings) is first-line; escalate to a second independent opinion where the assessment is contested or borderline; monitor for fluctuating capacity (e.g. in psychosis or intoxication states) that may require reassessment closer to the trial date rather than relying on a single point-in-time evaluation.
    • Elder abuse (suspected): Safeguarding assessment, thorough documentation, and referral to adult protective services is first-line; escalate to removal from the abusive environment or involvement of legal/guardianship services where the individual lacks capacity to protect themselves; monitor for financial as well as physical/psychological abuse, which frequently co-occurs and is often under-recognised; involve the individual in decision-making to the extent their capacity allows, respecting autonomy alongside protection.
    • Non-accidental injury in infants: Skeletal survey, multidisciplinary safeguarding review, and referral to child protection services is first-line once suspicion arises, given infants cannot self-report and injuries may otherwise go unrecognised; escalate to admission for safety pending assessment; monitor for patterns inconsistent with the reported mechanism (e.g. injuries at different healing stages, injury patterns atypical for developmental stage) which should heighten rather than resolve suspicion.
    • Custodial death investigation: Independent forensic autopsy with full, meticulous chain-of-custody documentation is first-line, mirroring the approach to death in custody given overlapping legal and procedural requirements; escalate to external/independent review bodies where local conflict of interest exists; monitor and preserve all relevant records (medical, custody logs, CCTV where available) promptly before they can be altered or lost.
    • Sexual assault of a minor: Immediate safeguarding referral is first-line and takes priority, alongside forensic examination by specialists trained in paediatric forensic assessment and psychological support tailored to the child's developmental stage; escalate to urgent multidisciplinary child protection team involvement; monitor for ongoing risk to the child (returning to an unsafe environment) and ensure age-appropriate, non-leading interview techniques are used throughout to preserve both welfare and evidential integrity.
    • Human trafficking (suspected victim): Confidential safeguarding assessment conducted separately from any accompanying person (who may be the trafficker) is first-line, followed by referral to specialist support services; escalate to law enforcement involvement only with the victim's informed understanding, as coercive control often makes victims fearful of authorities; monitor for indicators of coercion (accompanying "helper" answering for the patient, inconsistent stories, signs of control) and prioritise the individual's immediate safety and trust-building over information extraction.
  22. Community Medicine & Public Health
    • Outbreak of acute diarrhoeal disease: Case isolation and oral rehydration at the point of care is first-line for individual management, alongside water source investigation and chlorination to control the outbreak; escalate to broader public health response (case reporting, community education) if a common source is identified; monitor case numbers and geographic spread to detect ongoing transmission, and prioritise identifying and closing the source (contaminated water, food) over treating cases individually.
    • Suspected foodborne illness cluster: Epidemiological investigation (case interviews, exposure mapping) and source tracing is first-line, alongside mandatory public health notification; escalate to product recall or premises closure if a specific food source is confirmed; monitor for additional cases during the investigation period, as clusters can grow before the source is identified and controlled.
    • Vaccine-preventable disease case (e.g. measles): Case isolation and contact tracing is first-line, alongside outbreak-response immunisation of susceptible contacts (within the appropriate window post-exposure); escalate to broader community vaccination campaigns if secondary transmission is occurring; monitor vaccination coverage in the affected area and identify under-immunised pockets that allowed transmission, addressing access or hesitancy barriers as part of the response.
    • Malnutrition in under-five children: Community-based management of acute malnutrition with therapeutic feeding (ready-to-use therapeutic food for uncomplicated severe cases) and growth monitoring is first-line; escalate to inpatient stabilisation for complicated severe malnutrition with medical complications (oedema, infection, severe wasting with appetite loss); monitor weight-for-height/MUAC serially and screen for and treat underlying infections, as malnutrition and infection compound each other.
    • Iodine deficiency disorder (endemic goitre): Universal salt iodisation is first-line population-level prevention, with targeted supplementation (iodised oil) in severely affected regions where salt iodisation coverage is incomplete; monitor urinary iodine levels at a population level to assess programme adequacy; screen pregnant women specifically given the severe neurodevelopmental consequences of maternal iodine deficiency on the fetus.
    • Vitamin A deficiency (xerophthalmia): High-dose vitamin A supplementation is first-line treatment for at-risk/affected children, alongside dietary diversification programmes for sustainable prevention; escalate to urgent treatment for corneal involvement (keratomalacia) given risk of irreversible blindness; monitor programme coverage in supplementation campaigns and screen for concurrent measles/diarrhoeal disease, which both deplete and are worsened by vitamin A deficiency.
    • Zoonotic disease exposure (e.g. animal bite): Wound care (thorough washing) and rabies risk assessment is first-line, with post-exposure prophylaxis (vaccine, and immunoglobulin for high-risk exposures) given promptly as indicated; escalate to full post-exposure regimen without delay if the biting animal cannot be observed/tested, given the near-uniform fatality of untreated rabies; monitor the biting animal where possible (10-day observation for dogs/cats) to potentially avoid unnecessary immunoglobulin, but never delay vaccination while awaiting this.
    • Occupational lead poisoning: Removal from exposure is first-line and essential, as continued exposure will undermine any treatment; chelation therapy for significant toxicity (based on blood lead level and symptoms) as an adjunct; workplace safety review and monitoring of coworkers for exposure; monitor blood lead levels serially during and after treatment, and address the exposure source at a systems level rather than treating affected individuals in isolation.
    • Silicosis (occupational): Removal from further silica exposure is first-line, as there is no curative therapy once fibrosis is established; supportive respiratory care (bronchodilators, pulmonary rehabilitation, oxygen if hypoxic) for symptom management; monitor for disease progression and increased tuberculosis risk (silicotuberculosis), screening periodically; workplace dust control and respiratory protection are the key preventive measures for at-risk workers.
    • Antenatal care for a low-risk pregnancy: Scheduled screening visits (per recommended antenatal visit schedule), supplementation (iron/folate, calcium where indicated), and health education is first-line; escalate to increased surveillance if risk factors emerge during pregnancy (hypertension, growth concerns, gestational diabetes); monitor for the development of risk factors at each visit rather than assuming continued low-risk status throughout pregnancy.
    • Tuberculosis contact tracing: Screening of close contacts (symptom screen, chest imaging/testing) is first-line once an index case is identified, with preventive therapy offered to those found to have latent infection, particularly high-risk contacts (children, HIV-positive, immunosuppressed); escalate to wider community screening if transmission beyond the household is suspected; monitor contacts who decline or cannot access preventive therapy for symptom development over the following months.
    • Water-borne disease prevention (community): Safe water supply interventions (protected sources, household treatment) alongside chlorination and hygiene education is first-line; escalate to emergency water trucking/treatment during outbreaks or disaster settings where the usual supply is compromised; monitor water quality testing regularly and disease surveillance data to evaluate intervention effectiveness.
    • Vector control for dengue/malaria (community): Source reduction (eliminating breeding sites) and larvicidal measures are first-line, alongside community education campaigns to sustain behaviour change; escalate to indoor residual spraying or insecticide-treated nets for malaria in high-transmission settings; monitor vector density and disease incidence to guide the intensity and targeting of control measures, adapting to insecticide resistance patterns where relevant.
    • School health screening programme: Structured periodic screening for growth, vision, hearing, and immunisation status is first-line, with referral pathways established for identified abnormalities; monitor screening coverage and follow-up completion rates, as identifying a problem without ensuring referral completion limits programme impact; integrate with broader school health education (nutrition, hygiene) for maximum benefit.
    • Non-communicable disease screening (community): Population-based screening for hypertension, diabetes, and obesity with clear referral pathways to confirmatory testing and management is first-line; monitor screening uptake, particularly in underserved populations where non-communicable disease burden is often highest but access to screening lowest; ensure referral pathways are actually resourced to manage the volume of positive screens identified, avoiding screening without capacity to treat.
    • Occupational noise-induced hearing loss: Workplace hearing protection programmes (engineering controls, personal protective equipment) are first-line prevention, alongside periodic audiometric screening to detect early hearing loss before it becomes disabling; monitor audiometric trends over time in exposed workers, and remove/reassign workers with significant threshold shifts from further high-noise exposure where feasible.
    • Snakebite prevention (rural community): Community education (on avoidance behaviours, appropriate first aid) and appropriate footwear promotion is first-line prevention; ensured antivenom access at accessible health facilities is essential given the time-critical nature of envenomation treatment; monitor antivenom stock and cold-chain maintenance at peripheral facilities, as stockouts in high-incidence rural areas are a major driver of preventable mortality.
  23. Genetics, Metabolic & Congenital Disorders
    • Down syndrome: Multidisciplinary surveillance for associated cardiac, endocrine (thyroid), haematological, and developmental issues is first-line, as there is no curative treatment for the underlying trisomy; escalate to targeted treatment of specific complications as they arise (e.g. cardiac surgery for AV septal defect); monitor growth using Down-syndrome-specific charts, hearing/vision periodically, and thyroid function annually, given the high prevalence of treatable comorbidities that affect development.
    • Phenylketonuria: Lifelong dietary phenylalanine restriction, detected via newborn screening and started as early as possible, is first-line, as delayed treatment risks irreversible intellectual disability; escalate to sapropterin (for responsive genotypes) or pegvaliase as adjunct/alternative therapy in eligible patients with poor dietary control; monitor phenylalanine levels regularly (more frequently in infancy and pregnancy) and reinforce dietary adherence, particularly around puberty when adherence often lapses.
    • Galactosaemia: Immediate lifelong exclusion of galactose/lactose from the diet is first-line, started as soon as diagnosis is suspected (often before genetic confirmation) given risk of rapid deterioration (sepsis, liver failure) with continued exposure; monitor growth, liver function, and developmental progress, as some long-term complications (cognitive, ovarian insufficiency) may occur despite early and strict dietary treatment.
    • Maple syrup urine disease: Dietary restriction of branched-chain amino acids is first-line long-term management, with acute management of metabolic crises (IV glucose/insulin, temporary protein restriction, escalation to dialysis for severe decompensation) during intercurrent illness; monitor plasma amino acid levels regularly and educate families on sick-day management, as intercurrent illness is the most common trigger for life-threatening metabolic crisis.
    • Glycogen storage disease (type I): Frequent feeding/cornstarch therapy to maintain euglycaemia is first-line, with overnight nasogastric feeding or extended-release cornstarch used to prevent nocturnal hypoglycaemia; monitor growth, lactate, uric acid, and lipid profile regularly, and screen for long-term complications (hepatic adenomas, renal disease) with periodic imaging; strict avoidance of fasting is essential even during minor illness.
    • Wilson's disease: Copper chelation therapy (penicillamine or trientine) is first-line for symptomatic disease, with zinc used for maintenance therapy or in presymptomatic/pregnant patients given its lower side-effect profile; dietary copper restriction as an adjunct; monitor liver function, 24-hour urinary copper excretion, and neurological status regularly, as treatment is lifelong and abrupt discontinuation risks rapid decompensation; screen siblings given the autosomal recessive inheritance pattern.
    • Haemochromatosis: Regular therapeutic phlebotomy to reduce iron overload is first-line, with an intensive induction phase followed by maintenance phlebotomy guided by ferritin levels; escalate to iron chelation therapy for patients unable to tolerate phlebotomy (e.g. anaemia, poor venous access); monitor ferritin, transferrin saturation, and screen for end-organ damage (liver, cardiac, endocrine) at diagnosis and periodically; screen first-degree relatives given the genetic basis.
    • Marfan syndrome: Beta-blocker or ARB therapy to slow aortic root dilation is first-line, alongside surveillance echocardiography (and periodic broader aortic imaging) and elective aortic surgery when the aortic diameter or growth rate crosses established thresholds; monitor aortic dimensions serially, restrict high-intensity/contact sports given dissection risk, and screen for lens dislocation and skeletal features as part of comprehensive multisystem surveillance.
    • Neurofibromatosis type 1: Surveillance for tumour development (optic pathway glioma, plexiform neurofibroma, malignant peripheral nerve sheath tumour) is first-line, with management of complications as they arise (surgery for symptomatic tumours, MEK inhibitor therapy for inoperable symptomatic plexiform neurofibromas); monitor growth, blood pressure (renal artery stenosis, phaeochromocytoma risk), vision, and skeletal development (scoliosis) at regular intervals given the multisystem nature of the condition.
    • Tuberous sclerosis complex: mTOR inhibitor therapy (everolimus) for significant tumour burden (subependymal giant cell astrocytoma, renal angiomyolipoma, refractory epilepsy) is first-line where surgery is not appropriate; seizure management (including vigabatrin as first-line for infantile spasms in this condition specifically) and developmental support alongside; monitor with periodic brain and renal imaging, and screen for autism spectrum features and developmental delay proactively.
    • Fragile X syndrome: Supportive developmental, educational, and behavioural intervention is first-line, as there is no curative therapy; target specific behavioural symptoms (hyperactivity, anxiety) pharmacologically where significantly impairing; monitor developmental progress and screen for common comorbidities (ADHD, autism spectrum features, seizures); genetic counselling for the family regarding X-linked inheritance and premutation carrier risks (including premature ovarian insufficiency and tremor-ataxia syndrome in carriers) is important.
    • Congenital adrenal hyperplasia: Lifelong glucocorticoid replacement (and mineralocorticoid replacement if salt-wasting) is first-line, with stress dosing during illness/surgery to prevent adrenal crisis; monitor growth, bone age, and biochemical markers (17-hydroxyprogesterone, electrolytes) regularly to balance adequate androgen suppression against growth suppression from over-treatment; educate families thoroughly on emergency stress-dose administration and provide a steroid emergency card/injection kit.
    • Cystic fibrosis (CFTR modulator eligible): CFTR modulator therapy (e.g. elexacaftor-tezacaftor-ivacaftor) for eligible mutations is first-line where genotype allows, alongside continued airway clearance and pancreatic enzyme replacement as needed; monitor lung function, liver function (modulators can affect hepatic enzymes), and growth on treatment; modulator therapy has transformed prognosis in eligible patients but does not eliminate the need for ongoing multidisciplinary CF care.
    • Sickle cell disease (chronic management): Hydroxyurea to reduce vaso-occlusive crisis frequency is first-line for most patients with recurrent crises, with transfusion therapy (simple or exchange) for select complications (stroke prevention, acute chest syndrome, pre-operative optimisation); monitor for hydroxyurea-related cytopenias and long-term organ damage (renal, pulmonary, retinal) with periodic screening; penicillin prophylaxis and pneumococcal vaccination in childhood reduce infection risk from functional asplenia.
    • Gaucher disease: Enzyme replacement therapy (imiglucerase) or substrate reduction therapy (for those unable to tolerate/access enzyme replacement) is first-line for type 1 disease; monitor haematological parameters, organomegaly, and bone involvement (via imaging) to assess treatment response; type 2/3 neuronopathic disease has more limited treatment options given poor CNS penetration of current enzyme replacement therapies.
    • Pompe disease: Enzyme replacement therapy (alglucosidase alfa) is first-line, with earlier initiation in infantile-onset disease strongly associated with better outcomes given rapid cardiac and respiratory deterioration otherwise; monitor cardiac function, respiratory status, and motor development closely, particularly in the infantile form; screen for antibody development against the enzyme, which can reduce treatment efficacy over time.
    • Mucopolysaccharidosis (type I): Enzyme replacement therapy is first-line for attenuated disease, with haematopoietic stem cell transplant considered for severe early-onset (Hurler) disease, ideally performed early before significant neurological involvement develops; monitor growth, joint mobility, cardiac and respiratory function, and neurodevelopment; enzyme replacement does not cross the blood-brain barrier, limiting its effect on neurological manifestations in severe disease.
    • Osteogenesis imperfecta: Bisphosphonate therapy to increase bone density and reduce fracture rate is first-line for moderate-severe disease; supportive orthopaedic management (fracture care, rodding for recurrent long bone fractures) and physiotherapy alongside; monitor bone density, growth, and hearing (progressive hearing loss is common); genetic counselling given variable inheritance patterns across the different types.
    • Achondroplasia: Symptomatic management of complications (spinal stenosis, sleep apnoea, recurrent otitis media) is first-line, as this is the mainstay of care; growth hormone therapy has limited height benefit and is not routinely used, while limb-lengthening or CNP-analogue therapy (vosoritide, where available) are options in select cases; monitor for foramen magnum stenosis in infancy (a cause of sudden death if severe) and spinal stenosis in adulthood proactively with periodic assessment.
    • Ehlers-Danlos syndrome: Supportive management (physiotherapy for joint stabilisation, joint protection strategies, pain management) is first-line, as there is no curative therapy; monitor for vascular complications specifically in the vascular subtype (arterial/organ rupture risk), which requires closer surveillance and activity modification than the more common hypermobile subtype; screen for cardiac valve involvement and counsel on pregnancy risks in the vascular subtype given significant maternal risk.
    • Turner syndrome: Growth hormone therapy in childhood to optimise final height is first-line, with oestrogen replacement introduced from the expected age of puberty to induce secondary sexual characteristics and protect bone health; monitor cardiac (coarctation, bicuspid aortic valve), renal, thyroid, and hearing status regularly given multisystem associations; fertility counselling is important given the high prevalence of ovarian insufficiency.
    • Klinefelter syndrome: Testosterone replacement therapy from the expected age of puberty is first-line as needed for hypogonadism, supporting secondary sexual characteristic development and bone/muscle health; monitor for learning difficulties, gynaecomastia, and metabolic syndrome features, which are more common in this condition; discuss fertility options early, as most affected men have impaired spermatogenesis though assisted reproduction may be possible.
    • Prader-Willi syndrome: Growth hormone therapy is first-line, improving height, body composition, and muscle tone, alongside structured dietary management (calorie restriction, food security measures) for hyperphagia, which is the dominant long-term management challenge; monitor growth, weight, and behavioural/psychiatric symptoms (which are common and can be severe) closely; screen for sleep apnoea and scoliosis, and involve a multidisciplinary team given the complexity of lifelong management.
    • Angelman syndrome: Supportive developmental and seizure management is first-line, as there is no curative therapy for the underlying genetic cause; monitor seizure control closely given epilepsy is common and can be difficult to control; involve multidisciplinary support (speech/communication aids given typically absent or limited verbal speech, physiotherapy for gait abnormalities, behavioural support) tailored to the individual's needs and communication profile.
  24. Radiology & Interventional Management
    • Acute ischaemic stroke (large vessel occlusion): Endovascular mechanical thrombectomy within the eligible time window (extended further with favourable perfusion imaging) is first-line, alongside IV thrombolysis where not contraindicated and within its own separate time window; monitor neurological status and repeat imaging for haemorrhagic transformation post-procedure; time from symptom onset to reperfusion is the dominant driver of outcome, making rapid triage and transfer to a thrombectomy-capable centre essential.
    • Symptomatic uterine fibroids (fertility-sparing): Uterine artery embolisation is first-line as a minimally invasive alternative to surgery for women wishing to avoid myomectomy/hysterectomy or preserve the uterus; monitor for post-embolisation syndrome (pain, fever, malaise in the days following the procedure, usually self-limiting) and for fibroid expulsion; counsel that fertility outcomes after embolisation are less well-established than after myomectomy, and discuss this alongside surgical alternatives for those specifically pursuing pregnancy.
    • Hepatocellular carcinoma (unresectable): Transarterial chemoembolisation or radiofrequency/microwave ablation depending on tumour characteristics (size, number, location) is first-line for unresectable disease confined to the liver with preserved liver function; escalate to systemic therapy for more advanced or refractory disease; monitor liver function and imaging response, and screen for post-embolisation syndrome; treatment choice is guided by staging systems that integrate tumour burden with underlying liver function.
    • Biliary obstruction (malignant, unresectable): Percutaneous transhepatic or endoscopic biliary stenting for decompression is first-line for symptomatic relief of jaundice/cholangitis where surgical resection is not feasible; monitor for stent occlusion/cholangitis over time, with stent exchange or upsizing as needed; choice between percutaneous and endoscopic approach depends on the level of obstruction and local expertise/anatomy.
    • Abscess (deep-seated, e.g. hepatic/renal): Image-guided percutaneous drainage with concurrent targeted antibiotics is first-line for most deep-seated abscesses amenable to a safe drainage route; escalate to surgical drainage if percutaneous access is unsafe, the abscess is multiloculated and not responding, or there is associated bowel perforation requiring source control; monitor clinical response, inflammatory markers, and repeat imaging to confirm resolution before catheter removal.
    • Peripheral arterial occlusive disease (critical limb ischaemia): Endovascular angioplasty/stenting is first-line for suitable lesion patterns given lower morbidity than surgery; surgical bypass for extensive disease not amenable to endovascular repair or where durability requirements favour surgery; monitor limb perfusion, wound healing, and patency with surveillance imaging; time to revascularisation is critical in critical limb ischaemia given amputation risk with delay.
    • Pulmonary embolism (intermediate-high risk): Catheter-directed thrombolysis is an option for select patients with a contraindication to (or failure of) systemic thrombolysis, alongside standard anticoagulation; escalate to surgical embolectomy or ECMO for massive PE with haemodynamic collapse refractory to other measures; monitor haemodynamic status, right heart strain on echocardiography, and bleeding risk closely given the competing risks of thrombolytic therapy.
    • Varicocele (image-guided): Percutaneous embolisation is first-line as a minimally invasive alternative to surgical ligation, particularly for symptomatic varicocele or those affecting fertility; monitor semen parameters where fertility is the indication, and for varicocele recurrence post-embolisation; both embolisation and surgical approaches have comparable efficacy, with choice often guided by local expertise and patient preference.
    • Deep vein thrombosis (iliofemoral, limb-threatening): Catheter-directed thrombolysis is first-line to reduce post-thrombotic syndrome risk in select patients with extensive iliofemoral DVT and low bleeding risk, alongside standard anticoagulation; monitor for bleeding complications during thrombolysis and for adequate anticoagulation continuation afterward; phlegmasia cerulea dolens with limb-threatening ischaemia warrants urgent intervention given risk of venous gangrene.
    • Gastrointestinal bleeding (refractory to endoscopy): Transarterial embolisation of the bleeding vessel is first-line once endoscopic haemostasis has failed or is not feasible; escalate to surgery if embolisation fails to control bleeding or is not technically possible given vascular anatomy; monitor haemodynamic status and haemoglobin closely, and identify the bleeding source with CT angiography or tagged red cell scan pre-procedure where the source is unclear.
    • Nephrostomy for obstructed infected kidney: Percutaneous nephrostomy placement for urgent decompression is first-line in an obstructed, infected kidney (infected hydronephrosis), given the risk of urosepsis with delay; concurrent IV antibiotics alongside drainage; monitor for improvement in sepsis markers and renal function post-drainage, and plan definitive treatment of the underlying obstruction (stone removal, stricture management) once the acute infection is controlled.
    • Portal hypertension (refractory variceal bleeding/ascites): Transjugular intrahepatic portosystemic shunt (TIPS) is first-line for variceal bleeding refractory to endoscopic and pharmacological therapy, or for refractory ascites requiring frequent large-volume paracentesis; monitor for hepatic encephalopathy post-TIPS, a common complication given the portosystemic shunting; assess liver function and cardiac status pre-procedure, as TIPS is contraindicated in significant hepatic or cardiac decompensation.
    • Renal artery stenosis (fibromuscular dysplasia): Percutaneous transluminal angioplasty is first-line, often curative in this population (younger patients with fibromuscular disease, distinct from atherosclerotic renal artery stenosis where medical therapy is usually preferred); monitor blood pressure response and renal function post-procedure; stenting is generally reserved for angioplasty failure or dissection given good angioplasty-alone outcomes in fibromuscular disease.
    • Bronchial artery bleeding (haemoptysis): Bronchial artery embolisation is first-line for massive or recurrent haemoptysis once the source is localised (bronchoscopy/CT angiography); monitor for recurrent bleeding, which can occur from collateral vessels not embolised initially or from the original vessel if incompletely occluded; identify and treat the underlying cause (bronchiectasis, tuberculosis, malignancy) alongside acute haemorrhage control.
    • Pelvic congestion syndrome: Ovarian/pelvic vein embolisation is first-line for confirmed symptomatic disease after excluding other causes of chronic pelvic pain; monitor symptom response over weeks to months, as improvement is often gradual; recurrence or incomplete symptom relief may prompt evaluation for additional incompetent veins requiring further embolisation.
    • Splenic artery aneurysm: Endovascular coil embolisation is first-line for aneurysms above the size threshold for intervention (or smaller aneurysms in pregnancy/women of childbearing age given rupture risk in pregnancy) rather than open surgical repair given lower morbidity; monitor for post-embolisation splenic infarction (usually well-tolerated if partial) and confirm aneurysm exclusion with follow-up imaging; asymptomatic small aneurysms below threshold are typically monitored with serial imaging rather than treated.
